On Rough Frobenius-type Theorems
Liding Yao

Abstract




On Rough Frobenius-type Theorems and Their Hölder Estimates
†
†
footnotetext:
The work in this paper represents the author’s PhD thesis at the University of Wisconsin-Madison. It includes the results in
arXiv:2002.07973
,
arXiv:2004.07288
,
arXiv:2202.07729
and a part of
arXiv:2105.10120
.

Liding Yao

Abstract

The thesis is dedicated to giving several results on Frobenius-type theorems in non-smooth settings, and giving Hölder regularity estimates for the respective coordinate systems.

For the real Frobenius theorem, we extend the definition of involutivity to non-Lipschitz tangent subbundles using generalized functions. We prove the Frobenius Theorem with sharp regularity when the subbundle is log-Lipschitz: if
\mathcal{V}
is a log-Lipschitz involutive subbundle of rank
r
, then for any
\varepsilon>0
, locally there is a homeomorphism
\Phi(u,v)
such that
\Phi,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
, and
\mathcal{V}
is spanned by the continuous vector fields
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{r}}
.

We also develop a singular version of the Frobenius theorem on log-Lipschitz vector fields. If
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are log-Lipschitz vector fields such that
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for some generalized functions
c_{ij}^{k}
that can be written as the derivatives of log-Lipschitz functions, then for any point
p
there is a
C^{1}
-manifold
\mathfrak{S}
containing
p
such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
span the tangent space at every point in
\mathfrak{S}
.

Nirenberg’s famous complex Frobenius theorem gives necessary and sufficient conditions on a locally integrable structure for when the manifold is locally diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
through a coordinate chart
F
in such a way that the structure is locally spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
, where we have given
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
coordinates
(t,z,s)
. When the structures are differentiable, we give the optimal Hölder-Zygmund regularity for the coordinate charts which achieve this realization. Namely, if the structure has Hölder-Zygmund regularity of order
\alpha>1
, then the coordinate chart
F
that maps to
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
may be taken to have Hölder-Zygmund regularity of order
\alpha
, and this is sharp. Furthermore, we can choose this
F
in such a way that the vector fields
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
on the original manifold have Hölder-Zygmund regularity of order
\alpha-\varepsilon
for every
\varepsilon>0
, and we give an example to show that the regularity for
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
is optimal.

Similarly we give a counterexample for the
C^{k}
-version of the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem: we give an example of
C^{k}
-integrable almost complex structure that does not admit a corresponding
C^{k+1}
-complex coordinate system.

Combining the technics from the log-Lipschitz real Frobenius theorem and the sharp complex Frobenius theorem, we show that if a complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
(
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq 1
) such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is log-Lipschitz, then for every
0<\varepsilon<2\alpha-1
there is a homeomorphism
\Phi(t,z,s)
such that
\Phi,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}
, and
\mathcal{S}
is spanned by the
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}
vector fields
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

On the quantitative side, we show that if
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are log-Lipschitz real vector fields such that
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
holds for some
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
where
1<\alpha<2
, then on each leaf where
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
span the tangent spaces we can find a regular parameterization
\Phi
such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, and their
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
norm depend only on the diffeomorphic invariant quantities of
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
, like the norms
\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{C^{0,\alpha-1}_{X}}
.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a smooth manifold, and let
\mathcal{S}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
(or
\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
) be a real or complex tangent subbundle. We call
\mathcal{S}

involutive
, if for any (real or complex) vector fields
X,Y
that are sections of
\mathcal{S}
, their Lie bracket
[X,Y]
is also a section of
\mathcal{S}
.

The Frobenius-type theorems describe how certain types of involutive subbundles are related to the span of coordinate vector fields. In the thesis, we are going to talk about the following theorems in nonsmooth settings: Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional smooth manifold,
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a smooth involutive subbundle, and
p\in\mathfrak{M}
be a fixed point.

•

The real Frobenius theorem: If
1
1

1

We use
\overline{\mathcal{S}}
as the complex conjugate subbundle, see Definition
2.3.12
.

\mathcal{S}=\overline{\mathcal{S}}
, then there is a smooth parameterization
\Phi(t,s):\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}\to\mathfrak{M}
near
p
such that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,s)
span
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi(t,s)}
for all
(t,s)\in\Omega
. Here
r=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}
.

•

The Newlander-Nirenberg theorem: If
\mathcal{S}\oplus\overline{\mathcal{S}}=\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
, then there is a smooth parameterization
\Phi(z):\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
near
p
such that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}}(z),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}}(z)
span
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi(z)}
for all
z\in\Omega
. Here
m=n/2
.

•

Nirenberg’s complex Frobenius theorem: If
\mathcal{S}+\overline{\mathcal{S}}
is also a smooth subbundle and is involutive, then there is a smooth parameterization
\Phi(t,z,s):\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r-2m}\to\mathfrak{M}
near
p
such that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,z,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,z,s),\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}}(t,z,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}}(t,z,s)
span
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi(t,z,s)}
for all
(t,z,s)\in\Omega
. Here
r=\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\overline{\mathcal{S}})
and
m=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}-r
. This result generalizes the above two special cases.

In the thesis we discuss several estimates of these type of theorems in the non-smooth setting, as well as some examples that indicate some of the Hölder exponents are optimal.

We remark that for the results in Section
1.1
, Theorem
1
follows from
[
Yao20
]
; Theorems
3
and
6
follow from
[
Yao22
]
; Theorem
7
follows from
[
Yao21
]
; and Theorem
8
is the part of
[
SY22
]
. The Theorems
2
,
4
and
5
are new results.

1.1
The Main Results

For
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
, let
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
be the class of Hölder-Zygmund functions
2
2

2

For non-integer exponents, the Hölder-Zygmund space agrees with the classical Hölder space. When
\alpha\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have
C^{\alpha-1,1}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. See Remark
2.1.9
.
of order
\alpha
(See Definitions
1.2.1
). Let
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}=\bigcap_{\varepsilon>0}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}=\bigcup_{\varepsilon>0}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
. We use
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}=C^{\infty}
as the class of smooth functions.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open subset, we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}
the class of Lipschitz functions.
We say a continuous function
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is bounded
log-Lipschitz
, denoted by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, if

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})}:=\sup_{x\in U}|f(x)|+\sup_{x,y\in U;0<|x-y|<1}|f(x)-f(y)|\Big(|x-y|\log\frac{e}{|x-y|}\Big)^{-1}<\infty.

We say
f:U\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is bounded
little log-Lipschitz
, denoted by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})
, if in addition
\lim_{r\to 0}\sup_{x,y\in U;0<|x-y|<r}|f(x)-f(y)|(|x-y|\log\frac{e}{|x-y|})^{-1}=0
. See Definition
2.1.20
.

Note that we have
\mathscr{C}^{1+\varepsilon}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{1}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{1-}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
.

For
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
-subbundles (in particular for log-Lipschitz subbundles) we introduce the notion of distributional
3
3

3

Throughout the thesis, we use
distributions
for
generalized functions
, which are defined to be linear functionals on some test function spaces. For the corresponding terminology in differential geometry, we always use
tangent subbundles
, or
singular tangent subbundles
.
involutivity as follows.

Definition 0
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{3}{2}+}
manifold and let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
complex tangent subbundle. We say
\mathcal{S}
is
involutive in the sense of distributions
, if for every
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
vector fields
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
,
Y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
on
\mathcal{S}
and every
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
1-form
\theta=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\theta_{k}dx^{k}
on
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
, the following generalized function

\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle:=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\Big(\theta_{i}X^{j}\frac{\partial Y^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}-\theta_{j}Y^{i}\frac{\partial X^{j}}{\partial x^{i}}\Big),

(1.1.1)

is identically zero in the sense of distributions on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Here
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}:=\{(p,l)\in\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}:p\in\mathfrak{M},\ l(v)=0,\forall v\in\mathcal{S}_{p}\}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
is the dual bundle of
\mathcal{S}
.
Note that the right hand side of (
1.1.1
) is not necessarily a
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-function. It makes sense as a distribution, see Lemma
2.1.18
and Corollary
2.1.19
. Also see Definition
3.1.1
. Replacing real subbundles by complex subbbundles, the same definition also work for complex subbundles.

We see that distributional involutivity is the natural generalization to the classical involutivity. See
3.2
for a detailed discussion.

The result on real Frobenius theorem is the following:

Theorem 1

(The Frobenius theorem for log-Lipschitz subbundles)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be
n
-dimensional
C^{1,1}
manifold, and let
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be a rank-
r
log-Lipschitz real tangent subbundle, which is involutive in the sense of distributions.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there is a neighborhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}_{s}
of
0
and a continuous map
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
such that

(1)

\Phi(0)=p
.

(2)

\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
is a topological embedding, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}:\Omega\to T\mathfrak{M}
are continuous.

In order words the pushforward vector fields
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\circ\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
(
1\leq j\leq r
) are defined pointwise.

(3)

\mathcal{V}|_{\Phi(\Omega)}
is spanned by
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
. Equivalently, for any
(t,s)\in\Omega
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,s)\in T_{\Phi(t,s)}\mathfrak{M}
form a basis of the (real) linear subspace
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,s)}
.

Moreover,

(4)

For any
\varepsilon>0
there is an open subset
\Omega_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\Omega
of
(0,0)
such that
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};\mathfrak{M})
, its inverse
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Phi(\Omega_{\varepsilon});\mathfrak{M})
and the partial derivatives
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\Omega_{\varepsilon};T\mathfrak{M})
.

(4’)

Suppose
\mathcal{V}
is little log-Lipschitz, then automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}(\Omega;\mathfrak{M})
,
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}(\Phi(\Omega);\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}(\Omega;T\mathfrak{M})
.

(4”)

Let
\alpha\in\{\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup(1,\infty]
. Suppose
\mathfrak{M}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
, then automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathfrak{M})
,
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Phi(\Omega);\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;T\mathfrak{M})
.

Here
\mathscr{C}^{k+\mathrm{Lip}}=C^{k,1}=W^{k+1,\infty}
,
k=0,1,2,\dots
are the classical Lipschitz spaces. See Definition
2.1.20
.

Remark
.

For a general version of Theorem
1
, see Theorem
5.1
. Here Theorem
1
is slightly stronger than
[
Yao20
, Theorems 1.4 and 1.7]
, where we treat the cases of log-Lipschitz and little log-Lipschitz separately.

The result Theorem
1

(4)
shows that both
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
. In general
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
does not imply
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
and vice versa, since we do not have the inverse function theorem for
\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
-maps. This
\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
-regularity is sharp, in the sense that there exists a log-Lipschitz subbundle such that either
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{1-}
or
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\notin\mathscr{C}^{1-}
, see Corollary
5.3.2

(ii)
.

By considering the setting of singular tangent subbundles, as an analog to
[
MM13
]
we have the following

Theorem 2

(A singular Frobenius theorem on log-Lipschitz vector fields)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be some locally log-Lipschitz vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose there are some functions
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
(see Definitions
2.1.21
and
3.1.1
) such that in the sense of distributions in
\mathfrak{M}
,

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq m.

(1.1.2)

Then the span of
X_{1},\dots,X_{q}
is pointwisely integrable. That is, for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a
r
-dimensional
C^{1}
embedded submanifold
\mathfrak{S}
containing
p
, where
r
is the rank of
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}|_{p},\dots,X_{q}|_{p})\leq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
, such that
X_{1}|_{\mathfrak{S}},\dots,X_{q}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
are vector fields on
\mathfrak{S}
and span the tangent space of
\mathfrak{S}
at every point.

Moreover, the Carnot-Carathéodory ball
B_{(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})}(p,\infty)
is an immerse submanifold, where
\mathfrak{S}\subset B_{X}(p,\infty)
is an open subset with respect to the (immersed) differential structure.

Here for a collection
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
of
C^{0}
-vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
and for
R\in(0,\infty]
, we use
B_{X}(p,R)=\{q\in\mathfrak{M}:\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q)<R\}
where

\operatorname{dist}_{X}(x,y):=\inf\Big\{d>0:\exists\gamma\in C^{0,1}([0,d];\mathfrak{M}),\gamma(0)=x,\gamma(d)=y\text{ s.t. }\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}a_{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma(t))\text{ where }\sum_{j=1}^{m}|a_{j}(t)|^{2}\leq 1\Big\}.

(1.1.3)

In fact we can take
\mathfrak{S}=B_{X}(p,R)
for every
R<\infty
. The set
B_{X}(p,\infty)
may not be an embedded submanifold as we can consider an irrational torus in
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Remark
.

See Section
3.4
for a further discussion of singular tangent subbundles and singular Frobenius theorems. I would like to thank Sylvain Lavau and Andrew Lewis for many informative discussion on the related concepts and theorems.

For complex Frobenius theorems we have the following:

Theorem 3

(Sharp Hölder regularity for Nirenberg’s complex Frobenius theorem)
.

Let
\alpha\in(1,\infty)
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
n
-dimensional smooth manifold, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure such that
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
. Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there is a neighborhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r-2m}_{s}
of
0
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-regular parameterization
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
(i.e.
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\Phi(\Omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
) near
p
, such that

(1)

\Phi(0)=p
.

(2)

For any
(t,z,s)\in\Omega
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,z,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,z,s),\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}}(t,z,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}}(t,z,s)\in\mathbb{C}T_{\Phi(t,z,s)}\mathfrak{M}
form a basis of the complex linear subspace
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi(t,z,s)}
.

(3)

\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
-complex vector fields on
\Phi(\Omega)
.

Suppose there is a
\beta\in[\alpha+1,\infty)
such that
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-complex tangential subbundle. Then we can find a
\Phi
such that in addition to the results above, we have

(4)

\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-vector fields on
\Phi(\Omega)
.

We remark that the constructions of
\Phi
for
(1)
-
(3)
and for
(1)
-
(4)
are different.

Combining the real and the complex Frobenius Theorem (Theorems
1
and
3
), along with the definition of distributional involutivity (Definition
0
), we have the following:

Theorem 4

(Estimates of non-differentiable complex Frobenius structures I)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
n
-dimensional smooth manifold, and let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure such that
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
. Assume the subbundle
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is log-Lipschitz. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
and any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
\Omega=\Omega_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r-2m}_{s}
of
0
and a map
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
near
p
, such that

(1)

\Phi(0)=p
.

(2)

\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
is a topological embedding, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
are continuous.

(3)

\mathcal{S}|_{\Phi(\Omega)}
is spanned by
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

(4)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}(\Omega;\mathfrak{M})
,
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}(\Phi(\Omega);\Omega)
and
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}
vector fields on
\Phi(\Omega)
.

Suppose there is a
\beta\in\{1+\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup(2,\infty)
such that
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. Then we can find a (possibly different)
\Phi
such that in addition to the results above, we have

(5)

\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
vector fields on
\Phi(\Omega)
.

If
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is little-Log Lipschitz, then all the
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}
regularity estimates in Theorem
4
can be improved by
\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}
. If
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is Lipschitz, then they can all improved to be
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}
:

Theorem 5

(Estimates of non-differentiable complex Frobenius structures II)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
, and let
\mathfrak{M},\mathcal{S}
be as in the assumption of Theorem
4
.

Suppose the subbundle
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is little log-Lipschitz. Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r-2m}_{s}
of
0
and a map
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
near
p
, such that the results
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
in Theorem
4
hold, with regularity estimate:

(4’)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}(\Omega;\mathfrak{M})
,
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}(\Phi(\Omega);\Omega)
and
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}
vector fields on
\Phi(\Omega)
.

Suppose
\alpha<1
and the subbundle
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is Lipschitz. Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r-2m}_{s}
of
0
and a map
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
near
p
, such that the results
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
in Theorem
4
hold, with regularity estimate:

(4”)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}(\Omega;\mathfrak{M})
,
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}(\Phi(\Omega);\Omega)
and
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}
vector fields on
\Phi(\Omega)
.

Suppose there is a
\beta\in\{1+\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup(2,\infty)
such that
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. Then we can find a (possibly different)
\Phi
such that
(5)
in Theorem
4
also holds.

For a general version of Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
, see Theorems
5.4
and
5.5
in Section
5.2.2
. Also see Section
5.2.1
for an overview of the proofs and their historical remarks.

In Theorem
3
(that
\alpha>1
), the results
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
are all optimal, in the sense that given
\alpha>1
, we have examples of complex Frobenius structures
\mathcal{S}
, such that if
\Phi
is a
C^{1}
-chart representing
\mathcal{S}
, then
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, respectively. See Section
5.3.0
. In Section
5.3.3
we construct an example that meets the following:

Theorem 6

(Sharpness of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
in Theorem
3
)
.

Let
\alpha>1
. There is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, such that
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=1
,
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=2
, and there does not exist a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
F:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{3})
.

See also Corollary
5.3.20
for a generalization.
The construction for Theorem
6
is modified from a counterexample to
C^{k}
-regularity for the Newlander-Nirenberg Theorem:

Theorem 7

(A counterexample to
C^{k}
-regularity for the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem)
.

For every positive integer
k
there is a
C^{k}
integrable almost complex structure
J
on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that we cannot find a neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
of
0
and a complex coordinate chart
\phi\in C^{k+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{n})
which maps
J
to the standard complex structure.

So far all results above are qualitative, since we do not have additional objects to label the scale. When we are handed in a specified collection of generators
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
for our subbundle
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
(or
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
), we can further ask what is the behaviour of the vector fields under the pullback of
\Phi
, and whether we are allowed to choose a good
\Phi
such that the new vector fields
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
are “normalized”.

In a collaboration with my advisor Brian Street, we manage to give the following:

Theorem 8

(A quantitative Frobenius theorem in low regularity)
.

Let
n,m\geq 1
,
\alpha\in(1,2)
,
\mu_{0}>0
and
M_{0}>0
. There are constants
\widehat{K}=\widehat{K}(n,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0})>0
and
K_{0}=K_{0}(n,m,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0})>0
that satisfy the following:

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold and let
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be log-Lipschitz real vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose
n:=\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(p_{0}),\dots,X_{m}(p_{0}))
and by re-ordering the indices we have the following:

•

|X_{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge X_{i_{n}}(p_{0})|\leq M_{0}|X_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge X_{n}(p_{0})|
for all
1\leq i_{1},\dots,i_{n}\leq m
.

•

The canonical map
t\mapsto e^{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{n}X_{n}}(p_{0})
is defined for
t\in B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})
.

•

For every
0<r\leq\mu_{0}
and every
p
such that
e^{t\cdot X}(p)
is defined for
t\in B^{n}(0,r)
, we have
e^{t\cdot X}(p)\neq p
for all
t\in B^{n}(0,r)\backslash\{0\}
and
p\in B_{X}(p_{0},r)
.

•

We have
(c_{ij}^{k})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i,j\leq m\\
1\leq k\leq n\end{subarray}}
such that

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with }\sup_{i,j,k}\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{C^{0,\alpha-1}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})}<M_{0}.

(1.1.4)

Then for any
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a map
\Phi:B^{n}(0,1)\to\mathfrak{M}
such that

•

\Phi(0)=p_{0}
and
\Phi
is
\mathscr{C}^{2-}
-diffeomorphism onto its image.

•

\Phi^{*}X^{\prime}=[\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}]^{\top}
and
\Phi^{*}X^{\prime\prime}=[\Phi^{*}X_{n+1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}]^{\top}
are collections of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields on
B^{n}(0,1)
that can be written as

\Phi^{*}X^{\prime}=\widehat{K}(I+A^{\prime})\nabla,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{*}X^{\prime\prime}=\widehat{K}A^{\prime\prime}\nabla.

(1.1.5)

Where
A=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}\\
A^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m\times n})
satisfy
A^{\prime}(0)=0
,
\|A^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and
\|A^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{(m-n)\times n})}\leq K_{0}
.

Here
C^{0,\alpha-1}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})\subset C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
is the Hölder space along with vector fields
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
, whose norm is given by the following: recall (
1.1.3
) for the Carnot-Carathéodory distance,

\|f\|_{C^{0,\alpha-1}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})}:=\|f\|_{C^{0}}+\sup\limits_{x,y\in\mathfrak{M};x\neq y}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{\operatorname{dist}_{X}(x,y)^{\alpha-1}}.

(1.1.6)

The results for
\alpha\geq 2
is also true, with some modifications on the spaces
C^{0,\alpha-1}_{X}
. The case
\alpha>2
is already known in
[
Str21
, Theorem 2.14]
. There is also an analogy involving complex vector fields, see
[
Str20c
]
.

1.2
Notations and Organizations

We use
distributions
for generalized functions, rather than tangential subbundles on manifolds.

Given a bijection
f:U\to V
, we use
f^{\mathrm{Inv}}:V\to U
as the inverse function of
f
. We do not use the notion
f^{-1}
in order to reduce confusion when
(\cdot)^{-1}
is used for inverting a matrix.

We use
A\Subset B
to denote that
A
is a relatively compact subset of
B
.

By
A\lesssim B
we mean that
A\leq CB
where
C
is a constant independent of
A,B
. We use
A\approx B
for “
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
”. And we use
A\lesssim_{x}B
to emphasize the constant dependence on quantity
x
.

We use
\mathbb{B}^{n}=B^{n}(0,1)
as the unit ball in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
r\mathbb{B}^{n}=B^{n}(0,r)
for
r>0
. We use a complex cone
\mathbb{H}^{n}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},4|y|<1-|x|\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

For functions
f:U\to\mathbb{R}
,
g:V\to\mathbb{R}
, we use the tensor notation
f\otimes g:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
as
(f\otimes g)(x,y):=f(x)g(y)
.

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
the class of Hölder-Zygmund functions:

Definition 1.2.1
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty]
, let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a convex open subset and let
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach space. The space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
of bounded (
\mathscr{X}
vector-valued) Hölder-Zygmund functions on
\Omega
, is given recursively as follows:

(i)

For
0<\alpha<1
:
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
consists of those continuous functions
f:\Omega\to\mathscr{X}
such that:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y\end{subarray}}|x-y|^{-\alpha}|f(x)-f(y)|_{\mathscr{X}}<\infty.

(ii)

For
\alpha=1
:
\mathscr{C}^{1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
consists of those continuous functions
f:\Omega\to\mathscr{X}
such that:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y\end{subarray}}|x-y|^{-1}|\frac{f(x)+f(y)}{2}-f(\frac{x+y}{2})|_{\mathscr{X}}<\infty.

(iii)

For
\alpha>1
:
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
consists of
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
such that the following norm is finite:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{j}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}.

(iv)

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):=\bigcap_{\beta>0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):=\bigcap_{\beta<\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
, both endowed with the standard limit Fréchet topologies. That is, we say
f_{k}\to f_{0}
in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
if
f_{k}\to f_{0}
in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
for all
\beta<\alpha
. See also Remark
2.1.10
.

(v)

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):=\bigcup_{\beta>\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
, whose topologies are not used in the thesis.

(vi)

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
,
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
to be the spaces of all
f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
such that for every precompact open
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
, we have
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathscr{X})
,
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathscr{X})
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathscr{X})
respectively. (See Remark
2.1.10
.)

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R})
for
\beta\in\{\alpha,\alpha-,\alpha+:\alpha>0\}\cup\{\infty\}
.

We use
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)}
either when
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
is a function or when the codomain of
f
is clear. And we use
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
when both the domain and the codomain are clear.

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
for
\alpha\leq 0
in Definition
2.1.6
using the Besov space
\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{s}
be two open subsets. For
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty]
, we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)
the space of functions on
U\times V
that is bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
in
x
and bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in
s
separately, see Definition
2.2.12
. For
\alpha,\beta\in(-\infty,\infty]
we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
the space of functions on
U\times V
that is bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
in
x
and bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in
s
simultaneously, see Definition
2.2.1
. In most of the cases we work on the spaces like
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(U,V)
.

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\Omega)
and
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}(\Omega)
as the spaces of functions on
\Omega
with are bounded log-Lipschitz and bounded Lipschitz respectively. For
k\geq 1
, we use
\mathscr{C}^{k+\mathrm{LogL}}(\Omega)
and
\mathscr{C}^{k+\mathrm{Lip}}(\Omega)
as the spaces of function whose first
k
-derivatives are log-Lipschitz/ Lipschitz. See Definition
2.1.20
.

For the index of regularity, we use the following extended index set from Definition
2.1.23
:

\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}:=\{\alpha,\alpha-:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=-1,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}.

For
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
, we define order
\sigma_{1}\leq\sigma_{2}
if
\mathscr{C}^{\sigma_{1}}\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\sigma_{2}}
.

We denote by
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}:=\{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}:\sigma>\mathrm{Lip}-1\}
the set of positive generalized indices. Equivalently
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subset C^{0}
.

We denote by
\mathbb{R}^{-}:=\{\alpha-:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{-}:=\{\alpha-:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
.

We use the convention
\infty=\infty+1=\infty-1=\infty+=\infty-=\frac{\infty}{2}
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, we use the formal operations
(\alpha-)+\beta=\alpha+(\beta-)=(\alpha-)+(\beta-):=(\alpha+\beta)-
; and if
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, we use
(\alpha-)\cdot\beta=\alpha\cdot(\beta-)=(\alpha-)\cdot(\beta-):=(\alpha\beta)-
. See Convention
2.1.25
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
such that
\alpha,\beta>0
, we use
\alpha\circ\beta
to be the unique positive index in
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
such that
f\circ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for every
m,n\geq 1
,
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
. See Definition
2.1.29
and Corollary
2.1.31
.

In Section
4.1
we use notations
\beta^{\sim\alpha}
,
\beta^{\wedge\alpha}
for
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\mathbb{R}_{+}^{-}
, that labels the Hölder exponents for the coordinate charts and the coordinate vector fields (see (
4.1.1
)): for
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\sim\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma,\alpha+1\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma^{\wedge\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma-,\alpha+1\right),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\alpha\in(1,\infty);

\displaystyle\gamma^{\sim\alpha}=\gamma^{\wedge\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma-,(2-\tfrac{1}{\alpha})\gamma,\alpha+1\right),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\alpha\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1];

\displaystyle(\gamma-)^{\sim\alpha}=(\gamma-)^{\wedge\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma-,(2-\tfrac{1}{\alpha})\gamma-,\alpha+1\right),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\alpha\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\infty).

For the matrix space
\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
, we use the standard
\ell^{2}
-matrix norm, that is the operator norm of the linear map
(\mathbb{R}^{n},\ell^{2})\to(\mathbb{R}^{m},\ell^{2})
:

|A|_{\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}}=|A|_{\ell^{2}}:=\sup\limits_{v\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}}|A\cdot v|_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}/|v|_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}.

(1.2.1)

Note that for
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times n}
and
B\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
we have
|AB|\leq|A||B|
. In particular
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a Banach algebra.

Given a coordinate chart
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
, we write
dx:=[dx^{1},\dots,dx^{n}]
as a row vector and
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}:=\begin{bmatrix}\partial_{x^{1}}\\
\vdots\\
\partial_{x^{n}}\end{bmatrix}
as a column vector. We may use
X
sometimes as a single vector field, and sometimes as a (column) collection of vector fields
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
. We use these conventions when there is no ambiguity.

In a mixed real and complex domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
with standard (real and complex) coordinate system
(t,z)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
, for a function
f
on
\Omega
, we use
\nabla f,\nabla_{z}f,\partial_{z}f
as functions taking values in column vectors, which have rows
r+2m
,
2m
and
m
respectively as follows,

\begin{gathered}\textstyle\nabla f=\nabla_{t,z}f=\partial_{t,z,\bar{z}}f=\big[\frac{\partial f}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial t^{r}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\big]^{\top},\\
\textstyle\nabla_{z}f=\partial_{z,\bar{z}}f=\big[\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\big]^{\top},\qquad\partial_{z}f=\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}=\big[\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{m}}\big]^{\top}.\end{gathered}

(1.2.2)

Notice that
df
is a row vector while
\nabla f
is a column vector.

For a map
F:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we usually use the notation
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
where
F^{\prime}:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
F^{\prime\prime}:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
are the corresponding components of
F
.

On a manifold, we refer a
topological coordinate chart
to a map
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
which is homeomorphism onto its image. We refer
topological parameterization
to a map
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{M}
which is homeomorphism onto its image. For
\alpha>1
, we say that
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
coordinate chart, equivalently that
\Phi
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
regular parameterization, if they and their inverses are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

The paper is organized as follows: the proof of theorems are mostly separated into four parts:

•

The real Frobenius-type theorems: Theorems
1
and
2
. The proofs are done in Section
5.1
. They require Sections
2.1.4
and
2.3.2
.

•

The “pure complex” part of the complex Frobenius theorems: Theorem
4.1
in Section
4.1
, which is the key part to the proof of Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
. The proof requires Sections
2.2
,
2.3.4
and
2.4
.

•

The quantitative Frobenius theorem: Theorem
8
. The proof is done in Section
5.4.2
. It requires Sections
2.3.3
. The key is to prove Theorem
4.2
, which is the Section
4.2
.

•

The examples: Theorems
6
and
7
. The proofs are mostly self-contained.

Once the real part and the pure complex part are done, we get the proof of Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
in Section
5.2.2
. The proof is essentially the combinations of Theorem
1
(see also Theorems
5.1
and
5.3
) and Theorem
4.1
, though tracing the indices of composition maps, which are done in Section
2.1.2
. See also Theorems
5.4
and
5.5
for more general versions of Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
.

The Sections
2.1
and
2.2
give discussions of Hölder-Zygmund spaces in the bi-parameter setting and set up necessary technics, which are the based stones of the thesis. A major part of the results come from
[
Yao22
, Section 2]
. Here in Section
2.2.2
the Proposition
2.2.27
is not appeared in arXiv before.

In Section
2.1.4
we prove Theorem
2.1.48
, a result from
[
Yao20
, Section 2.2]
that show good approximation of the product functions if their product is identically zero.
We then use Theorem
2.1.48
to Section
2.3.2
and show the flow commutativity between two commutative log-Lipschitz vector fields, see Proposition
2.3.27
. The flow commuting is the key to the proof of Theorems
1
and
2
. We prove Theorem
1
in Section
5.1.2
and Theorem
2
in Section
5.1.5
.
In order to prove Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
, we generalize Theorem
1
into the bi-layer case: the Theorem
5.3
in Section
5.1.4
.

Most results in Section
2.3.2
(the part after Definition
2.3.24
) are not appeared in arXiv before.

In Section
2.3.3
we give some basic properties of the canonical coordinates of the second kind. The proof of these results are not appeared in arXiv before but they are mostly inspired from
[
SS18
]
. This section is only used in the proof of Theorem
8
in Section
5.4.2
.

Section
2.4
follows from
[
Yao22
, Appendix B]
, where we construct an inverse Laplacian that can be holomophically extended to some complex domain. The proof is inspired by
[
Mor58
]
. The main result in this section, Proposition
2.4.1
, is only used in Proposition
4.1.12
in Section
4.1.4
.

Chapter
3
discusses some distributional characterizations for sections and involutivity, along with some discussion on rough integrability and singular subbundles. The results in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
come from
[
Yao20
, Section 5]
. This part is independent of the later sections and the proof of the main theorems. Also the Sections
3.3
and
3.4
are not appeared in arXiv before.

Chapter
4
gives two theorems using Malgrange’s methods of factorizing coordinate changes: Theorem
4.1
in Section
4.1
and Theorem
4.2
in Section
4.2
. See overview of their proofs in Section
4.1.1
and Section
4.2.1
, respectively.
Both proofs consist of finding an intermediate coordinate change using some existence theorem of elliptic PDEs, and showing the objects in the new coordinate chart have improved regularity using some interior regularity theorem of nonlinear elliptic PDEs. In Theorem
4.1
, to deal with the case
\alpha=1
or when the subbundle is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta-}
, we need an extra result of Schauder’s estimate in Section
4.1.3
, which is not appeared in arXiv before.

In Section
5.1
we prove the real Frobenius type theorems. We prove Theorem
1
in Section
5.1.2
, Theorem
2
in Section
5.1.5
and a generalization of Theorem
1
in Section
5.1.4
. Section
5.1.3
comes from
[
Yao20
, Section 6.1]
, which gives a PDE interpretation of Theorem
1
and is independent of the other sections.

In Section
5.2
we give a general version of complex Frobenius theorem, the Theorems
5.4
and
5.5
. We give the proof using by combining Theorem
4.1
in Section
4.1
and Theorem
5.3
in Section
5.1.4
.

Section
5.3
lists several examples which indicate the sharpness of some Hölder exponents in Theorems
1
and
3
. We prove Theorem
7
in Section
5.3.2
and Theorem
6
in Section
5.3.3
. Section
5.3.4
discusses the regularity loss of the canonical coordinates, which is independent of the other subsections in Section
5.3
.

In Section
5.4
we prove Theorem
8
. The proof follows from
[
SY22
, Section 9]
.

Chapter 2
The Tools And The Necessities

2.1
On Single parameter Hölder-Zygmund Spaces

In this section we recall some properties of Hölder-Zygmund spaces. The results in this section are mostly for technical purposes.

2.1.1
Basic characterizations and paraproducts

Recall the classical (single-parameter) Hölder-Zygmund spaces in Definition
1.2.1
. We first recall some results from functions and distributions that take value in Banach spaces.

Definition 2.1.1
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open subset and let
\mathscr{X}
be a complete locally convex topological vector space. The space
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U;\mathscr{X})
of
\mathscr{X}
-valued distributions on
U
, is the set of all continuous linear map
F:C_{c}^{\infty}(U)\to\mathscr{X}
. The space
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
of
\mathscr{X}
-valued tempered distributions, is the set of all continuous linear map
F:\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{X}
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two open subsets. By the Schwartz Kernel Theorem (see for example
[
Trè06
, Corollary 51.6 and Theorem 51.7]
), we have canonical correspondence
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U;\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(V))\cong\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U\times V)
and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{q}))\cong\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Thus in applications we can limit our focus on distributions defined on the product domain
U\times V
.

On the space
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
we can talk about the Littlewood-Paley decomposition of
\mathscr{X}
-valued functions

Definition 2.1.2
.

A
(Fourier) dyadic resolution of unity
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a sequence of Schwartz functions
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
that are all real-valued even functions, such that

•

The Fourier transform
\hat{\phi}_{0}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\phi(x)e^{-2\pi ix\xi}dx
satisfies
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{0}\subset\{|\xi|<2\}
and
\hat{\phi}_{0}\big|_{\{|\xi|<1\}}\equiv 1
.

•

For
j\geq 1
,
\phi_{j}(x):=2^{nj}\phi_{0}(2^{j}x)-2^{n(j-1)}\phi_{0}(2^{j-1}x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We define the Littlewood-Paley block operators
\{\mathsf{S}_{j},\mathsf{P}_{j}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}=\{\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi},\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\phi}\}_{j=0}^{\infty}
associated to
\phi
, as

\mathsf{P}_{j}f=\mathsf{P}_{j}^{\phi}:=\phi_{j}\ast f,\hskip 20.00003pt\mathsf{S}_{j}f=\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi}:=\sum_{k=0}^{j}\mathsf{P}_{k}f=2^{jn}\phi_{0}(2^{j}\cdot)\ast f,\hskip 10.00002ptj\geq 0,\ f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(2.1.1)

Immediately we see that

\operatorname{supp}\phi_{0}\subset\{|\xi|<2\},\hskip 10.00002pt\operatorname{supp}\phi_{j}\subset\{2^{j-1}<|\xi|<2^{j+1}\},\hskip 10.00002ptj\geq 1.

(2.1.2)

In other words,

\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{P}_{k}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{if }|j-k|\geq 2;\hskip 20.00003pt\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{S}_{k},&\text{if }j\leq k-2,\\
0,&\text{if }j\geq k+2.\end{cases}

(2.1.3)

Let
\lambda\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a Schwartz function and let
F:\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{X}
be a
\mathscr{X}
-valued tempered distribution. The convolution
\lambda\ast F:\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{X}
is defined to be the same as the one for numerical tempered distribution: for
u\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\textstyle(\lambda\ast F)(u):=F(\widetilde{\lambda}\ast u)\in\mathscr{X},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }\widetilde{\lambda}(x)=\lambda(-x).

One can see that when
\mathscr{X}
is a Banach and
F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
is a strongly measurable and Bochner integrable function, we have
\lambda\ast F\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
and
\lambda\ast F(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\lambda(x-y)F(y)dy
with the right hand side be a convergence Bochner integral. See
[
HvNVW16
, Section 2.4.d]
.

Lemma 2.1.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
, let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set.
Let
T:\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}
be a bounded linear operator between two Banach spaces. Then
(\tilde{T}f)(x):=T(f(x))
define a bounded linear map
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{Y})
. Moreover
\|\tilde{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathscr{X})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathscr{Y})}=\|T\|_{\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}}
has the same operator norm.

Proof.

We only prove the case
\alpha\in(0,1)
since the proof of other cases follow from the same argument. Clearly
\|\tilde{T}\|\geq\|T\|
. By Definition
1.2.1
for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{T}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{Y})}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|T(f(x))|_{\mathscr{Y}}+\sup_{x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y}|T(f(x))-T(f(y))|_{\mathscr{Y}}\cdot|x-y|^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\|T\|_{\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}}\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\|T\|_{\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}}\sup_{x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y}|f(x)-f(y)|_{\mathscr{X}}\cdot|x-y|^{\alpha}=\|T\|_{\mathscr{X}\to\mathscr{Y}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}.

Thus
\tilde{T}
is bounded with the same operator norm.
∎

Lemma 2.1.4
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution. Let
\mathscr{X}
be an arbitrary Banach space.

(i)

f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
if and only if
\phi_{j}\ast f\in C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
for every
j\geq 0
and
\sup_{j\geq 0;x\in\mathscr{X}}2^{j\alpha}|\phi_{j}\ast f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}<\infty
. Moreover there is a
C=C(\phi,\alpha)>0
that does not depend on
f
such that

\textstyle C^{-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\leq\sup_{j\geq 0;x\in\mathscr{X}}2^{j\alpha}|\phi_{j}\ast f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(2.1.4)

(ii)

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded Lipschitz domain. Then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
has an equivalent norm

f\mapsto\inf\{\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}:\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X}),\tilde{f}\big|_{\Omega}=f\}.

(iii)

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be either the whole space or a bounded smooth domain. Suppose
0<\alpha<2
, then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
has an equivalent norm

\textstyle f\mapsto\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\sup_{x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y}\big|\frac{f(x)+f(y)}{2}-f\big(\frac{x+y}{2}\big)\big|_{\mathscr{X}}\cdot|x-y|^{-\alpha}.

(2.1.5)

See
[
Tri10
, Chapters 2.2.2, 2.5.7 and 3.4.2]
for the proof of
\mathscr{X}=\mathbb{R}
. For general Banach space
\mathscr{X}
, the convolution
\phi_{j}\ast f(x)=\int\phi_{j}(x-y)f(y)dy
is defined via Bochner integral (see for example
[
HvNVW16
, Chapter 2]
).

Proof of Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
and
(iii)
with
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

For convenience we let
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}^{\prime}
to be (
2.1.5
) and
\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}:=\sup_{j\geq 0;x\in\mathscr{X}}2^{j\alpha}|\phi_{j}\ast f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}
.

Since
\phi_{j}
are even functions and satisfies
\int\phi_{j}=0
for
j\geq 0
, for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{j}\ast f(x)=\int f(x-y)\phi_{j}(y)dy=\int(f(x-y)-f(x))\phi_{j}(y)dy

(2.1.6)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\big(\int(f(x-y)-f(x))\phi_{j}(y)dy+\int(f(x+y)-f(x))\phi_{j}(-y)dy\big)=\int\big(\frac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)}{2}-f(x)\big)\phi_{j}(y)dy.

(2.1.7)

Recall for
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=\|f\|_{C^{0}}+\sup_{x,h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|h|^{-\alpha}|f(x)-f(x-h)|_{\mathscr{X}}
.
Clearly
\|\phi_{0}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\|f\|_{C^{0}}\leq\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. By (
2.1.6
), for
j\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle\leq\int|f(x-y)-f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}|\phi_{j}(y)|dy\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\int|y|^{\alpha}|\phi_{j}(y)|dy

(2.1.8)

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle=2^{(1-j)\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\int|y|^{\alpha}|\phi_{1}(y)|dy\approx 2^{-j\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

Thus
\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
.

To see
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
, we denote
\mu_{j}=\sum_{k=j-1}^{j+1}\phi_{j}
with the convention
\phi_{-1}=0
. So
\phi_{j}\ast f=\mu_{j}\ast\phi_{j}\ast f
for all
j\geq 0
.
Clearly
\|f\|_{C^{0}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j\alpha}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
. Let
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
and let
h\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfies
2^{-k-\frac{1}{2}}\leq|h|\leq 2^{-k+\frac{1}{2}}
, what we need is to show
|f(x+h)-f(x)|\leq 2^{-k\alpha}
.

Write
f=f_{k}^{0}+f_{k}^{\infty}
where

f_{k}^{0}:=\sum_{j=0}^{k}\phi_{j}\ast f,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{k}^{\infty}:=\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\phi_{j}\ast f.

(2.1.9)

For
f_{k}^{\infty}
, clearly
|f_{k}^{\infty}(x+h)-f_{k}^{\infty}(x)|\leq 2\|f_{k}^{\infty}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{-k\alpha}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
. For
f_{k}^{0}
, we use
f_{k}^{0}(x+h)-f_{k}^{0}(x)=\int_{0}^{1}h\cdot(\nabla f_{k}^{0})(x+th)dt
, so

\displaystyle|f_{k}^{0}(x+h)-f_{k}^{0}(x)|\leq\int_{0}^{1}|h||(\nabla f_{k}^{0})(x+th)|dt\leq 2^{\frac{1}{2}-k}\|\nabla f_{k}^{0}\|_{L^{\infty}}=2^{\frac{1}{2}-k}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\nabla\mu_{j}\ast\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{-k}\sum_{j=0}^{k}2^{j}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{-k}2^{(1-s)k}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}=2^{-k\alpha}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}.

This completes the proof of
(i)
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.

The proof of
(iii)
for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
is similar. Similar to (
2.1.8
), by (
2.1.7
) we get
\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}^{\prime}
:

\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\int\big|\tfrac{f(x+y)+f(x-y)}{2}-f(x)\big||\phi_{j}(y)|dy\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}^{\prime}\int|y|^{s}|\phi_{j}(y)|dy\approx 2^{-j\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}^{\prime}.

To see
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}^{\prime}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
, it suffices to show
\big|\tfrac{f(x+h)+f(x-h)}{2}-f(x)\big|\lesssim 2^{-k\alpha}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
when
2^{-\frac{1}{2}-k}\leq|h|\leq 2^{\frac{1}{2}-k}
. We write
f=f_{k}^{0}+f_{k}^{\infty}
as in (
2.1.9
). Clearly
\big|\tfrac{f_{k}^{\infty}(x+h)+f_{k}^{\infty}(x-h)}{2}-f_{k}^{\infty}(x)\big|\leq 2\|f_{k}^{\infty}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=k+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{-k\alpha}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
. For
f_{k}^{0}
, we use

f_{k}^{0}(x+h)-2f_{k}^{0}(x)+f_{k}^{0}(x-h)=\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}h_{i}h_{j}(\partial_{ij}^{2}f_{k}^{0})(x+(t+s-1)h)dtds.

Therefore

\displaystyle|f_{k}^{0}(x+h)-2f_{k}^{0}(x)+f_{k}^{0}(x-h)|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq\int_{[0,1]^{2}}|h|^{2}|(\nabla^{2}f_{k}^{0})(x+(t+s-1)h)|_{\mathscr{X}}dtds\leq 2^{1-2k}\|\nabla^{2}f_{k}^{0}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2^{1-2k}\sum_{j=0}^{k}\|\nabla^{2}\mu_{j}\ast\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}})}\lesssim 2^{-2k}\sum_{j=0}^{k}2^{2j}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}})}\lesssim 2^{-2k}2^{(2-s)k}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}=2^{-k\alpha}\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}.

This completes the proof of
(iii)
for
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
, in particular we get
(i)
for
\alpha=1
.

To prove the case
\alpha>1
in
(i)
, it suffices to show
\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}\approx\|f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}+\|\nabla f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}
. We recall the notation
\mu_{j}
from above, which gives for every
j\geq 0
,

\|\nabla\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|\nabla\mu_{j}\ast\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\nabla\mu_{j}\|_{L^{1}}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}\approx 2^{j}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}.

(2.1.10)

Thus
\|\nabla f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
and we get
\|f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}+\|\nabla f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}
.

Conversely, we can use
\phi_{j}\ast f=(I-\Delta)(I-\Delta)^{-1}(\phi_{j}\ast f)
where
\Delta=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{k}}^{2}
:

\displaystyle\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}=\|(I-\Delta)^{-1}\mu_{j}\ast(I-\Delta)(\phi_{j}\ast f)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|(I-\Delta)^{-1}\mu_{j}\|_{L^{1}}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|(I-\Delta)^{-1}\partial_{k}\mu_{j}\|_{L^{1}}\|\partial_{k}\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle 2^{-2j}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}2^{-j}\|\partial_{k}\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}\lesssim 2^{-j}(\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}+\|\nabla\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{X})}).

This implies
\|f\|_{\alpha;\phi}\lesssim\|f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}+\|\nabla f\|_{\alpha-1;\phi}
and finish the proof of
(i)
.
∎

To prove
(ii)
and
(iii)
for bounded smooth
\Omega
, we use the following extension operator from
[
Tri10
]
:

Let
M
be a fixed integer and let
(a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{M}
be numbers such that
b_{j}>0
and
\sum_{j=1}^{M}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=1
for all integer
-M/2<k<M/2
. We define the “half-plane extension”
E_{\mathbf{H}}=E_{\mathbf{H};M,\{a_{j},b_{j}\}}
as

E_{\mathbf{H}}f(x^{\prime},x_{n}):=\begin{cases}f(x^{\prime},x_{n})&x_{n}>0,\\
\sum_{j=1}^{M}a_{j}\cdot f(x^{\prime},-b_{j}x_{n})&x_{n}<0.\end{cases}

(2.1.11)

Following Definition
1.2.1
we see that
E_{\mathbf{H}}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\{x_{n}>0\};\mathscr{X})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
is bounded for all
0<\alpha<M/2
. See also
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.9]
, we leave the checking and computations to reader.

We pick an open cover
U_{1},\dots,U_{N}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
\partial\Omega
, and invertible affine linear transformations
\Phi_{\nu}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U_{\nu}
such that
\Phi_{\nu}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\cap\{x_{n}>0\})=U_{\nu}\cap\Omega
. We pick
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
and
\chi_{\nu}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{\nu};[0,1])
(
1\leq\nu\leq N
) to be a partition of unity for
\Omega
. Define

E_{\Omega}f:=\chi_{0}f+\sum_{\nu=1}^{N}\sqrt{\chi_{\nu}}(E_{\mathbf{H}}[(\sqrt{\chi_{\nu}}f)\circ\Phi_{\nu}]\circ\Phi_{\nu}^{\mathrm{Inv}}).

(2.1.12)

From direct computations we see that

Lemma 2.1.5

(
[
Tri10
, Sections 2.9.2 and 3.3.4]
)
.

For
E_{\Omega}
defined in (
2.1.12
),
E_{\Omega}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
is bounded linear for
0<\alpha<\frac{M}{2}
as well.

Proof of Lemma
2.1.4

(ii)
and
(iii)
for
\Omega
bounded smooth.

The direction
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\leq\inf_{\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}=f}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}
is trivial. The boundedness
E_{\Omega}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
gives
\inf_{\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}=f}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\leq\|E_{\Omega}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}
. Thus
(ii)
holds. The same argument shows
(iii)
for
\Omega
bounded smooth.
∎

Following the notations in
[
Tri10
, Definition 2.3.1/2(i)]
for
\alpha>0
the Hölder-Zygmund spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
are indeed the Besov spaces
\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
. Thus it is natural to define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-spaces for
\alpha\leq 0
.

Definition 2.1.6
.

Let
\alpha\leq 0
, let
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach space, and let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution of unity in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}:=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}
. More precisely,
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
is the space of all temper distributions
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
such that

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}<\infty.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded domain, we define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):=\{\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}:\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})\}
with norm

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\inf\{\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}:\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}=f\}.

We use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})=\bigcap_{\beta<\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
with standard (projective) limit topology.

We also define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
to be the spaces of functions/distributions
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
such that for every precompact open
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
, we have
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathscr{X})
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathscr{X})
respectively.

The
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-norms depend on
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
, while the spaces themselves are not. See
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.3.2]
.

Remark 2.1.7
.

(i)

By (
2.1.10
) we see that
\nabla:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus by Lemma
2.1.4

(ii)
we see that
\nabla:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and all bounded smooth domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(ii)

Lemma
2.1.3
is still true for
\alpha\leq 0
when
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
since we have
(\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{T}f)(x)=T(\phi_{j}\ast f(x))
. For bounded smooth domains, by the existence of extension operator we know
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{Y})
is bounded, but we do not know whether
\|\tilde{T}\|=\|T\|
is still true.

(iii)

In Section
2.2
we consider the spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega_{1};\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega_{2}))
. In our discussions only the case
\beta>0
is needed.

Lemma 2.1.8
.

x\log|x|\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
,
\log|x|\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
and
\operatorname{p.\!v.}\frac{1}{x}\in\mathscr{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

To show
x\log|x|\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
one can use the direct computation via Definition
1.2.1

(ii)
. We use scaling via Lemma
2.1.4

(iii)
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution. We have
\phi_{j}(x)=2^{j-1}\phi_{1}(2^{j-1}x)
and
\int x^{k}\phi_{j}(x)dx=0
for
j\geq 1
and
k\geq 0
. Let
f(x):=\log|x|
. Since
f\in L^{1}+L^{\infty}
and
\phi_{j}\in L^{\infty}\cap L^{1}
, we know
f\ast\phi_{j}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
for each
j\geq 0
. By scaling we have
f(cx)=f(x)+\log|c|
for
c>0
. Therefore

\textstyle f\ast\phi_{j}(x)=f(2^{j-1}\cdot)\ast\phi_{1}(x)=(f\ast\phi_{1})(x)+(j-1)\log 2\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi_{1}(y)dy=(f\ast\phi_{1})(x).

Therefore
\|f\ast\phi_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f\ast\phi_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
for all
j\geq 1
. We conclude that
\log|x|\in\mathscr{C}^{0}(\mathbb{R})
.

We see that
\operatorname{p.\!v.}\frac{1}{x}=\frac{d}{dx}\log|x|
because for every
g\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
,

\textstyle-\int(\log|x|)g^{\prime}(x)dx=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|x|>\varepsilon}\frac{1}{x}g(x)dx-(g(\varepsilon)\log\varepsilon-g(-\varepsilon)\log\varepsilon)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{|x|>\varepsilon}\frac{1}{x}g(x)dx=\langle\operatorname{p.\!v.}\frac{1}{x},g\rangle.

By Remark
2.1.7

(i)

\operatorname{p.\!v.}\frac{1}{x}\in\mathscr{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{R})
.

Since
x\log|x|-x=\int_{0}^{x}\log|t|dt
, and clearly
x\in C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})\subset\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
, we get
x\log|x|\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and finish the proof.
∎

Remark 2.1.9
.

x\log|x|
is an example in
\mathscr{C}^{1}
that does not belong to
C^{0,1}=\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}
. Taking definite integrals
k
times we see that
\mathscr{C}^{k+1}\subsetneq C^{k,1}(=\mathscr{C}^{k+\mathrm{Lip}})
for all integers
k
.

Remark 2.1.10

(The
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
topologies)
.

In a topological vector space
\mathscr{X}
, recall that a set
\mathscr{B}\subset\mathscr{X}
is (von Neumann) bounded, if for any open neighborhood
\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{X}
of
0
, there is a
M>0
such that
\mathscr{B}\subseteq M\cdot\mathscr{U}(=\{M\cdot x:x\in\mathscr{U}\})
.

(i)

On
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
we use the standard limit topology a.k.a. the initial topology:
\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
is open if and only if for every
\varepsilon>0
there is an open set
\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
such that
\mathscr{U}=\mathscr{U}_{\varepsilon}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
. It is a separable Fréchet space and the topology is metrizable. Moreover,

•

\big\{\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega):\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}}<\delta\}:\varepsilon,\delta>0\big\}
form a neighborhood basis of
0\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
.

•

f_{j}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)}f_{0}
if and only if
f_{j}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}(\Omega)}f_{0}
for every
\varepsilon>0
.

•

\mathscr{B}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
is a bounded set if and only if
\mathscr{B}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}(\Omega)
is bounded for all
\varepsilon>0
, i.e. for every
\varepsilon>0
there is a
M_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
f\in\mathscr{B}
implies
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}}<M_{\varepsilon}
.

One can view
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
as the inverse limit
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)=\varprojlim\{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\varepsilon}(\Omega):\varepsilon>0\}
, whose arrows are natural inclusions.

Similarly we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)=\varprojlim\{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega^{\prime}):\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega\}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)=\varprojlim\{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega^{\prime}):\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega\}
, both of whose arrows are natural restriction maps.

(ii)

We do not use the topologies of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}(\Omega)
in the thesis. But we point out that in general
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
is endowed with the standard colimit topology a.k.a. the final topology:
\mathscr{U}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}(\Omega)
is open if and only if for every
\varepsilon>0
, the subset
\mathscr{U}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}(\Omega)
is open in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}(\Omega)
. This is not a metrizable space.

Alternatively, using Besov spaces, we have duality
\mathscr{B}_{11}^{-\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})^{\prime}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, see
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.11.2(i)]
. We can define
\mathscr{B}_{11}^{(-\alpha)-}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and its topology analogous to
(i)
. One can show that the colimit topology of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
coincides with the weak-* topology as the dual space of
\mathscr{B}_{11}^{(-\alpha)-}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

We leave the proof to the above properties to readers.

For Hölder functions with negative indices it is sometimes more convenient to write it as derivatives of classical functions.

Lemma 2.1.11
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, and let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be either the total space or a bounded smooth domain. Then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega)=\{g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}g_{j}:g_{0},\dots,g_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)\}
.

Proof.

For a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega)
, by Definition
2.1.6
there is an extension
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Take
\tilde{g}_{0}:=(I-\Delta)^{-1}f
and
\tilde{g}_{j}:=-\partial_{j}\tilde{g}_{0}
for
1\leq j\leq n
, we see that
\tilde{g}_{0},\dots,\tilde{g}_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Hence for
g_{j}:=\tilde{g}_{j}|_{\Omega}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
,
0\leq j\leq n
, we have
f=g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}g_{j}
.

Conversely, let
g_{0},\dots,g_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
, by Lemma
2.1.4

(ii)
and Definition
2.1.6
they have extensions
\tilde{g}_{0},\dots,\tilde{g}_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Thus by Remark
2.1.7

(i)

\tilde{g}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}\tilde{g}_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Taking restrictions to
\Omega
we get
g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}g_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\Omega)
, finishing the proof.
∎

In Sections
4.1.3
and
4.2.4
we need to do scaling on functions.

Lemma 2.1.12
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\mu_{0}>0
, there is a
C=C_{n,\alpha,\mu_{0}}>0
such that for any Banach space
\mathscr{X}
and any
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})
,

\|f(\mu\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\leq|f(0)|_{\mathscr{X}}+C\mu^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<\mu<\mu_{0}.

(2.1.13)

Proof.

By Definition
1.2.1
, the constant function
x\mapsto f(0)
always has norm
\|f(0)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})}=|f(0)|_{\mathscr{X}}
. Thus replacing
f(x)
by
f(x)-f(0)
we can assume
f(0)=0
.

By taking a scaling
x\mapsto\mu_{0}x
, we can assume
\mu_{0}=1
without loss of generality. Thus,
f
is defined on the unit ball.

For
\mu\in(0,1]
set
f_{\mu}(x):=f(\mu x)
.
For
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
\mu\in(0,1]
, by Definition
1.2.1

(i)
,

|f_{\mu}(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}=|f(\mu x)-f(0)|_{\mathscr{X}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})}|\mu x-0|^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\mu^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}.

(2.1.14)

When
\alpha\in(0,2)
, using Lemma
2.1.4

(iii)
, for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
,

\textstyle|\frac{f_{\mu}(x_{1})+f_{\mu}(x_{2})}{2}-f_{\mu}(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2})|_{\mathscr{X}}=\left|\frac{f(\mu x_{1})+f(\mu x_{2})}{2}-f(\mu\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2})\right|_{\mathscr{X}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}}|\mu(x_{1}-x_{2})|^{\alpha}\leq\mu^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{\alpha}.

(2.1.15)

Combining (
2.1.14
) and (
2.1.15
), we get (
2.1.13
) for the case
0<\alpha<2
, since

\textstyle\|f_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\approx\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|f_{\mu}(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\sup\limits_{x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{-\alpha}\left|\frac{f_{\mu}(x_{1})+f_{\mu}(x_{2})}{2}-f_{\mu}(\frac{x_{1}+x_{2}}{2})\right|_{\mathscr{X}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\mu^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

For
\alpha\geq 2
, we proceed by induction. We prove the result for
\alpha\in[l,l+1)
, for
l\in\{1,2,\ldots\}
. The base case,
l=1
was shown above. We assume the result for
l-1
and prove it for
l
.

Assume
\alpha\in[l,l+1)
where
l\geq 2
. Note that
\nabla f_{\mu}(x)=\mu(\nabla f)(\mu x)
, so
\|\partial_{x^{j}}(f_{\mu})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\mu\|(\partial_{x^{j}}f)_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\|(\partial_{x^{j}}f)_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
for
j=1,\dots,n
. Here
(\partial_{x^{j}}f)_{\mu}(x)=(\partial_{x^{j}}f)(\mu x)
.

By the inductive hypothesis
\|f_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq C_{\alpha-1}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
and for
j=1,\dots,n
,
\|(\partial_{x^{j}}f)_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq C_{\alpha-1}\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\partial_{x^{j}}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
. So by Definition
1.2.1

(iii)
we get

\displaystyle\|f_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\approx\|f_{\mu}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathscr{X})}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{x^{j}}(f_{\mu})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\partial_{x^{j}}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\Big)\approx\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\mu^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},

completing the proof.
∎

For products of functions and distributions the paraproduct is of great importance:

Lemma 2.1.13

(Paraproduct decomposition)
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a dyadic resolution. For bounded continuous functions
u,v
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, consider the following decomposition

uv=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v).

(2.1.16)

Then for every
l\geq 0
,

\phi_{l}\ast(uv)=\phi_{l}\ast\bigg(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)\bigg).

(2.1.17)

Proof.

(
2.1.17
) can be obtained by investigating the Fourier support condition in Definition
2.1.2
. Note that
\phi_{l}\ast\left((\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)\right)\neq 0
only when
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{l}\cap(\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j}+\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j^{\prime}})\neq\varnothing
, and by (
2.1.2
) we have on the following:

\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{l}\subset\begin{cases}\{2^{l-1}<|\xi|<2^{l+1}\},&l\geq 1,\\
\{|\xi|<2\},&l=0;\end{cases}\ \operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j}+\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j^{\prime}}\subset\begin{cases}\{2^{\max(j,j^{\prime})-2}<|\xi|<2^{\max(j,j^{\prime})+2}\},&|j-j^{\prime}|\geq 3,\\
\{|\xi|<2^{\max(j,j^{\prime})+2}\},&|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2.\end{cases}

For details, see
[
Tri10
, Proof of Theorem 2.8.2]
or
[
BCD11
, Proof of Theorem 2.52]
. Note that their choice of decompositions (
2.1.16
) may have slight differences.
∎

Definition 2.1.14
.

We define
\mathfrak{P}(u,v):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)
and
\mathfrak{R}(u,v):=\sum_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}(\phi_{j}\ast u)(\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast v)
to be the paraproduct operators associated with
\phi
.

Remark 2.1.15
.

From (
2.1.16
) we see that
uv=\mathfrak{P}(u,v)+\mathfrak{P}(v,u)+\mathfrak{R}(u,v)
. In
[
BCD11
, Chapter 2.8.1, (2.41)]
this is denoted by
uv=T(v,u)+T(u,v)+R(u,v)
.

Lemma 2.1.16
.

The paraproduct operators have the following boundedness:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha\in\mathbb{R};

(2.1.18)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 10.00002pt\beta<0;

(2.1.19)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),

\displaystyle\hskip 20.00003pt\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha+\beta>0.

(2.1.20)

In particular
\mathfrak{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,
\beta>0
and
\mathfrak{R}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds for
\alpha>0
.

Also see Lemma
2.1.47
for a precise control of their norms.

Proof.

See
[
Tri10
, Theorem 3.3.2(ii)]
for (
2.1.18
), (
2.1.19
) and
[
BCD11
, Corollary 2.86]
for (
2.1.20
). Note that by Lemma
2.1.4
and Definition
2.1.6
Hölder-Zygmund spaces are a special case to Besov spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(U)
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

For the product itself we have

Lemma 2.1.17
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be either the total space of a bounded smooth domain.

(i)

There is a
C=C(\Omega,\alpha,\beta)\geq 0
such that
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)}
for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
,
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)
.

(ii)

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach algebra (that is
\mathscr{X}
is a Banach space with product structure such that
\|uv\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq\|u\|_{\mathscr{X}}\|v\|_{\mathscr{X}}
). Then
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}
holds for all
f,g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
.

In particular
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\leq 2\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}
.

Proof.

To prove
(i)
, by Lemma
2.1.16
we see that
(u,v)\mapsto\mathfrak{P}(u,v)
,
(u,v)\mapsto\mathfrak{P}(v,u)
and
(u,v)\mapsto\mathfrak{R}(u,v)
are all bounded as
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by Lemma
2.1.13
we get
(u,v)\mapsto uv:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Using Lemma
2.1.4

(ii)
and taking restriction on
\Omega
, we get the boundedness
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)
.

The result
(ii)
follows from direct computation using Definition
1.2.1
. We remark that there is no non-trivial in the result. We leave the detail to reader.
∎

We also remark that the product operator has the local property.

Lemma 2.1.18
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an arbitrary open set, and let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\alpha+\beta>0
.

(i)

Suppose
\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\tilde{g}_{1},\tilde{g}_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfy
\tilde{f}_{1}|_{\Omega}=\tilde{f}_{2}|_{\Omega}
and
\tilde{g}_{1}|_{\Omega}=\tilde{g}_{2}|_{\Omega}
. Then
(\tilde{f}_{1}\tilde{g}_{1})|_{\Omega}=(\tilde{f}_{2}\tilde{g}_{2})|_{\Omega}
as distributions on
\Omega
.

(ii)

In particular for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, the product
fg
is well-defined distribution in
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
.

Also see
[
RS96
, Section 4]
for more details.

Proof.

(i)
: Let
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,2\}
. By Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
and Definition
2.1.6
we have

\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}f_{i_{1}}-f_{i_{1}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\delta}}\leq\sum_{j=\sigma+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast f_{i_{1}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\delta}}\lesssim_{\phi,\alpha,\delta}\sum_{j=\sigma+1}^{\infty}2^{-j\alpha+j(\alpha-\delta)}\|f_{i_{1}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\approx_{\delta}2^{-\sigma\delta}\|f_{i_{1}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\xrightarrow{\sigma\to\infty}0.

Therefore
\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}f_{i_{1}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}f_{i_{1}}
and similarly
\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}g_{i_{2}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}g_{i_{2}}
for all
\delta>0
. Thus by Lemma
2.1.17

(i)
we have convergence
\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\tilde{f}_{i_{1}}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\tilde{g}_{i_{2}}=\tilde{f}_{i_{1}}\tilde{g}_{i_{2}}
in
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for every
\delta>0
. By
[
RS96
, Lemma 4.2.2]
we see that

(\tilde{f}_{1}\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{f}_{2}\tilde{g}_{2})|_{\Omega}=((\tilde{f}_{1}-\tilde{f}_{2})\tilde{g}_{1}+\tilde{f}_{2}(\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{g}_{2}))|_{\Omega}=\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}\big(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\tilde{f}_{1}-\tilde{f}_{2})\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\tilde{g}_{1}+\mathsf{S}_{j}\tilde{f}_{2}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\tilde{g}_{1}-\tilde{g}_{2})\big)|_{\Omega}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\Omega).

(ii)
: Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
. By
(i)
it suffices to prove the well-definedness of
fg|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\Omega^{\prime})
on any precompact open
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
.

Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
be such that
\chi|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\equiv 1
. By taking zero extension outside
\Omega
we have
\chi f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\chi g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By Lemma
2.1.17

(i)
we have
\chi f\cdot\chi g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Since
\chi f|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=f|_{\Omega^{\prime}}
and
\chi g|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=g|_{\Omega^{\prime}}
, by
(i)
we see that
fg|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=\chi f\cdot\chi g|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\Omega^{\prime})
is defined, finishing the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.1.19
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, and let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set. If
X
and
Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
vector fields on
