\Omega
, then
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a vector field with distributional coefficients. In particular
[X,Y]
is defined when
X
and
Y
are log-Lipschitz.

Recall that a vector field
X
on
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a vector-valued map
X=(X^{1},\dots,X^{n}):\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as well as a differential operator
Xf=\sum_{j=1}^{n}X^{j}\partial_{j}f
. For
X=\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
and
Y=\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
is given by

[X,Y]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\Big(X^{j}\frac{\partial Y^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}-Y^{j}\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}.

(2.1.21)

Proof.

Write
X=:\sum_{j=1}^{n}X^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
and
Y=:\sum_{j=1}^{n}Y^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}
, we have
X^{j},Y^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and
\frac{\partial Y^{i}}{\partial x^{j}},\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Applying Lemma
2.1.18

(ii)
since
\alpha>1-\alpha
, we get
X^{j}\frac{\partial Y^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}-Y^{j}\frac{\partial X^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
. By (
2.1.21
), we conclude that
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
∎

In the thesis we often discuss Lipschitz spaces and log-Lipschitz spaces.

Definition 2.1.20
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open subset and
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach space. We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
the class of
\mathscr{X}
-valued Lipschitz functions with norm

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\sup_{x,y\in\Omega;x\neq y}|f(x)-f(y)|_{\mathscr{X}}\cdot|x-y|^{-1}.

We say a continuous function
f:\Omega\to\mathscr{X}
is bounded
log-Lipschitz
, denoted by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
, if

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{x\in\Omega}|f(x)|_{\mathscr{X}}+\sup_{x,y\in\Omega;0<|x-y|<1}|f(x)-f(y)|_{\mathscr{X}}\Big(|x-y|\log\frac{e}{|x-y|}\Big)^{-1}<\infty.

We say
f:\Omega\to\mathscr{X}
is bounded
little log-Lipschitz
, denoted by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
, if in addition
\lim_{r\to 0}\sup_{x,y\in U;0<|x-y|<r}|f(x)-f(y)|(|x-y|\log\frac{e}{|x-y|})^{-1}=0
.

Let
\sigma\in\{\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{LogL}\}
, we denoted by
\mathscr{C}^{k+\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
the space of all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
whose first
k
-order (Fréchet) derivatives are
\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}
, with norm
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k+\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}:=\sum_{|\nu|\leq k}\|\partial^{\nu}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}
.

One can check that
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
is a closed subspace. We have correspondence of the classical notation
\mathscr{C}^{k+\mathrm{Lip}}=C^{k,1}=W^{k+1,\infty}
.

Definition 2.1.21
.

Let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a fixed dyadic resolution and let
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach space. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
to be the space of all tempered distributions
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
such that

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{j\geq 1}j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}_{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}<\infty.

For an arbitrary open set
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):=\{\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}:\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})\}
with
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}=\inf_{\tilde{f}|_{\Omega}=f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}
.

We denote
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X}):=L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
.

The
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
-norm depends on the choice of the dyadic resolution
\phi
, but the norm topology is not. See Lemma
2.1.22

(i)
below.

Lemma 2.1.22
.

(i)

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
and
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be two dyadic resolutions, then there is a
C=C(n,\phi,\psi)>0
such that
\sup_{j\geq 1}j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\leq C\sup_{j\geq 1}j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}^{\psi}_{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}
. In other words different choices of dyadic resolutions result in equivalent norms for
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
.

(ii)

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be either the total space or a bounded smooth domain. Then the product map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega)
is bounded bilinear for every
\alpha>0
. Moreover taking derivative
\nabla:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is also bounded linear.

(iii)

Let
L
be an invertible linear map on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then
[f\mapsto f\circ L]:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Moreover
\|f\circ L\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}\leq C\max(1,\log|L|_{\ell^{2}})\cdot\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}
where
C
depends only on
\phi
.

Here for the linear map
L
we use matrix norm in (
1.2.1
).
We are going to extend
(iii)
to the composition of general diffeomorphism in Lemma
2.1.36
.

Proof.

(i)
:
Using the property (
2.1.2
) we have
\mathsf{S}_{j+2}^{\psi}\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi}=\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi}
, thus

j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}\|\mathsf{S}_{j+2}^{\psi}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 3\|2^{jn}\phi_{0}(2^{j}\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}(j+2)^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}_{j+2}^{\psi}f\|_{L^{\infty}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall j\geq 1.

Taking sup over
j
we get
\sup_{j\geq 1}j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}\lesssim_{\phi}\sup_{j\geq 1}j^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}^{\psi}_{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})}
.

Thus the norms (using
\phi
or
\psi
) for
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
are equivalent. Taking restrictions to
\Omega
we get the equivalent norms result for
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})
.

(ii)
: By passing to extensions it suffices to consider
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The boundedness of
\nabla:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
follows from
[
BCD11
, Page 119 Example]
.

For
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we use Lemma
2.1.13
to estimate
fg=\mathfrak{P}(f,g)+\mathfrak{P}(f,g)+\mathfrak{R}(f,g)
.

Since
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{-\alpha/2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, by Lemma
2.1.16
we have
\mathfrak{P},\mathfrak{R}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\times\mathscr{C}^{-\alpha/2}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha/2}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
. It remains to prove the boundedness for
(f,g)\mapsto\mathfrak{P}(g,f)
. Indeed for
\sigma\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathfrak{P}(g,f)=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\sum_{j=0}^{\sigma+2}\mathsf{P}_{j}g\cdot\mathsf{S}_{j-3}f=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma+2}g\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-1}f)-\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\sum_{j=0}^{\sigma+1}\mathsf{S}_{j}g\cdot\mathsf{P}_{j-2}f;

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(g,f)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\Big((\sigma+2)-\sum_{j=0}^{\sigma+1}j2^{-\alpha(j-2)}\Big)\lesssim\sigma\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

Thus we get
(ii)
.

(iii)
: Using integration by substitution we have
\phi_{j}\ast(f\circ L)(x)=((\phi_{j}\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}})\ast f(Lx))\cdot|\det L|^{-1}
. In other words
\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}(f\circ L)(x)=|\det L|^{-1}\cdot\mathsf{S}^{\phi\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}}_{j}f(Lx)
.

We have the Fourier transform
(\phi_{0}\ast L^{\mathrm{Inv}})^{\wedge}(\xi)=|\det L|\hat{\phi}_{0}(L\xi)
. Thus by (
2.1.2
)
\operatorname{supp}(\phi_{0}\ast L^{\mathrm{Inv}})^{\wedge}\subset\{|\xi|\leq 2^{j_{0}-1}\}
where
j_{0}\geq 1
is the smallest number such that
2^{j_{0}-2}\geq|L|_{\ell^{2}}
. Therefore
\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}}\mathsf{S}_{j+j_{0}}^{\phi}=\mathsf{S}_{j}^{\phi\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}}
for all
j\geq 0
, and we have

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}(f\circ L)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=|\det L|^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}}_{j}f\|_{L^{\infty}}=|\det L|^{-1}\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}}_{j}\circ\mathsf{S}_{j+j_{0}}^{\phi}f\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq|\det L|^{-1}\|\phi_{0}\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}\|_{L^{1}}(j+j_{0})\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\phi}}=(j+j_{0})\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\phi}}.

Since
j+j_{0}\leq j_{0}j\leq\max(1,2+\log_{2}|L|_{\ell^{2}})\cdot j
, taking the supremum over
j\geq 1
we complete the proof.
∎

Definition 2.1.23
.

We use the extended index set

\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}:=\{\alpha,\alpha-:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\}\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=-1,0,1,2,\dots\}\cup\{\infty\}.

For two generalized indices
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
, we define order
\sigma_{1}\leq\sigma_{2}
if
\mathscr{C}^{\sigma_{1}}\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\sigma_{2}}
.

We denote by
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}:=\{\sigma\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}:\sigma>\mathrm{Lip}-1\}
the set of positive generalized indices. Equivalently
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
if and only if
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subset C^{0}
.

We denote by
\mathbb{R}^{-}:=\{\alpha-:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{-}:=\{\alpha-:\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
.

Clearly
\mathscr{C}^{1+}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{1}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{1-}
. The Zygmund space
\mathscr{C}^{1}
is contained in the log-Lipschitz space
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
(see
[
BCD11
, Proposition 2.107]
). Therefore
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
is a total order set and we have order
(k+1)-<k+\mathrm{LogL}<k+1<k+\mathrm{Lip}<k+1+\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon>0
and all
k=-1,0,1,2,\dots
.

Remark 2.1.24
.

We do not include the
\mathrm{logl}
, the index for little log-Lipschitz in the index set
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
. On one hand
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}
is just the closure of smooth functions in
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
, which reduces many discussion to the log-Lipschitz index
\mathrm{LogL}
. On the other hand, the little log-Lipschitz space
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}
and the Zygmund space
\mathscr{C}^{1}
do not have inclusions each other, which will make the index set not totally ordered.

Convention 2.1.25
.

We use the convention
\infty-=\infty+1=\infty-1=\frac{1}{2}\infty=\infty
.

For
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
, we use the convention
(\alpha-)-=\alpha-
. We use the formal operations
(\alpha-)+\beta=\alpha+(\beta-)=(\alpha-)+(\beta-):=(\alpha+\beta)-
; and if
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{\infty\}
, we use
(\alpha-)\cdot\beta=\alpha\cdot(\beta-)=(\alpha-)\cdot(\beta-):=(\alpha\beta)-
.

Later in Sections
5.1.4
and
5.2.2
, we consider the products and compositions of function spaces with the extended indices.

We can extend Lemma
2.1.17
to spaces of extended indices.

Lemma 2.1.26
.

Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be either the total space or a bounded smooth domain. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. Then the product map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\Omega)
is always bilinearly continuous.

Proof.

First we prove special case
\alpha=\beta
. The boundedness for
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
is the result of Lemma
2.1.17
. For
\alpha\in\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{Lip}\}
the standard argument shows
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}
which implies the boundedness. For
\alpha=k+\mathrm{LogL}
or
k+\mathrm{Lip}
, the boundedness follows from the product rule
\nabla(fg)=f\nabla g+g\nabla f
since by definition
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\approx\|\nabla(fg)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}+\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}
.

By Definition
1.2.1

(iv)
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}(\Omega)
for all
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Similarly
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}(\Omega)
holds as well.

Now if
\alpha<\beta
then the inclusion map
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
is bounded, thus
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\Omega)\times\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\Omega)
is also bounded. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 2.1.27
.

Using Definition
2.1.20
, Lemma
2.1.17

(ii)
is still valid if
\alpha\in\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
, namely
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k+\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k+\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k+\sigma}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathscr{X})}
holds for every
\sigma\in\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{Lip}\}
,
k\geq 0
,
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
open, and every Banach algebra
\mathscr{X}
. We leave the details to reader.

In application we need the result on matrix-valued functions. Recall the matrix norm from (
1.2.1
).

Lemma 2.1.28
.

Let
m,n\geq 1
, let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open subset, and let
\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})\subseteq L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
be a Banach space of matrix functions that is closed under product and contains the identity matrix.

(i)

Let
C_{1}>0
be the constant such that
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C_{1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{X}}
.

Then the map of inverting the matrices

[A\mapsto A^{-1}]:\{A\in\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}):\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<\tfrac{1}{2C_{1}}\}\to\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}),

(2.1.22)

is real-analytic and in particular Fréchet differentiable. Moreover
\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}
for all
A\in\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<\tfrac{1}{2C_{1}}
.

In particular for any
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
there exists a
c_{0}=c_{0}(\Omega,\alpha,m)>0
such that
\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
, for all
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq c_{0}
.

(ii)

Suppose there is a
C_{2}>0
such that
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C_{2}(\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{X}})
. Then (
2.1.22
) can be upgraded to the following map, which is also real-analytic (in particular Fréchet differentiable).

[A\mapsto A^{-1}]:\{A\in\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}):\|A-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<\tfrac{1}{2C_{2}}\}\to\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}).

(2.1.23)

Moreover
\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq 4\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}
for all
A\in\mathscr{X}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<\tfrac{1}{2C_{2}}
.

Proof.

(i)
: By assumption
\|(A-I_{m})^{k}\|_{\mathscr{X}}=\|(A-I_{m})^{k-1}(A-I_{m})\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C_{1}\|(A-I_{m})^{k-1}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}
for each
k\geq 1
. Thus
\|(A-I_{m})^{k}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C_{1}^{k-1}\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}
for all
k\geq 1
. Using power series
A^{-1}=(I_{m}-(I_{m}-A))^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(I_{m}-A)^{k}
and applying the assumption
\|A-I\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq 1/(2C_{1})
, we have

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\|(I_{m}-A)^{k}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}C_{1}^{k-1}\|I_{m}-A\|_{\mathscr{X}}^{k}\leq\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{k-1}}=2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}.

(2.1.24)

In particular and the power series converges in
\mathscr{X}
, and
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{X}
holds. Therefore the map
[A\mapsto A^{-1}]
is real-analytic.

By Lemma
2.1.26

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega)
is closed under product, taking
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
the estimate applies and we get the constant
c_{0}
.

(ii)
: Now
\|(A-I_{m})^{k}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C_{2}(\|(A-I_{m})^{k-1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}+\|(A-I_{m})^{k-1}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\|A-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}})
, we get,

\|(A-I_{m})^{k}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq C_{2}\|(A-I_{m})\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k-1}\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}+C_{2}\|(A-I_{m})^{k-1}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\|A-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,2,3,\dots.

Using induction on
k
we get

\|(A-I_{m})^{k}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq kC_{2}^{k-1}\|A-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k-1}\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,2,3,\dots.

Therefore when
\|A-I\|_{L^{\infty}}<1/(2C_{2})
, similar to (
2.1.24
), we have

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}kC_{2}^{k-1}\|I_{m}-A\|_{L^{\infty}}^{k-1}\|I_{m}-A\|_{\mathscr{X}}\leq\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{k}{2^{k-1}}=4\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{X}}.

In particular
A^{-1}
exists and the inverting matrix map (
2.1.23
) is real-analytic.
∎

2.1.2
The compositions

The results for compositions is a bit complicated especially for indices smaller than 1.

Definition 2.1.29
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. We define
\alpha\circ\beta
to be largest index
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
such that
[(f,g)\mapsto f\circ g]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
holds for all integer
m,n\geq 1
.

In order to let
\alpha\circ\beta
make sense, we need to show that the set
\{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}:[(f,g)\mapsto f\circ g]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ \forall m,n\geq 1\}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
always has a maximum in
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. This is guaranteed by Corollary
2.1.31
.

When
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
is such that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}
is a Banach space (rather than a general Fréchet space or locally convex space, namely
\alpha\circ\beta\notin\{\gamma-:\gamma>0\}\cup\{\infty\}
), we have the following quantitative estimates.

Proposition 2.1.30
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
and let
m,n\geq 1
be two integers. For any
M>1
there is a
K_{1}=K_{1}(m,n,\alpha,\beta,M)>0
such that for every
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
satisfies
g(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{m}
and
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\leq M
, the following holds

(i)

For
\max(\alpha,\beta)\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\{\alpha,\beta\}\neq\{1,\mathrm{Lip}\}
, for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})
, we have
f\circ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
with
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq K_{1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}
.

(ii)

For
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
, for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})
, we have
f\circ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
with
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq K_{1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}
.

Proof.

See
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.8]
for the case
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha>1
. For the others we start with the case
0<\alpha\leq 1<\beta
.

By Lemma
2.1.4

(iii)
we have
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\approx_{n,\alpha}\|f\circ g\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sup\limits_{x_{0}\neq x_{1}}|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}))|\cdot|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{-\alpha}
and
|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2})|\lesssim_{m,\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}
for
x_{0},x_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Therefore

\displaystyle|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}))|\leq|\tfrac{f(g(x_{0}))+f(g(x_{1}))}{2}-f(\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2})|+|f(\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2})-f(g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2}))|

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{m,\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}+\|f\|_{C^{0,1}}|\tfrac{g(x_{0})+g(x_{1})}{2}-g(\tfrac{x_{0}+x_{1}}{2})|

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{n,\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha/\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}}^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\alpha}+\|f\|_{C^{0,1}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\beta}\lesssim_{M}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}|x_{0}-x_{1}|^{\alpha}.

In particular we can choose
K_{1}(m,n,\alpha,\beta,M)\approx_{m,n,\alpha,\beta}(1+M)^{\min(\beta,\frac{3}{2})}
.

Now we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\max(\alpha,\beta)>1
. When
\max(\alpha,\beta)\in\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k\geq 1\}
, in this case
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in(0,1)
is a real number and we have the embedding
\mathscr{C}^{\max(\alpha,\beta)}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\frac{3}{2}}
, thus by enlarging
K_{1}
, the estimate
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}}\leq K_{1}(\frac{3}{2})\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{3/2}}\leq K_{1}(\beta)\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
. Therefore we get
(i)
for all
\max(\alpha,\beta)>1
and
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

We postpone the proof of
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=1,2,\dots\}
to the end.

When
\min(\alpha,\beta)\leq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\max(\alpha,\beta)\geq\mathrm{Lip}
, using the embedding
\mathscr{C}^{\max(\alpha,\beta)}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}
, it suffices to consider the case
\max(\alpha,\beta)=\mathrm{Lip}
.

We are about the prove the case where
\max(\alpha,\beta)=\mathrm{Lip}
,
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in(0,1)\cup\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{Lip}\}
. To unify the argument we consider general modulus of continuity, which is a strictly increasing map
\mu:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that
\mu(0)=0
. For an open set
\Omega
we denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega)
the space of continuous function
f
such that

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega)}:=\|f\|_{C^{0}(\Omega)}+\sup_{0<|x-y|<1}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{\mu(|x-y|)}.

(2.1.25)

Clearly for modulus

\mu_{\alpha}(t):=t^{\alpha},\hskip 10.00002pt0<\alpha\leq 1;\hskip 20.00003pt\mu_{log}(t):=t\log(e/t),

we have
\mathscr{C}^{\mu_{\alpha}}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
when
0<\alpha<1
,
\mathscr{C}^{\mu_{1}}=\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\mu_{\log}}=\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
, all of whose corresponding norms are equivalent. Note that
\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{log}
are all satisfy doubling condition, in particular
\sup_{0<t<1/M}\mu(Mt)/\mu(t)<\infty
for
\mu=\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{log}
and for all
M>1
.

Let
\mu,\nu
be two modulus of continuity, the composition
\mu\circ\nu:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is also a modulus of continuity. Suppose
\sup_{0<t<1/M}\mu(Mt)/\mu(t)<\infty
. Then for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\mathbb{B}^{m})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\nu}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{B}^{m})
such that
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\nu}}<M
, we have

\displaystyle\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu\circ\nu}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\|f\circ g\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sup_{0<|x_{0}-x_{1}|<1}\frac{|f(g(x_{0}))-f(g(x_{1}))|}{\mu\circ\nu(|x_{0}-x_{1}|)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}\sup_{0<|x_{0}-x_{1}|,|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|<1}\frac{\mu(|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|)}{\mu\circ\nu(|x_{0}-x_{1}|)}+\sup_{0<|x_{0}-x_{1}|<1\leq|g(x_{0})-g(x_{1})|}\frac{2\|f\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}}{\mu\circ\nu(|x_{0}-x_{1}|)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}\big(1+\sup_{0<t<1/M}\mu(Mt)/\mu(t)+2\cdot\mu(1/M)^{-1}\big)\lesssim_{\mu,M}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}.

In particular we can choose
K_{1}=1+3M^{\alpha}
if
\mu(t)=\mu_{\alpha}(t)=t^{\alpha}
and
K_{1}=1+3M\log M
if
\mu(t)=\mu_{log}(t)=t\log(e/t)
.

Take
\mu,\nu\in\{\mu_{\alpha},\mu_{log}:0<\alpha\leq 1\}
, we get
(i)
for
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in(0,1)\cup\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{Lip}\}
and
\max(\alpha,\beta)=\mathrm{Lip}
, and
(ii)
.

Finally we prove the case
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=1,2,\dots\}
, using the embedding
\mathscr{C}^{\max(\alpha,\beta)}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
, it suffices to consider
\alpha=\beta\in\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=1,2,\dots\}
.

Now
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subset C^{1}
, by chain rule
\nabla(f\circ g)=((\nabla f)\circ g)\cdot\nabla g
. When
k=1
and
\sigma\in\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{Lip}\}
, for the proven case
\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}
we have

\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1+\sigma}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}}+\|\nabla(f\circ g)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}}\leq K_{1}(\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}})(\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}}+\|\nabla f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}}\|\nabla g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}}).

Since
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}}+\|\nabla g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1+\sigma}}
, by tracking the constant we get the constant
K_{1}
.

The case
k\geq 2
is done by the induction along with the fact from Lemma
2.1.26
that
\|f\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k+\sigma}}\leq\|(\nabla f)\circ g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k-1-\sigma}}\|\nabla g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k-1-\sigma}}
.
∎

The following shows the complete classification of
\alpha\circ\beta
.

Corollary 2.1.31
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
be two generalized indices. The operator
\alpha\circ\beta
is given by the following:

(i)

When
\max(\alpha,\beta)\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\{\alpha,\beta\}\neq\{1,\mathrm{Lip}\}
, we have
\alpha\circ\beta=\max(\alpha,\beta)
.

(ii)

When
\max(\alpha,\beta)<1
,
\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha\beta
in the sense of Convention
2.1.25
.

(iii)

When
\max(\alpha,\beta)\in\{1-,\mathrm{LogL},1\}
,
\alpha\circ\beta=\min(\alpha-,\beta-)
.

(iv)

1\circ\mathrm{Lip}=\mathrm{Lip}\circ 1=\mathrm{LogL}
.

Moreover, let
U_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V_{2},U_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be open subsets such that
V_{2}\Subset U_{2}
is precompact, let
\mathscr{U}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{2})
and
\mathscr{V}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};V_{2})
be two bounded subsets with respect to their topologies, then
\{f\circ g:f\in\mathscr{U},g\in\mathscr{V}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1})
is also a bounded subset.

Here
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};U_{2})
is the subset of
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{m})
such that
g(U_{1})\subseteq U_{2}
.

Remark 2.1.32
.

In Corollary
2.1.31

(ii)
, we formally say
\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha\beta
by setting
(\gamma-)\cdot\delta=\gamma\cdot(\delta-)=(\gamma-)\cdot(\delta-):=(\gamma\delta)-
for real numbers
\gamma,\delta
, following from Convention
2.1.25
.

Remark 2.1.33
.

In our setting for a bounded set
\mathscr{V}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};V_{2})
, the closure
\overline{\mathscr{V}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{m})
may not be contained in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};V_{2})
.

Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-2,2)
be such that
\chi|_{[-1,1]}\equiv 1
. Then for any
\beta>0
,
\{c\cdot\chi:-1<c<1\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};(-1,1))
is a bounded set, but its closure
\{c\cdot\chi:-1\leq c\leq 1\}\not\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};(-1,1))
.

Note that under the usual subset topology,
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};\overline{V_{2}})
is a completed topological vector space, but
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};V_{2})
is completed unless
V_{2}=\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

Proof of Corollary
2.1.31
.

Clearly
\alpha\circ\beta\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)
whenever the left hand side is defined, because for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
we have
f\circ\operatorname{id}=f
and
\operatorname{id}\circ g=g
where
\operatorname{id}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
. Taking
f
such that
f\notin\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
for all
\sigma<\alpha
we see that
\alpha\circ\beta\leq\alpha
, similar argument for
g
shows
\alpha\circ\beta\leq\beta
.

By passing to local, Proposition
2.1.30
shows that when
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
,

(a)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\circ\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\max(\alpha,\beta)\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\{\alpha,\beta\}\neq\{1,\mathrm{Lip}\}
;

(b)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\circ\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
.

Moreover the above two composition maps map every bounded subset of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
to bounded subset of
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
or
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, respectively.

When
\alpha,\beta
satisfy the assumptions of
(i)
, and if
\min(\alpha,\beta)\in\{\gamma-:\gamma\in\mathbb{R}\}
, then taking
\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}\in(0,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
such that
\mathrm{Lip}\leq\max(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\leq\max(\frac{3}{2},\max(\alpha,\beta))
and
\min(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\in\{\gamma+\varepsilon,\gamma-\varepsilon\}
for number
\varepsilon>0
, we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}_{\mathrm{loc}}
from
(a)
. Letting
\varepsilon\to 0
we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\circ\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all extended indices such that
\max(\alpha,\beta)\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\{\alpha,\beta\}\neq\{1,\mathrm{Lip}\}
. Moreover the composition map bounded subset to bounded subset as well.

Therefore
\min(\alpha,\beta)\leq\alpha\circ\beta\leq\min(\alpha,\beta)
, which give the proof of
(i)
.

When
\alpha,\beta\in(0,1)
, since
|t|^{\alpha}\circ|t|^{\beta}=|t|^{\alpha\beta}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\beta-}
we see that
\alpha\circ\beta\leq\alpha\beta
if the left hand side is defined. By
(ii)
we see that
\alpha\beta\leq\alpha\circ\beta
, thus
\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha\beta
. The standard limit argument shows that
\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha\beta
holds for
\alpha
or
\beta\in\{\gamma-:\gamma\in(0,1)\}
, in the sense of Remark
2.1.32
. This proves
(ii)
. Note that in this case the composition map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
still preserve boundedness of the subsets.

By Lemma
2.1.8

f_{1}(t):=t\log|t|
satisfies
f_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}\backslash\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}
, but
f_{1}\circ f_{1}(t)=t\log^{2}|t|+t(\log|t|+\log|\log|t||)
is not log-Lipschitz. Thus
1\circ 1\leq 1-
. And for
\gamma\in(0,1)
,
|t|^{\gamma}\circ f_{1}(t)=|t|^{\gamma}|\log|t||^{\gamma}
,
f_{1}(|t|^{\gamma})=|t|^{\gamma}\gamma\log|t|
, neither of which belongs to
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
. Thus
\gamma\circ 1,1\circ\gamma\leq\gamma-
for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

On the other hand by
(b)
we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-}\circ\mathscr{C}^{1-}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-}
and
\mathscr{C}^{1-}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-}
for all
\gamma\in(0,1)
, thus for
\max(\alpha,\beta)\in\{1-,\mathrm{LogL},1\}
we have
\min(\alpha-,\beta-)\leq\alpha\circ\beta\leq\min(\alpha-,\beta-)
, which gives
(iii)
. The boundedness of the map follows from
(b)
along with the limit argument.

Finally the function
g_{1}(t):=\max(t,0)
is locally Lipschitz, but
g_{1}\circ f_{1}(t)=f_{1}\circ g_{1}(t)=\max(t,0)\cdot\log|t|
is log-Lipschitz but not Zygmund-1 (
\mathscr{C}^{1}
). Thus
1\circ\mathrm{Lip},\mathrm{Lip}\circ 1\leq\mathrm{LogL}
. By
(a)
we see that
\mathrm{LogL}\circ\mathrm{Lip}=\mathrm{Lip}\circ\mathrm{LogL}=\mathrm{LogL}
, therefore
\mathrm{LogL}\leq 1\circ\mathrm{Lip}\leq\mathrm{LogL}
and
\mathrm{LogL}\leq\mathrm{Lip}\circ 1\leq\mathrm{LogL}
, which gives
(iv)
. The boundedness of the map follows from
(a)
.

Now we have completely classified the operators
\alpha\circ\beta
for all positive
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. Moreover we know the composition map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
preserves boundedness of their subsets.

Finally for every open sets
U_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
U_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
, and for any precompact
V_{1}\Subset U_{2}
, take
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{1})
and
\chi_{2}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{2})
be such that
\chi_{1}|_{V_{1}}\equiv 1
,
\chi_{2}|_{V_{2}}\equiv 1
, we see that
f\circ g|_{V_{1}}=(\chi_{2}f)\circ(\chi_{1}g)|_{V_{1}}
for all
f:U_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
and
g:U_{1}\to V_{2}
. On the other hand if
[f\mapsto\chi_{2}f]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
[g\mapsto\chi_{1}g]:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};U_{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
are continuous linear maps which preserve boundedness. Therefore by the boundedness of the composition map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{m})\circ\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
we see that
\{(\chi_{2}f)\circ(\chi_{1}g):f\in\mathscr{U},g\in\mathscr{V}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded. Since
V_{1}
is arbitrary, we conclude that
\{f\circ g:f\in\mathscr{U},g\in\mathscr{V}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1})
is a bounded subset.
∎

If the second element of the composition is a diffeomorphisms, we are allow to take the first element as distributions. This is useful in defining distributions on manifolds.

Lemma 2.1.34
.

Let
\kappa\in(1,\infty]
,
\alpha\in(0,\kappa]
and
\beta\in(\min(1-\kappa,-\alpha),\alpha]
. Let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two open sets and let
\varphi:U\to V
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-diffeomorphism (i.e.
\varphi,\varphi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-maps). Let
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

If
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
then
\rho\cdot(f\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
as well.

Remark 2.1.35
.

Here
f\circ\varphi
is a well-defined distribution in the sense that if
\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}f_{\sigma}=f
in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
then
\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}f_{\sigma}\circ\varphi=f\circ\varphi
in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
holds. See also
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.10]
.

Proof.

We only need to prove the case
\alpha=\kappa
. Indeed in this case gives the property
f\circ\varphi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Since
\alpha+\beta>0
, by Lemma
2.1.18
we get
\rho(f\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
holds.

We process by induction. We prove the result for
\beta>\max(1-\kappa,-l)
with
l=0,1,2,\dots
. The base
l=0
is automatically true since in this case
\beta>0
and we can apply Corollary
2.1.31

(i)
. We assume the result for
l-1
and prove it for
l
.

Now let
\beta>\max(1-\kappa,-l)
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
.
By passing to a precompact subset and applying Lemma
2.1.11
, we can write
f=g_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{j}g_{j}
where
g_{0},\dots,g_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
. By the inductive hypothesis, since
\beta+1>\max(1-\kappa,1-l)
, we have
g_{j}\circ\varphi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
j=0,\dots,n
.

Denote
\psi=(\psi^{1},\dots,\psi^{n}):=\varphi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:V\to U
, which is a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-map by assumption. Using chain rule and product rule,

\rho\cdot((\partial_{j}g_{j})\circ\varphi)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\partial_{k}(g_{j}\circ\varphi))\cdot\rho\cdot((\partial_{j}\psi^{k})\circ\varphi).

We have
g_{j}\circ\varphi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
, by Remark
2.1.7

(i)
we get
\partial_{k}(g_{j}\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Since
\rho((\partial_{j}\psi^{k})\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.
Thus by Lemma
2.1.18

(ii)
, since
\beta\in(1-\kappa,0]
, we have the product
(\partial_{k}(g_{j}\circ\varphi))\cdot\rho((\partial_{j}\psi^{k})\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Taking sum over
k=1,\dots,n
we get
\rho\cdot((\partial_{j}g_{j})\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
for
j=1,\dots,n
.

Clearly
\rho\cdot(g_{0}\circ\varphi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Taking sum over
j=0,\dots,n
we get the result.
∎

To define
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
-distribution on manifold (for Theorem
2
), we need a similar result to Lemma
2.1.34
as well. But the proof is more complicated.

Lemma 2.1.36
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
U,V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two open sets. Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V)
and let
\Phi:U\xrightarrow{\sim}V
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism. Then
f\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
is defined.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we assume
0<\alpha<1
. By passing to local we can assume
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{c}(V)
. By shrinking
U
and
V
we can assume
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
both have bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-norms.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a fixed dyadic resolution that defines the
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
norm. The integration by substitution formula yields

\phi_{j}\ast(f\circ\Phi)(x)=\int_{U}\phi_{j}(x-t)f(\Phi(t))dt=\int_{V}\phi_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s))\cdot f(s)\cdot|\det\nabla(\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}})(s)|ds,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\hskip 10.00002ptj\geq 0.

Since
\nabla\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
is pointwise invertible, by Lemma
2.1.22

(ii)
we see that
g(s):=f(s)|\det\nabla\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s)|
is a distribution in
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{c}(V)
. For
j\geq 0
, let
h_{j}:=\Delta^{-1}(g-\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}g)=\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}(\Delta^{-1}\phi_{k})\ast g
. Using
\phi_{k}\ast g=\phi_{k}\ast\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{k+1}g
from (
2.1.3
) we have for
j\geq 1
,

\|\nabla h_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}\|(\nabla\Delta^{-1}\phi_{k})\ast\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{k+2}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}\|\nabla\Delta^{-1}\phi_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{k+2}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\phi}\sum_{k=j+1}^{\infty}2^{-k}k\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}\lesssim_{g}j2^{-j}.

Let
\eta_{j}:=\sum_{k=0}^{j}\phi_{k}
, we have
\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}(f\circ\Phi)=\int_{V}\eta_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s))\cdot g(s)ds
for all
j\geq 0
. Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(V)
be such that
\chi|_{\operatorname{supp}g}\equiv 1
, then we have for
j\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}(f\circ\Phi)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big|\int_{V}\chi(s)\eta_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s))\cdot(\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}g(s)+\Delta h_{j}(s))ds\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{V}|\chi(s)\eta_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s))\cdot\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}g(s)|ds+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big|\int_{V}\nabla_{s}(\chi(s)\eta_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s)))\cdot\nabla h_{j}(s))ds\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\|\eta_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\cdot))\|_{L^{1}(V)}\|\mathsf{S}^{\phi}_{j}g\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\|\eta_{j}(x-\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\cdot))\cdot\chi\|_{W^{1,1}(V)}\|\nabla h_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\eta_{j}\|_{L^{1}}\|\det\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\cdot j\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}+\|\eta_{j}\|_{W^{1,1}}\|\det\nabla\Phi\|_{L^{\infty}(U)}\|\chi\|_{C^{1}}\|\nabla h_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{\phi,\Phi,g}j+2^{j}\cdot(j2^{-j})\approx j.

Taking supremum over
j\geq 1
we get
f\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
hence
f\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{c}(U)
.
∎

Remark 2.1.37
.

The same proof also work for the case
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
for
-1<\beta<0
.

2.1.3
Inverse Laplacian and inverse functions

Notation 2.1.38
.

We use the co-differential,
\vartheta=\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, which is a linear operator
taking
k
forms to
k-1
forms, satisfying for
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n
,

\vartheta(fdx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}})=\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x^{i_{l}}}(-1)^{l}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{l-1}}\wedge dx^{i_{l+1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}.

In particular on 1-forms,
-\vartheta
is the divergence operator, namely

\text{For }\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}dx^{i},\hskip 10.00002pt\vartheta\lambda=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\lambda_{i}}{\partial x^{i}}.

For any form
\omega
, we have
d(\vartheta\omega)+\vartheta(d\omega)=-\Delta\omega
, where

\Delta=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{x^{i}}^{2}
is the classical Laplacian acting on the components of
\omega
; in this
setting
\Delta
is called the (negative) Hodge Laplacian.

Notation 2.1.39
.

We use
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as the Newtonian potential in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, namely

\mathbf{\Gamma}(x):=\begin{cases}\frac{|x|}{2},&n=1,\\
\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|,&n=2,\\
-|\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|^{-1}|x|^{2-n},&n\geq 3.\end{cases}

(2.1.26)

We will often convolve functions with
k
-forms.
Formally, if
\omega=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
is a
k
-form, and
\phi
is a function, we set
\phi*\omega=\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq n}(\phi*\omega_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}})dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx^{i_{k}}
.

Lemma 2.1.40
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded open subset, then for every
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
the map
[f\mapsto(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast f)|_{U}]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)
is continuous for all
0\leq k\leq n
. Moreover there is a
C=C(U,\alpha)>0
such that
\|\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(U)}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}
for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(U;\wedge^{k}T^{*}U)
.

Proof.

By passing to the coordinate components it suffices to prove the case
k=0
.

Let
\chi\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
whose Fourier transform is
C_{c}^{\infty}
. We write
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}:=\chi\ast\mathbf{\Gamma}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\infty}:=(\delta_{0}-\chi)\ast\mathbf{\Gamma}
. Since
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is well-known to be a fundamental solution for
the Laplacian, we have the Fourier transform
((I-\Delta)\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\infty})^{\wedge}(\xi)=(1-\hat{\chi}(\xi))(4\pi^{2}|\xi|^{-2}+1)
, which is a bounded smooth function. Therefore by Hörmander-Mikhlin multiplier theorem (see
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.3.7]
),
[f\mapsto(I-\Delta)\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\infty}\ast f]:\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.3.8]
we get
(I-\Delta)^{-2}:\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Recall from Lemma
2.1.4
and Definition
2.1.6
that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, so
[f\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{\infty}\ast f]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(U)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear.

On the other hand
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\mathbf{\Gamma}}_{0}\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\hat{\chi}
is compact, thus
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Clearly
[f\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}\ast f]:\mathscr{E}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is continuous and thus
[f\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}_{0}\ast f]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is also bounded linear.

Now
[f\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast f]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear, taking restriction to
U
we get the continuity.

Let
\tilde{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a larger bounded open subset such that
U\Subset\tilde{U}
is precompact. We know the Banach subspace
\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):f|_{U^{c}}\equiv 0\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a bounded subset of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\tilde{U})
. Since
[f\mapsto(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast f)|_{\tilde{U}}]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\tilde{U})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\tilde{U})
is continuous, we know the following map is bounded:

[f\mapsto(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast f)|_{U}]:\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}):f|_{U^{c}}\equiv 0\}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\tilde{U})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\tilde{U})\xrightarrow{(-)|_{U}}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(U).

Thus such constant
C(U,\alpha)>0
exists.
∎

We also consider the Dirichlet problem of the Laplacian equation:

Lemma 2.1.41

(The Dirichlet Problem)
.

Let
\alpha>0
, and let
U
be a bounded domain with smooth boundary. Then for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}(U)
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial U)
there is a unique
u\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
such that
\Delta u=f
and
u\big|_{\partial U}=g
.

Moreover, the solution map
(f,g)\mapsto u
is continuous linear map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}(U)\oplus\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial U)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
.

See
[
FR95
, Theorem 15]
for a proof of Lemma
2.1.41
.

Remark 2.1.42
.

In Lemma
2.1.41
, for
\alpha>0
,
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial U)
is the Zymgund-Hölder space on manifold. One can use Definition
3.1.1
to define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-functions on it.
Note that the sphere is a compact manifold, and therefore
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\partial U)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial U)
.
The norm can be defined using any finite atlas,
and the equivalence class of the norm does not depend on
the choice of atlas. Moreover we have

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial U)}\approx_{\alpha}\inf\{\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}:\tilde{f}\big|_{\partial U}=f\},

(2.1.27)

since the trace operator
(\cdot)|_{\partial U}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial U)
is continuous and surjective; see
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.7.2]
.

In the case of unit ball
\mathbb{B}^{n}
and its boundary
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
, given a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1})
, we can take
\tilde{f}(x)=f(\frac{x}{|x|})\chi(|x|)
where
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-\frac{1}{2},2)
such that
\chi(1)=1
, then we have
\tilde{f}\big|_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}=f
and
\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\approx_{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{S}^{n-1})}
.

Notation 2.1.43
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain. We denote
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{U}
to be the Laplacian solution operator with zero Dirichlet boundary. Namely
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is the map
(f,0)\mapsto u
in Lemma
2.1.41
.

In Propositions
4.2.7
and
4.2.7
we need the following quantitative version inverse function theorem and the pushforwards of the exact forms.

Proposition 2.1.44
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
and
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
be two real numbers, then there is a constant
K_{2}=K_{2}(n,\alpha,\beta)>1
satisfying the following:

Suppose
R\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
and
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq K_{2}^{-1}
, then the map
F:=\operatorname{id}+R:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Moreover,

(i)

Let
\Phi=(\phi^{1},\dots,\phi^{n}):\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}
be the inverse map of
F
. Then
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K_{2}
and
\|\nabla\Phi-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq K_{2}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
.

(ii)

If
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
satisfies
\operatorname{supp}\lambda\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
d\lambda\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
, then
\operatorname{supp}F_{*}\lambda\subsetneq\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
and

\|d(F_{*}\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq K_{2}\|d\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

(2.1.28)

Proof.

We let
K_{2}
be a large constant which may change from line to line.
In particular, we will choose
K_{2}
large enough such that
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq K_{2}^{-1}
implies
\|R\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq c_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\alpha,n)
, where
c_{0}
is in Lemma
2.1.28

(i)
.

By Lemma
2.1.28

(i)
,
\nabla F(x)=I+\nabla R(x)
is an invertible matrix for every
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, and we have

\|(\nabla F)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=\|(I+\nabla R)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(2.1.29)

Since
\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, we have
|R(x_{1})-R(x_{2})|\leq\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|\leq\frac{1}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
, which implies

|F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})|\geq|x_{1}-x_{2}|-|R(x_{1})-R(x_{2})|\geq\textstyle\frac{3}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|.

(2.1.30)

This implies
F
is injective. By the Inverse Function Theorem, we know
F:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}F(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism.

The assumption
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
gives
F(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Since
F(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is contractible and
\overline{F(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\supset\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we get that
F(\mathbb{B}^{n})=\mathbb{B}^{n}
. We conclude
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Proof of
(i)
:
First, we claim that there is a
C_{1}(n,\alpha)>0
, which does not
depend on
R
, such that whenever
R
satisfies the assumptions
of the proposition, we have

\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{1}.

(2.1.31)

In particular
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq K_{2}
holds by taking
K_{2}\geq C_{1}
.

Since
\Phi
is the inverse map of
F
, by
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.9]
or by induction on
\alpha
with chain rule, we know
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
only depends on
n
,
\alpha
,
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, and
\|(\nabla F)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}
.
We will show that
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
and
\|(\nabla F)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}
have bounds that do not depend on
R
.

We have
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\operatorname{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\operatorname{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\frac{1}{4}
. The right hand side of this inequality does not depend on
R
.

By (
2.1.30
),
|F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})|\geq\frac{3}{4}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
implies
\sup\limits_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|(\nabla F(x))^{-1}|\leq\frac{4}{3}
, so
\|(\nabla F)^{-1}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{4}{3}
, which does not depend on
R
as well. This establishes (
2.1.31
).

Note that the identity matrix
I
can be viewed as a constant function defined on the unit ball. Since
\Phi
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we get the equality
I=I\circ\Phi
as a matrix function on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By the chain rule
I=\nabla(F\circ\Phi)=((\nabla F)\circ\Phi)\cdot\nabla\Phi
, so
\nabla\Phi-I=(\nabla F)^{-1}\circ\Phi-I=((\nabla F)^{-1}-I)\circ\Phi
.

By Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
, we know
\|G\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq K_{1}(n^{2},n,\alpha,\alpha,C_{1})\|G\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}
holds when (
2.1.31
) is satisfied, where
K_{1}
is the constant in Proposition
2.1.30
.
Therefore by taking
G=(\nabla F)^{-1}-I
, we see that

\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}=\|((\nabla F)^{-1}-I)\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq K_{1}\|(\nabla F)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2K_{1}\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq 2K_{1}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

and we obtain
\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq K_{2}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}
by replacing
K_{2}
with
\max(K_{2},2K_{1})
.

Proof of
(ii)
: Let
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
be as in the assumption of
(ii)
. In particular,
\operatorname{supp}\lambda\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By the assumption
\|R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, we have
F(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}+\frac{1}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}=\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, so
\operatorname{supp}F_{*}\lambda=F(\operatorname{supp}\lambda)\subseteq\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

We define a 1-form
\rho
and a function
\xi
by

\rho=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\rho_{i}dx^{i}:=-\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast\vartheta d\lambda,\hskip 10.00002pt\xi:=-\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast\vartheta\lambda.

\rho
and
\xi
are globally defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
because
\lambda
is compactly supported in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By Lemma
2.1.40
, we have
\rho\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

\|\rho\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\|\vartheta d\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}}\lesssim_{\beta}\|d\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\xi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\vartheta\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(2.1.32)

Since convolution commutes with
d
and
\vartheta
, by construction
\lambda-\rho=-\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast(d\vartheta+\vartheta d)\lambda+\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast\vartheta\lambda=-d(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast\vartheta f)=d\xi
. By direct computation along with Lemma
2.1.17

F_{*}df=d(f\circ F^{\mathrm{Inv}})=dF_{*}f
for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\subset C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, therefore

d(F_{*}\lambda)=d(F_{*}\rho)+d(F_{*}d\xi)=d(F_{*}\rho)+d^{2}(\xi\circ F^{\mathrm{Inv}})=d(F_{*}\rho)=d\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\rho_{i}\circ\Phi)d\phi^{i}\Big),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(2.1.33)

Thus, to prove (
2.1.28
), by (
2.1.32
) and the fact that
\operatorname{supp}dF_{*}\lambda\subseteq\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
it suffices to show

\|d(F_{*}\rho)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|\rho\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

(2.1.34)

Fix
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that
\chi|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 1
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
, set

\tilde{\rho}_{i}:=\chi(\rho_{i}\circ\Phi),\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{\phi}^{i}:=\chi\phi^{i}.

So
\tilde{\rho}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
d\tilde{\phi}^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
are globally defined 1-forms for each
i
, such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\big|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left((\rho_{i}\circ\Phi)d\phi^{i}\right)\big|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=(F_{*}\rho)\big|_{\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

(2.1.35)

By (
2.1.35
) and (
2.1.33
) we have

\|d(F_{*}\lambda)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\Big\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\Big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|d(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

By Lemma
2.1.17
we have
\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\|\chi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\rho_{i}\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
and
\|\tilde{\phi}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\chi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, by the result
(i)
and Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
we have
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 1
and
\|\rho_{i}\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|\rho_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
. Combining them we get

\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|\rho_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\tilde{\phi}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}1,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq n.

(2.1.36)

Thus, to obtain (
2.1.34
) and complete the proof, it
suffices to show

\|d(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq n.

(2.1.37)

To prove (
2.1.37
) we define 1-form
\tau_{i}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by

\tau_{i}:=\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})-\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\phi}^{i},d\tilde{\rho}_{i})+\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i}),\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq n.

By Lemma
2.1.13
, we have
\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i}=\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})+\mathfrak{P}(d\tilde{\phi}^{i},\tilde{\rho}_{i})+\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})
. Since as vector-valued functions
d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\phi}^{i},\tilde{\rho}_{i})=\mathfrak{P}(d\tilde{\phi}^{i},\tilde{\rho}_{i})+\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\phi}^{i},d\tilde{\rho}_{i})
holds for
1\leq i\leq n
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we have

d\tau_{i}=d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})-d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\phi}^{i},d\tilde{\rho}_{i})+d\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})=d\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})+d\mathfrak{P}(d\tilde{\phi}^{i},\tilde{\rho}_{i})+d\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})=d(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i}).

(2.1.38)

We claim that

\|\tau_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq n.

(2.1.39)

By Lemma
2.1.13
, along with the fact that
\|\tilde{\phi}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim 1
, we get
\|\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
and
\|\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|\mathfrak{R}(\tilde{\rho}_{i},d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta}}\lesssim\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
.

To complete the proof of (
2.1.39
), we
need to show

\|\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\phi}^{i},d\tilde{\rho}_{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq n.

(2.1.40)

We separate the proof of (
2.1.40
) into two cases:
\beta>1
and
0<\beta\leq 1
. We apply Lemma
2.1.16
.

For the case
\beta>1
, (
2.1.40
) follows from (
2.1.18
) with (
2.1.36
) that
\tilde{\phi}^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
d\tilde{\rho}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\subsetneq L^{\infty}
.
For the case
\beta\leq 1
, our assumption
0<\alpha\leq\beta
implies that
\alpha\in(0,1]
. So
\beta-\alpha-1<\beta-1\leq 0
and we have
d\tilde{\rho}_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\alpha-1}
. By (
2.1.19
) along with
\tilde{\phi}^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
from (
2.1.36
), we get
\mathfrak{P}(\tilde{\phi}^{i},d\tilde{\rho}_{i})\in\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha+1)+(\beta-\alpha-1)}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, which gives (
2.1.40
) and establishes (
2.1.39
).

Using (
2.1.38
) and (
2.1.39
),
we see

\|d(\tilde{\rho}_{i}d\tilde{\phi}^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}=\|d\tau_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\lesssim\|\tau_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|\tilde{\rho}_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
, establishing (
2.1.37
)
and completing the proof (by possibly increasing
K_{2}
).
∎

In Section
4.1.3
, we need the following existence of second order elliptic operators with zero Dirichlet boundary:

Proposition 2.1.45
.

Let
m,n\geq 1
and
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain. Then there is a
K_{3}=K_{3}(U,m,\alpha)>0
that satisfies the following:

Let
\displaystyle L_{B}:=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}B^{1}_{jk}\partial_{j}(B^{2}_{jk}\partial_{k})+\sum_{l=1}^{n}B^{3}_{l}\partial_{l}
be a second order differential operator on
\mathbb{C}^{m}
-valued functions, with
B^{1}_{jk},B^{2}_{jk}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
and
B^{3}_{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
. Suppose

\|B\|_{\alpha}=\|B\|_{\alpha;\phi,E}:=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\sum_{\nu=1}^{2}\|B^{\nu}_{jk}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|B^{3}_{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}<K_{3}^{-1}.

(2.1.41)

Then the following map is (both side) invertible,

\Delta+L_{B}:\{F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m}):F|_{\partial U}\equiv 0\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m}),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha].

The difficult part is to show the invertibility when
\beta
closed to
1-\alpha
, whereas
K_{3}
cannot depend on
\beta
.

In this subsection only, we use Littlewood-Paley characterizations to define
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-norms. More precisely:

Definition 2.1.46
.

Let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution (see Definition
2.1.2
) of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and let
E
be a fixed extension operator for
U
such that
E:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear for all
-1\leq\beta\leq 2
. Let
\mathscr{X}
be a finite dimensional vector space, for
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U;\mathscr{X})
we use the following norms

\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}(\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\beta}\|\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})},\hskip 10.00002pt\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}(\mathscr{X})}:=\|Ef\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}(\mathscr{X})}.

(2.1.42)

By Lemma
2.1.5
such
E
exists. By Lemma
2.1.4
and Definition
2.1.6
,
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}(\mathscr{X})}
is an equivalent norm of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathscr{X})
for all
-1\leq\beta\leq 2
.

Clearly for every
-1\leq\beta_{0}\leq\beta_{1}\leq 2
,

\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{0}}_{\phi}}\leq\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}_{\phi}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{0}}_{\phi,E}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}_{\phi,E}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\hskip 10.00002ptf\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}(U).

Note that both inequalities have no extra constant.

For
\beta\in(0,2]
, we see that for every
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
,

\textstyle\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-j\beta}=\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\tfrac{1}{1-2^{-\beta}}\leq\frac{3}{\beta}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}.

(2.1.43)

Thus
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\frac{3}{\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}}
as well.

Moreover by
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.4.7(i)]
,
\{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}:-1\leq\beta\leq 2\}
forms an exact complex interpolation family, see
[
Tri95
, Definition 1.2.2/2]
. In particular, for any
\beta_{0},\beta_{1},\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}\in[-1,2]
and for any linear operator
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{j}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma_{j}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded for
j=0,1
, then
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\gamma_{0}+\theta\gamma_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is also bounded for all
0<\theta<1
, with operator norm

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1}}_{\phi}\to\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\gamma_{0}+\theta\gamma_{1}}_{\phi}}\leq\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{0}}_{\phi}\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma_{0}}_{\phi}}^{1-\theta}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}_{\phi}\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma_{1}}_{\phi}}^{\theta}.

(2.1.44)

There is no extra constant in (
2.1.44
) as well.

Applying Lemma
2.2.35
via scaling,
\Delta:\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U):f|_{\partial U}\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U)
is invertible for all
0\leq\beta\leq 1
. Recall that we denote its inverse by
\mathcal{P}_{0}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U)
in Notation
2.1.43
.

By (
2.1.42
)
\|\mathcal{P}_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}=\|\tilde{f}\mapsto E\mathcal{P}_{0}(\tilde{f}|_{U})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi}}
. Therefore by (
2.1.44
), there is a
C_{0}=C_{0}(U,m,\phi,E)>0
such that

\|\mathcal{P}_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq C_{0},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq\beta\leq 1.

(2.1.45)

We need an improvement of Lemma
2.1.16
.

Lemma 2.1.47

(
[
BCD11
, Theorems 2.82 and 2.85]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{R}
be the paraproduct operators in Definition
2.1.14
associated with the same
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
in Definition
2.1.46
. There is
C_{2}=C_{2}(n,\phi)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{P}(f,g)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}},

\displaystyle\forall\beta\in[-1,2],\hskip 10.00002ptf\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n});

(2.1.46)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{P}(f,g)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\gamma}_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}}{-\gamma}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\phi}},

\displaystyle\forall\beta\in[-1,2],\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma\in[-1,0),\ f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n});

(2.1.47)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{R}(f,g)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\gamma}_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\phi}}}{\beta+\gamma},

\displaystyle\forall\beta,\gamma\in[-1,2],\ \beta+\gamma>0,\ f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),\ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(2.1.48)

Proof of Proposition
2.1.45
.

In the proof we use
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\phi}
-norm from Definition
2.1.46
instead of the classical
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-norm in Definition
1.2.1
.

Recall in Notation
2.1.43

\mathcal{P}_{0}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
is the inverse operator to
\Delta
with zero Dirichlet boundary, for all
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
. Thus if
\Delta+L_{B}
is invertible, then
(\Delta+L_{B})^{-1}=(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}
, and
\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B}
is an isomorphism on
\{F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m}):F|_{\partial U}\equiv 0\}
.

By (
2.1.45
)
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is uniformly bounded. Therefore to prove (
2.1.49
) it suffices to show
\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B}
is an isomorphism on
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
when (
2.1.41
) is satisfied.

We also assume that
K_{3}
can depend on
\phi
and
E
in the proof. The extra dependence can be eliminated when we switch the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi,E}}
norm back to the
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}
norm.

We claim that, by taking
K_{3}
suitably large, we have

\|(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi,E}(\mathbb{C}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}(\mathbb{C}^{m})}\leq K_{3}\cdot(\beta+\alpha-1)^{-1},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha].

(2.1.49)

For
F=(F^{1},\dots,F^{m})
, we write

(L_{B}F)^{i}=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\sum_{u,v=1}^{m}B^{1,iu}_{jk}\partial_{j}(B^{2,uv}_{jk}\partial_{k}F^{v})+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{u=1}^{m}B^{3,iu}_{l}\partial_{l}F^{u},\hskip 10.00002pti=1,\dots,m.

We take decomposition
L_{B}=L_{B,1}+L_{B,2}
where for
i=1,\dots,m
,

\displaystyle(L_{B,1}F)^{i}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\sum_{u,v=1}^{m}\left(\mathfrak{P}\big(EB^{1,iu}_{jk},E\partial_{j}(B^{2,uv}_{jk}\partial_{k}F^{v})\big)+\mathfrak{P}\big(E\partial_{j}(B^{2,uv}_{jk}\partial_{k}F^{v}),EB^{1,iu}_{jk}\big)\right)\Big|_{U}

\displaystyle+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{u=1}^{m}\left(\mathfrak{P}(EB^{3,iu}_{l},E\partial_{l}F^{u})+\mathfrak{P}(E\partial_{l}F^{u},EB^{3,iu}_{l})\right)\Big|_{U};

\displaystyle(L_{B,2}F)^{i}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\sum_{u,v=1}^{m}\mathfrak{R}\big(EB^{1,iu}_{jk},E\partial_{j}(B^{2,uv}_{jk}\partial_{k}F^{v})\big)\Big|_{U}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{u=1}^{m}\mathfrak{R}(EB^{3,iu}_{l},E\partial_{l}F^{u})\Big|_{U}.

By Lemma
2.1.47

\|B^{2,uv}_{jk}\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}}
is uniformly bounded in
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
and
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
:

\displaystyle\|B^{2,uv}_{jk}\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}}=\|\mathfrak{P}(EB^{2,uv}_{jk},E\partial_{k}F^{v})+\mathfrak{P}(E\partial_{k}F^{v},EB^{2,uv}_{jk})+\mathfrak{R}(EB^{2,uv}_{jk},E\partial_{k}F^{v})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}\min\big(\|EB^{2,uv}_{jk}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\|E\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{L^{\infty}},\tfrac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\|EB^{2,uv}_{jk}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi}}\|E\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\alpha}_{\phi}}\big)

\displaystyle+C_{2}\|EB^{2,uv}_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|E\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}+\tfrac{C_{2}}{\alpha}\|EB^{2,uv}_{jk}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi}}\|E\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\alpha}_{\phi}}

(by Lemma
2.1.47
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}(\min(\tfrac{3}{\beta},\tfrac{1}{\alpha-\beta})+1+\tfrac{1}{\alpha})\|EB^{2,uv}_{jk}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi}}\|E\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}\leq 11C_{2}\|B^{2,uv}_{jk}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi,E}}\|\partial_{k}F^{v}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}}.

(by (
2.1.43
))

Similarly
L_{B,1}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
is uniformly bounded in
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1],\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
:

\displaystyle\|L_{B,1}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi}}\lesssim

{}_{E}\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1-\beta}\|EB^{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi}}\|E\nabla(B^{2}\otimes\nabla F)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1-\alpha}_{\phi}}+\|EB^{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|E\nabla(B^{2}\otimes\nabla F)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi}}

\displaystyle+\min\big(\|EB^{3}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi}}\|E\nabla F\|_{L^{\infty}},\tfrac{1}{\alpha-\beta}\|EB^{3}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\phi}}\|E\nabla F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\alpha}}\big)+\|EB^{3}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{\phi}}\|E\nabla F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big(\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1-\beta}+\tfrac{1}{\alpha}\big)\|B_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi,E}}\|\nabla(B^{2}\otimes\nabla F)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi,E}}+\big(\min(\tfrac{1}{\beta},\tfrac{1}{\alpha-\beta})+1\big)\|B^{3}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\phi,E}}\|\nabla F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{U,m}\|B\|_{\alpha}(1+\|B\|_{\alpha})\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}.

Here we use
\frac{1}{\alpha+1-\beta}<\frac{1}{3/2-1}=2
and
\min(\frac{1}{\beta},\frac{1}{\alpha-\beta})\leq\frac{2}{\alpha}<4
.

L_{B,2}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
is not uniformly bounded but gains a few derivatives: we have
\alpha+\beta>1
,

\displaystyle\|L_{B,2}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\phi,E}}

\displaystyle\leq\|L_{B,2}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\beta-1}_{\phi,E}}\lesssim_{U,m,E}\tfrac{1}{\alpha+\beta-1}\big(\|B^{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\phi,E}}\|\nabla(B^{2}\otimes\nabla F)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\phi,E}}+\|B^{3}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\phi,E}}\|\nabla F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\phi,E}}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{U,m}(\alpha+\beta-1)^{-1}\|B\|_{\alpha}(1+\|B\|_{\alpha})\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}.

Combining (
2.1.45
), there is a
C_{3}=C_{3}(U,m,\phi,E)>1
such that for every
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
,
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
and
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
,

\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq C_{3}\|B\|_{\alpha}(1+\|B\|_{\alpha})\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq\frac{C_{3}\|B\|_{\alpha}(1+\|B\|_{\alpha})}{\alpha+\beta-1}\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}.

(2.1.50)

Take
K_{3}:=\frac{8C_{3}}{2\alpha-1}
, we see that
K_{3}=K_{3}(U,m,\phi,E,\alpha)\in(0,1)
. When (
2.1.41
) is satisfied, (
2.1.50
) implies that
\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq\frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{2\alpha-1}{\alpha+\beta-1}
. In particular
\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{4}<\frac{1}{2}
is a contraction mapping.

Therefore for every
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
and
\beta\in(1-\alpha,\alpha]
, using power series
(\operatorname{id}+T)^{-1}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}T^{k}
, provided that the right hand side converges, taking
T\in\{\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1},(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2}\}
we get

\displaystyle\|(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}^{k}\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}4^{-k}\leq\frac{4}{3}.

\displaystyle\|(\operatorname{id}+(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}

\displaystyle\textstyle\leq\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}\|(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}^{k}\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}^{k}\leq\sum\limits_{k=0}^{\infty}3^{-k}\leq\frac{3}{2}.

Finally for the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-boundedness of
(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B})^{-1}
, we use

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B})^{-1}=

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2})^{-1}=(\operatorname{id}+(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2})^{-1}(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}-((\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2})(\operatorname{id}+(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2})^{-1}(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\|(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}\leq

\displaystyle\|(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}}\big(1+\|\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}

\displaystyle\times\|(\operatorname{id}+(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,2})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}\|(\operatorname{id}+\mathcal{P}_{0}L_{B,1})^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\phi,E}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{4}{3}\times\big(1+\tfrac{2\alpha-1}{4(\alpha+\beta-1)}+\tfrac{3}{2}\cdot\tfrac{4}{3}\big)\leq\tfrac{13}{3}(\alpha+\beta-1)^{-1}.

Since
K_{3}=\frac{8C_{3}}{2\alpha-1}>\frac{3}{13}
, we get (
2.1.45
) and finish the proof.
∎

2.1.4
An approximation theorem on vanishing products

Recall the Littlewood-Paley operators
\mathsf{P}_{\sigma}=\phi_{\sigma}\ast(-)
and
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}=\sum_{j=0}^{\sigma}\phi_{\sigma}\ast(-)=2^{n\sigma}\phi_{0}(2^{\sigma}\cdot)\ast(-)
in Definition
2.1.2
, where
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
is a dyadic resolution.

The log-Lipschitz Frobenius theorems (Theorems
1
and
2
) rely heavily on the following:

Theorem 2.1.48
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha)
. Let
m,n\geq 1
. There is a
C=C_{n,m,\phi,\alpha,\beta}>0
such that, if
\mathbf{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
\mathbf{g}\in\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
satisfy
\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{g}=0
in the sense of distributions, then

\|(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C\cdot 2^{-(\alpha-\beta)\sigma}\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})},\hskip 20.00003pt\forall\sigma\geq 1.

(2.1.51)

By Lemma
2.1.17

(i)
, we know the equality
\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{g}=0
holds in
\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Note that we already have the convergence
\lim\limits_{\sigma\to\infty}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})=0
in
\mathscr{C}^{-\beta-\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, for every
\delta>0
.
Theorem
2.1.48
says that this sequence converges uniformly with quantitative speed, which is better than the convergence in some spaces of distributions.

In the proof we use the following modified paraproduct formula, which can be deduced using (
2.1.3
) and (
2.1.17
):

\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{g})=\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g})+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j,k\geq l-2;|j-k|\leq 5}(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}).

(2.1.52)

We leave the deduction to readers.

Proof of Theorem
2.1.48
.

We set
\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}}=1
without loss of generality.

Clearly
\|2^{n\sigma}\phi_{0}(2^{\sigma}\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}=\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}
for all
\sigma\geq 0
, and
\|\phi_{\sigma}\|_{L^{1}}=\|2^{n(\sigma-1)}\phi_{1}(2^{\sigma-1}\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}=\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{1}}
for all
\sigma\geq 1
. Therefore we have the following uniform bounds,
