\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}\leq\|2^{n\sigma}\phi_{0}(2^{\sigma}\cdot)\|_{L^{1}}=\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathsf{P}_{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}\leq\|\phi_{\sigma}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\|\phi_{0}\|_{L^{1}}+\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 0.

(2.1.53)

By (
2.1.2
),
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}f
and
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}g
both have Fourier supports in
\{|\xi|<2^{\sigma+1}\}
. Thus when
l\geq\sigma+3
,
\operatorname{supp}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}f\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}g)^{\wedge}\subseteq\{|\xi|<2^{\sigma+2}\}
is disjoint from
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{l}\subseteq\{|\xi|>2^{l-1}\}
, which means
\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}f\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}g)=0
. Therefore we have

\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g}=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})=\sum_{l=0}^{\sigma+2}\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})+\sum_{l=\sigma-3}^{\sigma+2}\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g}).

(2.1.54)

Our goal is to prove
\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{-(\alpha-\beta)\sigma}
and
\sum_{l=\sigma-3}^{\sigma+2}\|\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{-(\alpha-\beta)\sigma}
, where the implicit constants depend only on
m,n,\alpha,\beta
and
\phi
.

By (
2.1.52
) and the assumption
\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{g}=0
, we know,

\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g})+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j,k\geq l-2;|j-k|\leq 5}(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})=0,\hskip 10.00002ptl=0,1,2,\dots.

(2.1.55)

For
\sigma,l\geq 0
, we have decomposition

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})=\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathbf{g})=

\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\big((\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g}\big)

(2.1.56)

\displaystyle+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}\big((\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big)

(2.1.57)

\displaystyle+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j,k\geq l-2;|j-k|\leq 5}\big((\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big).

(2.1.58)

When
l\leq\sigma-4
and
|j-l|\leq 2
, we have
j\leq\sigma-2
. By (
2.1.3
) we have
\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{P}_{j}
and
\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{S}_{j-6}
, so in this case (
2.1.56
) is zero. Similarly (
2.1.57
) vanishes as well. Thus for
\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})
in (
2.1.54
) we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\sigma-4}\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j,k\geq l-2;|j-k|\leq 5}\big((\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l=0}^{\sigma-4}\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j,k\geq\sigma-2;|j-k|\leq 5}\big((\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}\sum_{j,k\geq\sigma-2;|j-k|\leq 5}\big((\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big).

Using (
2.1.53
) and Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
we have an estimate for (
2.1.58
): for every
l\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq l-2\\
|j-k|\leq 5\end{subarray}}\big\|(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big\|_{C^{0}}\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq l-2\\
|j-k|\leq 5\end{subarray}}\big(\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}^{2}+1\big)\|\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}

(2.1.59)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle{}_{\phi}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq l-2\\
|j-k|\leq 5\end{subarray}}\|\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim_{\phi,\alpha,\beta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq l-2\\
|j-k|\leq 5\end{subarray}}2^{-j\alpha}2^{k\beta}\lesssim\sum_{q\geq l}2^{-q\alpha}2^{q\beta}\lesssim 2^{-l(\alpha-\beta)}.

Taking
l=\sigma
in (
2.1.59
) and using (
2.1.53
) again we get

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}}\leq\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,k\geq l-2\\
|j-k|\leq 5\end{subarray}}\big\|(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}.

(2.1.60)

This gives the first estimate
\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma-4}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
for (
2.1.54
).

Next we prove
\|\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
when
\sigma-3\leq l\leq\sigma+2
. In this case (
2.1.56
) and (
2.1.57
) do not vanish.

The term (
2.1.56
) is still good: by (
2.1.53
) and Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\big((\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})(\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g}\big)\Big\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\big(\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}^{2}+1\big)\|\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}

(2.1.61)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}2^{-j\alpha}2^{j\beta}\approx 2^{-l(\alpha-\beta)}.

Combining (
2.1.61
) and (
2.1.59
) we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g})+\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{j,k\geq l-2;|j-k|\leq 5}(\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})\Big\|_{C^{0}}

(2.1.62)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{P}_{l}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}\Big(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}2^{-j\alpha}2^{j\beta}+\sum_{j,k\geq l-2;|j-k|\leq 5}2^{-j\alpha}2^{k\beta}\Big)\approx 2^{-l(\alpha-\beta)}.

By assumption
\sigma-3\leq l\leq\sigma+2
, so the right hand side above is bounded by
2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
.

It remains to estimate (
2.1.57
). Note that for
|k-l|\leq 2
and
l\leq\sigma+2
we have
k\leq\sigma+4
. By (
2.1.3
) we have
\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}=\mathsf{S}_{j-6}
. Therefore we can rewrite the summand in (
2.1.57
) as,

\textstyle(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}=\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g}-\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})=\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\big(\mathsf{P}_{k}\sum_{\mu=\sigma+1}^{\sigma+5}\mathsf{P}_{\mu}\mathbf{g}\big).

(2.1.63)

Here for the last equality above, we use the fact
\mathsf{P}_{k}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}-\operatorname{id})=\mathsf{P}_{k}\sum_{\mu=\sigma+1}^{\infty}\mathsf{P}_{\mu}=\mathsf{P}_{k}\sum_{\mu=\sigma+1}^{\sigma+5}\mathsf{P}_{\mu}
.
We define

\textstyle\psi_{\sigma}:=\sum_{\mu=\sigma+1}^{\sigma+5}\phi_{\mu},\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma\geq 0.

Thus
\mathsf{P}_{k}\sum_{\mu=\sigma+1}^{\sigma+5}\mathsf{P}_{\mu}\mathbf{g}=\mathsf{P}_{k}(\psi_{\sigma}\ast\mathbf{g})=\psi_{\sigma}\ast\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}
.
Therefore for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot(\psi_{\sigma}\ast\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f})(x)\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})(x-y)\psi_{\sigma}(y)dy

(2.1.64)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\psi_{\sigma}\ast(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})\big)(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\psi_{\sigma}(y)\big((\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f})(x)-(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f})(x-y)\big)\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})(x-y)dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\psi_{\sigma}\ast(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})\big)(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\Big(\int_{0}^{1}y\psi_{\sigma}(y)\cdot(\nabla\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f})(x-ty)dt\Big)\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})(x-y)dy.

By scaling we have
\|x\phi_{\mu}(x)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}=2^{1-\mu}\|x\phi_{1}(x)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}
and
\|\phi_{\mu}\|_{L^{1}}=\|\phi_{1}\|_{L^{1}}
for all
\mu\geq 1
, thus

\|x\psi_{\sigma}(x)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}=2^{-\sigma}\|x\psi_{0}(x)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\approx_{\phi}2^{-\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\psi_{\sigma}\|_{L^{1}}\approx_{\phi}1,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 0.

(2.1.65)

Combining (
2.1.65
) with (
2.1.55
) and (
2.1.64
), for
\sigma-3\leq l\leq\sigma+2
we have

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}\big((\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f})\cdot(\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})-\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\big)\Big\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\|\mathsf{P}_{l}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}\psi_{\sigma}\ast(\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g})\Big\|_{C^{0}}+\|\mathsf{P}_{l}\|_{C^{0}\to C^{0}}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|y\psi_{\sigma}(y)|\|\nabla\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\mathbf{f}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\|\psi_{\sigma}\ast\mathsf{P}_{l}\Big(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{j-6}\mathbf{g}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}|j-k|\leq 5\\
j,k\geq l-2\end{subarray}}\mathsf{P}_{j}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\Big)\Big\|_{C^{0}}+\|\phi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}\|y\psi_{\sigma}\|_{L^{1}_{y}}\|\mathsf{S}_{k-6}\nabla\mathbf{f}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}

by
(
2.1.55
)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\psi_{\sigma}\|_{L^{1}}2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}+\|x\mapsto x\psi_{\sigma}(x)\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{k=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{\mu=0}^{k-6}\|\mathsf{P}_{\mu}\nabla\mathbf{f}\|_{C^{0}}\|\mathsf{P}_{k}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}

by
(
2.1.62
)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|\psi_{\sigma}\|_{L^{1}}2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}+2^{1-\sigma}\|x\mapsto x\psi_{1}(x)\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{\mu=0}^{\sigma}\|\nabla\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}2^{\mu(1-\alpha)}\|\mathbf{g}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}}2^{l\beta}

by
(
2.1.4
)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}+2^{-\sigma}2^{\sigma(1-\alpha)}2^{\sigma\beta}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}.

by
(
2.1.65
)

Here for the last inequality we use the fact
\|\nabla\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\lesssim\|\mathbf{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=1
from Remark
2.1.7

(i)
.

Combining the above estimate with (
2.1.59
) and (
2.1.61
) we get
\|\mathsf{P}_{l}(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g})\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
when
\sigma-3\leq l\leq\sigma+2
, which is the second estimate for (
2.1.54
).

We therefore have
\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
and finish the proof.
∎

In application we take
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{g}
in Theorem
2.1.48
to be the coefficients of vector fields and their derivatives. The theorem give a systematical way of approximating vector fields.

Proposition 2.1.49
.

Let
m\geq 1
and
0<\alpha,\beta<1
be satisfy
\alpha>\max(\frac{1}{2},\beta)
. Let
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open set, Let
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
be a precompact open subset and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
satisfies
\chi|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\equiv 1
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
be vector fields on
\Omega
. Assuming there are distributions
(c_{ij}^{k})_{i,j,k=1}^{m}\subset\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
such that

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq m.

(2.1.66)

For
\sigma=0,1,2,\dots
, we define

X^{\sigma}_{i}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\chi X_{i})=(\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\chi a_{i}^{1}),\dots,\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\chi a_{i}^{n})),\hskip 10.00002ptc_{ij}^{k\sigma}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\chi c_{ij}^{k}),\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j,k\leq m.

(2.1.67)

Then there is a constant constant
C
that does not depend on
\sigma
(but can depend on
X_{i},c_{ij}^{k},\chi,\phi
), such that

\Big\|[X^{\sigma}_{i},X^{\sigma}_{j}]-\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k\sigma}X_{k}^{\sigma}\Big\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C2^{-\sigma\min(\alpha-\beta,2\alpha-1)},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 0.

(2.1.68)

Recall from Lemmas
2.1.18

(ii)
and Corollary
2.1.19
,
c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
[X_{i},X_{j}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
are defined as distributions.

Proof.

We can assume
\beta\geq 1-\alpha
, hence the right hand side of (
2.1.68
) becomes
C2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
. By computation,

[\chi X_{i},\chi X_{j}]-(X_{i}\chi)\cdot\chi X_{j}+(X_{j}\chi)\cdot\chi X_{i}-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\chi c_{ij}^{k}\cdot\chi X_{k}=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\mathbb{R}^{n},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq m.

(2.1.69)

By writing
X_{i}=:\sum_{l=1}^{n}a^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}
where
a^{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
, (
2.1.69
) becomes

\sum_{q=1}^{n}\Big(\chi a_{i}^{q}\frac{\partial(\chi a_{j}^{l})}{\partial x^{q}}-\chi a_{j}^{q}\frac{\partial(\chi a_{i}^{l})}{\partial x^{q}}\Big)+\sum_{q=1}^{n}\Big(\chi a_{i}^{l}\cdot a_{j}^{q}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial x^{q}}-\chi a_{j}^{l}\cdot a_{i}^{q}\frac{\partial\chi}{\partial x^{q}}\Big)-\sum_{k=1}^{m}\chi c_{ij}^{k}\cdot\chi a_{k}^{l}=0,\ 1\leq i,j\leq m,\ 1\leq l\leq n.

(2.1.70)

We see that the above equality holds in
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha-1,-\beta)}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

In order to apply Theorem
2.1.48
, we define a function
\mathbf{f}_{ij}^{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{2n+m+1})
and a distribution
\mathbf{g}_{ij}^{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{2n+m+1})
for
1\leq i,j\leq m
and
1\leq l\leq n
, as

\begin{array}[]{ccccccccccccccc}\@add@centering\mathbf{f}_{ij}^{l}:=\big(&\chi a_{i}^{1}&,\dots,&\chi a_{i}^{n}&,&-\chi a_{j}^{1}&,\dots,&-\chi a_{j}^{n}&,&-\chi a_{1}^{l}&,\dots,&-\chi a_{m}^{l}&,&\textstyle\sum_{q=1}^{n}\big(\chi a_{i}^{l}a_{j}^{q}\partial_{q}\chi-\chi a_{j}^{l}a_{i}^{q}\partial_{q}\chi\big)&\big),\\
\mathbf{g}_{ij}^{l}:=\big(&\partial_{1}(\chi a_{j}^{l})&,\dots,&\partial_{n}(\chi a_{j}^{l})&,&\partial_{1}(\chi a_{i}^{l})&,\dots,&\partial_{n}(\chi a_{i}^{l})&,&\chi c_{ij}^{1}&,\dots,&\chi c_{ij}^{m}&,&\centering 1&\big).\end{array}

The condition (
2.1.70
) is now the same as saying
\mathbf{f}_{ij}^{l}\cdot\mathbf{g}_{ij}^{l}=0\in\mathscr{C}^{-\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
1\leq i,j\leq m
,
1\leq l\leq n
.

By Theorem
2.1.48
we have
\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{f}_{ij}^{l}\cdot\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\mathbf{g}_{ij}^{l}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
for all
i,j,l
and
\sigma
, in other words,

\bigg\|[X_{i}^{\sigma},X_{j}^{\sigma}]+\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{j}\chi)\cdot X_{i}^{\sigma}-\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)\cdot X_{j}^{\sigma}-\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k\sigma}X_{k}^{\sigma}\bigg\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq m,\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma\geq 0.

To prove (
2.1.68
) it remains to show that
\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{j}\chi)\cdot X_{i}^{\sigma}-\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)\cdot X_{j}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq m
. Indeed, by assumption
\chi|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\equiv 1
, so
X_{i}\chi|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\equiv 0
. Hence for
1\leq i\leq m
,

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}=

\displaystyle\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)-X_{i}\chi\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}\leq\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)-X_{i}\chi\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\sum_{k=\sigma+1}^{\infty}\|\mathsf{P}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}

(2.1.71)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|X_{i}\chi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}2^{-\sigma\alpha}\lesssim_{\alpha,X,\chi}2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{j}\chi)\cdot X_{i}^{\sigma}-\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{i}\chi)\cdot X_{j}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\|\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(X_{j}\chi)\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}\|X_{i}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}\sup_{1\leq i\leq m}\|\chi X_{i}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{-\sigma(\alpha-\beta)}.

This completes the proof.
∎

As a direct application to Proposition
2.1.49
, we see that for log-Lipschitz subbundles, the distributional involutivity (see Definition
0
) implies the strongly asymptotically involutivity (see Definition
5.1.5
).

Corollary 2.1.50
.

Following the assumptions of Proposition
2.1.49
, and assuming
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\|X_{i}^{\nu}-X_{i}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0
for all
1\leq i\leq m
, and there is a
t_{0}>0
such that

\lim\limits_{\nu\to\infty}\max_{1\leq i,j\leq m}\Big\|[X^{\nu}_{i},X^{\nu}_{j}]-\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k\nu}X_{k}^{\nu}\Big\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\exp\Big(t_{0}\cdot\max_{1\leq l\leq m}\|\nabla X_{l}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\Big)=0.

(2.1.72)

In particular, for the case of two commutative vector fields (
m=2
and
c_{ij}^{k}\equiv 0
), namely
[X_{1},X_{2}]=0
in
\Omega
, we have

\exists t_{0}>0,\ \lim\limits_{\nu\to\infty}\|[X_{1}^{\nu},X_{2}^{\nu}]\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}e^{t_{0}\left(\|\nabla X_{1}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|\nabla X_{2}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\right)}=0.

(2.1.73)

Remark 2.1.51
.

For a distributional involutive log-Lipschitz subbundle
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
, by Lemma
2.3.9

(iii)
locally we can find a log-Lipschitz basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
for
\mathcal{V}
(
r=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{V}
) such that
[X_{i},X_{j}]=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq r
. So by (
2.1.72
) with
c_{ij}^{k}=c_{ij}^{k\nu}\equiv 0
, we see that the condition (
5.1.2
) is satisfied (see Section
5.1.1
). We conclude that a log-Lipschitz involutive subbundle is always strongly asymptotic involutive (see Definition
5.1.5
).

Proof of Corollary
2.1.50
.

By assumption
(X_{i}^{\nu}-X_{i})|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=(X_{i}^{\nu}-\chi X_{i})|_{\Omega^{\prime}}=(\mathsf{S}_{\nu}(\chi X_{i})-\chi X_{i})|_{\Omega^{\prime}}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
. Thus similar to (
2.1.71
),

\|X_{i}^{\nu}-X_{i}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\mathsf{S}_{\nu}(\chi X_{i})-\chi X_{i}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\sum_{\nu+1}^{\infty}\|\mathsf{P}_{\nu}(\chi X_{i})\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\chi X_{i}\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}2^{-\nu\alpha}.

Let
\nu\to\infty
we see that
\lim_{\nu\to\infty}\|X_{i}^{\nu}-X_{i}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}=0
.

By Proposition
2.1.49
there is a
C_{1}>0
and a
\delta>0
(in fact
\delta=\min(\alpha-\beta,2\alpha-1)
) such that

\Big\|[X^{\nu}_{i},X^{\nu}_{j}]-\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k\nu}X_{k}^{\nu}\Big\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{1}2^{-\delta\nu},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\nu=1,2,3,\dots.

By Lemma
2.1.22

(ii)
we can find a
C_{2}>0
such that

\|\nabla X_{i}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\nabla X_{i}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{m};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{2}\nu,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\nu=1,2,3,\dots.

Therefore for
t_{0}>0
we have

\Big\|[X^{\nu}_{i},X^{\nu}_{j}]-\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k\nu}X_{k}^{\nu}\Big\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n})}e^{t_{0}\cdot\max_{1\leq l\leq m}\|\nabla X_{l}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}}\leq C_{1}2^{-\delta\nu}e^{t_{0}C_{2}\nu},\hskip 10.00002pt\nu=1,2,3,\dots.

(2.1.74)

Taking
t_{0}=(2C_{2})^{-1}\delta\log 2
, the right hand side of (
2.1.74
) is
C_{1}2^{-\frac{\delta}{2}\nu}
, which goes to
0
as
\nu\to\infty
. Therefore we get (
2.1.72
) for such
t_{0}
.

Replacing
t_{0}
by
\frac{t_{0}}{2}
we get (
2.1.73
).
∎

2.2
The Bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund Spaces

In this section we set up some bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund spaces and prove some results of products and compositions.

2.2.1
Basic definitions

We define bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund structures using vector valued formulation.

Definition 2.2.1
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta>0
. Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
be the coordinate system of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
respectively. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two open subsets. Let
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach space.

(i)

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}(U,V;\mathscr{X})=L^{\infty}_{s}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}(V,U;\mathscr{X})
to be the set of all
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U\times V)
such that
x\mapsto\langle f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
for all
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(V)
and

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{u\in C_{c}^{\infty}(V);\|u\|_{L^{1}(V)}=1}\|x\mapsto\langle f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{L^{\infty}(V),L^{1}(V)}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}(U;\mathscr{X})}<\infty.

(2.2.1)

(ii)

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(U,V;\mathscr{X}):=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathscr{X}))
with the same norm given in Definition
1.2.1
.

(iii)

For
\mathscr{Y},\mathscr{Z}\in\{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}:\alpha\in\mathbb{R},\beta>0\}
, we define
\mathscr{Y}\cap\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X}):=\mathscr{Y}(U,V;\mathscr{X})\cap\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X})
and
\mathscr{Y}+\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X}):=\mathscr{Y}(U,V;\mathscr{X})+\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X})
with norms

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{Y}\cap\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}

\displaystyle:=\|f\|_{\mathscr{Y}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X})},

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{Y}+\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}

\displaystyle:=\inf\{\|f_{1}\|_{\mathscr{Y}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}+\|f_{2}\|_{\mathscr{Z}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}:f=f_{1}+f_{2}\}.

Remark 2.2.2
.

(i)

The
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
spaces are symmetric in the sense that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V,U)
when
\alpha>0
. This follows from Definition
1.2.1
since we have
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V))
.

(ii)

When
\alpha>0
, the pair
\langle f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}
can be defined pointwise for
x\in U
. When
\alpha\leq 0
, the map
[x\mapsto\langle f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}]
can be realized as a distribution on
U
in the way that
v\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U)\mapsto\langle f,v\otimes u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(U\times V)}
, where
v\otimes u(x,s)=v(x)u(s)
.

(iii)

(
2.2.1
) is a rigorous way of saying
\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}<\infty
, see Lemma
2.2.6
.

(iv)

For
\alpha,\gamma\leq 0
, the definition
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
means there are extensions
\tilde{f}_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
\tilde{f}_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q})
such that
\tilde{f}_{1}|_{U\times V}=\tilde{f}_{2}|_{U\times V}=f
. When
U
is a smooth domains, by the existence of common extension operator we can choose
\tilde{f}_{1}=\tilde{f}_{2}:=E_{x}f
where
E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is extension operator which is bounded for
\delta\in\{\alpha,\gamma\}
.

Let
U,V
be to arbitrary open sets, we use
\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(V),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))
as the space of all bounded linear operators
L^{1}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
endowed with the standard operator norm. This is in fact the same as
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)
, see Lemma
2.2.3
.

Let
T:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(U_{1})
be a bounded linear operator, by Lemma
2.1.3
and Definition
2.2.1

(ii)
, the operator
\tilde{T}f(x,s):=T(f(\cdot,s))(x)
defines a bounded linear operator
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1},V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1},V)
for
\beta>0
with the same operator norms. This is also true if we replace
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
by
L^{\infty}
.

To summary, for
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}
-spaces we have the following:

Lemma 2.2.3
.

Let
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and let
U_{1},U_{2},V
be arbitrary open sets.

(i)

There is a canonical correspondence
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}L^{\infty}(U_{1},V)\cong\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(V),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1}))
with the respective norms are the same.

(ii)

Let
T:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(U_{2})
be a bounded linear operator, then there is a unique operator
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1},V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(U_{2},V)
such that

[y\in U_{2}\mapsto\langle(\tilde{T}f)(y,\cdot),u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}]=T[x\in U_{1}\mapsto\langle f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}],\hskip 10.00002pt\forall f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1}),\hskip 10.00002ptu\in C_{c}^{\infty}(V).

(2.2.2)

Moreover
\tilde{T}
and
T
have the same operator norms.

Proof.

Let
F:L^{1}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})
be a bounded linear operator, clearly
F:C_{c}^{\infty}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})\subset\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U_{1})
is bounded. Thus by the Schwartz Kernel Theorem
F
corresponds to a distribution
f\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(U_{1}\times V)
such that
\langle F(u),v\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(U)}=\langle f,u\otimes v\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(U\times V)}
. Since
\|x\mapsto\langle f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})}=\|F(u)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})}
, taking supremum over
u\in C_{c}^{\infty}
we get
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}L^{\infty}}\leq\|F\|_{L^{1}\to\mathscr{C}^{{\alpha_{1}}}}
. In particular such
F
gives a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}L^{\infty}(U_{1},V)

Conversely, for a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}L^{\infty}(U_{1},V)
, we have a map
F:C_{c}^{\infty}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})
given by
F(u)=\langle f,u\rangle_{\mathscr{D}^{\prime},C_{c}^{\infty}(V)}
. By (
2.2.1
),
\|F(u)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}L^{\infty}(U_{1},V)}\|u\|_{L^{1}(V)}
. Since
C_{c}^{\infty}(V)\subset L^{1}(V)
is dense,
F
uniquely extends to a bounded linear map
F:L^{1}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})
with
\|F\|_{L^{1}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}L^{\infty}}
. This finishes the proof of
(i)
.

Now the right hand side of (
2.2.2
) defines a bounded linear map
\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(V),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1}))\to\mathcal{L}(L^{1}(V),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(U_{2}))
, which has the same operator norm to
T
. By
(i)
this descends to a unique bounded linear map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1},V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(U_{2},V)
, which is our
\tilde{T}
. This finishes the proof of
(ii)
.
∎

Notation 2.2.4
.

For functions
f(x,s)
and
\rho(x)
, we use
\ast_{x}
as the convolution acting on
x
-variable:

\rho\ast_{x}f(x,s)=(\rho\ast f(\cdot,s))(x).

Note that if
f\in\mathscr{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s})
and
\rho\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, then
\rho\ast_{x}f
is a well-defined tempered distribution in
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
, since
\rho\ast_{x}f=(\rho\otimes\delta_{0^{q}})\ast f
. Here
\delta_{0^{q}}
is the Direc delta measure of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

Remark 2.2.5
.

In the thesis we use a different formulation of bi-parameter spaces from
[
Yao22
, Section 2.1]
. These two definitions coincide by the Lemma
2.2.6
below.

Definition
2.2.1

(ii)
is better than the characterization in Lemma
2.2.6

(iii)
because of Lemma
2.1.3
: for an operator
T:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U_{1})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(U_{2})
and its lift
\tilde{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1},V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1},V)
, we have
\|\tilde{T}\|=\|T\|
by Lemma
2.1.3
. However we do not know the relation between
\|\tilde{T}\|
and
\|T\|
if we use (
2.2.3
) to define
\|\tilde{T}\|
.

Lemma 2.2.6
.

Let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
\psi=(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
be two dyadic resolutions. Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta>0
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two bounded smooth domains.

(i)

f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V)
if and only if
\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V)
for all
j\geq 0
and
\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
. Moreover
f\mapsto\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}}
is an equivalent norm for
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V)
.

(ii)

f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
has the following equivalent norm:

f\mapsto\sup_{j,k\geq 0}2^{j\alpha+k\beta}\|(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k}(x,s)=\phi_{j}(x)\psi_{k}(s).

(iii)

Let
\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\}
.
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V)
has equivalent norm

f\mapsto\inf\{\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}:\tilde{f}|_{U\times V}=f\}.

(2.2.3)

(iv)

For
\alpha>0
,
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;L^{\infty}(V))
and the corresponding norms are equivalent. And the common space equals to the following

\{f\in L^{\infty}(U\times V):f(\cdot,s)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\text{ a.e. }s\in V\text{ with }\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}<\infty\}.

(2.2.4)

Remark 2.2.7
.

We postpone the proof of Lemma
2.2.6
after the proof of Lemma
2.2.8
. Thus from then on we can view
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
as a concrete function/distribution on product space
U\times V
satisfying the corresponding Littlewood-Paley characterization, rather than a vector-valued map. Thus by taking coordinate components components, we see that Lemma
2.2.6
is also true if we replace
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V)
(
\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
) by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{m})
or
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

On the domain case we need the following result of extension operators.

Lemma 2.2.8

(Common extensions)
.

Let
R>0
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two bounded domain with smooth boundaries.
Then there are extension operators
E_{x}:L^{\infty}(U)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
E_{s}:L^{\infty}(V)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
such that

(a)

E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
E_{s}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
are both bounded linear for all
-R<\alpha<R
.

(b)

E_{x}E_{s},E_{s}E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}L^{\infty}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{(-R)+}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
are well-defined and equal.

Moreover
\tilde{E}:=E_{x}E_{s}
is an extension operator for
U\times V
that has boundedness

\tilde{E}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}),\hskip 10.00002pt-R<\alpha<R,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\beta}:0<\beta<R\}.

(2.2.5)

Proof.

We define
E_{x}
and
E_{s}
as in (
2.1.11
) and (
2.1.12
) by taking
\Omega=U
and
\Omega=V
respectively and taking
M=2\lceil R\rceil
. By
[
Tri10
, Theorems 2.9.2, 2.9.4 (i) and 3.3.4 (i)]

E_{x}
and
E_{s}
are both
L^{\infty}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-bounded for
-R<\alpha<R
. The property
(b)
follows immediately from the expression themselves.

One can see that for the adjoint of
E_{s}
we have
E_{s}^{*}:L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{q})\to L^{1}(V)
with
\|E_{s}^{*}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{q})\to L^{1}(V)}=\|E_{s}\|_{L^{\infty}(V)\to L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q})}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{M}|a_{j}|
where
(a_{j})
are in (
2.1.11
). So for
U^{\prime}\in\{U,\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
,

\|x\mapsto\langle E_{s}f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{L^{\infty},L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U^{\prime})}=\|x\mapsto\langle f(x,\cdot),E_{s}^{*}u\rangle_{L^{\infty},L^{1}(V)}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U^{\prime})}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{M}|a_{j}|\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U^{\prime},V)}\|u\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{q})}.

Therefore we have the boundedness
E_{s}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U^{\prime},V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U^{\prime},\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

For
V^{\prime}\in\{V,\mathbb{R}^{q}\}
, by Lemma
2.1.3

E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
descends to
E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V^{\prime};\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V^{\prime};\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}))
, i.e.
E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V^{\prime})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V^{\prime})
. By Definition
2.2.1

(i)
we have
E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V^{\prime})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V^{\prime})
immediately. Therefore (
2.2.5
) is done by the following:

E_{s}E_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(U,V)\xrightarrow{E_{x}}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V)\xrightarrow{E_{s}}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}).\qed

Proof of Lemma
2.2.6
.

By the Schwartz Kernel Theorem (see
[
Trè06
, Theorem 51.7]
)
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V)
can be identified as a subspace of
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V)
. Thus we can view the element
f:L^{1}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
as a distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V
.

(i)
: By Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
(if
\alpha>0
) and Definition
2.1.6
(if
\alpha\leq 0
) we have
\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\approx 2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f(\cdot,s)\|_{L^{\infty}}
for every
s\in V
. Since
(L^{1})^{*}=L^{\infty}
, for a
g\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V)
we have
\|g(x,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(V)}=\sup_{\|u\|_{L^{1}}=1}\langle g(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{L^{\infty},L^{1}}
. Therefore

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V)}\approx\sup_{\|u\|_{L^{1}}=1}\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\langle\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f(x,\cdot),u\rangle_{L^{\infty},L^{1}(V)}=\sup_{j\geq 0}2^{j\alpha}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V)}.

Here we use that fact that
\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f
is continuous along
x
-variable, which means the sup and the essential sup of
x
are the same.

(ii)
: Since
\beta>0
, by Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
again we have

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\approx\sup_{k\geq 0}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}2^{k\beta}\|\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\approx\sup_{j,k\geq 0}2^{j\alpha+k\beta}\|\psi_{k}\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}.

This completes the proof since

\psi_{k}\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f)=\phi_{j}\ast_{x}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f)=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast f.

(2.2.6)

The proof of
(iii)
follows immediately from Lemma
2.2.8
.

Finally to prove
(iv)
, clearly by definition, the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;L^{\infty}(V))
and (
2.2.4
) are equal.
By
(i)
and Lemma
2.1.4

2.1.4
we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n};L^{\infty}(V))=\{f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V):\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|<\infty\}
with equivalent norms. Using
(iii)
we can replace
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by bounded smooth domain
U
.
∎

Remark 2.2.9
.

Now
f\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,V)
is saying that
f(\cdot,s)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
almost every
s\in V
and is essentially bounded. However
L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,V)
does not coincide with the vector-valued space
L^{\infty}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))
. In classical definitions
L^{\infty}(U;\mathscr{X})
is the set of all
\mathscr{X}
-valued strong measurable function (quotiented by almost everywhere) which is essentially bounded. Such functions in
L^{\infty}(U;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V))
must be
almost everywhere separable valued
. But
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
is
not
a separable space. So
L^{\infty}(U;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V))\subsetneq L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,V)
and the inclusion is strict, and moreover
L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,V)\not\subset L^{1}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))
. See for example
[
HvNVW16
, Remark 1.2.16]
.

Remark 2.2.10
.

Let
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\beta_{0},\beta_{1}>0
.
Clearly
\min(2^{j\alpha_{0}+k\beta_{0}},2^{j\alpha_{1}+k\beta_{1}})\leq 2^{j((1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1})+k((1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1})}
for
\theta\in[0,1]
. Thus we have inclusion: for
U,V
either the total space or bounded smooth domains,

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{0}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{0}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{1}}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{\theta}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta_{\theta}}(U,V),\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1},\ \beta_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1}.

(2.2.7)

In particular
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\theta\beta}(U,V)
for all
\alpha,\beta>0
.

As a corollary to Lemma
2.2.6
, the paraproduct operators can be adapted to bi-parameter Hölder spaces.

Lemma 2.2.11
.

Let
(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
be the standard coordinate system for
\mathbb{R}^{q}
and let
\phi=(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

U,V,W
be three open sets and let
\Pi:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}(U)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}(V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{3}}(W)
be a bounded bilinear operator where
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the
s
-variable paraproduct operators

\mathfrak{P}^{\Pi}(f,g):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}\Pi(\phi_{k}\ast_{s}f,\phi_{k^{\prime}}\ast_{s}g),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathfrak{R}^{\Pi}(f,g):=\sum_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}\Pi(\phi_{k}\ast_{s}f,\phi_{k^{\prime}}\ast_{s}g),

define bilinearly bounded maps
\mathfrak{P}^{\Pi},\mathfrak{R}^{\Pi}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{2}}L^{\infty}(V,\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{3}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(W,\mathbb{R}^{q})
, for every
\beta>0
.

Proof.

Direct computation using Lemmas
2.1.13
and
2.1.16
.
∎

Next we define the mixed Hölder-Zygmund space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
as the following

Definition 2.2.12
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
be two generalized indices. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two open sets, and let
\mathscr{X}
be a Banach space. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}(U,V;\mathscr{X})
to be the set of all continuous functions
f:U\times V\to\mathscr{X}
such that
\{f(x,\cdot):x\in U\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{X})
and
\{f(\cdot,s):s\in V\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathscr{X})
are two bounded sets with respect to the topologies of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{X})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathscr{X})
respectively.

When
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we use the norm,

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}(U,V;\mathscr{X})}:=\sup_{x\in U}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{X})}+\sup_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathscr{X})}.

We use abbreviation
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
when the domain is clear. We use
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
when the variables are also clear.

Remark 2.2.13
.

(i)

The norm definition is symmetric and we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\alpha}_{s,x}
. But in applications we mostly refer the left variable as space variable, and the right variable as parameter. For the notation
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
, we usually use
\alpha\geq\beta
.

(ii)

When
\alpha=\beta
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. See Lemma
2.2.15

(ii)
below. So our
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces is a generalization to the single variable cases.

(iii)

We can define three variables mixed Hölder-Zygmund spaces and the version on manifold analogously. See Definition
2.2.18

(ii)
and Definition
2.3.2
.

For
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, the spaces
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-,\beta}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+,\beta}
are just for convenient purpose:

Lemma 2.2.14
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two open subsets. Then
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-,\beta}(U,V)
if and only if
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon,\beta}(U,V)
for all
0<\varepsilon<\gamma
.

Thus the discussion of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-regularities for positive
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
can be reduced to the case where
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
.

Proof.

f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-,\beta}(U,V)
if and only if
\{f(\cdot,s):s\in V\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-}(U)
is bounded, if and only if
\{f(\cdot,s):s\in V\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}(U)
is bounded for all
\varepsilon>0
, if and only if
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon,\beta}(U,V)
for all
\varepsilon>0
.
∎

Lemma 2.2.15
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two bounded smooth domains. Then

(i)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}(V,U)
with equivalent norms.

(ii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)
with equivalent norms.

Proof.

(i)
: By Lemma
2.2.8
we have an extension operator
\tilde{E}:=E_{x}E_{s}=E_{s}E_{x}:L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
, thus by Lemma
2.2.6

(iii)
,

\textstyle\sup_{x\in U}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}\geq\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{x\in U}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}=\|E_{s}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{q},U)}\approx\|\tilde{E}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\approx\|f\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)}.

(2.2.8)

Similarly
\sup_{s\in V}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\gtrsim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)}
. Therefore
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)}\gtrsim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)}
.

For the converse direction, it suffices to show the first inequality in (
2.2.8
) is indeed an equality.
By Remark
2.2.10
we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(U,V)\subset C^{0}(U,V)
. Thus
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
can be pointwise defined, and the essential supremum is indeed the supremum. This completes the proof.

(ii)
: Clearly
\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)}
and
\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)}
for each
x\in U
and
s\in V
. Therefore
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}(U,V)}\leq 2\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)}
.

For the converse, we can choose
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
as dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
respectively, such that
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{0}\subset B^{n}(0,\sqrt{2})
and
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{0}\subset B^{q}(0,\sqrt{2})
additionally. Define
(\pi_{l})_{l=0}^{\infty}
as
\pi_{0}=\phi_{0}\otimes\psi_{0}
and
\pi_{l}=2^{l(n+q)}\pi_{0}(2^{l}\cdot)-2^{(l-1)(n+q)}\pi_{0}(2^{l-1}\cdot)
for
l\geq 1
. We see that
(\pi_{l})_{l}
is a dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{R}^{n+q}
and

\textstyle\pi_{l}=\sum_{\max(j,k)=l}\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k}=\phi_{l}\otimes\delta_{0\in\mathbb{R}^{q}}+\delta_{0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\otimes\psi_{l}-\sum_{\min(j,k)=l}\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ l\geq 0.

Therefore for
\tilde{f}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n+q})
, by Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
and (
2.2.6
),

\displaystyle\textstyle\|\pi_{l}\ast\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|\phi_{l}\ast_{x}\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|\psi_{l}\ast_{s}\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{j=l}^{\infty}\|\psi_{l}\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}\tilde{f})\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{k=l+1}^{\infty}\|\phi_{l}\ast_{x}(\psi_{k}\ast_{s}\tilde{f})\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{\alpha}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}}2^{-l\alpha}+\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}2^{-l\alpha}+\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}}\sum_{j=l}^{\infty}2^{-j\alpha}+\|\phi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\|\tilde{f}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}\sum_{k=l+1}^{\infty}2^{-k\alpha}\lesssim\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}2^{-l\alpha}.

Thus
\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|\tilde{f}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}
. Restricting to the domain
U\times V
we finish the proof.
∎

Remark 2.2.16
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
and let
U,V
be two bounded smooth domains. Since
\mathscr{C}^{0}\subset L^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{C}^{1}
, using Lemma
2.2.6
we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
.

Remark 2.2.17

(Comparison of different working spaces)
.

The bi-parameter structures used in
[
Gon20
, Section 2]
and
[
NW63
, Section 4.4]
are the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces, which are similar to our
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces. We recall them here:

Let
k\geq l\geq 0
be integers, let
r,s\in[0,1)
and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two open (bounded smooth) subsets. The space
\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)
is the set of all continuous functions
f:U\times V\to\mathbb{R}
whose norm below is finite:

\textstyle\|f\|_{\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)}:=\sum_{j=0}^{l}\sum_{i=0}^{k-j}\big(\sup_{s\in V}\|\nabla_{x}^{i}\nabla_{s}^{j}f(\cdot,s)\|_{C^{0,r}(U)}+\sup_{x\in U}\|\nabla_{x}^{i}\nabla_{s}^{j}f(x,\cdot)\|_{C^{0,s}(V)}\big).

Here
C^{0,\gamma}
is the standard Hölder space
(0\leq\gamma<1)
.

One can see that
\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)
when
r,s\neq 0
and
\mathcal{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{k+r,l+s}(U,V)
when
r
or
s=0
. We leave the proof to readers.

The
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces is better than the
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces in some sense. On one hand
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
are only defined for
\lfloor\alpha\rfloor\geq\lfloor\beta\rfloor
, while
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
are defined for all positive
\alpha,\beta
. On the other hand, the discussions of
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
are always separated between the integer cases and non-integer cases, while for
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces the discussion can be unified as we have complex interpolations,

[\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(U,V),\mathscr{C}^{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1}}(U,V)]_{\theta}=\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha_{0}+\theta\alpha_{1},(1-\theta)\beta_{0}+\theta\beta_{1}}(U,V),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all }\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\beta_{0},\beta_{1}>0,\hskip 10.00002pt\theta\in[0,1].

(2.2.9)

We also leave the proof of (
2.2.9
) to readers.

Here are more definitions.

Definition 2.2.18
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be two open sets. We define the following spaces:

(i)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V):=\{f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times V):f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U^{\prime},V^{\prime}),\ \forall U^{\prime}\Subset U,\ V^{\prime}\Subset V\}
.

(ii)

Let
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
. Let
W\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
be an open convex set. We define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}(U,V,W)
to be the set of all continuous functions
f:U\times V\times W\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\{f(\cdot,s,t):s\in V,t\in W\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)
,
\{f(x,\cdot,t):x\in U,t\in W\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)
and
\{f(x,s,\cdot):x\in U,s\in V\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(W)
are all bounded subsets with respect to their topologies.

When
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in(0,\infty)
are numbers, we use the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}_{x,s,t}(U,V,W)}:=

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in U,s\in V}\|f(x,s,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(W)}+\sup_{x\in U,t\in W}\|f(x,\cdot,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V)}+\sup\limits_{s\in V,t\in W}\|f(\cdot,s,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U)}.

Remark 2.2.19
.

By Lemma
2.2.15

(i)
we can say that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}_{x,s,t}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}L^{\infty}_{x,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}_{s,t}
, where
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}L^{\infty}_{x,t}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{t}L^{\infty}_{x,s}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s,t}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}_{s,t}
are defined analogously by Definitions
2.2.1
and
2.2.12
.

For
0<\alpha<\infty
,
0<\beta<\infty
, that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
does not imply
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
.

Example 2.2.20
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. We consider the following
f:\mathbb{R}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}
:

f(x,s):=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}2^{-l(\alpha+1)}\exp(i2^{l}x+i2^{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l}s).\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ We have }\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}(x,s)=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}i2^{-l\alpha}\exp(i2^{l}x+i2^{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l}s).

One can check that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}
and
f\in L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
. So
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
.

Let
\psi=(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution. By (
2.1.2
)
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{k}=\operatorname{supp}2^{k-1}\hat{\psi}_{1}\subsetneq(-2^{k+1},-2^{k-1})\cup(2^{k-1},2^{k+1})
for
k\geq 1
, so there is an
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{k}\subseteq 2^{k}\cdot\big([-2+\varepsilon_{0},-\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}]\cup[\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0},2-\varepsilon_{0}]\big)
. Note that there are infinitely many integer pairs
(k,l)
such that
|k-\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l|<\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}
, and for such a pair
(\phi_{k}\ast_{s}f)(x,s)=i2^{-l\alpha}\exp(i2^{l}x+i2^{\frac{\alpha+1}{\beta}l}s)
.

So
\|\psi_{k}\ast_{s}f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\approx 2^{-k\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}}
holds for infinite many
k\geq 0
, which means
f\notin L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}+\varepsilon}_{s}
for any
\varepsilon>0
.

In general, for
0<\alpha,\beta<\infty
and a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
, the best we can guarantee is
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}}_{x,s}
, where the index
\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}
is optimal.
For applications we need the following endpoint optimal result.

Lemma 2.2.21
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)
and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two bounded convex smooth domains. Then
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}-}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded.
In particular, if
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}(U,V)
, then
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta-}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

Clearly
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
is bounded. By Remark
2.2.10
we have embedding
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha-\theta}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\theta\beta}_{s}
for all
\theta\in(0,1)
. Thus
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\subset\bigcap_{0<\theta<\frac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}}\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha-\theta}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\theta\beta}_{s}\subset L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}-}_{s}
. This proves the boundedness
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1}-}_{x,s}
.

Let
\alpha\to+\infty
we get
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{x,s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta-}_{x,s}
.
∎

On the other hand, if
f(x,s)
is, say a harmonic function in
x
, then the assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}
can imply
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{x,s}
. In fact we have
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
by the following.

Lemma 2.2.22
.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
, and let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two bounded convex open sets with smooth boundaries. Suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
satisfies
\Delta_{x}f=0
. Then
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
. In particular
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V)
and
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\times V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

As a corollary, let
\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}
be an open set. If
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U}\times V;\mathbb{C})
is holomorphic in
z
, then
\partial_{z}g\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U}\times V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
holds.

Here for
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}\backslash\mathbb{R}_{+}
, we use the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V):=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U))
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V):=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V;\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U))
adapted from Definition
2.2.1

(ii)
.

Proof.

Since the result is local in
x\in U
, it is enough to consider the case where
U=\mathbb{B}^{n}
is the unit ball. By Definition
1.2.1

(iv)
it suffices to consider the case
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
.

Applying the spherical average formula, (or by Lemma
2.4.4

(iv)
) we have

|\nabla^{k}h(x)|\lesssim_{n,k}(1-|x|)^{-k-1}\|\nabla^{k}h\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-k-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{k}(1-|x|)^{-k-1}\|h\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall h\in\mathscr{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),\ \Delta h=0.

When
\beta\in(0,1)
, for
s_{1},s_{2}\in V
, by taking
h_{s_{1},s_{2}}(x)=\frac{f(x,s_{1})-f(x,s_{2})}{|s_{1}-s_{2}|^{\beta}}
we see that for every
\Delta_{x}f=0
,

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(r\mathbb{B}^{n},V)}=\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in V}\|h_{s_{1},s_{2}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}(r\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{k}(1-r)^{-k-1}\sup_{s_{1},s_{2}\in V}\|h_{s_{1},s_{2}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}=(1-r)^{-k-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)}.

Similarly arguments show that
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{k}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(r\mathbb{B}^{n},V)}\lesssim_{k}(1-r)^{-k-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)}
for
\beta\in[1,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
, since all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-norms are defined by a supremum over some linear functionals.

Therefore
f\in\mathscr{C}^{k}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(r\mathbb{B}^{n},V)
for all
r<1
and all
k
, which means
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},V)
. The results
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
follow immediately.

When
g(z,s)
is holomorphic in
z
, we know
g
is complex harmonic in
z
-variable. So we reduce it to the real case by considering
\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2m}
.
∎

2.2.2
Propositions on bi-parameter products

In this subsection we compute the mixed regularity of products using bi-parameter paraproducts.

As a motivation, in Section
4.1.2
we need to solve a non-linear elliptic pde system to find a suitable coordinate change that has best possible component-wise regularity. We start with a coefficient function
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)
and we want to find a solution
H\in\mathscr{X}^{1}(U,V)
in a suitable bi-parameter Hölder space
\mathscr{X}^{1}
. To show the existence of
H
we need spaces
\mathscr{X}^{1}
,
\mathscr{X}^{0}
and
\mathscr{X}^{-1}
that are as small as possible, and satisfy the following (see Proposition
4.1.6
):

•

\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{X}^{1}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\nabla_{x}:\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
are bounded; and
\Delta_{x}:\mathscr{X}^{1}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(U,V)
is right invertible.

•

We have product maps
\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V)\times\mathscr{X}^{-1}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(U,V)
and
\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V)\times\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V)
.

•

\mathscr{X}^{0}(U,V)\supseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V)
.

In application
\mathscr{X}^{1}
labels the mixed regularity for the coordinate change, and
\mathscr{X}^{0}
labels the mixed regularity for the pushforward vector fields which are the generators for a given subbundle.

We shall see that for
k=1,0,-1
, we can pick
\mathscr{X}^{k}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+k}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{k}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
when
\alpha>1
, and
\mathscr{X}^{k}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+k}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{k}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+k}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{(2-1/\alpha)\beta}_{s}
when
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
. Note that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+k}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{(2-1/\alpha)\beta}_{s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{k}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
when
\alpha>1
.

Firstly we extend Lemma
2.1.17

(i)
to the bi-parameter case.

Lemma 2.2.23
.

Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be either the total spaces or bounded smooth domains. The product map
[(f,g)\mapsto fg]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}L^{\infty}(U,V)
is bounded for all
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha+\beta>0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.2.6

(iii)
it suffices to prove
U=\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V=\mathbb{R}^{q}
. By symmetry we can assume
\beta\leq\alpha
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By Lemma
2.2.6

(i)
it suffices to prove
\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}(fg)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim 2^{-j\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}}
. Indeed by Lemma
2.1.13
,

\displaystyle\|\phi_{l}\ast_{x}(fg)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\phi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\bigg(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\bigg)\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\phi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{x}g\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}}\bigg(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\bigg)2^{-j\alpha-j\beta}\approx 2^{-j\beta}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}L^{\infty}}.

Here we use the assumption
\alpha+\beta>0
and
\beta\leq\alpha
.
∎

In this subsection, we fix
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
as two dyadic resolutions for their spaces. For bounded functions
f
and
g
defined on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
, we denote

f_{jk}:=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast f,\hskip 10.00002ptg_{jk}:=(\phi_{j}\otimes\psi_{k})\ast g,\hskip 10.00002ptj,k\geq 0.

We write
\displaystyle fg=\bigg(\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}+3\leq j}+\sum\limits_{j+3\leq j^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\bigg)\bigg(\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}+3\leq k}+\sum\limits_{k+3\leq k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}\bigg)(f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}})
as
3\times 3
sums:

\displaystyle fg=\sum_{\mu,\nu=1}^{3}S^{\mu\nu}=

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}

(2.2.10)

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\sum\limits_{k=3}^{\infty}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}+\sum\limits_{|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2}\sum\limits_{|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2}f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}.

Here
S^{\mu\nu}(f,g)
,
1\leq\mu,\nu\leq 3
, is defined to be the
\mu
-th row
\nu
-th column term in (
2.2.10
).

The discussion on
\alpha>1
follows from the following

Proposition 2.2.24
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\infty)
,
\eta\in(-\alpha,\alpha]
and
\theta\in[0,\alpha-\eta)
. There is
C=C_{\alpha,\beta,\eta,\theta}>0
such that

\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\leq C\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})},

for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

For applications, we use the cases
(\eta,\theta)=(1,0)
and
(\eta,\theta)=(0,0)
to prove Corollary
4.1.8
that the left hand side of (
4.1.6
) behave well. And we use
(\eta,\theta)=(0,1)
to prove Proposition
2.2.36
which is required in Corollary
4.1.8

(iii)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.2.23
, since
\alpha-\theta>\eta\geq-\alpha
, we have boundedness
[(f,g)\mapsto fg]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
.
Our goal is to show the bilinear control
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
.

By assumption
\alpha>\max(0,\eta+\theta)
and
\eta>-\alpha
. Using Lemma
2.1.16
we have the boundedness:

\mathfrak{P}_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to\begin{cases}\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})&0\leq\theta<\alpha\\
\mathscr{C}^{(-\eta)-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{n})(\forall\varepsilon>0)&\alpha=\theta\\
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\eta-\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})&\alpha<\theta\leq\alpha-\eta\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

Similarly argument shows that for all
\alpha,\beta,\eta,\theta
that satisfy the assumption:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}_{x},\mathfrak{R}_{x}\text{ are bounded }\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}\times\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\to\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta},\ \mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\times\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\to\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta},\ \mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\to\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}.

Thus applying Lemma
2.2.11
with
\Pi(u,v)\in\{\mathfrak{P}_{x}(u,v),\mathfrak{P}_{x}(v,u),\mathfrak{R}_{x}(u,v)\}
in the lemma, we get:

\displaystyle S^{11},S^{13},S^{21},S^{23},S^{31},S^{33}

\displaystyle:\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q});

\displaystyle S^{12},S^{13},S^{22},S^{23},S^{32},S^{33}

\displaystyle:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}).

Therefore
\|S^{\mu\nu}(f,g)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
for all
\mu,\nu\in\{1,2,3\}
, finishing the proof.
∎

Remark 2.2.25
.

The proof above is shorter than the proof in
[
Yao22
, Proposition 2.30]
.

Corollary 2.2.26
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\eta,\theta
satisfy the assumption of Proposition
2.2.24
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be two bounded open convex subsets with smooth boundaries. Then the following product is bilinearly bounded:

[(f,g)\to fg]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V).

(2.2.11)

In particular it is bilinearly bounded between the following spaces:

(i)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
for
\alpha>1
.

(ii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
for
\alpha>0
.

(iii)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V)
for
\alpha>1
.

Proof.

Indeed
(i)
,
(ii)
and
(iii)
are the cases where
(\eta,\theta)=(0,1)
,
(\eta,\theta)=(0,0)
and
(\eta,\theta)=(1,0)
in (
2.2.11
) respectively.

By Lemma
2.2.8
there is an extension operator such that
\tilde{E}:\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
and
\tilde{E}:\mathscr{Y}(U,V)\to\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
. Here we use
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
\mathscr{Y}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.
Applying Proposition
2.2.24
,

\|fg\|_{\mathscr{Y}(U,V)}\leq\|\tilde{E}f\tilde{E}g\|_{\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim\|\tilde{E}f\|_{\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\|\tilde{E}g\|_{\mathscr{Y}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{X}(U,V)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{Y}(U,V)}.

So we obtain (
2.2.11
).
∎

For
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, the bi-parameter spaces are unfortunately a bit convoluted.

Proposition 2.2.27
.

Let
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
and
\beta>0
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be either the total spaces or bounded smooth domains. Then the product map

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(U,V)\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(U,V)

is bounded bilinear.

Remark 2.2.28
.

(i)

There is no need to put parentheses on the spaces: we have
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta})=(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta})+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}
because
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}
. Later it more convenient to consider
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap(\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta})
.

(ii)

For
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, by Remarks
2.2.10
and
2.2.16

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon+1-\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\alpha})\beta}
holds for all
0<\varepsilon<2\alpha-1
. Applying Proposition
2.2.24
with
\eta=\alpha-1-\varepsilon
and
\theta=1
, we have the product map

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon+1-\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\alpha})\beta}\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\alpha})\beta}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1+\varepsilon}{\alpha})\beta},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0<\varepsilon<2\alpha-1.

Thus for
f,g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})
we get
(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(f\cdot\nabla g)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta-}
which is endpoint optimal. By introducing the term
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}
in Proposition
2.2.27
, we get
(-\Delta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(f\cdot\nabla g)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}
, which is sharp. See Example
2.2.29
.

Proof of Proposition
2.2.27
.

Firstly by Lemma
2.2.11
, we see that
\|fg\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}
.

It is more convenient to introduce
\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(u,v):=\mathfrak{P}(v,u)
, thus we have
uv=\mathfrak{P}(u,v)+\mathfrak{P}^{\prime}(u,v)+\mathfrak{R}(u,v)
.

By Lemma
2.1.16

\mathfrak{P}_{x},\mathfrak{P}_{x}^{\prime}
are both bounded as
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\times\mathscr{C}^{-1}\to\mathscr{C}^{-1}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\times\mathscr{C}^{0}\to\mathscr{C}^{-1}
. Therefore by Lemma
2.2.11
, again with
\Pi(u,v)\in\{\mathfrak{P}_{x}(u,v),\mathfrak{P}_{x}(v,u),\mathfrak{R}_{x}(u,v)\}
in the lemma,

\displaystyle S^{11},S^{13},S^{21},S^{23}

\displaystyle:\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q});

\displaystyle S^{12},S^{13},S^{22},S^{23}

\displaystyle:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}).

Similarly, the boundedness
\mathfrak{P}_{x},\mathfrak{P}_{x}^{\prime},\mathfrak{R}_{x}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
gives

\displaystyle S^{11},S^{13},S^{21},S^{23},S^{31},S^{33}

\displaystyle:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q});

\displaystyle S^{12},S^{13},S^{22},S^{23},S^{32},S^{33}

\displaystyle:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{q}).

It remains prove the following:

S^{31},S^{32},S^{33}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\times\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\to\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{(2-1/\alpha)\beta}(\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-1/\alpha)\beta}).

(2.2.12)

The assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}
implies that
\|f_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\phi,\psi}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}2^{-j\alpha}
, and the assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
implies that
\|f_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\phi,\psi}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}2^{j-k\beta}
. Similar control works for
g
. To conclude this, we have

\displaystyle\|f_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\min(2^{-j\alpha},2^{-k\beta})\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|g_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\min(2^{j(1-\alpha)},2^{j-k\beta})\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}.

(2.2.13)

For convenience we normalize
f
and
g
so that
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}=\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}=1
. Therefore what we need is to show for
\nu=1,2,3
,

\textstyle S^{3\nu}_{lm}:=\|(\phi_{l}\otimes\psi_{m})\ast S^{3\nu}(f,g)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\phi,\psi,\alpha,\beta}2^{-m(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta},\text{ for }l,m\geq 0.

(2.2.14)

Applying Lemma
2.1.13
on
x
-variable and
s
-variable separately we get

(\phi_{l}\otimes\psi_{m})\ast(S^{31}+S^{32}+S^{33})(f,g)=\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\\
j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\end{subarray}}\Bigg(\sum\limits_{k=m-2}^{m+2}\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}+\sum\limits_{k^{\prime}=m-2}^{m+2}\sum\limits_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}+\sum\limits_{\begin{subarray}{c}|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2\\
k,k^{\prime}\geq m-3\end{subarray}}\Bigg)(f_{jk}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}).

(2.2.15)

Notice that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}
give the following improvement of (
2.2.13
):

\displaystyle\textstyle\big\|\sum_{k=1}^{m}f_{jk}\big\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\|2^{mq}\psi_{0}(2^{m}\cdot)\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}f\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{-j\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}};

\displaystyle\textstyle\big\|\sum_{k=1}^{m}g_{jk}\big\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\|2^{mq}\psi_{0}(2^{m}\cdot)\ast_{s}(\phi_{j}\ast_{x}g)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|\phi_{j}\ast_{x}g\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{j(1-\alpha)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}.

Therefore for
l,m\geq 0
,

\displaystyle S^{31}_{lm}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq l-3\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=m-2}^{m+2}\|f_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\bigg\|\sum_{k^{\prime}=0}^{k-3}g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq l-3\end{subarray}}\sum_{k=m-2}^{m+2}\min(2^{-j\alpha},2^{-k\beta})2^{j^{\prime}(1-\alpha)}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq l}\min(2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha},2^{-m\beta})2^{l^{\prime}(1-\alpha)}=\sum_{l\leq l^{\prime}\leq m\beta/\alpha}2^{l^{\prime}(1-\alpha)-m\beta}+\sum_{l^{\prime}>m\beta/\alpha}2^{-l^{\prime}(2\alpha-1)}\lesssim 2^{-m(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta};

\displaystyle S^{32}_{lm}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq l-3\end{subarray}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=m-2}^{m+2}\|g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\bigg\|\sum_{k=0}^{k^{\prime}-3}f_{jk}\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq l-3\end{subarray}}\sum_{k^{\prime}=m-2}^{m+2}\min(2^{j^{\prime}(1-\alpha)},2^{j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\beta})2^{-j\alpha}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq l}\min(2^{l^{\prime}(1-\alpha)},2^{l^{\prime}-m\beta})2^{-l^{\prime}\alpha}\lesssim 2^{-m(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta};

\displaystyle S^{33}_{lm}\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq l-3\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,k^{\prime}\geq m-3\\
|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\|f_{jk}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|g_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq l-3\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,k^{\prime}\geq m-3\\
|k-k^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\min(2^{-j\alpha},2^{-k\beta})\min(2^{j^{\prime}(1-\alpha)},2^{j^{\prime}-k^{\prime}\beta})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sum_{l^{\prime}\geq l}\sum_{m^{\prime}\geq m}\min(2^{-l^{\prime}(2\alpha-1)},2^{l^{\prime}-2m^{\prime}\beta})=\sum_{m^{\prime}\geq m}\big(\sum_{l\leq l^{\prime}\leq m^{\prime}\beta/\alpha}2^{l^{\prime}-2m^{\prime}\beta}+\sum_{l^{\prime}>m^{\prime}\beta/\alpha}2^{-l^{\prime}(2\alpha-1)}\bigg)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{m^{\prime}\geq m}2^{-m^{\prime}(2-1/\alpha)\beta}\lesssim 2^{-m(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}.

We get (
2.2.14
) hence (
2.2.12
), finishing the proof.
∎

We should point out that when
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, the regularity loss in parameter is inevitable.

Example 2.2.29
.

For
\alpha,\beta>0
,
\eta>-\alpha
and
0<\theta<2\alpha
. Let

f(x,s):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-\frac{j\alpha\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}e^{2\pi i(2^{\frac{j\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}x+2^{j}s)}\sum_{k=1}^{j-1}2^{\frac{\theta\beta}{\alpha-\eta}(k-j)}e^{2\pi i2^{k}s},\hskip 10.00002ptg(x,s):=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}2^{-\frac{j(\alpha-\theta)\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}e^{2\pi i(2^{\frac{j\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}x+2^{j}s)}.

One can see that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\bigcap_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{C}^{(1-\lambda)\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\lambda\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\bigcap_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}}\mathscr{C}^{(1-\lambda)(\alpha-\theta)}\mathscr{C}^{\lambda\frac{(\alpha-\theta)\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
. Taking
\lambda=\frac{\alpha-\eta}{\alpha}
and
\lambda=\frac{\alpha-\eta}{\alpha-\theta}
respectively we get
