f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-\theta}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta-\theta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}\subset\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{R})
be a dyadic resolution, we have

(\phi_{0}\ast_{s}(fg))(x,s)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}2^{-j(2\alpha-\theta)\frac{\beta}{\alpha-\eta}+\frac{\theta\beta(k-j)}{\alpha-\eta}}e^{2\pi i2^{k}s}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-k(2\alpha-\theta)\frac{\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}e^{2\pi i2^{k}s}\frac{1}{2^{\frac{2\alpha\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}-1}.

Note that the assumption
\theta\leq 2\alpha
is necessary otherwise the sum diverges.

Thus
\phi_{0}\ast_{s}(fg)\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-\theta)\frac{\beta}{\alpha-\eta}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
but
fg\notin\mathscr{C}^{-M}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
for all
M>0
and
\gamma>(2\alpha-\theta)\frac{\beta}{\alpha-\eta}
.

Therefore when
\eta+\theta>\alpha
we see that
\frac{2\alpha-\theta}{\alpha-\eta}<1
, thus
fg\notin\mathscr{C}^{-M}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
for all
M>0
. This example shows that in order for Proposition
2.2.24
to be true we must assume
\eta+\theta\leq\alpha
.

One can find a more delicate example to show that the case
\eta+\theta=\alpha
also fails. We leave the detail to readers.

As a special case, when
\eta=0
and
\theta=1
, we see that
fg\notin\mathscr{C}^{-M}\mathscr{C}^{(2-1/\alpha)\beta+}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
for all
M>0
. Thus the index
(2-1/\alpha)\beta
in Proposition
2.2.27
cannot be improved.

In application we need Corollary
2.2.26
and Proposition
2.2.27
on matrix-valued functions,

Lemma 2.2.30
.

For any
\alpha,\beta>0
,
0\leq\eta<\alpha
and any bounded smooth domains
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}
, there exists a
\tilde{c}=\tilde{c}(U,V,\alpha,\beta,\eta,m)>0
such that

(i)

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}
, for all
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\leq\tilde{c}
.

(ii)

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
, for all
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\tilde{c}
.

(iii)

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}}
, for all
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}}\leq\tilde{c}
.

(iv)

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
, for all
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-\eta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\tilde{c}
.

(v)

\|A^{-1}-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq 2\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
, for all
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
with
\|A-I_{m}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\tilde{c}
. Here
\tilde{c}=\tilde{c}(U,\alpha,m)>0
.

Proof.

These are immediate from Lemmas
2.1.28
and
2.1.17
, where
(iv)
comes from Corollary
2.2.26
.
∎

2.2.3
Compositions and inverse mapping with parameters

In this subsection we work on the concept of compositions between parameter dependent maps. We are going to prove an auxiliary result, Proposition
2.2.36
, that is used in Corollary
4.1.8

(iii)
.

We recall that for a bijection
f:U_{1}\to U_{2}
, we denote its inverse map by
f^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U_{2}\to U_{1}
.

In this subsection, for maps
F(x,s):U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
, usually we would denote by
\tilde{F}
the map
\tilde{F}(x,s):=(F(x,s),s)
. Note that if
F(\cdot,s)
has inverse for every
s
, then in this notation we have

\tilde{F}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y,s)=(F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y),s).

Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
and
y=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n})
be two standard coordinate systems of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
be the standard coordinate system of
\mathbb{R}^{q}
. We let
\mathcal{I}(x,s):=x
for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

The quantitative composition estimate Proposition
2.1.30
can be generalized to the case with parameters.

Lemma 2.2.31
.

Let
m,n,q\geq 1
be integers. Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
satisfy
\max(\alpha,\beta)>1
and
\max(\beta,\gamma)>1
. For any
M>0
there is a
K_{4}(m,n,q,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,M)>0
satisfying the following:

Suppose
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{m})
satisfies
g(\mathbb{B}^{n},s)\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{m}
for all
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
and
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\leq M
. Then for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{m},\mathbb{B}^{q})
, the function
h(x,s):=f(g(x,s),s)
satisfies
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta),\min(\beta,\gamma)}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
with
\|h\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta),\min(\beta,\gamma)}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq K_{4}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{m},\mathbb{B}^{q})}
.

Proof.

Applying Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
, when
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\gamma}}=\sup_{s}\|g(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\sup_{x}\|g(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}<M
we have,

\displaystyle\|f(g(\cdot,s),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq

\displaystyle K_{1}(m,n,\alpha,\beta,M)\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}

\displaystyle\forall s\in\mathbb{B}^{q};

\displaystyle\|f(g(x,\cdot),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq

\displaystyle K_{1}(m,q,\gamma,\beta,M)\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q};

\displaystyle\|f(g(x,s),\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq

\displaystyle\textstyle\displaystyle C(q,\beta,\gamma)\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{m}}\|f(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}.

Here
C(q,\beta,\gamma)>0
is the constant such that
\|u\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq C\|u\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}
. Therefore by Lemma
2.2.15

(ii)
,

\displaystyle\|h\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta),\min(\alpha,\gamma)}}\lesssim_{q,\beta}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|h(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}(\|f(g(x,\cdot),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}}+\|f(g(x,s),\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}})

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{m,n,q,\alpha,\beta,\gamma,M}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}+\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{m})}+\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{m}}(\|f(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{m},\mathbb{B}^{q})}.

By keeping track of the constant above we obtain
K_{4}
.
∎

The quantitative inverse function theorem, Proposition
2.1.44

(i)
, can also be generalized to the case with parameters.

Proposition 2.2.32
.

Let
n,q\geq 1
,
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha+1]
. Write
\mathcal{I}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
as
\mathcal{I}(x,s):=x
. There is a
K_{5}=K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>1
that satisfies the following:

Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfies
\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{5}^{-1}
and
(F-\mathcal{I})|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}\equiv 0
. Then

(i)

For each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
,
F(\cdot,s):\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
is bijective.

(ii)

Define
\Phi(y,s):=F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y)
for
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. Then
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and satisfies

\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K_{5}.

(2.2.16)

To prove Proposition
2.2.32
we need a lemma on inverse function theorem with non-
C^{1}
parameters.

Lemma 2.2.33
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
0<\beta<\min(\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1},\frac{3}{2})
. Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y})
be a map such that
F(\cdot,s)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image and
F(\mathbb{B}^{n},s)\supseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
for each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. Then
\Phi(y,s):=F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y)
satisfies
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s};\mathbb{R}^{n}_{y})
.

Moreover, for any
M>1
there is a
K_{5}=K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\beta,M)>0
that does not depend on
F
, such that if
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<M
and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>M^{-1}
then
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{5}
.

Remark 2.2.34
.

The result is indeed true for all
0<\beta\leq\alpha+1
. When
\beta>1
, the proof is simpler by applying the Inverse Function Theorem on the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-map
(x,s)\mapsto(F(x,s),s)
. In our case, we deal with the difference quotiences directly.

Proof of Lemma
2.2.33
.

It suffices to prove the quantitative result: we assume
\|F(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}<M
and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>M^{-1}
for each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
.

Applying
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.9]
we get
\|\Phi(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{n,\alpha,M}1
.
Thus to prove the lemma, it suffices to prove
\|\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}1
uniformly in
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
Without loss of generality, we assume
\alpha<1
from now on, which means
\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1}<\frac{3}{2}
.

Let
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
. For
j=0,1
, applying mean value theorem to
F(\cdot,s_{j})
,

\Phi(y,s_{0})-\Phi(y,s_{1})=\nabla_{x}F\big((1-\theta_{j})\Phi(y,s_{0})+\theta_{j}\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{j}\big)^{-1}\cdot\big(F(\Phi(y,s_{0}),s_{j})-F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{j}))\big),\ \exists\theta_{j}\in(0,1).

Now
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. By Lemma
2.2.21
(since
\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}<(\frac{\alpha\beta}{\alpha+1})-
), we have
\nabla_{x}F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
with
\|\nabla_{x}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}_{x,s}}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}}\leq M
. By
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.7]
(or a modification of Lemma
2.1.28

(ii)
) and the cofactor representation of
(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}
, we can find a
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,q,\alpha,\beta,M)>0
such that

\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\nabla_{x}F(x,\cdot)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<C_{1},

(2.2.17)

when the assumptions
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<M
and
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>M^{-1}
are satisfied.

We separate the discussions between
\beta<1
and
\beta\geq 1
.

Case
\beta\in(0,1)
: It suffices to show
\sup_{y,s_{0},s_{1}}|\Phi(y,s_{0})-\Phi(y,s_{1})|\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}
.

We have
y=F(\Phi(y,s_{0}),s_{0})=F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{1})
since
\Phi(\cdot,s_{j})=F(\cdot,s_{j})^{\mathrm{Inv}}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\textstyle|\Phi(y,s_{0})-\Phi(y,s_{1})|\leq\big(\sup_{\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}|\big)\cdot|F(\Phi(y,s_{0}),s_{0})-F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{0}))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\textstyle C_{1}|F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{1})-F(\Phi(y,s_{1}),s_{0}))|\leq C_{1}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|F(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}\leq C_{1}M|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}.

This complete the proof of
0<\beta<1
.

Case
\beta\in[1,\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1}]
: It suffices to show
\sup_{y,s_{0},s_{1}}|\Phi(y,s_{0})+\Phi(y,s_{1})-2\Phi(y,\frac{s_{0}+s_{1}}{2})|\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}
.

Fix a
\tilde{y}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. For
s_{0},s_{1}\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
we denote
s_{\frac{1}{2}}:=\frac{s_{0}+s_{1}}{2}
and
x_{i}:=\Phi(\tilde{y},s_{i})
for
i=0,\frac{1}{2},1
.

Take
\varepsilon:=1-\frac{\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}\beta
, so
0<\varepsilon<1-\frac{\beta}{\alpha+1}
. Since
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,1-\varepsilon}_{x,s}}\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}}\leq M
we have

\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,1-\varepsilon}_{x,s}}\leq\tilde{M},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for some }\tilde{M}=\tilde{M}(n,q,\alpha,M)>0.

Let
C_{2}:=K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,1-\varepsilon,\tilde{M})
. By the proven case
\beta=1-\varepsilon
above we have
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\Phi(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{2}
. In particular
|x_{i}-x_{j}|=|\Phi(\tilde{y},s_{i})-\Phi(\tilde{y},s_{j})|\leq C_{2}\cdot|s_{i}-s_{j}|^{1-\varepsilon}
.

By mean value theorem we can find
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
contained in the triangle with vertices
x_{0},x_{\frac{1}{2}},x_{1}
such that,

F(x_{0},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0})=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})\cdot(x_{0}-x_{\frac{1}{2}}),\hskip 10.00002ptF(x_{1},s_{1})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})\cdot(x_{1}-x_{\frac{1}{2}}).

Clearly
|\xi_{0}-\xi_{1}|\leq\max_{i,j=0,\frac{1}{2},1}|x_{i}-x_{j}|
. Therefore by (
2.2.17
) and the property
|x_{i}-x_{j}|\leq C_{2}\cdot|s_{i}-s_{j}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}
,

|\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}-\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})^{-1}|\leq C_{1}(|\xi_{0}-\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}+|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}})\leq C_{1}(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}.

(2.2.18)

Here we use the fact that
|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2\alpha+1}}=|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}
and
|\xi_{0}-\xi_{1}|^{\alpha}\leq C_{2}^{\alpha}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\alpha(1-\varepsilon)}\leq C_{2}^{\alpha}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}
.

Therefore using
\tilde{y}=F(x_{j},s_{j})
for
j=0,\frac{1}{2},1
, we have

\displaystyle x_{0}+x_{1}-2x_{\frac{1}{2}}=\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}\cdot(F(x_{0},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0}))+\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})^{-1}\cdot(F(x_{1},s_{1})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}\cdot(2\tilde{y}-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1}))+\big(\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{0},s_{0})^{-1}-\nabla_{x}F(\xi_{1},s_{1})^{-1}\big)\cdot(\tilde{y}-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})).

Applying (
2.2.17
) and (
2.2.18
) to the above equality:

\displaystyle|x_{0}+x_{1}-2x_{\frac{1}{2}}|\leq C_{1}|2F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{\frac{1}{2}})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{0})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})|+C_{1}(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon}|F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{\frac{1}{2}})-F(x_{\frac{1}{2}},s_{1})|

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{q,\beta}C_{1}\|F\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}+C_{1}(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)\|F\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}}|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta-1+\varepsilon+1-\varepsilon}\leq C_{1}(M+(C_{2}^{\alpha}+1)\tilde{M})|s_{0}-s_{1}|^{\beta}.

Thus
\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta,M}1
and we complete the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.2.32
.

We let
K_{5}
be a large constant which may change from line to line.

Let
K_{2}=K_{2}(n,\alpha,\alpha)>0
be the constant in Proposition
2.1.44
. So we can choose
C_{1}>0
such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{1}^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|F(\cdot,s)-\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{2}^{-1},\ \forall s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}.

By assumption
(F(\cdot,s)-\mathcal{I}(\cdot,s))|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=(F(\cdot,s)-\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}})|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 0
, so by Proposition
2.1.44

F(\cdot,s):\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism for each
s\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and we have

\textstyle\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{1}^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<K_{2}.

(2.2.19)

In particular
\Phi(y,s)
is pointwise defined for
(y,s)\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
. This finishes the proof of
(i)
.

Let
c_{0}=c_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n},n,\alpha)\in(0,1)
be the constant in Lemma
2.1.28

(i)
, we have, for every
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
,

\|\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s)-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<c_{0}\ \Rightarrow\ \|(\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s))^{-1}-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<2c_{0}.

(2.2.20)

Thus we can find a
C_{2}\geq\max(2c^{\prime}_{0}{}^{-1},C_{1},K_{2},1)
, such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{2}^{-1}\ \Rightarrow\ \sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}|(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|\leq\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|(\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<C_{2}.

(2.2.21)

In particular
\inf_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n},s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}|\det(\nabla_{x}F(x,s))^{-1}|>C_{2}^{-n}
. Thus when
0<\beta<\min(\frac{2\alpha+1}{\alpha+1},\frac{3}{2})
(in particular when
0<\beta\leq\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}
), we get
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
by applying Lemma
2.2.33
.

It remains to prove the case
\beta\geq\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}
. By chain rule, we have

0_{q\times n}=\nabla_{s}(F(\Phi(y,s),s))=\big((\nabla_{x}F)(\Phi(y,s),s)\big)\cdot\nabla_{s}\Phi(y,s)+(\nabla_{s}F)(\Phi(y,s),s).

Therefore

\nabla_{s}\Phi(y,s)=-((\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F)\circ(\Phi(y,s),s).

(2.2.22)

What we need is to show
\|\nabla_{s}\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{q\times n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
. We will proceed by induction on
k=\lceil\beta-\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}\rceil
. The base case
k=1
has done since we have obtained
K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
for
\beta\leq\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}
from the above discussion.

Suppose the case
k\geq 1
is done. Let
\beta
satisfies
\lceil\beta-\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1}\rceil=k+1
. We denote
\gamma(\beta):=\max(\beta-1,\frac{3\alpha+1}{2\alpha+1})>1
. By induction hypothesis and
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma(\beta)}_{(x,s)}}\lesssim\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\gamma(\beta)}_{x,s}}
there is a
C_{3}=C_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\gamma(\beta)}}\leq K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\gamma(\beta))^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma(\beta)}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{3}.

(2.2.23)

Applying Lemma
2.2.31
to (
2.2.22
), taking
f
to be the components of
(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{q\times n})
, we have, when the assumption of (
2.2.25
) is satisfied,

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{s}\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sup_{x,s}\|((\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F)(\Phi(y,\cdot),s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}+\sup_{x,s}\|((\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{s}F)(\Phi(y,s),\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}

(2.2.24)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle K_{1}(n,q,\gamma(\beta),\beta-1,C_{3})\sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\nabla_{s}F(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle+K_{1}(q,q,\gamma(\beta),\beta-1,\|\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma(\beta)}})\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\nabla_{s}F(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q})}.

Similar to the argument of (
2.2.21
), by considering the constants
c_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n},n,\beta-1)
and
c_{0}(\mathbb{B}^{q},n,\beta-1)
in Lemma
2.1.28

(i)
, we can find a
C_{4}=C_{4}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>\max(C_{2},C_{3},K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\gamma(\beta)))
, such that

\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<C_{4}^{-1}\ \Rightarrow\ \sup_{s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}}\|(\nabla_{x}F(\cdot,s))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|(\nabla_{x}F(x,\cdot))^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<C_{4}.

Therefore
\|(\nabla_{x}F)^{-1}\nabla_{s}F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{q\times n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
. Combining this fact with (
2.2.24
) we see that
\sup_{y\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\|\Phi(y,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{n,q,\alpha,\beta}1
. Thus we get a
K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)
for such
\beta
, finishing the induction and hence the whole proof.
∎

The result for inverse Laplacian can be adapted to parameter cases as well.

Lemma 2.2.35
.

Let
\alpha>-1
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
and
V\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be two bounded convex open sets with smooth boundaries. Then the
x
-variable Laplacian
\Delta_{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\partial_{x^{j}}^{2}
has a bounded linear inverse
\mathcal{P}
as the following map

\Delta_{x}:\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}\mathscr{X}_{s}(U,V):f|_{(\partial U)\times V}=0\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{x}\mathscr{X}_{s}(U,V),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\beta}:\beta>0\}.

In particular
\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{X}(U,V)\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\mathscr{X}(U,V)
is bounded linear.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.1.41
and Lemma
2.1.3
.
∎

The following result will be used in Corollary
4.1.8

(iii)
in Section
4.1.2
.

Proposition 2.2.36
.

Let
\alpha>1
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha+1]
. Then for any
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta=\delta(n,q,\alpha,\beta,\varepsilon)>0
such that the following is true:

Suppose
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
satisfy

(F-\mathcal{I})|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}\equiv 0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}<\delta.

(2.2.25)

Then
\tilde{F}(x,s):=(F(x,s),s)
is bijective on
\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
.
Moreover, by setting
\tilde{\Phi}(y,s):=\tilde{F}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y,s)
for
(y,s)\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
, we have
g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
and

\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}<\varepsilon.

(2.2.26)

Remark 2.2.37
.

In Proposition
2.2.36
we may have
g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
.
But in applications, the Proposition
4.1.12
, our result in Proposition
2.2.36
is enough.

Proof of Proposition
2.2.36
.

We have
\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
by Remark
2.2.16
. Therefore there is a
\delta_{1}>0
such that if (
2.2.25
) is satisfied with
\delta=\delta_{1}
, then
\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq K_{5}^{-1}
, where
K_{5}=K_{5}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
is the constant in Proposition
2.2.32
. Thus by Proposition
2.2.32

(i)

\Phi(y,s)=F(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(y)
is well-defined and
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}}\leq K_{5}
holds.

Applying Lemma
2.2.31
along with its constant
K_{4}
, we have

\|f\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq K_{4}(n,n,q,\alpha+1,\alpha+1,\beta)\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q}).

(2.2.27)

By taking
f
to be the components of
F-\mathcal{I}
, we have
\|\mathcal{I}-\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
. By
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in Remark
2.2.16
and that
\nabla_{y}\mathcal{I}(y,s)=I_{n}
, we know
\|\nabla_{y}\Phi-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{y}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{y}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}}\lesssim\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{x,s}}
. Thus, we can find a
C_{2}=C_{2}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that

\|\nabla_{y}\Phi-I_{n}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq C_{2}\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})},\text{ when }\eqref{Eqn::Hold::CompThm::Assum}\text{ is satisfied with }\delta=\delta_{1}.

By Lemma
2.2.30

(iv)
(with
\eta=1
), we see that if
C_{2}\|F-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
is small enough, then
(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Therefore we can find a
\delta_{2}\in(0,\delta_{1})
such that

\|(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\delta_{2}^{-1},\text{ when }\eqref{Eqn::Hold::CompThm::Assum}\text{ is satisfied with }\delta=\delta_{2}.

(2.2.28)

Now for
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
, we take
G_{j}=\partial_{x^{j}}\mathcal{P}g
where
\mathcal{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{2}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})
is the operator in Lemma
2.2.35
. Thus
G=(G_{1},\dots,G_{n})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and

g\circ\tilde{\Phi}=(\operatorname{div}_{x}G)\circ\tilde{\Phi},\hskip 20.00003pt(\nabla_{x}G)\circ\tilde{\Phi}=\nabla_{y}(G\circ\tilde{\Phi})\cdot(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}.

By Corollary
2.2.26

(iii)
we have

\displaystyle\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\nabla_{y}(G\circ\tilde{\Phi})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\|(\nabla_{y}\Phi)^{-1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}.

Therefore by (
2.2.28
), (
2.2.27
) and Remark
2.2.16
, we have: when (
2.2.25
) is satisfied with
\delta=\delta_{2}
,

\displaystyle\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|G\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|G\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|G\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|G\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}.

In other words, there is a
C_{3}=C_{3}(n,q,\alpha,\beta)>0
, such that

\|g\circ\tilde{\Phi}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})}\leq C_{3}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{B}^{q}).

Now for every
\varepsilon>0
, take
\delta=\min(C_{3}^{-1}\varepsilon,\delta_{2})
, we get (
2.2.26
) and complete the proof.
∎

In applications, we also need a local version of the composition estimate. We recall the notation
\alpha\circ\beta
in Definition
2.1.29
and Corollary
2.1.31
.

Lemma 2.2.38
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
be positive generalized indices. Let
U_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
,
V_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{p}_{t}
,
U_{2}\Subset\tilde{U}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{y}
and
V_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{s}
be five open sets.

(i)

Let
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};U_{2})
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U}_{2},V_{2})
. We define
h:U_{1}\times V_{1}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{R}
as
h(x,t,s):=f(g(x,t),s)
. Then
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma\circ\alpha,\gamma\circ\beta,\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1},V_{2})

In particular
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\gamma),\min(\beta,\gamma),\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1},V_{2})
if
\max(\alpha,\gamma)>1
and
\max(\beta,\gamma)>1
.

(ii)

Let
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
be a matrix function such that
A(x,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is invertible for every
(x,s)
. Then
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
as well.

(iii)

Assume
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
are numbers. Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and
(x_{0},t_{0})\in U_{1}\times V_{1}
. Suppose
\nabla_{x}F(x_{0},t_{0})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is an invertible matrix. Then there are neighborhoods
U_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq U_{1}
of
x_{0}
,
V_{1}^{\prime}\subseteq V_{1}
of
t_{0}
and
\Omega_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
F(x_{0})
such that

•

For each
t\in V^{\prime}_{1}
,
F(\cdot,t):U^{\prime}_{1}\to F(U^{\prime}_{1},t)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is bijective and
F(U^{\prime}_{1},t)\supseteq\Omega_{1}
.

•

Set
\Phi(x,t):=F(\cdot,t)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(x)
, then
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1},V^{\prime}_{1};U^{\prime}_{1})
.

In particular for the map
\tilde{F}(x,t):=(F(x,t),t))
we have that
\tilde{F}:U_{1}^{\prime}\times V_{1}^{\prime}\to\tilde{F}(U^{\prime}_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1})
is homeomorphism.

Proof.

(i)
: Replacing
U_{1},V_{1},\tilde{U}_{2},V_{2}
by their precompact subsets, we can assume that
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};U_{2})
and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\delta}(\tilde{U}_{2},V_{2})
. So
\{f(\cdot,s):s\in V_{2}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\tilde{U}_{2})
,
\{f(y,\cdot):y\in\tilde{U}_{2}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(V_{2})
,
\{g(\cdot,t):t\in V_{1}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r})
and
\{g(x,\cdot):x\in U_{1}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r})
are all bounded subset.

Therefore by Corollary
2.1.31
, the following subsets are all bounded in their topologies:

\displaystyle\{h(x,t,\cdot):x\in U_{1},t\in V_{1}\}\subseteq\{f(y,\cdot):y\in\tilde{U}_{2}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\delta}(V_{2});

\displaystyle\{h(\cdot,t,s):t\in V_{1},s\in V_{2}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma\circ\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1});\hskip 10.00002pt\{h(x,\cdot,s):x\in U_{1},s\in V_{2}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V_{1}).

This completes the proof of
(i)
.

Proof of
(ii)
: Fix a
(x_{0},s_{0})\in U_{1}\times V_{1}
, it suffices to show
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
near
(x_{0},s_{0})
. Thus we can assume that
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
.

By Lemma
2.1.17

(ii)
and Remark
2.1.27
, for every open set
\Omega
and every
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
,
\|B_{1}B_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}}\leq\|B_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}\|B_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}+\|B_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}\|B_{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})}
for all
B_{1},B_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
. Therefore by Lemma
2.1.28
,

(ii)
,

B\in\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}),\ \|B-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\tfrac{1}{2}\Rightarrow B^{-1}\in\|B^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}}\leq 4\|B-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mu}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}\}.

(2.2.29)

By considering
\mu-\varepsilon
for
\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and letting
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
in (
2.2.29
), we see that for
\sigma\in\{\mu-:\mu\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
:

(a)

For every
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that
\|B-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\tfrac{1}{2}
, we have
B^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
.

(b)

Moreover, if
\mathscr{U}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
is a bounded subset (with respect to the
\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}
-topology) such that
\mathscr{U}\subset\{B\in\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}):\|B-I\|_{L^{\infty}}<\tfrac{1}{2}\}
, then the set
\{B^{-1}:B\in\mathscr{U}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\sigma}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
is also bounded.

Now by passing to an invertible linear transform, we can assume that
A(x_{0},s_{0})=I_{m}
, thus
\|A-I_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}}<\frac{1}{2}
in a small neighborhood of
(x_{0},s_{0})
. By shrinking
U_{1}
and
V_{1}
(since we only need to prove the result near
(x_{0},s_{0})
), we assume that
|A(x,s)-I_{m}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}<\frac{1}{2}
holds for all
(x,s)\in U_{1}\times V_{1}
.

By assumption
\{A(x,\cdot):x\in U_{1}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
and
\{A(\cdot,s):s\in V_{1}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
are both bounded. Applying the property
(b)
with
\mu\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
and
\Omega\in\{U_{1},V_{1}\}
, we see that
\{A(x,\cdot)^{-1}:x\in U_{1}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
and
\{A(\cdot,s)^{-1}:s\in V_{1}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
are also bounded. Therefore
A^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
holds, finishing the proof of
(ii)
.

Proof of
(iii)
: Applying the Inverse Function Theorem on
F(\cdot,t_{0})
, we can find a neighborhood
\tilde{U}_{1}\subseteq U_{1}
of
x_{0}
such that
F(\cdot,t_{0}):\tilde{U}_{1}\to F(\tilde{U}_{1},t_{0})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism. Shrinking
\tilde{U}_{1}
if necessary, we can assume
F(\cdot,t_{0}),F(\cdot,t_{0})^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\tilde{U}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
both have bounded
C^{1}
-norm.

By (
2.2.7
) we have
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\mathscr{C}^{0}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha\beta}{2(\alpha+1)}}_{s}
, thus
[t\mapsto F(\cdot,t)]:V_{1}\to C^{1}(\tilde{U}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is a continuous map (in fact Hölder) with respect to the
C^{1}
-norm topology. Since the set of bounded
C^{1}
-embedding map in
C^{1}(\tilde{U}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is open, we can now find a neighborhood
\tilde{V}_{1}\subseteq V_{1}
of
t_{0}
such that
F(\cdot,t)
is
C^{1}
-embedding for all
t\in\tilde{V}_{1}
. By Inverse Function Theorem again, the inverse map of
F(\cdot,t):\tilde{U}_{1}\to F(\tilde{U}_{1},t)
is also
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
.

Take
U^{\prime}_{1}:=\tilde{U}_{1}
and take
\Omega_{1}
such that
F(x_{0},t_{0})\in\Omega_{1}\Subset F(U^{\prime}_{1},t_{0})
. By continuity there is a neighborhood
V^{\prime}_{1}\subseteq\tilde{V}_{1}
of
t_{0}
such that
\Omega_{1}\Subset F(U^{\prime}_{1},t)
for all
t\in V^{\prime}_{1}
as well. Now we get
U^{\prime}_{1},V^{\prime}_{1},\Omega_{1}
as desired and
\Phi:\Omega_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1}\to U^{\prime}_{1}
is pointwise defined.

Since
F(\cdot,t)
is
C^{1}
-embedding which is homeomorphic to its image, the result
\sup_{t\in V^{\prime}_{1}}\|\Phi(\cdot,t)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\Omega_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
then follows from
[
Str21
, Lemma 5.9]
.

When
\beta\leq 1
, the result
\sup_{x\in\Omega_{1}}\|\Phi(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
follows from Lemma
2.2.33
via a scaling. When
\beta>1
, applying Inverse Function Theorem to the
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}
-map
[(x,t)\in U^{\prime}_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1}\mapsto(F(x,t),t)]
, we see that its inverse
[(x,t)\in\Omega_{1}\times V^{\prime}_{1}\mapsto(\Phi(x,t),t)]
is also
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}
, thus
\sup_{x\in\Omega_{1}}\|\Phi(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}(V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}<\infty
.

Therefore either case we have
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{t}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}_{t}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\min(\alpha+1,\beta)}_{x,t}
.

Now
\tilde{F}
and
\tilde{F}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}\subset C^{0}
so
\tilde{F}
is homeomorphic to its image.
∎

2.3
On Involutive Structures and ODE Flows

In this section we review basic property of involutive tangent subbundle, give the regularity estimates of ODE flows, and prove the commuting property for commutative log-Lipschitz vector fields.

2.3.1
Review of differentiable manifolds and subbundles

For
\kappa>1
, we can define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-maps between two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds for
0<\alpha\leq\kappa
, since by Corollary
2.1.31
we have compositions
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. (See also Definition
3.1.1
.)

Notation 2.3.1
.

Let
\kappa>1
,
\alpha\in(0,\kappa]
, and let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-differential manifolds.
We use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathfrak{N})
as the set of all (locally)
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-maps from
\mathfrak{M}
to
\mathfrak{N}
. We do not use the topology of this space in the thesis.

In fact we can define
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-functions on
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold whenever
\alpha>1-\kappa
, see Definition
3.1.1
and Lemma
2.1.34
.

We can define the mixed regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
on product manifolds.

Definition 2.3.2
.

Let
\kappa>1
and
\alpha,\beta\in(0,\kappa]
. Let
\mathfrak{M}^{m},\mathfrak{N}^{n},\mathfrak{P}^{q}
be three
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds.

We say a continuous map
f:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathfrak{P}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, denoted by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathfrak{P})
, if for every
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-coordinate charts
\phi:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
\psi:V\subseteq\mathfrak{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\rho:W\subseteq\mathfrak{P}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}
that satisfy
f(U\times V)\subseteq W
, we have

\rho\circ f\circ(\phi^{\mathrm{Inv}},\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U),\psi(V);\mathbb{R}^{q}).

(2.3.1)

Remark 2.3.3
.

(i)

The definition still has symmetry for indices:
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathfrak{P})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N},\mathfrak{M};\mathfrak{P})
.

(ii)

To check
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
it suffices to show that (
2.3.1
) is valid on a coordinate cover
\{(\phi_{i},\psi_{i},\rho_{i})\}_{i\in I}
for
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\times f(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\times\mathfrak{P}
such that
f(U_{i}\times V_{i})\subseteq W_{i}
for each
i\in I
. The proof can be done using Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
. Such cover always exists because of the continuity of
f
. We leave the proof to readers.

(iii)

In applications we only consider differentiable maps localized near a fixed point. By passing to a precompact coordinate chart near a point, everything is reduced to the case of Euclidean space, and by shrinking the domains every functions or vector fields would have bounded Hölder-Zygmund regularities.

Definition
2.3.2
is applied for labeling component-wise regularity for vector fields and parameterizations with parameters.

Definition 2.3.4
.

Let
1<\alpha\leq\kappa
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
m
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. A
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-regular parameterization
of
\mathfrak{M}
is a map
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\Phi
is injective and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\Phi(\Omega)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coordinate chart. A
topological parameterization
of
\mathfrak{M}
is a map
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
that is a topological embedding, i.e.
\Phi
is continuous and injective, and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\Phi(\Omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is also continuous.

When
\kappa>2
, the (real) tangent bundle
T\mathfrak{M}
of a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
has a natural
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-differential structure. Its complexification, the complex tangent bundle
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}:=T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{C}=T\mathfrak{M}\oplus iT\mathfrak{M}
is also a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-differential manifold. So we can define (real or complex)
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields when
\alpha\in(0,\kappa-1]
.

Similarly, the cotangent bundle
T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
and the complex cotangent bundle
\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
are also
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-manifolds.

Definition 2.3.5
.

Let
\kappa>1
,
0<\alpha\leq\kappa
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. A
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
is a subset
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
satisfying that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
,

•

\mathcal{S}_{p}:=\mathcal{S}\cap\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
is a
r
-dimensional complex vector subspace.

•

There is an open neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
on
U
, such that
X_{1}|_{q},\dots,X_{r}|_{q}
form a complex linear basis for
\mathcal{S}_{q}
for all
q\in U
.

We denote it as
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
. And we say that
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
for
\mathcal{S}
on
U
.

We use “subbundle”, or “regular subbundle” in Chapter
3
, as the abbreviation of (real or complex) tangent subbundle.

The real tangent subbundles can be defined in the same way through replacing every
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
by
T\mathfrak{M}
.
But we focus mostly on complex subbundles since every real subbundle
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
can be identified as a complex subbundle
\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathbb{C}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
.

Alternatively a rank
r

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
can be equivalently defined as a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-section of the complex Grassmannian bundle
\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})=\coprod_{p\in\mathfrak{M}}\{\text{rank }r\text{ complex spaces of }\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}\}
. For their equivalence we leave the proof to readers.

In Theorem
4.1
we consider tangent subbundles with mixed regularities.

Definition 2.3.6
.

Let
\kappa>1
,
0<\alpha,\beta\leq\kappa-1
and let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds. A
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
-subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})
such that, for any
(u_{0},v_{0})\in\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
there is a neighborhood
U\times V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
of
(u_{0},v_{0})
and complex vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}))
, such that
X_{1}|_{(u,v)},\dots,X_{r}|_{(u,v)}
form a complex linear basis for
\mathcal{S}_{(u,v)}
for all
(u,v)\in U\times V
.

Equivalently this is saying
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})))
as a section of the Grassmannian bundle.

Recall the notion of involutive structure in Definition
0
. Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
be a local coordinate system, for
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
vector fields
X=:\sum_{j=1}^{n}X^{j}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}
and
Y=:\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
, we recall that

[X,Y]=\sum_{j,k=1}^{n}\Big(X^{j}\frac{\partial Y^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}-Y^{j}\frac{\partial X^{k}}{\partial x^{j}}\Big)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}.

(2.3.2)

When
X,Y
are
C^{1}
,
[X,Y]\in C^{0}
is pointwise defined. In general (
2.3.2
) is a vector field with distributional coefficients. In Proposition
3.1.10
we will see that the
\mathscr{C}^{(-\frac{1}{2})+}
-distribution
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=0
if and only if
[X,Y]
is a “
\mathscr{C}^{(-\frac{1}{2})+}
-vector field” in section.

For completeness we give definition of the involutivity on subbundles with mixed regularities.

Definition 2.3.7
.

Let
\kappa>2
,
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},\kappa-1]
and
\beta\in(0,\kappa-1]
. Let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds and
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-complex subbundle such that
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
. We say
\mathcal{S}
is involutive, if for every
C^{0}
-sections
X,Y:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathcal{S}
of
\mathcal{S}
that are
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}_{\mathrm{loc}}
in
\mathfrak{M}
, and for every
C^{0}
-section
\theta:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
that are
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}_{\mathrm{loc}}
in
\mathfrak{M}
, the pairing
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathbb{C}
is identically zero.

Equivalently, we say
\mathcal{S}
is involutive, if
\mathcal{S}|_{\mathfrak{M}\times\{s\}}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is involutive in the sense of Definition
0
, for all
s\in\mathfrak{N}
.

Remark 2.3.8
.

Definition
2.3.7
does not require
X,Y
or
\theta
to be differentiable on
\mathfrak{N}
, since when
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
the differentiations in (
1.1.1
) are only taken on
\mathfrak{M}
but not on
\mathfrak{N}
, and by Lemma
2.1.18
one have the well-defined product map
C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N};\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M}))\times C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N};\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M}))\to C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{N};\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M}))
for all
\varepsilon>0
. This is critical for the case
\beta\leq\frac{1}{2}
.

An important fact for involutive structures is that we can pick a collection of good basis.

Lemma 2.3.9

(Canonical local basis)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}\cup\{\mathrm{logl}\}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
be two smooth-manifolds. Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-complex subbundle over
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
such that
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
. Then

(i)

There exist numbers
r,m,q\geq 0
. For every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
and
\lambda_{0}\in\mathfrak{N}
, there is a (mixed real and complex)
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-coordinate system
(t,z,s)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m},s^{1},\dots,s^{q}):\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
near
p
, such that

\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\partial_{\bar{z}^{m}}|_{p},\partial_{s^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\partial_{s^{q}}|_{p})=\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}.

(2.3.3)

(ii)

Let
(t,z,s):\tilde{U}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
be a coordinate system such that (
2.3.3
) holds for some
(p,\lambda_{0})\in\tilde{U}\times\mathfrak{N}
. For any product set
U\times V\subseteq\{(p,\lambda_{0})\in\tilde{U}\times\mathfrak{N}:\eqref{Eqn::ODE::GoodGen::DirectSumAssumption}\text{ holds}\}
, there exist a unique
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
-local basis
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{S}
on
U\times V
, such that the collection vectors
X^{\prime}=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
and
X^{\prime\prime}=[X_{r+1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
are of the form

\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
X^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&&A^{\prime}&B^{\prime}\\
&I_{m}&A^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial/\partial t\\
\partial/\partial z\\
\partial/\partial{\bar{z}}\\
\partial/\partial s\end{pmatrix},

(2.3.4)

where
A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{r\times m})
,
A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
,
B^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{r\times q})
,
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m\times q})
.

(iii)

If
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and if
\mathcal{S}
is involutive, then
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
in
(ii)
are pairwise commutative.

(iv)

Suppose
\mathcal{S}=\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, then we must have
m=0
,
A^{\prime}=0
,
A^{\prime\prime}=0
,
B^{\prime\prime}=0
, and
B^{\prime}
is real-valued.

Proof.

(i)
: Clearly
\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}
and
\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}
are both complex linear subspaces of
\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. We have
r:=\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})})
,
m:=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}-r
and
q:=\dim\mathfrak{M}-\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}_{(p,\lambda_{0})}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{(p,\lambda_{0})})
. Note that
\dim\mathfrak{M}=r+2m+q
. See
[
BCH08
, Lemma I.8.5]
for details of (
2.3.3
).

(ii)
: The formula (
2.3.4
) follows from the standard linear algebra argument. See
[
Lun92
, Lemma 1]
or
[
BCH08
, Page 18]
. Note that by Lemma
2.2.38

(ii)
, if
M\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times(r+m)})
is a matrix function invertible at every point in the domain, then
M^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times(r+m)})
holds as well. This is also true when
\alpha
or
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
.

We leave the details to readers, where the argument can be found in the proof of Lemma
5.1.10
as well.

We also remark that in the statement of
(ii)
,
U\times V
are arbitrary subset that does not depend on
\alpha,\beta
.

If
\tilde{X}=[\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\tilde{X}_{r+m}]^{\top}
is another local basis that has expression (
2.3.4
), then
\tilde{X}-X
are linear combinations of
\partial_{\bar{z}}
and
\partial_{s}
. But
\mathcal{S}|_{U}\cap\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}},\partial_{s})=\{0\}
, so
\tilde{X}=X
is the unique collection.

(iii)
: We use
(\partial_{u^{1}},\dots,\partial_{u^{r+m}}):=(\partial_{t^{1}},\dots,\partial_{t^{r}},\partial_{z^{1}},\dots,\partial_{z^{m}})
,
(\partial_{v^{1}},\dots,\partial_{v^{m+q}}):=(\partial_{\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\partial_{\bar{z}^{m}},\partial_{s^{1}},\dots,\partial_{s^{q}})
and
\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&B^{\prime}\\
A^{\prime\prime}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\left(f_{j}^{k}\right)_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq r+m\\
1\leq k\leq m+q\end{subarray}}
for simplicity.

In this notation, we see that
(\theta^{k})_{k=1}^{m+q}:=(dv^{k}-\sum_{j=1}^{r+m}f_{j}^{k}du^{j})_{k=1}^{m+q}
is a collection of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
1-forms that form a basis for
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}|_{U\times V}
.

To show that
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
are commutative, by direct computation, for
1\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq r+m
,

[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]=\sum_{k=1}^{r+m}\bigg(\frac{\partial f_{j^{\prime}}^{k}}{\partial u^{j}}-\frac{\partial f_{j}^{k}}{\partial u^{j^{\prime}}}+\sum_{k^{\prime}=1}^{m+q}\Big(f_{j}^{k^{\prime}}\frac{\partial f_{j^{\prime}}^{k}}{\partial v^{k^{\prime}}}-f_{j^{\prime}}^{k^{\prime}}\frac{\partial f_{j}^{k}}{\partial v^{k^{\prime}}}\Big)\bigg)\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{k}}\in\operatorname{Span}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}},\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Big).

(2.3.5)

Therefore
\langle dv^{k},[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]\rangle=\langle\theta^{k},[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]\rangle=0
for all
1\leq k\leq r+m
, which means the coefficients of
[X_{j},X_{j^{\prime}}]
are all vanishing.

(iv)
: When
\mathcal{S}=\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, the complex conjugate
\bar{X}_{1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
also form a local basis for
\mathcal{S}
. By
(ii)
we see that
\bar{X}^{\prime}=X^{\prime}
, so
B^{\prime}=\bar{B}^{\prime}
is a real-valued matrix.

By (
2.3.4
) we have
X^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{z}+A^{\prime\prime}\partial_{\bar{z}}+B^{\prime\prime}\partial_{s}
and
\bar{X}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{\bar{z}}+\bar{A}^{\prime\prime}\partial_{z}+\bar{B}^{\prime\prime}\partial_{s}
. They are linearly independent since
\partial_{z}
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}
are linearly independent. So
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
are all sections
\mathcal{S}
and are also linearly independent, which means
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}\geq r+2m
. Since
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
we must have
m=0
, which means
A^{\prime}=0
,
A^{\prime\prime}=0
,
B^{\prime\prime}=0
and
B^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{r\times q})
.
∎

We recall the definition of the complex Frobenius structure.

Definition 2.3.10
.

Let
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex subbundle. We call
\mathcal{S}
a
complex Frobenius structure
of
\mathfrak{M}
, if
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle, and both
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are involutive.

Remark 2.3.11
.

In
[
Gon20
]

\mathcal{S}
is also called a
Levi-flat CR vector bundle
on
\mathfrak{M}
. When
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\{0\}
and
\dim\mathfrak{M}=1+2\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}
is indeed a classical CR-structure of
\mathfrak{M}
.

Definition 2.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a complex tangent subbundle, we use
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
as the
dual bundle
of
\mathcal{S}
, and
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
as the
complex conjugate
of
\mathcal{S}
. They are given by

\mathcal{S}^{\bot}:=\{(p,\theta)\in\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}:\theta(v)=0,\forall v\in\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}\}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M};\ \bar{\mathcal{S}}:=\{(p,\bar{v})\in\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}:(p,v)\in\mathcal{S}\}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}.

(2.3.6)

Remark 2.3.13
.

(i)

One can check that
\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is indeed a subbundle, since
\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{p}\leq\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
is a complex subspace (which is closed under the scalar multiplication by
i
) for each
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(ii)

The fact that
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is a subbundle does not necessarily implies
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
or
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle. For example, the
Mizohata operator

X=\partial_{t}+it\partial_{x}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{t,x}
generates a rank 1 complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathbb{R}^{2}
, but
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
do not have constant rank:

(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})_{(t,x)}=\begin{cases}0,&t\neq 0,\\
\mathbb{C}_{t}\times\{0\},&t=0,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})_{(t,x)}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{C}_{t,x}^{2},&t\neq 0,\\
\mathbb{C}_{t}\times\{0\},&t=0.\end{cases}

(iii)

Suppose
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle. Then it is possible that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is more regular than
\mathcal{S}
. For example, in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with coordinates
(x,y,z)
,
\mathcal{S}
is spanned by
\partial_{x}+i(1+|y|)\partial_{y}
.
\mathcal{S}
is merely a Lipschitz subbundle, but
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
can be spanned by
\partial_{x},\partial_{y}
. So
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a real-analytic subbundle. Similarly if
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a subbundle than it can be more regular than
\mathcal{S}
as well. In this case
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\{0\}
is a rank 0 subbundle which formally speaking is real-analytic as well.

In the definition of complex Frobenius structure, we have no regularity assumption on
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. But the following lemma shows that once
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
has constant rank then
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are both subbundles with at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-regularities.

Lemma 2.3.14
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold. Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex subbundle such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
has constant rank at every point, then
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundles.

Proof.

Fix a point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
. By Lemma
2.3.9

\mathcal{S}
has a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m})
that has the form (
2.3.4
).

At the point
p
, by (
2.3.4
) we see that
X_{1}|_{p},\dots,X_{r+m}|_{p},\bar{X}_{r+1}|_{p},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}|_{p}
are linearly independent and span
\mathcal{S}_{p}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{p}=(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})_{p}
. By assumption
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
has constant rank, so
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r+2m
. Clearly by (
2.3.4
)
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
are still linear independent near
p
. Therefore
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle since
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m},\bar{X}_{r+1},\dots,\bar{X}_{r+m}
form a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis.

Since
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\bot}=(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})^{\bot}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
and
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}_{q}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{q})=2\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}_{q}-\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}_{q}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}_{q})=r
is constant along
q\in\mathfrak{M}
, we see that
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\bot}
is complex cotangent subbundle of rank
2m+q
. Since
T\mathfrak{M}
and
T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
are locally
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
isomorphic and that
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
is also
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, repeating the above argument for
(\mathcal{S}^{\bot},\mathcal{S}^{\bot}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\bot})
, we see that
(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})^{\bot}
is also a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
subbundle. Taking duality back we get
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.
∎

Definition 2.3.15
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
m
-dimensional complex manifold. A holomorphic vector field on
\mathfrak{M}
is a complex vector field such that locally it has form
\sum_{k=1}^{m}f^{k}(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}
where
(z^{1},\dots,z^{m}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a complex (holomorphic) coordinate chart for
\mathfrak{M}
, and
f^{k}
are holomorphic functions on
U
.

A rank
r
holomorphic tangent subbundle over
\mathfrak{M}
is a complex tangent subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and holomorphic vector fields
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{r}
on
U
that that form a local basis for
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
.

Equivalently
\mathcal{S}
is a holomorphic section of the Grassmannian bundle
\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathbf{Gr}_{\mathbb{C}}(r,\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
, where
T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}})
is the holomorphic tangent bundle. In literature we also use
T^{1,0}\mathfrak{M}
for
T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}
.

We have a similar result to Lemma
2.3.9
.

Lemma 2.3.16
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
is a
(r+q)
-dimensional complex manifold and
\mathcal{S}\leq T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}
be a rank
r
holomorphic subbundle. Then for any point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a holomorphic chart
(z,w)=(z^{1},\dots,z^{r},w^{1},\dots,w^{q}):\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{C}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}
such that
\mathcal{S}_{p}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}|_{p})=T^{\mathcal{O}}_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. Here
T^{\mathcal{O}}\mathfrak{M}
is the holomorphic tangent bundle.

Moreover for such chart
(z,w)
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\tilde{U}
of
p
, and a unique holomorphic local basis
Z=[Z_{1},\dots,Z_{r}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{S}
on
U
that has the form
Z=\partial_{z}+A\partial_{w}
where
A\in\mathcal{O}(U;\mathbb{C}^{r\times n})
.

Proof.

The existence of
(z,w)
follows the same but simpler argument from the existence of the chart
(t,z,s)
in Lemma
2.3.9
.

Fix
(z,w)
, we can find a holomorphic local basis
W=[W_{1},\dots,W_{r}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{S}
near
p
that has expression
W=M^{\prime}\partial_{z}+M^{\prime\prime}\partial_{w}
for some holomorphic matrix functions
M^{\prime},M^{\prime\prime}
defined near
p
. Since
\mathcal{S}_{p}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}|_{p})=T^{\mathcal{O}}_{p}\mathfrak{M}
,
M^{\prime}(p)\in\mathbb{C}^{r\times r}
is invertible. By continuity
M^{\prime}
is invertible in some neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
. And by cofactor representation of a holomorphic matrix we know
M^{\prime-1}
is also a holomorphic matrix function on
U
.

Take
Z=M^{\prime-1}W=\partial_{z}+M^{\prime-1}M^{\prime\prime}\partial_{w}
in
U
, so
A:=M^{\prime-1}M^{\prime\prime}
is the desired holomorphic matrix function.

Since
\mathcal{S}
is a complex tangent subbundle of
\mathfrak{M}
and we
\mathcal{S}_{p}\oplus\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{\bar{z}}|_{p},\partial_{\operatorname{Re}w}|_{p},\partial_{\operatorname{Im}w}|_{p})=\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. By Lemma
2.3.9

(ii)
,
A
is uniquely determined by the mixed real and complex coordinate system
(z,\operatorname{Re}w,\operatorname{Im}w)
, thus determined by
(z,w)
.
∎

Finally we give an auxiliary result to illustrate the regularity of the pullback subbundle. Recall Definition
2.3.6
for the subbundles with mixed regularity.

Lemma 2.3.17
.

Let
\kappa>2
,
\alpha\in(0,\kappa-1]
,
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
such that
\beta>\mathrm{Lip}
and
\beta,\gamma\leq\kappa
. Let
0\leq r\leq m
,
q\geq 0
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
(m+q)
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
manifold and
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
.

Let
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be an open subset. Suppose that
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
is a topological parameterization of
\mathfrak{M}
such that
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega);T\mathfrak{M})
and
\mathcal{S}|_{\Phi(\Omega)}\leq\operatorname{Span}(\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}})
(here
\operatorname{Span}
is for the complex span). Then
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\Omega^{\prime})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
is a subbundle that has regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta),\alpha\circ\gamma}
.

In particular by Corollary
2.1.31
, when
\beta\geq\alpha
,

•

\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
if
\gamma>\mathrm{Lip}
and
\gamma\geq\alpha
.

•

\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-}
if
\alpha\leq 1
and
\gamma=1-
.

•

\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha\gamma}
if
\max(\alpha,\gamma)<1
.

In general, say
\beta=\gamma<\alpha+1
, it is possible that
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m+1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}
are only
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\supset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha})
.

Proof.

Let
(x_{0},s_{0})\in\Omega
, we need to show that
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}
has a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha\circ\gamma}
-local basis near
(x_{0},s_{0})
.

By assumption
\mathcal{S}
has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
near
\Phi(u_{0})\in\mathfrak{M}
. Thus
(\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{r})
is a local basis for
\Phi^{*}\mathcal{S}
near
u_{0}
.

Since
\mathcal{S}|_{\Phi(\Omega)}\leq\operatorname{Span}(\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}})
, we can write
X_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}f_{j}^{k}\cdot\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
for
1\leq j\leq r
, for some functions
f_{j}^{k}
defined near
\Phi(u_{0})
. Since
f_{j}^{k}
are uniquely determined by
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
and
\Phi
, we see that
f_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

Taking pullback by
\Phi
, we have
\Phi^{*}X_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}(f_{j}^{k}\circ\Phi)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
, since
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta,\gamma}_{x,s}
, by Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
we get
f_{j}^{k}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta,\alpha\circ\gamma}_{x,s}
, so
(\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{r})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha\circ\beta,\alpha\circ\gamma}
-local basis for
\mathcal{S}
. By Corollary
2.1.31

(i)

\alpha\circ\beta=\min(\alpha,\beta)
, we finish the proof.
∎

2.3.2
The flow commuting of log-Lipschitz vector fields

In this subsection we estimate the ODE flow regularity for log-Lipschitz vector fields, and show that if two log-Lipschitz vector fields commute then their ODE flows also commute.

We define the log-Lipschitz modulus of continuity
{\mu_{\log}}:(0,1]\to(0,\infty)
and a function
\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}:(0,1]\to[0,\infty)
(also see
[
BCD11
, (3.4)]
) as

{\mu_{\log}}(r):=r\log\tfrac{e}{r}=r(1-\log r),\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}(r):=\int_{r}^{1}\frac{ds}{{\mu_{\log}}(s)}=\log\log\frac{e}{r},\hskip 10.00002pt0<r\leq 1.

(2.3.7)

Thus (
1.1
) can be rewritten as

\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})}=\|f\|_{C^{0}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})}+\sup_{x,y\in U;0<|x-y|<1}|f(x)-f(y)|\cdot{\mu_{\log}}(|x-y|)^{-1}.

(2.3.8)

Clearly
\lim\limits_{r\to 0}\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}(r)=+\infty
, so
{\mu_{\log}}
satisfies the Osgood condition, namely

\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dr}{{\mu_{\log}}(r)}=+\infty.

For a log-Lipschitz vector field
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
and a point
p\in U
, By Osgood’s uniqueness result
[
Osg98
]
(see
[
BCD11
, Theorem 3.2]
), the autonomous ODE
\dot{\gamma}(t)=X(\gamma(t))
,
\gamma(0)=p
has a unique solution.

Definition 2.3.18
.

Let
X
be a log-Lipschitz vector field on a
C^{1,1}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. The
flow map
of
X
, denoted by
\exp_{X}:\mathcal{D}_{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}\times\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
, is defined in the way that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
,
t\mapsto\exp_{X}(t,p)
is the unique maximal solution
\gamma=\gamma_{p}
to the ODE

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\dot{\gamma}(t)=X(\gamma(t))\in T_{\gamma(t)}\mathfrak{M},\\
\gamma(0)=p.\end{array}\right.

Here
\mathcal{D}_{X}\cap(\mathbb{R}\times\{p\})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the maximal existence interval of
\gamma
, which is an open interval containing
0
.

We denote
e^{tX}(p):=\exp_{X}(t,p)
.

Remark 2.3.19
.

There is no ambiguity to use
e^{tX}
since
(t,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
if and only if
(1,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{tX}
and
\exp_{X}(t,p)=\exp_{tX}(1,p)
for all
(t,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
.

Suppose in addition that
X
has compact support, by taking the zero extension outside
U
we can say
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. In this case
e^{tX}(p)\equiv p
holds for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in U^{c}
, so by the maximal existence theorem (see
[
BCD11
, Proposition 3.11]
) we see that
e^{tX}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is defined for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Later on we often assume the vector fields to have compact supports in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
so that we do not need to worry about the domains of their ODE flows.

In order to give the regularity estimate of
e^{tX}
, we use the following Gronwall-Osgood inequality:

Lemma 2.3.20

(
[
BCD11
, Lemma 3.4 and Corollary 3.5]
)
.

Let
\mu:(0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a Osgood modulus of continuity, that is,
\mu
is a continuous increasing function satisfying
\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\mu(r)=0
and
\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dr}{\mu(r)}=+\infty
. Let
\tau\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\gamma\in L^{1}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\rho:[0,\tau]\to[0,1]
satisfy

\begin{gathered}\rho(0)<1;\qquad\rho(t)\leq\rho(0)+\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)\mu(\rho(s))ds,\quad 0\leq s\leq\tau;\\
\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma(t)dt\leq\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\rho(0)),\quad\text{where }\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(t):=\int_{t}^{1}\frac{ds}{\mu(s)}.\end{gathered}

(2.3.9)

Then

\rho(t)\leq\mathcal{M}_{\mu}^{-1}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{\mu}(\rho(0))-\int_{0}^{t}\gamma(s)ds\Big),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau.

(2.3.10)

For the special case where
\mu={\mu_{\log}}
we have the following:

Lemma 2.3.21
.

Let
0<a<1
,
b>0
and
0<\tau\leq\frac{1}{b}\log\log\frac{e}{a}
. Let
\rho:[0,\tau]\to\mathbb{R}
be an integrable function such that
\rho(0)=a
and

\rho(t)\leq a+b\int_{0}^{t}\rho(s)(1-\log\rho(s))ds,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau.

(2.3.11)

Then

\rho(t)\leq e\cdot a^{e^{-bt}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau.

Note that from the assumption of
\tau
we have
e\cdot a^{e^{-bt}}\leq 1
.

Proof.

Take
\gamma(t):\equiv b
in Lemma
2.3.20
. Note that by (
2.3.7
)
\int_{0}^{\tau}\gamma(s)ds=b\tau\leq\log\log\frac{e}{a}=\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}(\rho(0))
, so the assumptions in (
2.3.9
) are all satisfied. Thus we have the conclusion (
2.3.10
), since

\rho(t)\leq\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}^{-1}(\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}(a)-bt)=e^{1-e^{\mathcal{M}_{\mu_{\log}}(a)-bt}}=e^{1-e^{\log\log\frac{e}{a}}\cdot e^{-bt}}=e^{1-e^{-bt}}\cdot a^{e^{-bt}}\leq e\cdot a^{e^{-bt}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau.\qed

Lemma
2.3.21
establishes the Hölder estimate for log-Lipschitz ODE flows.

Lemma 2.3.22
.

Let
X
be a bounded log-Lipschitz vector field on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded open set. We consider the map
\exp_{X}(t,x):=e^{tX}(x)
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(i)

For any
\varepsilon>0
there is a small
\tau_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-\varepsilon}((-\tau_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
(also see
[
BCD11
, Theorem 3.7]
)

(ii)

If
X\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
, then
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}L^{\infty}((-T,T),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
. In particular for any
\varepsilon>0
there is a small
\tau_{\varepsilon}>0
such that
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2,1-\varepsilon}((-\tau_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

(iii)

If
X
is little log-Lipschitz, then
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-}((-T,T),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
T>0
.

(iv)

In particular if
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}
, then
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2,1-}((-T,T),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
T>0
.

Proof.

Since
X
has compact support, we know
\exp_{X}
is globally defined. Since we have
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\exp_{X}=X\circ\exp_{X}
, we have
|e^{t_{1}X}(x)-e^{t_{2}X}(x)|\leq|t_{1}-t_{2}|\|X\|_{C^{0}}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, thus
\{\exp_{X}(\cdot,x):x\in U\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}(-T,T)
is bounded. By Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
,
\{X\circ\exp_{X}(\cdot,x):x\in U\}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(-T,T)
,
therefore
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL}}L^{\infty}((-T,T),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
T>0
.

If in addition
X\in\mathscr{C}^{1}
, then by Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
again, since
1\circ(1+\mathrm{LogL})=1
, we have
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}L^{\infty}((-T,T),U;\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
T>0
.

Thus to prove
(i)
and
(ii)
it suffices to show the following:

\forall\varepsilon>0,\ \exists\tau_{\varepsilon},C_{1}>0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{such that }|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq C_{1}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }|t|<\tau_{\varepsilon}\text{ and }x_{1},x_{2}\in U.

(2.3.12)

And if in addition
X
is little log-Lipschitz, then to prove
(iii)
and
(iv)
it suffices to prove the following:

\forall\varepsilon,T>0,\ \exists C_{2}>0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{such that }|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq C_{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }|t|<T\text{ and }x_{1},x_{2}\in U.

(2.3.13)

We now prove (
2.3.12
) and (
2.3.13
).

Proof of (
2.3.12
): Since
U
is a bounded set, by enlarging the constant
C_{1}
it suffices to prove (
2.3.12
) with
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\frac{1}{e}
. And by replacing
X
by
-X
we only need to prove the case
t\geq 0
.

Note that by (
2.3.8
) and (
2.3.7
), we have for every
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
t\geq 0
,

|e^{tX}x_{1}-e^{tX}x_{2}|\leq\Big|x_{1}-x_{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\big(X(e^{sX}x_{1})-X(e^{sX}x_{2})\big)ds\Big|\leq|x_{1}-x_{2}|+\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}\int_{0}^{t}{\mu_{\log}}\big(|e^{sX}x_{1}-e^{sX}x_{2}|\big)ds.

(2.3.14)

For
x_{1},x_{2}
that satisfy
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\frac{1}{e}
, we take

a:=|x_{1}-x_{2}|,\hskip 10.00002ptb:=\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}},\hskip 10.00002pt\rho(t):=|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|,\hskip 10.00002pt\tau:=b^{-1}\cdot\log 2\leq\tfrac{1}{b}\log\log\tfrac{e}{a}.

Now (
2.3.11
) is satisfied, and by Lemma
2.3.21
we get

|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{e^{-bt}}
for all
0\leq t\leq\tau
and
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\frac{1}{e}
.

Therefore for
\varepsilon>0
, taking
\tau_{\varepsilon}=\min(\tau,\frac{1}{b}\ln\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon})
we get

|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{e^{-bt}}\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{e^{-b\tau_{\varepsilon}}}\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau_{\varepsilon}\text{ and }|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\tfrac{1}{e}.

Since
U
is a bounded domain, let
L=\delta^{-1}\operatorname{diam}U
, we then have
|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq eL|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in U
. We obtain (
2.3.12
) and complete the proof of
(i)
and
(ii)
.

Proof of (
2.3.13
):
Fix a small
\varepsilon>0
and a large
T>0
from the assumption, the key is to find a
\delta=\delta(\varepsilon,T)>0
such that
|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}
for all
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\delta
and
-T<t<T
. Replacing
X
by
-X
it suffices to estimate the case
0\leq t<T
.

Let
b:=\frac{1}{T}\log\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}>0
. By assumption
X
is little log-Lipschitz, so there is a
\delta_{1}>0
such that

|X(x_{1})-X(x_{2})|\leq(b/e)^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}|x_{1}-x_{2}|(1-\log|x_{1}-x_{2}|),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ satisfying }|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\delta_{1}.

Take
\delta=\min(e^{1-e^{bT}},\delta_{1})
, we have
T\leq\frac{1}{b}\log\log\frac{e}{\delta}
. Now for any
x_{1},x_{2}
such that
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\delta
, we take

a:=|x_{1}-x_{2}|,\hskip 10.00002pt\rho(t):=|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|,\hskip 10.00002pt\tau:=T\leq\tfrac{1}{b}\log\log\tfrac{e}{a}.

So (
2.3.11
) is satisfied, and by Lemma
2.3.21
we get

|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{e^{-bt}}\leq e|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}
for all
0\leq t<T
and
|x_{1}-x_{2}|<\delta
.

Since
U
is a bounded domain, let
L=\delta^{-1}\operatorname{diam}U
, we then have
|e^{tX}(x_{1})-e^{tX}(x_{2})|\leq eL|x_{1}-x_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}
for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in U
. This gives (
2.3.13
) and finishes the proof of
(iii)
and
(iv)
.
∎

Remark 2.3.23
.

In fact in
(iii)
we have
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{logl},1-}
, if we define
\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{logl}}
to be the space of function whose derivatives are all
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}
.

In Lemma
2.3.22

(i)
, even though
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
near
t=0
holds for arbitrary
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, it is possible that
\exp_{X}
cannot be
\mathscr{C}^{1-}
near
t=0
. See Corollary
5.3.2

(ii)
.

For a log-Lipschitz vector field
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
with compact support, we can consider the Schwartz approximation
X^{\nu}:=\mathsf{S}_{\nu}X
for
\nu\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where
\mathsf{S}_{\nu}
is the Littlewood-Paley summation operator given in Definition
2.1.2
. The following result shows the uniform convergence of the log-Lipschitz integral curves.

Definition 2.3.24
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1}
-manifold and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be continuous vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
. We say a curve
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathfrak{M}
is
a subunit curve along
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
, if
\gamma
is Lipschitz, and
\dot{\gamma}(t)\overset{a.e}{=}\sum_{j=1}^{m}u^{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma(t))
holds for some
u^{1},\dots,u^{m}\in L^{\infty}[0,T]
such that
\sum_{j=1}^{m}|u^{j}(t)|^{2}\leq 1
for almost every
0\leq t\leq T
.

Thus the Carnot-Carathéodory distance for
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
(see (
1.1.3
)) can be restated as

\operatorname{dist}_{X}(x,y)=\inf\{T>0:\exists\text{ subunit curve }\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathfrak{M}\text{ along }X\text{ such that }\gamma(0)=p,\gamma(T)=q\}.

(2.3.15)

Lemma 2.3.25
.

Let
m\geq 1
. Let
(\phi_{\sigma})_{\sigma=0}^{\infty}
be a fixed dyadic resolution that defines
\{\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma=0}^{\infty}
. For any
0<\delta<1
and
M>1
there exist a
0<\tau_{1}<1
and a
C_{1}>1
that satisfy the following:

Let
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
be log-Lipschitz vector fields such that
\sup_{1\leq j\leq m}\|X_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}<M
. Let
u=(u^{1},\dots,u^{m})\in L^{\infty}([0,1];\mathbb{R}^{m})
satisfies
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}=\mathop{\operatorname{essup}}_{t\in[0,\tau_{1}]}\sum_{i=1}^{m}|u^{j}(t)|^{2}\leq 1
. Let
X_{j}^{\sigma}:=\mathsf{S}_{\nu}X_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq m
and
\sigma=1,2,3\dots
. Consider the ODEs

\dot{\gamma}^{\sigma}(t)\overset{\text{a.e.}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{m}u^{i}(t)X_{i}^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t)),\hskip 10.00002pt\dot{\gamma}(t)\overset{\text{a.e.}}{=}\sum_{i=1}^{m}u^{i}(t)X_{i}(\gamma(t)),\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma^{\sigma}(0)=\gamma(0)=p,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }\sigma\geq 1.

(2.3.16)

Then
\gamma^{\sigma},\gamma:[0,\tau_{1}]\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
are uniquely defined Lipschitz curves, and satisfy

\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau_{1}}|\gamma^{\sigma}(t)-\gamma(t)|\leq C_{1}2^{-(1-\delta)\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 1.

(2.3.17)

In particular
\lim\limits_{\sigma\to\infty}\gamma^{\sigma}(t)=\gamma(t)
uniformly for
t\in[0,\tau_{1}]
. And as a special cases,
\lim\limits_{\sigma\to\infty}e^{tX_{j}^{\sigma}}(p)=e^{tX_{j}}(p)
holds uniformly for
1\leq j\leq m
,
t\in[-\tau_{1},\tau_{1}]
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

The existence and uniqueness of
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\sigma}
can be founded in, for example
[
BCD11
, Theorem 3.2]
. Our focus is to prove (
2.3.17
).

Firstly for
1\leq j\leq m
,

\displaystyle\|X^{\sigma}_{j}-X_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}|2^{n\sigma}\phi_{0}(2^{\sigma}\cdot)\ast X_{j}(x)-X_{j}(x)|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}|2^{n\sigma}\phi_{0}(2^{\sigma}y)||X_{j}(x-y)-X_{j}(x)|dy

\displaystyle\leq\|X_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}2^{n}\sigma|y|\max(\log\tfrac{e}{y},1)|\phi_{0}(2^{\sigma}y)|dy\leq C_{\phi}\|X_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}\cdot\sigma 2^{-\sigma}.

Here
C_{\phi}>0
is a constant depending only on
\phi_{0}
. Therefore

\displaystyle|\dot{\gamma}^{\sigma}(t)-\dot{\gamma}(t)|
