\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq m}|X_{j}^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))-X_{j}(\gamma(t))|\leq\sum_{1\leq j\leq m}\Big(\|X_{j}^{\sigma}-X_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}+|X_{j}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))-X_{j}(\gamma(t))|\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|X_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}\Big(C_{\phi}\cdot\sigma 2^{-\sigma}+\mu_{\log}(|\gamma^{\sigma}(t)-\gamma(t)|)\Big).

Applying Lemma
2.3.21
in the way similar to the proof of Lemma
2.3.22
, we see that there is a
C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}^{\prime}(\phi,M)>0
such that

\text{When }\sigma 2^{-\sigma}<\tfrac{1}{C_{1}^{\prime}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{then }|\gamma^{\sigma}(t)-\gamma(t)|\leq e\cdot(C_{1}^{\prime}\sigma 2^{-\sigma})^{e^{-C_{1}^{\prime}t}}.

Thus we can take
\tau_{1}>0
such that
e^{-C_{1}^{\prime}\tau_{1}}=1-\delta/2
and
C_{1}>0
be such that

C_{1}>2^{\sigma_{0}}\tau_{1}\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|X_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}}+eC_{1}^{\prime}\max_{\sigma\geq\sigma_{0}}\sigma 2^{-\frac{\delta}{2}\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }\sigma_{0}\text{ is the largest number such that }\sigma_{0}2^{-\sigma_{0}}\geq\tfrac{1}{C_{1}^{\prime}}.

We see that
\tau_{1},C_{1}
depends only on
\phi,\delta,M
but not on the choice of
\gamma
. Thus (
2.3.17
) holds.

Let
\sigma\to\infty
we see that
\gamma^{\sigma}(t)\to\gamma(t)
uniformly for
t\in[0,\tau_{1}]
and all such
\gamma
. Take
\gamma(t):=e^{tX_{j}}(p)
or
\gamma(t):=e^{-tX_{j}}(p)
we see that
\gamma^{\sigma}(t)=e^{\pm tX_{j}^{\sigma}}(p)
and therefore
e^{tX_{j}^{\sigma}}(p)\to e^{tX_{j}}(p)
uniformly for
t\in[-\tau_{1},\tau_{1}]
and
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
∎

Lemma
2.3.25
extends to the estimate of
\gamma(t)
precomposing a log-Lipschitz function
F
:

Lemma 2.3.26
.

Let
m,n\geq 1
. Let
(\phi_{\sigma})_{\sigma=0}^{\infty}
be a fixed dyadic resolution that defines
\{\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}\}_{\sigma=0}^{\infty}
. For any
0<\delta<1
and
M>1
there is a
\tau_{2}>0
and
C_{2}>0
that satisfy the following:

Let
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{1\times n})
be log-Lipschitz vector fields as well as row-vector valued functions. Let
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})
be a vector-valued function and let
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m\times m})
be matrix-valued distributions. Suppose

•

F(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

•

X_{j}\cdot\nabla F=A_{j}\cdot F
as
\mathbb{R}^{m}
-valued distributions for
1\leq j\leq m
.

•

\sup_{1\leq j\leq m}\|X_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}+\|A_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}<M
.

For
\sigma\geq 1
, let
F^{\sigma}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}F
and let
\gamma,\gamma^{\sigma}
be defined in (
2.3.16
). Then

\sup_{0\leq t\leq\tau_{2}}|F^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))|\leq C_{2}2^{-(1-\delta)\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 1.

(2.3.18)

In particular
F(\gamma(t))\equiv 0
for
0\leq t\leq\tau_{2}
.

Proof.

By assumption we have
(X_{j}\cdot\nabla F)^{\top}-F^{\top}\cdot A_{j}^{\top}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times 1}
where
X_{j},F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
\nabla F,A_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
for all
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
. Applying Theorem
2.1.48
to their components we see that there is a
C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}^{\prime}(m,n,M,\delta)>1
such that

\|X_{j}^{\sigma}\cdot\nabla F^{\sigma}-A_{j}^{\sigma}\cdot F^{\sigma}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{m})}\leq C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-(1-\delta/2)\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 1,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq m.\hskip 10.00002pt\text{Here }X_{j}^{\sigma}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}X_{j},\hskip 10.00002ptA_{j}^{\sigma}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}A_{j}.

Since
A_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
, we have
\|A_{j}^{\sigma}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sigma\|A_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}
by Definition
2.1.21
. Thus by (
2.3.16
) we have: for every
\sigma\geq 0
and every
t
in the domain of
\gamma
,

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{d}{dt}F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(t)\bigg|

\displaystyle=\bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{m}u^{j}(t)(X_{j}^{\sigma}\cdot\nabla F^{\sigma})(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))\bigg|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}|u^{j}(t)||(A_{j}^{\sigma}\cdot F^{\sigma})(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))|+C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-(1-\delta/2)\sigma}

(2.3.19)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma|F^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))|\cdot\sup_{1\leq j\leq m}\|A_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}+C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-(1-\delta/2)\sigma}\leq C_{2}^{\prime}\sigma|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(t)|+C_{2}^{\prime}2^{-(1-\delta/2)\sigma}.

Here
C_{2}^{\prime}:=\max_{1\leq j\leq m}\|A_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}}+C_{1}^{\prime}>1
depends only on
m,n,\phi,\delta,M
.

Therefore by Gronwall’s inequality (or taking
\mu(t):=t
in Lemma
2.3.20
) we have

|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(t)|\leq|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(0)|e^{C_{2}^{\prime}\sigma t}+\sigma^{-2}2^{-(1-\delta/2)\sigma}(e^{C_{2}^{\prime}\sigma t}-1)\leq|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(0)|2^{\frac{C_{2}^{\prime}t}{\log 2}\sigma}+2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2}-\frac{C_{2}^{\prime}t}{\log 2})\sigma}.

(2.3.20)

On the other hand we have
|\gamma^{\sigma}(0)|=|\gamma^{\sigma}(0)-\gamma(0)|\leq C_{1}2^{-(1-\delta/4)\sigma}
where
C_{1}=C_{1}(\delta/4,M)>1
is the constant in Lemma
2.3.25
depend only on
m,n,\phi,\delta,M
. Thus

\displaystyle|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(0)|=|F^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(0))-F(\gamma(0))|\leq|F(\gamma^{\sigma}(0))-F(\gamma(0))|+|F^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(0))-F(\gamma^{\sigma}(0))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\frac{\delta}{4}}}|\gamma^{\sigma}(0)-\gamma(0)|^{1-\frac{\delta}{4}}+\|F^{\sigma}-F\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\frac{\delta}{4}}}C_{1}^{1-\frac{\delta}{4}}2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{4})^{2}\sigma}+\sum_{j=\sigma+1}^{\infty}\|\mathsf{P}_{j}F\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

{}_{\delta}\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\delta/4}}2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2})\sigma}+\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\delta/2}}2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2})\sigma}\lesssim_{\delta,M}2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2})\sigma}.

We see that there is a
C^{\prime}_{3}=C^{\prime}_{3}(m,n,\phi,\delta,M)>0
such that
|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(0)|\leq C_{3}^{\prime}2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2})\sigma}
. Plugging this to (
2.3.20
) we get

|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(t)|\leq C_{3}^{\prime}2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2}-\frac{C_{2}^{\prime}t}{\log 2})\sigma}+2^{-(1-\frac{\delta}{2}-\frac{C_{2}^{\prime}t}{\log 2})\sigma}.

Taking
\tau_{2}:=\min(\tau_{1},\frac{\delta}{2}\cdot\frac{\log 2}{C_{2}^{\prime}})
and
C_{2}:=C_{3}^{\prime}+1
, where
\tau_{1}
is the constant in Lemma
2.3.25
, we obtain (
2.3.18
).

Finally both convergence
F^{\sigma}\to F
and
\gamma^{\sigma}\to\gamma
are uniform, we conclude that
F(\gamma(t))=\lim_{\sigma\to\infty}F^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))=0
uniformly in
t\in[0,\tau_{2}]
.
∎

The key to prove the Frobenius theorem is to show that if two vector fields commute, then their ODE flows also commute. In order to prove the singular Frobenius theorem, Theorem
2
, we need a stronger version to this property.

Proposition 2.3.27

(Strong flow commuting for log-Lipschitz vector fields)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
manifold and let
S\Subset\mathfrak{M}
be a precompact set. Let
1\leq r\leq m
and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be log-Lipschitz real vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose we have

•

X_{1}(p),\dots,X_{r}(p)\in T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
are linearly independent, and
X_{r+1}(p)=\dots=X_{m}(p)=0
for all
p\in S
.

•

There are
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq j\leq m
and
r+1\leq k\leq m
, such that as distributions,

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=r+1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq r,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq m.

Then there is a
\tau_{0}>0
such that

(i)

X_{j}(e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p))\equiv 0
for all
r+1\leq j\leq m
and
t^{1},\dots,t^{r}\in(-\tau_{0},\tau_{0})
.

(ii)

the following equality holds with both sides are defined on
\mathfrak{M}
:

e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=e^{t^{\sigma_{1}}X_{\sigma_{1}}}\dots e^{t^{\sigma_{r}}X_{\sigma_{r}}}(p),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }t^{1},\dots,t^{r}\in(-\tau_{0},\tau_{0})\hskip 10.00002ptp\in S,

(2.3.21)

for every permutation
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{r})
of
\{1,\dots,r\}
. In particular

\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=X_{j}\circ e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }t^{1},\dots,t^{r}\in(-\tau_{0},\tau_{0})\hskip 10.00002ptp\in S.

(2.3.22)

Remark 2.3.28
.

Here unlike (
1.1.2
) we do not need
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
to hold for
r+1\leq i,j\leq m
.

When
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
pairwise commute, (
2.3.21
) holds automatically, as we can take
c_{ij}^{k}\equiv 0
.

Proof.

By passing to a local, with possibly a partition of unity, we can assume
\mathfrak{M}=\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open subset.
We can take a precompact subset
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
containing
\bar{S}
and a cut-off
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
such that
\chi|_{\Omega^{\prime}}\equiv 1
. Let

T_{0}:=(r\sup_{1\leq i\leq m}\|X_{i}\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime})})^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{dist}(S,\partial\Omega^{\prime}),

(2.3.23)

we see that the following equality holds with both sides are defined.

e^{t^{i_{1}}X_{i_{1}}}\dots e^{t^{i_{r}}X_{i_{r}}}(p)=e^{t^{i_{1}}(\chi X_{i_{1}})}\dots e^{t^{i_{r}}(\chi X_{i_{r}})}(p)\in\Omega^{\prime},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\in\{1,\dots,r\},\hskip 10.00002pt|t^{i_{1}}|,\dots,|t^{i_{r}}|\leq T_{0},\hskip 10.00002ptp\in S.

(2.3.24)

First we note that it suffices find a
\tau_{0}\in(0,T_{0})
that satisfies the following: let
p\in S
be a fixed point,

•

Let
\gamma\in C^{0,1}([0,r\tau_{0}];\Omega^{\prime})
that satisfies
\gamma(0)=p
and
\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}u^{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma(t))
for some
u=(u^{1},\dots,u^{r})\in L^{\infty}[0,r\tau_{0}]
with
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
, we have

e^{t_{i}X_{i}}e^{t_{j}X_{j}}(\gamma(t))=e^{t_{j}X_{j}}e^{t_{i}X_{i}}(\gamma(t)),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 0\leq t\leq r\tau_{0},\hskip 10.00002ptt_{i},t_{j}\in[-\tau_{0},\tau_{0}],\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq r.

(2.3.25)

Once the (
2.3.25
) is proved, by taking
\gamma
such that
(u^{1}(t),\dots,u^{r}(t))\in\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
(the unit coordinate directions) for all
t\in[0,k\tau_{0}]
, we see that for all
2\leq k\leq r
,

e^{t^{i_{1}}X_{i_{1}}}e^{t^{i_{2}}X_{i_{2}}}e^{t^{i_{3}}X_{i_{3}}}\dots e^{t^{i_{k}}X_{i_{k}}}(p)=e^{t^{i_{2}}X_{i_{2}}}e^{t^{i_{1}}X_{i_{1}}}e^{t^{i_{3}}X_{i_{3}}}\dots e^{t^{i_{k}}X_{i_{k}}}(p),\ 1\leq i_{1}\dots,i_{k}\leq r,\ t^{i_{1}},\dots,t^{i_{k}}\in(-\tau_{0},\tau_{0}).

By decomposing a permutation into transpositions, (
2.3.21
) then follows.

To prove (
2.3.25
), let
\gamma:[0,T_{0}]\to\Omega^{\prime}
be a such fixed curve. We take approximations
X^{\sigma}_{j}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\chi X_{j})
,
c_{ij}^{k\sigma}:=\mathsf{S}_{\sigma}(\chi c_{ij}^{k})
for
\sigma\geq 1
,
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq j\leq m
and
r+1\leq k\leq m
. And we define
\gamma^{\sigma}:[0,T_{0}]\to\Omega^{\prime}
be the unique curve such that
\gamma^{\sigma}(0)=p
and
\dot{\gamma}^{\sigma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}u^{j}(t)X_{j}^{\sigma}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))
.

By Lemma
2.3.25
there is a
0<\tau_{1}<1
that depends on
X_{1},\dots,X_{r},\chi,T_{0}
but not on
\gamma
, such that
e^{uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{vX_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))\xrightarrow{\sigma\to\infty}e^{uX_{i}}e^{vX_{j}}(\gamma(t))
(since
\chi X_{j}\equiv X_{j}
in
\Omega^{\prime}
) uniformly for all
1\leq i,j\leq r
,
t\in[0,\tau_{1}]
and
u,v\in[-\tau_{1},\tau_{1}]
. On the other hand, using the commutator formula from
[
RS07
, Lemma 4.1]
, for such
u,v,t
,

\displaystyle e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-vX_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{vX_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))-\gamma^{\sigma}(t)

(2.3.26)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{u}\int_{0}^{v}\left([X_{i}^{\sigma},X_{j}^{\sigma}]\cdot\nabla(e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{-u^{\prime}X_{i}^{\sigma}})\right)\circ\left(e^{(u-u^{\prime})X_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))\right)du^{\prime}dv^{\prime}.

Since
\tau_{1}\leq T_{0}
is small, by (
2.3.23
) we see that
e^{(u-u^{\prime})X_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))\in\Omega^{\prime}
for
u,u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in[-\tau_{1},\tau_{1}]
and
t\in[0,\tau_{1}]
. Applying Proposition
2.1.49
we see that

|[X_{i}^{\sigma},X_{j}^{\sigma}](q)|\leq\sum_{k=r+1}^{m}|c_{ij}^{k\sigma}(q)||X_{k}^{\sigma}(q)|+C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-\frac{2}{3}\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 1,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq m,\hskip 10.00002ptq\in\Omega^{\prime}.

Here
C_{1}^{\prime}=C_{1}^{\prime}(X,\chi,(c_{ij}^{k}))>1
does not depend on
\sigma
and
\gamma
. Thus,

\displaystyle|e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-vX_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{vX_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))-\gamma^{\sigma}(t)|\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=r+1}^{m}\|c_{ij}^{k\sigma}\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}\bigg|\int_{0}^{u}\int_{0}^{v}X_{k}^{\sigma}\circ e^{(u-u^{\prime})X_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))du^{\prime}dv^{\prime}\bigg|

(2.3.27)

\displaystyle+C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-\frac{2}{3}\sigma}\bigg|\int_{0}^{u}\int_{0}^{v}\bigg\|\nabla\left(e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{-u^{\prime}X_{i}^{\sigma}}\right)\bigg\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}du^{\prime}dv^{\prime}\bigg|.

(2.3.28)

For (
2.3.28
), by Gronwall’s inequality
\|\nabla(e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{-u^{\prime}X_{i}^{\sigma}})\|_{C^{0}}\leq e^{|u|\|\nabla X_{i}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}}+|v^{\prime}|\|\nabla X_{j}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}}+|u^{\prime}|\|\nabla X_{i}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}}}
. Since
\|\nabla X_{l}^{\sigma}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\sigma\|X_{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}}
for all
1\leq l\leq r
, we can find a
C_{2}^{\prime}>0
that does not depend on
\sigma
and
\gamma
, such that

\displaystyle C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-\frac{2}{3}\sigma}\Big|\int_{0}^{u}\int_{0}^{v}\Big\|\nabla\left(e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{-u^{\prime}X_{i}^{\sigma}})\right)\Big\|_{C^{0}(\Omega^{\prime})}du^{\prime}dv^{\prime}\Big|\leq C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-\frac{2}{3}\sigma}e^{(2|u|+|v|)C_{2}^{\prime}\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\sigma\geq 1,\hskip 10.00002ptu,v\in[-\tau_{1},\tau_{1}].

Taking
\tau_{0}^{\prime}:=(9C_{2})^{-1}\log 2
, we see that when
|u|,|v|\leq\tau_{0}^{\prime}
, (
2.3.28
) is bounded by
C_{1}^{\prime}2^{-\sigma/3}
, which goes to
0
as
\sigma\to\infty
.

For (
2.3.27
), write
X_{i}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}a_{i}^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{l}}=[a_{i}^{1},\dots,a_{i}^{n}]
for
1\leq i\leq m
, we define
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{(m-r)n}
to be the coefficients of
(\chi X_{r+1},\dots,\chi X_{m})
, i.e.
F=(\chi a_{j}^{l})_{r+1\leq j\leq m;1\leq l\leq n}
. The assumption
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
implies
[\chi X_{i},\chi X_{j}]=\sum_{k=r+1}^{m}\chi c_{ij}^{k}\cdot\chi X_{k}+(X_{i}\chi)\cdot\chi X_{j}-(X_{j}\chi)\cdot\chi X_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq r
,
r+1\leq j\leq m
. Therefore

\chi X_{i}\cdot\nabla(\chi a_{j}^{l})=\sum_{k=r+1}^{m}\chi c_{ij}^{k}\cdot\chi a_{k}^{l}+(X_{i}\chi)\cdot\chi a_{j}^{l}-\sum_{q=1}^{n}(\chi a_{i}^{l}\partial_{q}\chi)\cdot\chi a_{j}^{q}.\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i\leq r<j\leq m,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq l\leq n.

Therefore there is a
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{c}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{(m-r)n\times(m-r)n})
such that
\chi X_{i}\cdot\nabla F=A\cdot F
as
\mathbb{R}^{(m-r)n}
-valued distributions in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Now by the assumption
X_{r+1}(p),\dots,X_{m}(p)=0
we have
F(p)=0
. Therefore applying Lemma
2.3.26
, we get
(i)
and we can find a
0<\tau_{2}<\tau_{0}^{\prime}
and
C_{3}^{\prime}>1
that does not depend on
\gamma
and
\sigma
, such that

|X_{k}^{\sigma}\circ e^{(u-u^{\prime})X_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{v^{\prime}X_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))|\leq C_{3}^{\prime}2^{-\frac{1}{2}\sigma},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall 1\leq i,j\leq r<k\leq m,\hskip 10.00002ptu,u^{\prime},v^{\prime}\in[-\tau_{2},\tau_{2}],\hskip 10.00002ptt\in[0,\tau_{2}],\hskip 10.00002pt\sigma\geq 1.

Plugging this into (
2.3.27
) and since
\|c_{ij}^{k\sigma}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim\sigma
, we see that (
2.3.27
) goes to
0
as
\sigma\to\infty
.

We now conclude that
e^{-uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{-vX_{j}^{\sigma}}e^{uX_{i}^{\sigma}}e^{vX_{j}^{\sigma}}(\gamma^{\sigma}(t))-\gamma^{\sigma}(t)\to 0
uniformly for
u,v\in[-\tau_{2},\tau_{2}]
,
t\in[0,\tau_{2}]
and all subunit
\gamma
along
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
such that
\gamma(0)=p
. Take
\tau_{0}:=\tau_{2}/r
we get (
2.3.25
).

Finally, for
1\leq j\leq r
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t^{j}X_{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{j-1}X_{j-1}}e^{t^{j+1}X_{j+1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{j}\circ e^{t^{j}X_{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{j-1}X_{j-1}}e^{t^{j+1}X_{j+1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=X_{j}\circ e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p).

This completes the proof of
(ii)
.
∎

Now we see that for two log-Lipschitz vector fields
X
and
Y
, if
[X,Y]=0
in the sense of distributions, then
e^{tX}\circ e^{sY}=e^{sY}\circ e^{tX}
holds locally for small
t
and
s
. This is not true for large time in general.

Example 2.3.29
.

Consider the vector fields
X(x,y)=(e^{-x}\cos y,-e^{-x}\sin y)
and
Y(x,y)=(e^{-x}\sin y,e^{-x}\cos y)
defined in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. By direct computation,
[X,Y]=0
so
X
and
Y
commute.

One can see that we have the following ODE solutions, for
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
:

\displaystyle e^{tX}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(t^{2}+2t+2),\tfrac{3}{2}\pi+\arctan(t-1)),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pte^{2X}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{7}{4}\pi);

\displaystyle e^{sY}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(s^{2}+2s+2),\pi-\arctan(s-1)),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pte^{2Y}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi);

\displaystyle e^{sY}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{7}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(s^{2}+2s+2),2\pi+\arctan(s-1)),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pte^{2Y}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{7}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{9}{4}\pi);

\displaystyle e^{tX}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi)=(\tfrac{1}{2}\ln(t^{2}+2t+2),\tfrac{1}{2}\pi-\arctan(t-1)),

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pte^{2X}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{1}{4}\pi).

Therefore
e^{2X}e^{2Y}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{5}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{9}{4}\pi)
and
e^{2Y}e^{2X}(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{3}{4}\pi)=(\ln\sqrt{2},\tfrac{1}{4}\pi)
are not equal.

A related fact is that for the holomorphic vector field
Z(z)=e^{-z}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
defined on
\mathbb{C}^{1}
, we cannot talk about its holomoprhic ODE flow globally.

2.3.3
The canonical coordinate system along vector fields

In the quantitative Frobenius theorem the canonical coordinates give an important initial normalization of the given collection of vector fields. Roughly speaking, if we have a collection of vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
on a manifold that span the tangent space at every point, then under the pullback of the canonical coordinates the size of
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
depends only on their intrinsic structures and lose at most 1 (intrinsic) derivative.

Recall the flow map defined in Definition
2.3.18
.

Definition 2.3.30
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold and let
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
be a collection of log-Lipschitz vector fields. The multi-flow
\exp_{X}
is a map
\exp_{X}:\mathcal{D}_{X}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathfrak{M}\to\mathfrak{M}
given by

\mathcal{D}_{X}:=\{(t^{1},\dots,t^{m},p)\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathfrak{M}:(1,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{m}X_{m}}\},\hskip 10.00002pt\exp_{X}(t,p):=\exp_{t\cdot X}(1,p)=e^{t\cdot X}(p).

By definition if
(t,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
then
(rt,p)\in\mathcal{D}_{X}
holds for
0\leq r\leq 1
, thus
\mathcal{D}_{X}\cap(\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\{p\})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{m}
is star-shaped convex at
0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
.

For a direct link to Proposition
2.3.27
, we have

Lemma 2.3.31
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
be log-Lipschitz vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
which are pairwise commutative. Let
U\Subset\mathfrak{M}
be a precompact open subset. There is a
\tau_{0}>0
such that
e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=\exp_{X}(t,p)
with both sides are defined for all
t\in B^{m}(0,\tau_{0})
and
p\in S
. In particular
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t\cdot X}(p)=X_{j}(e^{t\cdot X}(p))
holds for all
p\in U
,
t\in B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})
and
1\leq j\leq r
.

Proof.

Let
\tau_{0}
be the same number from the result of Proposition
2.3.27
. By (
2.3.21
) we have for
p\in U
,
t\in B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})
and
1\leq j\leq r
,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t^{j}X_{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{j-1}X_{j-1}}e^{t^{j+1}X_{j+1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle X_{j}\circ e^{t^{j}X_{j}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{j-1}X_{j-1}}e^{t^{j+1}X_{j+1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=X_{j}\circ e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p).

Thus for a fixed
p\in U
and
t\in B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})
, by chain rule,

\frac{d}{ds}e^{st^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{st^{r}X_{r}}(p)=(t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{r}X_{r})\circ(e^{st^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{st^{r}X_{r}})(p),\hskip 10.00002pts\in[0,1].

In other words
s\mapsto e^{st^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{st^{r}X_{r}}(p)
is the solution to the log-Lipschitz ODE
\dot{\gamma}(s)=(t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{r}X_{r})(s)
,
\gamma(0)=p
for
s\in[0,1]
. On the other hand, by definition
\gamma(s)=e^{s(t\cdot X)}(p)
whenever the ODE is solvable in the domain, thus taking
s=1
we get
e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(p)=e^{t\cdot X}(p)
and in particular
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}e^{t\cdot X}(p)=X_{j}(e^{t\cdot X}(p))
holds for all
t\in B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})
and
1\leq j\leq r
.
∎

Definition 2.3.32
.

Following Definition
2.3.30
, suppose
m=\dim\mathfrak{M}
. Let
p\in\mathfrak{M}
. The
canonical coordinates
associated with
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
at
p
is the map
\Phi_{p}:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
,
\Phi_{p}(t):=e^{t\cdot X}(p)
, where
\Omega
is an open set containing
0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\Omega\times\{p\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}
and that
\Phi_{p}:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
is homeomorphism onto its image.

Note that
\Phi_{p}
is a canonical coordinates then necessarily we know
X_{1}(p),\dots,X_{m}(p)
form a (real) basis of
T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
.

Remark 2.3.33
.

For most case in the thesis, a parameterization is a map of the form
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}\to\mathfrak{M}
, and a coordinate system is a map of the form
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
. In this convention we should call such
\Phi_{p}
“the canonical parameterization”. The name “canonical coordinates” follows from
[
SS18
]
.

For convenience we use the following terminology for the topological assumption on the canonical coordinates mentioned in Theorem
8
:

Definition 2.3.34
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be log-Lipschitz vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
.

Let
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, the
almost injective radius
associated with
X=(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
at
p
is the number
R\in[0,\infty]
which is maximal among the following conditions:

•

\exp_{X}(t,p)
is defined for all
t\in B^{n}(0,R)
.

•

For every
0<r\leq R
and every
q\in B_{X}(p,R)
such that
B^{n}(0,r)\times\{q\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}
and
\exp_{X}(B^{n}(0,r)\times\{q\})\subseteq B_{X}(p,R)
, we have
e^{t\cdot X}(q)\neq q
for all
t\in B^{n}(0,r)\backslash\{0\}
.

Remark 2.3.35
.

In
[
SS18
]
the second condition is stated a bit differently: for such
r
and
q
we have, either
e^{t\cdot X}(q)\neq q
for all
0<|t|<r
, or
X_{1}(q),\dots,X_{n}(q)
are linearly dependent. The linear independence assumption is unnecessary because by the singular Frobenius theorem (Theorem
2
, or
[
MM13
]
when
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are Lipschitz), if
X_{1}(p),\dots,X_{n}(p)
are already linearly independent, then
X_{1}(q),\dots,X_{n}(q)
will always be linearly independent for all
q\in B_{X}(p,\infty)
.

Suppose in addition that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
satisfy
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for some
c_{ij}^{k}\in C^{0}(\mathfrak{M})
. Then for any
R_{0}>0
there is a
0<R_{1}\leq R_{0}
depending only
R_{0}
and the upper bounded of
\sup_{i,j,k}\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{C^{0}}
, such that,

•

If
B(0,R_{0})\times\{p\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}
, then
B^{n}(R_{1})\times B_{X}(p,R_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}
(see Proposition
2.3.36

(iv)
).

•

If
\mathfrak{M}
has almost injective radius
R_{0}
, then
\Phi_{p}:B^{n}(0,R_{1})\to\mathfrak{M}
is a
C^{1}
-embedding (see Corollary
2.3.38
).

In the following discussion we assume that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are all
C^{1}
. The definability and injectivity of the canonical coordinates between different points are related by the following. Recall that the
C^{0,s}_{X}
spaces are defined in (
1.1.6
) and (
1.1.3
).

Proposition 2.3.36
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be
C^{1}
vector fields on a manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose there are
c_{ij}^{k}\in C^{0}(\mathfrak{M})
,
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
such that
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

Let
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
be a fixed point. Let
\mu_{0}>0
be such that
B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})\times\{p_{0}\}\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}
. Let
\Phi_{0}=\Phi_{p_{0}}:B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
,
\Phi_{0}(t):=e^{t\cdot X}(p_{0})
be the canonical coordinates at
p_{0}
.

Let
r_{0}:=\min(\mu_{0},1/M_{0})
where

M_{0}:=\sup_{x\in\mathfrak{M}}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n};\\
|u|=|v|=|w|=1\end{subarray}}\bigg|\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}(x)u^{i}v^{j}w_{k}\bigg|.

(2.3.29)

Then we have the following:

(i)

\Phi_{0}:B^{n}(0,r_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is a
C^{1}
-locally diffeomorphism.

(ii)

Write
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{n}]^{\top}
and
Y=\Phi_{0}^{*}X
on
B^{n}(0,r_{0})
. Then
Y
is of the form
Y=(I+A)\nabla
where
A\in C^{0}(B^{n}(0,r_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
satisfies
|A(t)|\leq M_{0}|t|
. Here for a matrix
B=(b_{i}^{j})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
we use the standard
\ell^{2}
matrix operator norm
|B|:=\sup_{u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{n};|u|=|v|=1}|\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}u^{i}v_{j}b_{i}^{j}|
.

(iii)

In particular we have
\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2))=C^{0,s}_{Y}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2))
for all
0<s<1
with equivalent norms
(\frac{2}{3})^{s}\|f\|_{C^{0,s}_{Y}}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\leq 2^{s}\|f\|_{C^{0,s}_{Y}}
for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2))
.

(iv)

For every
0<r\leq r_{0}/2
we have
B_{X}(p_{0},r)\subseteq\Phi_{0}(B^{n}(0,2r))
. In particular
B^{n}(0,r_{1})\times B_{X}(p_{0},r_{2})\subseteq\Phi_{0}(B^{n}(0,2r_{1}+2r_{2}))\subseteq\mathcal{D}_{X}
for all
r_{1},r_{2}>0
such that
r_{1}+r_{2}\leq r_{0}/2
.

(v)

Let
0<s<1
. Suppose
c_{ij}^{k}\in C^{0,s}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})
, then
A\in\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4);\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Moreover, we let

M_{1}:=\sup_{u,v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n};|u|=|v|=|w|=1}\bigg\|\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}u^{i}v^{j}w_{k}c_{ij}^{k}\bigg\|_{C^{0,s}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})},

then
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4);\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq 5(1+M_{0}+r_{0}M_{1})
.

Remark 2.3.37
.

(i)

The constant
r_{0}
is almost a “0-admissible constant”, see
[
SS18
, Definitions 4.1]
except it depends on
\eta>0
in
[
SS18
, Section 3.2]
. The constant
5(1+M_{0}+r_{0}M_{1})
is called a “
\{s\}
-admissible constant”, see
[
SS18
, Definition 3.10]
. We do not know whether we still have the similar estimates if we relax
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in C^{1}
and
(c_{ij}^{k})\in C^{0}
to
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
and
(c_{ij}^{k})\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
.

(ii)

In
(v)
we see that
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}
. While under the same assumption, in Theorem
8
we can find an different pullback
\Phi
such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{s+1}
, which gain 1 more derivative. In the proof of Theorem
8
(see Section
5.4.2
), the
\Phi
is given by taking an additional pre-composition to
\Phi_{0}
. The additional pre-composition is necessary: in Lemma
5.3.22
we see that the regularity statement
\Phi_{0}^{*}X\in\mathscr{C}^{s}
in general is the best possible.

Proof of Proposition
2.3.36
.

Define a matrix function
C:B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
as

C(t)_{i}^{j}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}t^{k}\cdot c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t)).

(2.3.30)

Thus by definition
|C(t)|\leq M_{0}|t|
in the sense of the matrix operator norm. By
[
SS18
, Lemma 9.2 and Proposition 9.1]
we know if
\tilde{A}\in C^{0}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
be satisfied

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}(r\tilde{A}(r\theta))=-\tilde{A}(r\theta)^{2}-C(r\theta)\tilde{A}(r\theta)-C(r\theta),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }|r|<\mu_{0}\text{ and }\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with }\tilde{A}(0)=0,

(2.3.31)

then the tangent map
(d\Phi_{0})_{t}((I+\tilde{A}(t))\nabla)=X(\Phi_{0}(t))
holds for all
t\in B^{n}(0,\mu_{0})
.

First we give estimate for
\tilde{A}
. Indeed rewriting (
2.3.31
) we have

\tilde{A}(t)=-\int_{0}^{1}(\tilde{A}(\rho t)^{2}+C(\rho t)\tilde{A}(\rho t)+C(\rho t))d\rho.

(2.3.32)

Denote the right hand side by
\mathcal{T}\tilde{A}(t)
. We see that if
A_{1},A_{2}\in C^{0}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
satisfy
|A_{1}(t)|,|A_{2}(t)|\leq M_{0}|t|
for
|t|\leq\mu_{0}
, then

\displaystyle|\mathcal{T}A_{i}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}(M_{0}^{2}|\rho t|^{2}+M_{0}^{2}|\rho t|^{2}+M_{0}|\rho t|)d\rho\leq\tfrac{2M_{0}^{2}}{3}|t|^{2}+\tfrac{M_{0}}{2}|t|.

\displaystyle\tfrac{|\mathcal{T}A_{1}(t)-\mathcal{T}A_{2}(t)|}{|t|}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}|A_{1}(\rho t)+A_{2}(\rho t)+C(\rho t)|\tfrac{|A_{1}(\rho t)-A_{2}(\rho t)|}{|\rho t|}\rho d\rho\leq M_{0}|t|\sup_{0\leq\rho\leq 1}\tfrac{|A_{1}(\rho t)-A_{2}(\rho t)|}{|\rho t|}.

For
0<R<\frac{1}{M_{0}}
, we define
\mathscr{X}_{R}=\{A\in C^{0}(B^{n}(0,R);\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}):|A(t)|\leq M_{0}|t|\}
with metric
d(A_{1},A_{2}):=\sup_{|t|<R}|t|^{-1}|A_{1}(t)-A_{2}(t)|
, we see that
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{X}_{R}\to\mathscr{X}_{R}
is a contraction map. Thus the solution
\tilde{A}
for (
2.3.31
) is unique in
\mathscr{X}_{R}
for all
0<R<r_{0}
, hence
\tilde{A}\in C^{0}(B^{n}(0,r_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
|\tilde{A}(t)|\leq M_{0}|t|
holds for all
t\in B^{n}(0,r_{0})
. Therefore we get
(ii)
once we obtain
(i)
.

Now
|\tilde{A}(t)|<M_{0}|t|
, thus we have the following absolute series:

|(I+\tilde{A}(t))^{-1}|=\Big|\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\tilde{A}(t)^{k}\Big|\leq\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}M_{0}^{k}|t|^{k}=\frac{1}{1-M_{0}|t|}<\infty,\hskip 10.00002ptt\in B^{n}(0,r_{0}).

Therefore
(d\Phi_{0})_{t}\nabla=(I+\tilde{A}(t))^{-1}\cdot(X\circ\Phi_{0}(t))
with both sides being collections of
n
linearly independent vector fields. Since
r_{0}\leq\frac{1}{M_{0}}
, we conclude that
A=\tilde{A}
in
B^{n}(0,r_{0})
and
\Phi_{0}:B^{n}(0,r_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is
C^{1}
-locally diffeomorphism, finishing the proof of
(i)
.

Now in we have matrix norms
|I+A(t)|\leq\frac{3}{2}
and
|(I+A(t))^{-1}|\leq 2
for
t\in B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2)
, thus the distance
|t_{1}-t_{2}|=\operatorname{dist}_{\nabla}(t_{1},t_{2})
satisfies
\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}_{Y}(t_{1},t_{2})\leq|t_{1}-t_{2}|\leq\frac{3}{2}\operatorname{dist}_{Y}(t_{1},t_{2})
for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2)
. In other words for any continuous function
f:B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2)\to\mathbb{R}
we have
(\frac{3}{2})^{s}\frac{|f(t_{1})-f(t_{2})|}{\operatorname{dist}_{Y}(t_{1},t_{2})^{s}}\leq\frac{|f(t_{1})-f(t_{2})|}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{s}}\leq 2^{s}\frac{|f(t_{1})-f(t_{2})|}{\operatorname{dist}_{Y}(t_{1},t_{2})^{s}}
for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in B^{n}(0,r_{0}/2)
. Taking supremum over
t_{1},t_{2}
we get
(iii)
.

Let
0<r<r_{0}/2
and let
q\in B_{X}(p_{0},r)
, by definition since
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p_{0},q)<r
, we can find a Lipschitz curve
\gamma:[0,r]\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\gamma(0)=p_{0}
,
\gamma(r)=q
and
\dot{\gamma}(\tau)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b^{j}(\tau)X_{j}(\gamma(t))
(
0\leq\tau\leq r
) for some
b\in L^{\infty}([0,r];\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Thus we define a correspondent curve
\zeta
in
B^{n}(0,r_{0})
as

\dot{\zeta}(\tau)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b^{j}(\tau)Y_{j}(\zeta(\tau)),\hskip 10.00002pt\zeta(0)=0,

for
0\leq\tau\leq r
in the existence domain. Since
Y=\Phi_{0}^{*}X
we have
\gamma(\tau)=\Phi_{0}\circ\zeta(\tau)
for
\tau
in the domain.

By the Maximal Existence Theorem, if
\zeta
is only defined in
[0,r^{\prime}]
for some
r^{\prime}<r
then
\limsup_{\tau\to r^{\prime}}|\zeta(\tau)|=r_{0}
.

On the other hand, since
Y=(I+A)\nabla
with
|A(t)|\leq M_{0}|t|
, we have
|\dot{\zeta}(\tau)|\leq|Y(\zeta(\tau))|\leq 1+M_{0}|\zeta(\tau)|
for all
\tau
in the domain. Therefore by Gronwall’s inequality we have
|\zeta(\tau)|\leq\frac{1}{M_{0}}(e^{M_{0}\tau}-1)\leq 2\tau
for all
\tau\in[0,r]\subset[0,r_{0}\log 2]
in the domain, in particular
\zeta(\tau)\in B^{n}(0,2r)\subset B^{n}(0,r_{0})
. We conclude that
\zeta(\tau)\in B^{n}(0,2r)
for all
\tau
in the domain, thus
\zeta:[0,r]\to B^{n}(0,2r)
is defined. Using
\gamma=\Phi_{0}\circ\zeta
we get
q=\gamma(r)\in\Phi_{0}(B^{n}(0,2r))
, finishing the proof of
B_{X}(p,r)\subseteq\Phi_{0}(B^{n}(0,2r))
for
0<r<r_{0}/2
.

For every
r_{1}>0
and every
q\in B_{X}(p_{0},r_{1})
, we have
e^{t\cdot X}(q)\in B_{X}(p_{0},r_{1}+|t|)
whenever it is defined. By
(iv)
, when
|t|<r_{2}
we have
e^{t\cdot X}(q)\in B_{X}(p_{0},r_{1}+r_{2})\subset\Phi_{0}(B^{n}(0,2r_{1}+2r_{2}))
which is in particular defined for
r_{1}+r_{2}<r_{0}/2
. This completes the proof of
(iv)
.

When
t\in B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4)
, by
(ii)
and (
2.3.30
) we have
\|A\|_{C^{0}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4))}\leq\frac{1}{4}
and
\|C\|_{C^{0}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4))}\leq\frac{1}{4}
. Thus by Lemma
2.1.17

(ii)
and (
2.3.32
) (since
A=\tilde{A}
) we have for
\mathscr{C}^{s}=\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4);\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
C^{0}=C^{0}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4);\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
,

\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\leq 2\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\|A\|_{C^{0}}+\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\|C\|_{C^{0}}+\|C\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\|A\|_{C^{0}}+\|C\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\leq\tfrac{3}{4}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}+\tfrac{5}{4}\|C\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}\leq 5\|C\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}}.

To prove
(v)
it remains to show
\|C\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4);\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq 1+M_{0}+r_{0}M_{1}
.

Let
t\in B^{n}(0,\frac{r_{0}}{4})
, since
\nabla\Phi_{0}(t)=(I+A(t))^{-1}\cdot X(\Phi_{0}(t))
, by (
2.3.3
) (since
r_{0}\leq\frac{1}{M_{0}}
) we have
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(\Phi_{0}(t_{1}),\Phi_{0}(t_{2}))\leq|t_{1}-t_{2}|\sup_{t\in B^{n}(0,r_{0}/4)}|(I+A(t))^{-1}|\leq\frac{4}{3}|t_{1}-t_{2}|
for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in B^{n}(0,\frac{r_{0}}{4})
. Therefore

\displaystyle\|C\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}(B^{n}(0,\frac{r_{0}}{4}))}=\|C\|_{C^{0}}+\sup_{|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|<r_{0}/4}\frac{\big|\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}(t_{1}^{k}c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{1}))-t_{2}^{k}c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{2}))\big)_{i,j}\big|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}+\sup_{|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|<r_{0}/4}\frac{\big|\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}(t_{1}^{k}-t_{2}^{k})c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{1}))\big)_{i,j}\big|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}+\big|\big(\sum_{k=1}^{n}t_{2}^{k}(c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{1}))-c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{2}))\big)_{i,j}\big|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}+\sup_{|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|<r_{0}/4}\frac{M_{0}|t_{1}-t_{2}|}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{s}}+\tfrac{r_{0}}{4}\sup_{|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|<r_{0}/4}\frac{\sup_{|u|,|v|,|w|=1}|\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}u^{i}v_{j}w^{k}(c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{1}))-c_{ik}^{j}(\Phi_{0}(t_{2})))|}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{s}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{4}+M_{0}(\tfrac{r_{0}}{4})^{1-s}+\tfrac{r_{0}}{4}M_{1}\sup_{|t_{1}|,|t_{2}|<r_{0}/4}\frac{\operatorname{dist}_{X}(\Phi_{0}(t_{1}),\Phi_{0}(t_{2}))^{s}}{|t_{1}-t_{2}|^{s}}\leq\tfrac{1}{4}+\tfrac{M_{0}^{s}}{4^{1-s}}+\tfrac{(4/3)^{s}}{4}r_{0}M_{1}<1+M_{0}+r_{0}M_{1}.

This completes the proof of
(v)
.
∎

As a corollary to Proposition
2.3.36
, the almost injective radius gives a lower bounds the actual injective radius, which is also almost a “0-admissible constant” (see
[
SS18
, Definitions 4.1]
) except it depends on the quantity
\eta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
in
[
SS18
, Section 3.2]
.

Corollary 2.3.38

(Injective radius)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be
C^{1}
vector fields on a
C^{2}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
that span the tangent space at every point. Let
p\in\mathfrak{M}
and let
R_{0}
be the almost injective radius at
p
(see Definition
2.3.34
).

Suppose
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for some bounded functions
(c_{ij}^{k})\subset C^{0}(\mathfrak{M})
. Let
M_{0}
be as in (
2.3.29
). Then
\Phi_{p}(t):=e^{t\cdot X}(p)
defines a
C^{1}
-regular parameterization
\Phi_{p}:B^{n}(0,\frac{1}{10}\min(R_{0},\frac{1}{M_{0}}))\to\mathfrak{M}
, i.e.
\Phi_{p}
is
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image with injective radius at least
\frac{1}{10}\min(R_{0},\frac{1}{M_{0}})
.

Proof.

Let
r_{0}:=\min(R_{0},1/M_{0})
. By Proposition
2.3.36
,
\Phi_{p}:B^{n}(0,r_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is a locally
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism.

Let
q_{1},q_{2}\in\Phi_{p}(B^{n}(0,\frac{1}{10}r_{0}))
, clearly
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q_{1})\leq\frac{1}{10}r_{0}
. By Proposition
2.3.36

(iv)
with
\mu_{0}=R_{0}
,
p_{0}=p
and
r_{2}=\frac{1}{10}r_{0}
in the assumption, we get that
\Phi_{q_{1}}(t)=e^{t\cdot X}(q_{1})
is defined for
|t|<\frac{1}{2}(r_{0}-\frac{1}{5}r_{0})=\frac{2}{5}r_{0}
and satisfies
\Phi_{q_{1}}(B^{n}(0,\frac{2}{5}r_{0}))\subseteq\Phi_{p}(B^{n}(0,r_{0}))
. Therefore, applying Proposition
2.3.36

(iv)
again with
\mu_{0}=\frac{2}{5}r_{0}
and
p_{0}=q_{1}
, we see that
B_{X}(q_{1},\frac{1}{5}r_{0})\subseteq\Phi_{q_{1}}(B^{n}(0,\frac{2}{5}r_{0}))
. And since
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(q_{1},q_{2})\leq\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q_{1})+\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q_{2})\leq\frac{1}{5}r_{0}
, we get
q_{2}\in\Phi_{q_{1}}(B^{n}(0,\frac{2}{5}r_{0}))
, i.e. there is a
|t_{1}|<\frac{2}{5}r_{0}
such that
q_{2}=e^{t_{1}\cdot X}(q_{1})
.

Now by Definition
2.3.34
with
r=\frac{2}{5}r_{0}
we see that
q_{2}=e^{t_{1}\cdot X}(q_{1})\neq q_{1}
unless
t_{1}=0
. Therefore
\Phi_{p}:B^{n}(0,\frac{1}{10}r_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is injective. On the other hand from Proposition
2.3.36

(i)
we already know
\Phi_{p}:B^{n}(0,\frac{1}{10}r_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is locally a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism, thus
\Phi_{p}
is an actual
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image.
∎

When
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
form a basis of the tangent space at every point in
\mathfrak{M}
, we can endow
\mathfrak{M}
with a Riemannian metric
g
such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
form an orthonormal frame.

Lemma 2.3.39
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a connnected
C^{1,1}
-manifold. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be log-Lipschitz vector fields that form a basis of the tangent space at every point. Let
g
be the associated metric described as above. Let
\operatorname{dist}_{g}:\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{M}\to[0,\infty)
be the correspondent distance with respect to
g
. For
p\in\mathfrak{M}
and
R\in(0,\infty]
we denote
\bar{B}_{g}(p,R)=\{q\in\mathfrak{M}:\operatorname{dist}_{g}(p,q)\leq R\}
.

Then
\operatorname{dist}_{X}=\operatorname{dist}_{g}
. Moreover following are equivalent:

(a)

\bar{B}_{g}(p,R)
is a complete metric space. In other words,
\bar{B}_{g}(p,R)
is a compact space.

(b)

For any
b=(b^{1},\dots,b^{n})\in L^{\infty}([0,R];\mathbb{R}^{n})
there exists a unique Lipschitz curve
\gamma:[0,R]\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\gamma(0)=p
and
\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b^{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma(t))
for
0\leq t\leq R
.

In particular,
\mathcal{D}_{X}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
(\mathfrak{M},g)
is a complete Riemannian manifold.

Proof.

Recall that
\operatorname{dist}_{g}(p,q)
is given by the infimum of
T>0
such that there is a piecewise
C^{1}
-curve
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\gamma(0)=p
,
\gamma(T)=q
and
|\dot{\gamma}(t)|_{g}\leq 1
for almost every
0\leq t\leq T
.

For such
\gamma
, we can write
\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a^{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma(t))
for uniquely determined functions
a^{1},\dots,a^{n}\in L^{\infty}[0,T]
. By assumption
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are orthonormal, thus
\gamma
is a subunit Lipschitz path (see Definition
2.3.24
) with respect to
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
. Taking infimum over
T
with such curves
\gamma
, we conclude that
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q)\leq\operatorname{dist}_{g}(p,q)
.

On the other hand, if
T>\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q)
, and if
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathfrak{M}
is a subunit Lipschitz path joining
p
and
q
, then
|\dot{\gamma}(t)|_{g}\leq 1
for almost every
0\leq t\leq T
. Taking mollification we see that for any
\varepsilon>0
that is a
C^{1,1}
-curve
\gamma_{\varepsilon}:[0,T+\varepsilon]\to\mathfrak{M}
joining
p
and
q
, which is also subunit as well, thus
\operatorname{dist}_{g}(p,q)\leq T+\varepsilon
. Taking infimum over all
T>\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q)
and all
\varepsilon>0
we get
\operatorname{dist}_{g}(p,q)\leq\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,q)
, finishing the proof of
\operatorname{dist}_{X}=\operatorname{dist}_{g}
.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b)
: By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 3.2]
if such
\gamma
in the assumption exists, then it must be unique.

Suppose
(b)
is not true. There is a
b\in L^{\infty}([0,R];\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|b\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
, and the ODE
\dot{\gamma}=b\cdot(X\circ\gamma)
,
\gamma(0)=p
only exists in
[0,\tau)
for some
\tau\leq R
. By the maximal ODE existence theorem, for every compact subset
K\Subset\mathfrak{M}
there is a
\delta>0
such that
\gamma((\tau-\delta,\tau))\in\mathfrak{M}\backslash K
. Take
K=\bar{B}_{g}(p,R)
, which is compact by assumption, we see that
\operatorname{dist}_{g}(p,\gamma(\tau-\frac{\delta}{2}))>R
. Thus
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,\gamma(\tau-\frac{\delta}{2}))>R
as well.
However by Definition
2.3.24
and (
2.3.15
), we see that
\operatorname{dist}_{X}(p,\gamma(t))\leq t
for all
0<t<\tau
. Taking
t=\tau-\frac{\delta}{2}<R
we get a contradiction.

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a)
: We let
\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}
be the completion of
(\mathfrak{M},\operatorname{dist}_{g})
, which is still a metric space but may not be a manifold. We denote by
\tilde{\operatorname{dist}}_{g}
the completion of
\operatorname{dist}_{g}
in
\mathfrak{M}
.
Let
R_{0}\in(0,\infty]
be the smallest number such that
\bar{B}_{g}(p,r)\subset\mathfrak{M}
is completed for all
0<r<R_{0}
. In other words the distance ball in
B_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}}(p,r)\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}
satisfies
B_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}}(p,r)\subset\mathfrak{M}
for all
0<r<R_{0}
. Clearly such
R_{0}
exists. Note that if
\bar{B}_{g}(p,r)\subset\mathfrak{M}
is completed, since
\mathfrak{M}
is locally compact, we see that there is a
\delta>0
such that
B_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}}(p,r+\delta)=B_{g}(p,r+\delta)\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
holds. Thus if
R_{0}<\infty
then the closed ball
\bar{B}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}}(p,R_{0})=\{q\in\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}:\tilde{\operatorname{dist}}_{g}(p,q)\leq R_{0}\}\not\subset\mathfrak{M}
.

Therefore to prove
(a)
it suffices to prove
R_{0}>R
.

Suppose otherwise
R_{0}\leq R
, then there is a sequence
\{q_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\bar{B}_{g}(p,R_{0})(\subset\mathfrak{M})
such that
q_{k}\to q_{\infty}
for some
q_{\infty}\in\bar{B}_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}}(p,R_{0})\backslash\mathfrak{M}
.
Since we have
\operatorname{dist}_{g}=\operatorname{dist}_{X}
, by assumption there are subunit Lipschitz curves
\gamma_{k}:[0,R+\frac{1}{k}]\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\gamma_{k}(0)=p
and
\gamma_{k}(R+\frac{1}{k})=q_{k}
for all
k
.
Now
\{\gamma_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}([0,R_{0}];\mathfrak{M})\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
are equicontinuous since they are all subunit. By the Arzelà–Ascoli theorem there is a convergence subsequence in
C^{0}([0,R];\tilde{\mathfrak{M}})
, whose limit we denote by
\gamma_{\infty}:[0,R_{0}]\to\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}
. Since
\operatorname{dist}_{g}(q_{k},\gamma_{k}(R))\to 0
and
q_{k}\to q_{\infty}
, we see that
\gamma_{\infty}(R_{0})=q_{\infty}
.

Since
\|\dot{\gamma}_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}_{g}}\leq 1
for all
k
, we know
\tilde{\operatorname{dist}}_{g}(\gamma_{\infty}(t_{1}),\gamma_{\infty}(t_{2})\leq|t_{1}-t_{2}|
for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,R_{0}]
, in particular
\gamma_{\infty}(t)\in B_{\tilde{\mathfrak{M}}}(p,t)(\subseteq\tilde{\mathfrak{M}})
for all
0<t\leq R_{0}
. By assumption of
R_{0}
, we have
\gamma_{\infty}(t)\in\mathfrak{M}
for all
0\leq t<R_{0}
, thus
\gamma_{\infty}:[0,R_{0})\to\mathfrak{M}
is subunit and we can write
\dot{\gamma}_{\infty}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}u^{j}(t)X_{j}(\gamma_{\infty}(t))
for some
u\in L^{\infty}([0,R_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
.

Let
b(t):=u(t)
for
t<R_{0}
and
b(t):=0
for
t\in[R_{0},R]
, we see that
b\in L^{\infty}([0,R_{0}];\mathbb{R}^{n})
also satisfies
\|b\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq 1
. Therefore by assumption
(b)
, we get
\gamma_{\infty}(R_{0})\in\mathfrak{M}
, contradict to the assumption
q_{\infty}=\gamma_{\infty}(R_{0})\notin\mathfrak{M}
. This completes the direction
(b)
\Rightarrow
(a)
.

\mathcal{D}_{X}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathfrak{M}
if and only if
(b)
is true for all
R>0
, if and only if
(a)
is true for all
R>0
, if and only if
(\mathfrak{M},g)
is complete.
∎

Remark 2.3.40
.

By
\operatorname{dist}_{X}=\operatorname{dist}_{g}
in Lemma
2.3.39
, we have
C^{0,\alpha}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M},g)
for
0<\alpha<1
. Here the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M},g)
is defined by replacing
\operatorname{dist}_{X}
in (
1.1.6
) with
\operatorname{dist}_{g}
, or by replacing
|x-y|
in Definition
1.2.1

(i)
with
\operatorname{dist}_{g}(x,y)
. In particular by passing to local, we obtain
C^{0,\alpha}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
.

2.3.4
The Lipschitz ODE flow and a holomorphic Frobenius theorem

In the real Frobenius theorem (Theorem
5.3
) and the holomorphic Frobenius theorem (Proposition
2.3.45
) we need ODE flows to construct the parameterizations. We need the following regularity estimates for ODE flows:

Lemma 2.3.41

(Flow regularity)
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
such that
\alpha\geq\mathrm{Lip}
. Let
X
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector field on an open set
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
. Then for any
U_{1}\Subset U_{0}
and
0<\tau_{0}<\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\cdot\|X\|_{C^{0}(U_{0})}^{-1}
, the map
\exp_{X}
is defined on
(-\tau_{0},\tau_{0})\times U_{1}
,
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}((-\tau_{0},\tau_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
and
\nabla_{x}\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-1}((-\tau_{0},\tau_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
.

Here for
\alpha=\infty
we use the standard convention
\alpha+1=\infty
.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that case where
\alpha\in\{k+\mathrm{Lip},k+1+\mathrm{LogL}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}\cup(1,\infty)
, since by Lemma
2.2.14
, for
\gamma\in(1,\infty)
, if
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1-\varepsilon,\gamma-\varepsilon}_{t,x}
for all
\varepsilon>0
then
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma+1)-,\gamma-}_{x,s}
, and the same argument holds for
\nabla_{x}\exp_{X}
.

By scaling we can assume that
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{0})
be such that
\chi\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of
\{x:\operatorname{dist}(x,U_{1})\leq\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\}
. We see that
e^{tX(\cdot,s)}|_{U_{1}}=e^{t(\chi X(\cdot,s))}|_{U_{1}}
whenever
|t|<\tau_{0}
and
s\in V_{1}
. Therefore, in
U_{1}
we can replace
X
by
\chi X
in the discussion. In other words, we can assume
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
and the
\exp_{X}
is globally defined. Clearly in this case
\exp_{X}(t,x)\equiv x
for
|x|\geq\frac{1}{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

It suffices to show that there is a
\tau>0
such that
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Indeed since
\exp_{X}(t+s,x)=\exp_{X}(t,\exp_{X}(s,x))
for all
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
, by Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
that
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
implies
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. And since
\partial_{t}\exp_{X}=X\circ\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, we get
\sup_{x}\|\exp_{X}(\cdot,x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(-T,T)}<\infty
for all
T
, which concludes
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}_{t,x,\mathrm{loc}}
. Note that
\partial_{t}\nabla_{x}(e^{tX})=(\nabla X)(e^{tX})\cdot\nabla_{x}(e^{tX})
, thus
\nabla_{x}\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{t,x,\mathrm{loc}}
and
(\nabla X)\circ\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{t,x,\mathrm{loc}}
implies
\nabla_{x}\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-1}_{t,x,\mathrm{loc}}
, which will complete the proof.

First we see that
\phi(t,x):=\exp_{X}(t,x)
is the unique fixed point
\phi=\mathcal{T}\phi
where
\mathcal{T}:C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})\to C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
is given by

\mathcal{T}\phi(t,x):=x+\int_{0}^{t}X(\phi(r,x))dr,\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 10.00002ptx\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

We have

(\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{T}\phi_{2})(t,x)=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{1}(\nabla X)(u\phi_{1}(r,x)+(1-u)\phi_{2}(r,x))\cdot(\phi_{1}(r,x)-\phi_{2}(r,x))duds.

Set
\mathcal{I}(t,x):=x
. Therefore if
\alpha\geq 1+\mathrm{Lip}
, then by Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
along with a scaling, we see that there is a
M=M(n,\alpha)>1
such that

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq M\tau\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{T}\phi_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}

\displaystyle\leq M\tau\|\nabla X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})},

for every
\tau>0
and every
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\|\phi_{1}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}},\|\phi_{2}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\leq 1
.

Take
0<\tau_{1}<(2M\|X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}})^{-1}
, we see that
\mathcal{T}
is a contraction map in
\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau_{1},\tau_{1})\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}):\|\phi-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\leq 1\}
. By uniqueness of the fixed point we get that

\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau_{1},\tau_{1})\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n}),\text{ when }\alpha\geq 1+\mathrm{Lip}.

(2.3.33)

To get one derivative back, notice that
\nabla_{x}\exp_{X}-I_{n}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is unique solution
\psi\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
to the affine linear equation
\psi=I_{n}+\mathcal{S}\psi
, where

\mathcal{S}\psi(t,x):=\int_{0}^{t}(\nabla X)(\exp_{X}(r,x))\cdot\psi(r,x)dr.

By
[
BCD11
, Proposition 3.10]
, since
X
is Lipschitz, we see that
\exp_{X}-\operatorname{id}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
. Combining with (
2.3.33
) and Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
we see that
(\nabla X)\circ\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
, when
\alpha\neq 2
. Therefore when
\alpha\neq 2
, for every
0<\tau\leq\tau_{1}
,

\|\mathcal{S}\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\tau\|(\nabla X)\circ\exp_{X}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\|\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\psi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}).

(2.3.34)

When
\alpha\neq 2
, take
0<\tau_{2}<\tau_{1}
such that
\tau_{2}<(2\|(\nabla X)\circ\exp_{X}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}})^{-1}
, we see that
\mathcal{S}
is a contraction linear map in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau_{2},\tau_{2})\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Thus
\nabla\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}((-\tau_{2},\tau_{2})\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, which implies
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}((-\tau,\tau)\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for some small
\tau>0
. Therefore
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
\alpha\neq 2
.

For the remaining case
\alpha=2
. Note that in the above discussion we have
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
\varepsilon>0
. By Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
, since
\mathscr{C}^{1}\circ\mathscr{C}^{\frac{3}{2}}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{1}
, we see that
(\nabla X)\circ\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}
holds, thus (
2.3.34
) is still valid for
\alpha=1
and
\tau>0
. By taking
0<\tau_{2}<(2\|(\nabla X)\circ\exp_{X}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}})^{-1}
, we see that
\nabla\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}((-\tau_{2},\tau_{2})\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. This concludes that
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
when
\alpha=2
and finishes the proof.
∎

In applications, we consider the multi-flow associated with commutative vector fields.

Corollary 2.3.42
.

Let
\alpha\in\{\mathrm{logl},k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}\cup[1,\infty]
and let
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
be a collection of commutative
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields on an open set
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}
. Then for any
U_{1}\Subset U_{0}
there is a
\tau_{0}>0
such that the multi-flow
\exp_{X}(t,x):=e^{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{r}X_{r}}(x)
is defined for
t\in B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})
and
x\in U_{1}
. Moreover

(i)

When
\alpha\geq\mathrm{Lip}
,
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
.

When
\alpha=\mathrm{logl}
,
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
.

When
\alpha=\mathrm{LogL}
, for every
\varepsilon>0
there is a
\tau_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\tau_{0})
such that
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-\varepsilon}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}),U_{1};U_{0})
.

When
\alpha=1
, then
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2}L^{\infty}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),U_{1};U_{0})
. In particular for the same
\tau_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\tau_{0})
we have
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{2,1-\varepsilon}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}),U_{1};U_{0})
.

(ii)

\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}\exp_{X}(t,x)=X_{j}\circ\exp_{X}(t,x)
for
j=1,\dots,r
.

Proof.

(ii)
follows from Lemma
2.3.31
.

By Lemmas
2.3.41
and
2.3.22
where we have regularity estimates of
\exp_{X_{j}}
for each
1\leq j\leq r
. Taking compositions and applying Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
we get
(i)
.
∎

The ODE flows also work for holomorphic vector fields on complex manifolds. Here for a map
f:U\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we recall the notations
\partial_{z}f=(\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial z^{n}})
and
\partial_{\bar{z}}f=(\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}^{n}})
.

Lemma 2.3.43

(Holomorphic ODE flow with parameter)
.

Let
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
be bounded open sets that are endowed with standard complex coordinate system
z=(z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
and real coordinate system
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively.

Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
, and let
Z=(Z^{1},\dots,Z^{m}):U_{0}\times V_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-map that is holomorphic in
z
-variable. Then

(i)

For any
U_{1}\times V_{1}\Subset U_{0}\times V_{0}
, let
\tau_{0}:=\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\cdot(4\|Z\|_{C^{0}(U_{0}\times V_{0})})^{-1}
. There is a unique continuous map
\Phi^{Z}:\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}\to U_{0}
such that
\Phi^{Z}
is holomorphic in
(t,z)
and satisfies

\textstyle\frac{\partial\Phi^{Z}}{\partial t}(t,z,s)=Z(\Phi^{Z}(t,z,s)),\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial\Phi^{Z}}{\partial\bar{t}}(t,z,s)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi^{Z}(0,z,s)=z,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall t\in\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2},\ z\in U_{1},\ s\in V_{1}.

(2.3.35)

In addition,
\Phi^{Z}
is holomorphic in
(t,z)
and
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

(ii)

Moreover, suppose
W:U_{0}\times V_{0}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is another such map as
Z
, such that two vector fields
\sum_{j=1}^{m}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
and
\sum_{j=1}^{m}W^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
are commutative. Let
\Phi^{Z}
and
\Phi^{W}
be as in (
2.3.35
). Then for any for any
U_{1}\times V_{1}\Subset U_{0}\times V_{0}
there are an
\tau_{0}>0
such that

\Phi^{Z}(t_{1},\Phi^{W}(t_{2},z,s),s)=\Phi^{W}(t_{2},\Phi^{Z}(t_{1},z,s),s)=\Phi^{t_{1}Z+t_{2}W}(1,z,s),s),\ \forall t_{1},t_{2}\in\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2},\ z\in U_{1},\ s\in V_{1}.

(2.3.36)

Remark 2.3.44
.

If we forget the
s
-variable, the result is indeed the well-definedness of the holomorphic ODE flow
e^{tZ}(z)
. In this way, we can write
\Phi^{Z}
as

\Phi^{Z}(t,z,s)=\exp_{Z(\cdot,s)}(t,z)=e^{tZ(\cdot,s)}(z).

(2.3.37)

And thus (
2.3.36
) is saying that
e^{t_{1}Z(\cdot,s)}e^{t_{2}W(\cdot,s)}=e^{t_{2}W(\cdot,s)}e^{t_{1}Z(\cdot,s)}=e^{t_{1}Z_{1}(\cdot,s)+t_{2}W(\cdot,s)}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.3.43
.

By Lemma
2.2.22
we see that
Z\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{0},V_{0};\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
\partial_{z}Z\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{0},V_{0};\mathbb{C}^{m})
. By shrinking
U_{0},V_{0}
and expanding
U_{1},V_{1}
, we can assume that
U_{0},V_{0},U_{1},V_{1}
are all smooth domains, and
Z,\partial_{z}Z
both have bounded norms.

Write
(x,y):=(\operatorname{Re}z,\operatorname{Im}z)
. We define real vector fields
X
and
Y
on
U_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2m}_{x,y}
with parameter on
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2m+q}_{s}
as

X(x,y,s)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\operatorname{Re}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\operatorname{Im}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}},\hskip 10.00002ptY(x,y,s)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}-\operatorname{Im}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\operatorname{Re}Z^{j}(z,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}.

(2.3.38)

By assumption,
Z
is holomorphic in
z
, so
[X,Y]=0
is commutative. Clearly for each
s\in V_{1}
,
\exp_{(X(\cdot,s),Y(\cdot,s))}:\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\to U_{0}
is defined.

Let
\tilde{\Phi}=(\tilde{\Phi}^{R},\tilde{\Phi}^{I}):=\exp_{(X,Y)}
, where
\tilde{\Phi}^{R},\tilde{\Phi}^{I}:\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
are the components of
x
and
y
-coordinates respectively. Thus, by direct computation we see that
\Phi^{Z}:=\tilde{\Phi}^{R}+i\tilde{\Phi}^{I}
solves (
2.3.35
) and is holomorphic in
(t,z)
. Conversely if
\Phi^{Z}
solves (
2.3.35
) then we must have
\exp_{(X,Y)}=(\operatorname{Re}\Phi^{Z},\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{Z})
. Therefore we have the existence and uniqueness of
\Phi^{Z}
.

To show
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{t,z}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
in the domain, by Lemma
2.2.22
it suffices to show
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{(t,z),s}
. By Definition
1.2.1

(iv)
we only need to prove the case
\beta\in(0,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
, since the domain
\tau_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{0}
does not depend on
\beta
.

By the group law
\Phi^{Z}(t_{1}+t_{2},z,s)=\Phi^{Z}(t_{1},\Phi^{Z}(t_{2},z,s),s)
and Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
, it suffices to prove
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{(t,z),s}(\tau_{0}^{\prime}\mathbb{B}^{2},U_{1};U_{0})
for some
\tau_{0}^{\prime}\in(0,\tau_{0}]
.

Let
\tau_{1}:=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(U_{1},\partial U_{0})\cdot(1+\|Z\|_{C^{0}(U_{0}\times V_{0})})^{-1}
, and let
\mathcal{I}(t,z,s)=z
. Similar to the proof of Lemma
2.3.41
we see that the map
\Phi^{Z}
is the
\phi
for the equation
\phi=\mathcal{T}\phi
, where
\mathcal{T}:\{\phi\in C^{0}(\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m}):\partial_{\bar{t},\bar{z}}\phi=0,\|\phi-\mathcal{I}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tau_{1}\}\to C^{0}(\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
is given by

\mathcal{T}\phi(t,z,s):=z+\int_{0}^{t}Z(\phi(r,z,s),s)dr=z+\int_{0}^{1}t\cdot Z(\phi(\rho t,z,s),s)d\rho.

(2.3.39)

Here
\|\phi-\mathcal{I}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tau_{1}
implies
\phi(U_{1}\times V_{1})\subset U_{0}
, thus
Z(\phi(r,z,s),s)
is defined. Since
\phi
is holomorphic in
(t,z)
, the map
(t,z)\mapsto Z(\phi(t,z,s),s)
is also holomorphic. Thus
\mathcal{T}\phi
is holomorphic in
(t,z)
.

Clearly for
t\in\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2}
,
z\in U_{1}
and
s\in V_{1}
,

(\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{T}\phi_{2})(t,z,s):=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{1}\big((\partial_{z}Z)\circ(\lambda\phi_{1}(r,z,s)+(1-\lambda)\phi_{2}(r,z,s),s)\big)\cdot(\phi_{1}(r,z,s)-\phi_{2}(r,z,s))d\lambda dr.

(2.3.40)

Since
Z,\partial_{z}Z\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{z,s}
and
\phi(\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2},B^{m}(z,\tau_{1}),s)\subseteq B^{m}(z,2\tau_{1})\subseteq U_{0}
for every
B^{m}(z,\tau_{1})\subseteq U_{1}
and
s\in V_{1}
. By Lemma
2.2.31

(i)
along with a scaling, we see that there is a
M=M(U_{1},\tau_{0},\beta,Z)>\|Z\|_{C^{0}}+\|\partial_{z}Z\|_{C^{0}}
such that

\|\phi-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})}\leq\tau_{1}\Rightarrow\|Z(\phi,\operatorname{id}_{s})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})}+\|(\partial_{z}Z)(\phi,\operatorname{id}_{s})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau_{1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{n\times n})}\leq M.

(2.3.41)

For every
\tau\in(0,\tau_{1}]
, let

\displaystyle\mathscr{X}_{\tau}

\displaystyle:=\{\phi\in C^{0}(\tau\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m}):\partial_{\bar{t},\bar{z}}\phi=0,\|\phi-\mathcal{I}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tau_{1}\};

\displaystyle\mathscr{Y}_{\tau}

\displaystyle:=\{\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m}):\partial_{\bar{t},\bar{z}}\phi=0,\|\phi-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\tau_{1}\}.

Here recall that we assume
\beta\in(0,\infty)\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
, where
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a Banach space.

So
\mathscr{X}_{\tau}\subseteq C^{0}(\tau\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
\mathscr{Y}_{\tau}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
are both complete metric space, and
\mathscr{X}_{\tau}\supseteq\mathscr{Y}_{\tau}
for all
\tau
. By (
2.3.39
), (
2.3.40
) and (
2.3.41
) we get

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{I}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tau\|Z\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tau M,\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{T}\phi_{2}\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tau M\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{C^{0}},

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathscr{X}_{\tau};

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\tau M,\hskip 10.00002pt\|\mathcal{T}\phi_{1}-\mathcal{T}\phi_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\tau M\|\phi_{1}-\phi_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}},

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\phi_{1},\phi_{2}\in\mathscr{Y}_{\tau}.

Take
\tau_{0}^{\prime}:=\tau_{1}/(2M)
, we see that
\mathcal{T}
is a contraction map in both
\mathscr{X}_{\tau_{0}^{\prime}}
and
\mathscr{Y}_{\tau_{0}^{\prime}}
. Thus by uniqueness of the fixed point
\Phi^{Z}\in C^{0}(\tau_{0}^{\prime}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
is holomorphic in
(t,z)
and satisfies
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tau_{0}^{\prime}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
. By Lemma
2.2.22
again we get
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{t,z}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(\tau_{0}^{\prime}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{m})
, in particular
\Phi^{Z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{(t,z),s}
. This proves
(i)
.

(ii)
: We can fix a
s\in V_{1}
in the discussion below, thus
Z
and
W
are holomorphic vector fields in
U_{0}
.

Let
W=(W^{1},\dots,W^{m})
be from the assumption, we define vector fields
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
to be the “real and imaginary part of
W
” by replacing
Z^{j}
with
W^{j}
in (
2.3.38
). Similarly we get
\exp_{(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}=(\operatorname{Re}\Phi^{W},\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{W})
in the domain. Now
[X,Y]=0
and
[X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}]=0
since
Z
and
W
are holormorphic in
z
. The assumption
[Z,W]=0
implies that
X,X^{\prime},Y,Y^{\prime}
are all pairwise commutative.

By Corollary
2.3.42

(ii)
we have

e^{u_{1}X}e^{v_{1}Y}e^{u_{2}X^{\prime}}e^{v_{2}Y^{\prime}}=e^{u_{2}X^{\prime}}e^{v_{2}Y^{\prime}}e^{u_{1}X}e^{v_{1}Y}=e^{u_{1}X+v_{1}Y+u_{2}X^{\prime}+v_{2}Y^{\prime}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }U_{1},\text{ for }u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ small }.

(2.3.42)

By writing out the
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}
and
\mathbb{R}^{m}_{y}
components of (
2.3.42
), we get
\Phi^{Z}(t_{1},\Phi^{W}(t_{2},z))=\Phi^{W}(t_{2},\Phi^{Z}(t_{1},z))=\Phi^{t_{1}Z+t_{2}W}(1,z)
for
z\in U_{1}
and
t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{C}
closed to
0
. By shrinking
\tau_{0}
, we get (
2.3.36
) and finish the proof.
∎

We can now prove a holomorphic Frobenius theorem with parameters.

We endow
\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard (real and complex) coordinate system
\zeta=(\zeta^{1},\dots,\zeta^{n})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively.

Proposition 2.3.45

(Holomorphic Frobenius theorem with parameter)
.

Let
\tilde{U}_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
V_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}
. Let
1\leq m\leq n
and let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
be a positive generalized index. And let
Z_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{j}^{k}(\zeta,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial\zeta^{k}}
,
j=1,\dots,m
be complex vector fields on
\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
such that

•

Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
are linearly independent and pairwise commutative at every point in
\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
.

•

b_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0};\mathbb{C}^{n})
are holomorphic in
\zeta
-variable.

Then for any
(\zeta_{0},s_{0})\in\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
there is a neighborhood
\tilde{U}\times V\subseteq\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
of
(\zeta_{0},s_{0})
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-map
\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}:\tilde{U}\times V\to\mathbb{C}^{n-m}
, such that

(i)

\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta_{0},s_{0})=0
.

(ii)

\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\tilde{U},V;\mathbb{C}^{n-m})
is holomorphic in
\zeta
-variable. In particular
\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{\zeta,s,\mathrm{loc}}
and
\nabla_{\zeta}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{\zeta,s,\mathrm{loc}}
.

(iii)

For each
s\in V
, the contangent subbundle
\operatorname{Span}(Z_{1}(\cdot,s),\dots,Z_{m}(\cdot,s))^{\bot}|_{\tilde{U}}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}\tilde{U}
has a holomorphic local basis
(d\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime 1}(\cdot,s),\dots,d\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime n-m}(\cdot,s),d\bar{\zeta}^{1},\dots,d\bar{\zeta}^{n})
.

Let
U:=\tilde{U}\cap\mathbb{R}^{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the open set in the real space endowed with the real coordinates
(\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{n})
where
\xi=\operatorname{Re}\zeta
. Let
X_{j}:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}b_{j}^{k}(\xi+i0,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial\xi^{k}}
for
1\leq j\leq m
be the “real domain restriction” of
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
. Then

(iv)

For each
s\in V
, the cotangent subbundle
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(\cdot,s),\dots,X_{m}(\cdot,s))^{\bot}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}U
has a real-analytic local basis
\big(d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime 1}(\cdot,s)|_{U}),\dots,d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime n-m}(\cdot,s)|_{U})\big)
.

Remark 2.3.46
.

When
\beta>\mathrm{Lip}
, the result
(iv)
is the same as to say that the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-subbundle
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}(U\times V)
has a local basis
(d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime 1}|_{U\times V}),\dots,d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime n-m}|_{U\times V}),ds^{1},\dots,ds^{q})
. Similar result holds for
(iii)
.

We state
(iii)
and
(iv)
in this way because the pointwise span of
dG^{\prime\prime}
or
d(G^{\prime\prime}|_{U\times V})
does not make sense when
\beta\leq 1
. See also Convention
4.1.22
and Remark
4.1.23
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3.45
.

Endow two complex spaces
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathbb{C}^{n-m}
with standard complex coordinate
z=(z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
and
w=(w^{1},\dots,w^{n-m})
.

Since
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
are linearly independent, the span of
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
is a rank
m
complex subbundle on
\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
. Hence we can find an affine complex linear map
\Gamma:\mathbb{C}^{n-m}_{w}\to\mathbb{C}^{n}_{\zeta}
such that
\Gamma(0)=\zeta_{0}
and

\partial_{w^{1}}\Gamma,\dots,\partial_{w^{n-m}}\Gamma,Z_{1}|_{(\zeta_{0},s_{0})},\dots,Z_{m}|_{(\zeta_{0},s_{0})}\in\mathbb{C}T_{\zeta_{0}}\tilde{U}_{0}\text{ are $\mathbb{C}$-linearly independent}.

(2.3.43)

We define
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-maps
\Psi(z,w,s)
to
\tilde{U}_{0}
and
\widehat{\Psi}(z,w,s)
to
\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
as

\Psi(z,w,s):=e^{z\cdot Z}(\Gamma(w),s+s_{0})=\big(e^{z^{1}Z_{1}(\cdot,s+s_{0})+\dots+z^{m}Z_{m}(\cdot,s+s_{0})}(\Gamma(w))\big),\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\Psi}(z,w,s):=(\Psi(z,w,s),s+s_{0}).

Here
e^{z^{j}Z_{j}(\cdot,s)}(\zeta)=\Phi^{Z_{j}}(z_{j},\zeta,s)
is given by (
2.3.35
) and (
2.3.37
).

Clearly
\Psi(0,0,0)=(\zeta_{0},s_{0})
. By Lemma
2.3.43

(i)
and taking compositions,
\Psi
is defined in a neighborhood
\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
of
0
, such that
\Psi\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\tilde{U}_{0})
and
\Psi
is holomorphic in
(z,w)
.

Since
\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}e^{z^{j}Z_{j}}=Z_{j}\circ e^{z^{j}Z_{j}}
from (
2.3.35
), by Lemma
2.3.43

(ii)
we have

\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\widehat{\Psi}(z,w,s)=Z_{j}\circ\widehat{\Psi}(z,w,s),\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq m,\hskip 10.00002pt(z,w,s)\in\Omega.

(2.3.44)

By condition (
2.3.43
) we see that
\nabla_{z,w}\Psi(\cdot,0)
is of full rank in a neighborhood of
0
.
Therefore, by Lemma
2.2.38

(iii)
and shrinking
\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}
if necessary,
\Psi(\cdot,s):\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\to\tilde{U}_{0}
is locally biholomorphic for each
s\in\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
\widehat{\Psi}^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\tilde{U},V;\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
for some neighborhood
\tilde{U}\times V\subseteq\tilde{U}_{0}\times V_{0}
of
(\zeta_{0},s_{0})
. Thus we get the well-defined map
\widetilde{G}(\zeta,s):=\Psi(\cdot,s-s_{0})^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\zeta)
for
(\zeta,s)\in\tilde{U}\times V
with
\widetilde{G}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{\zeta,s}
.

Write
\widetilde{G}=(\widetilde{G}^{\prime},\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime},\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime\prime})
with respect to the
z
,
w
,
s
-coordinate components. We are going to show that
\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}:\tilde{U}\times V\to\mathbb{C}^{n-m}
is as desired.

Immediately
\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta_{0},s_{0})=0
because
\widehat{\Psi}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\zeta,s)=(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta,s),s-s_{0})
and
\widehat{\Psi}(0)=(\zeta_{0},s_{0})
. We have
(i)
.

Since
\widetilde{G}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{\zeta,s}(\tilde{U},V;\mathbb{C}^{n})
and
\Psi
is holomorphic in
z,w
, we know
\widetilde{G}
is holomorphic in
\zeta
. By Lemma
2.2.22
we get
\widetilde{G}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\zeta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
. In particular
\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\zeta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
is holomorphic in
\zeta
, giving
(ii)
.

To prove
(iii)
and
(iv)
we can fix a
s\in V
. Thus without loss of generality
V=\{\ast\}
is a singleton, and we can assume
Z(\zeta,s)=Z(\zeta)
and
\widetilde{G}(\zeta,s)=\widetilde{G}(\zeta)
in the following argument.

Proof of
(iii)
: Now
\widetilde{G}:\tilde{U}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a complex (holomorphic) coordinate chart, so
d\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime 1},\dots,d\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime n-m},d\bar{\zeta}^{1},\dots,d\bar{\zeta}^{n}
are linearly independent differentials on
\tilde{U}
.

By (
2.3.44
) we have
Z_{j}=\Psi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}=\widetilde{G}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}
, thus
Z_{j}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime k}=(\widetilde{G}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}})(\widetilde{G}^{*}dw^{k})=0
all vanish for
1\leq j\leq m
and
1\leq k\leq n-m
.
This finishes the proof of
(iii)
.

Proof of
(iv)
: By
(iii)
, we know the
n\times(n-m)
matrix
(\partial_{\zeta^{j}}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime k})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq n\\
1\leq k\leq n-m\end{subarray}}
has full rank
n-m
at every point in
\tilde{U}
.
By
(ii)

\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}
is holomorphic in
\zeta
, so
\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{\zeta}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}
. Therefore, the matrix-valued function
(\partial_{\xi^{j}}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime k})_{n\times(n-m)}:U\to\mathbb{C}^{n\times(n-m)}
still has rank
n-m
at every point. Since
d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime k}|_{U})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime k}}{\partial\xi^{j}}d\xi^{j}
, we conclude that
d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}|_{U})
is a collection of
(n-m)
-differentials that are linearly independent on
U
.

Since
\partial_{\xi}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}=\partial_{\zeta}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}
and
Z_{j}\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}=0
from above, we know
X_{j}(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}|_{U})=0
as well, for
1\leq j\leq m
. Therefore,
d(\widetilde{G}^{\prime\prime}|_{U})
annihilates
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
on
U
. This completes the proof of
(iv)
.
∎

2.4
Holomorphic Extension of An Inverse Laplacian

In this section, for a holomorphic function
f(z)
we use
\nabla f=\partial_{z}f=(\partial_{1}f,\dots,\partial_{n}f)
(cf. (
1.2.2
)).

We use
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n-1}
for unit sphere.
We use
\mathbb{H}^{n}:=\{x+iy\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:|x|<1,4|y|<1-|x|\}
for a fixed complex cone. We use
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
for the space of holomorphic functions in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, endowed with compact-open topology.

Our goal in this section is to prove the following:

Proposition 2.4.1

(Holormorphic property of Laplacian)
.

Let
\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\mathbb{H}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
be as above.

(i)

There is a linear operator
\mathbf{P}
acting on functions on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
\Delta\mathbf{P}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
and
\mathbf{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is bounded for all
-2<\alpha<1
.

(ii)

There is a linear operator
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
acting on functions on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, such that for every
-2<\alpha<1
:

•

\tilde{\mathbf{P}}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is bounded linear;

•

\Delta\tilde{\mathbf{P}}u=u
and
(\tilde{\mathbf{P}}u)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\mathbf{P}(u|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}})
for all
u\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
. Here
\Delta=\Delta_{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{j}}
.

(iii)

If
\Delta u=0
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, then
u
extend holomorphically into
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, call it
\mathbf{E}u\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
. Moreover the holomorphic extension operator
\mathbf{E}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{C})\cap\ker\Delta\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is bounded for all
-2<\alpha<1
.

The idea follows from Morrey
[
Mor58
]
, which only consider the cases for positive
\alpha
. However in our cases we need
\alpha
to be negative. So we include a complete proof.
By taking direct modification to our construction (see (
2.4.9
)) we can show that there exist an inverse Laplacian
\mathbf{P}
and its extension
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
such that
\mathbf{P}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is bounded for all
-M<\alpha<M
, where
M
is an arbitrarily fixed large number.

Remark 2.4.2
.

In
(ii)
since
u\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-2}_{\mathcal{O}}
is holomorphic, the statement
\Delta_{x}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}u=u
is equivalent as
\Delta_{z}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}u=u
where
\Delta_{z}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{j}\partial z^{j}}
. In this case
\Delta_{z}
is NOT an elliptic operator on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
: it is not the complex Laplacian
\square=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial z^{j}\partial\bar{z}^{j}}
.

Definition 2.4.3
.

Let
\alpha<1
. We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
the space of holomorphic function
g\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
such that

\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})}:=|g(0)|+\sup\limits_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla g(z)|<\infty.

We have in fact
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, see
[
Zhu05
, Chapter 7]
for some illustrations. For completeness we prove some partial results in Section
2.4.1
.

We construct the inverse Laplacian
\mathbf{P}
in Definition
2.4.7
and its holomorphic extension
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
in Definition
2.4.10
. We prove Proposition
2.4.1

(iii)
in Section
2.4.1
; we prove Proposition
2.4.1

(i)
in Section
2.4.2
; we show
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
preserve holomorphic functions in Section
2.4.3
and prove Proposition
2.4.1

(ii)
in Section
2.4.4
.

Recall in Notation
2.1.39
we use
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as the Newtonian potential in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

\mathbf{\Gamma}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{|x|}{2}&n=1,\\
\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|x|&n=2,\\
-\frac{|x|^{2-n}}{(2-n)|\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}&n\geq 3,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002ptx\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}.

(2.4.1)

Note that
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
is contained in
\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\operatorname{Re}(z\cdot z)>0\}=\{z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}
, which is a connected set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. So
[x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}\mapsto|x|]
has unique holomorphic extension to
\{|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}
as

z\in\big\{|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\big\}\mapsto(z\cdot z)^{\frac{1}{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with principle argument }-\tfrac{\pi}{2}<2\arg z<\tfrac{\pi}{2}.

(2.4.2)

We still use
\mathbf{\Gamma}
as the holomorphic extended fundamental solution on
\{|\operatorname{Re}z|>|\operatorname{Im}z|\}
.

Therefore, for each
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
, the function
[x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-\theta)]
can be holomorphic extended to
[z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\theta)]
. Moreover, for every multindex
\nu\neq 0
, since
|\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma}(x)|\lesssim_{\nu}|x|^{2-n-|\nu|}
, we have the following:

\displaystyle|(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-t)|\lesssim_{\nu}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{2-n-|\nu|}

\displaystyle,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall t\in\mathbb{R}^{n},z\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\text{ satisfying }|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 2|\operatorname{Im}z|.

(2.4.3)

2.4.1
Holomorphic Hölder spaces and extension of harmonic functions

For holomorphic functions in a complex domain whose Hölder norms are finite, we can use the blow-up speed of its derivative near the boundary.

Lemma 2.4.4

(Hardy-Littlewood type characterizations)
.

(i)

Let
\alpha<1
. The differentiation
[f\mapsto\nabla f]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C}^{n})
is bounded linear.

(ii)

Let
\alpha<0
. Then
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
has equivalent norm

f\mapsto\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|
.

(iii)

The restriction map
[f\mapsto f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{C})
and the inclusion map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
are both bounded linear for
0<\alpha<1
.

(iv)

Let
\alpha<1
. If
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
satisfies
\Delta g=0
, then
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb{S}^{n-1})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla g(x)|<\infty
. Moreover there is a
C=C(n,\alpha)>0
such that
