\sup_{x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathbb{S}^{n-1})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla g(x)|\leq C_{3}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\ker\Delta.

Proof.

(i)
: By assumption
|\partial_{j}f(z)|\leq\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha-1}
. By Cauchy’s integral formula,

\partial_{j}g(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi r}\int_{0}^{2\pi}e^{-i\theta}g(z+re^{i\theta}\mathbf{e}_{j})d\theta,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq n,\hskip 10.00002pt0<r<\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}),\hskip 10.00002ptg\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n}).

(2.4.4)

Taking
r=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
g=\partial_{k}f
, we get
|\partial_{jk}f(z)|\leq\frac{1}{r}\sup_{|w-z|=r}|\partial_{k}f(w)|\leq 2^{2-\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha-2}
, for
1\leq j,k\leq n
, which means
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{2-\alpha}|\nabla^{2}f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}
. This proves
(i)
.

(ii)
: Now
\alpha<0
. Clearly
|f(0)|\leq\operatorname{dist}(0,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}\cdot\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|
. If
\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|<\infty
, then by (
2.4.4
) with
g=f
and
r=\frac{1}{2}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
we get
|\nabla f(z)|\leq\frac{1}{r}\sup_{|w-z|=r}|f(w)|\lesssim\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}
. So
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\lesssim\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|
.

Conversely since
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is a convex set, we have
f(z)=f(0)+\int_{0}^{1}z\cdot\nabla f(tz)dt
for all
f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Note that
\operatorname{dist}(tz,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\geq t\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})+(1-t)\operatorname{dist}(0,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
for all
0\leq t\leq 1
. Therefore for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
,

\displaystyle|f(z)|

\displaystyle\leq|f(0)|+|z|\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{1}\big(t\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})+(1-t)\operatorname{dist}(0,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\big)^{\alpha-1}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}+\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{1-\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})}(1-t)^{\alpha-1}dt\lesssim_{\alpha}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}.

So
\sup_{z}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{-\alpha}|f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}
, completing the proof of
(ii)
.

(iii)
: Now
0<\alpha<1
. It suffices to show
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
. The boundedness of the inclusion and restriction would then follow from the natural maps
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\hookrightarrow L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
respectively.

Now for
z_{0},z_{1}\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, what we need is to show that
|f(z_{0})-f(z_{1})|\lesssim_{n,\alpha}|z_{0}-z_{1}|^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})}
.

Since
\mathbb{H}^{n}
is convex, by its geometry we can find a point
w\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
(depending on
z_{0},z_{1}
) such that
|w-z_{j}|\leq|z_{0}-z_{1}|
and
\operatorname{dist}(tw+(1-t)z_{j},\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\geq\frac{1}{100}t|z_{0}-z_{1}|
for
j=0,1
and
0\leq t\leq 1
. Therefore

\displaystyle|f(z_{0})-f(z_{1})|

\displaystyle\leq|f(z_{0})-f(w)|+|f(z_{1})-f(w)|\leq\sum_{j=0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}|z_{j}-w||\nabla f(tw+(1-t)z_{j})|dt

\displaystyle\lesssim|z_{0}-z_{1}|\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{1}(|z_{0}-z_{1}|t)^{\alpha-1}dt\lesssim|z_{0}-z_{1}|^{\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}.

Thus
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
and we finish the proof of
(iii)
.

(iv)
: Let
\rho\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be a radial function such that
\int\rho=1
. Let
\rho_{j}(x):=2^{jn}\rho(2^{j}x)
for
j\geq 0
. Therefore, for a harmonic function
g
in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, by the ball average formula we have
g(x)=\rho_{k}\ast g(x)
whenever
1-|x|>2^{-k}
.

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution (see Definition
2.1.2
) for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Since
\nabla\rho
is Schwartz for every
M>0
we have
\|\phi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{\phi,\rho,M}2^{-Mj}
for
j\geq 0
, so by scaling, we get
\|\phi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=2^{k}\|\phi_{j-k}\ast\nabla\rho\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{\phi,\rho,M}2^{k-M(j-k)}
if
j\geq k
. Therefore
\|\phi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{\phi,\rho,M}2^{k}\min(1,2^{-M(j-k)})
for all
j,k\geq 0
.

Let
\psi_{j}:=\sum_{k=j-1}^{j+1}\phi_{j}
for
j\geq 0
(we use
\phi_{-1}:=0
). By (
2.1.2
) we have
\psi_{j}\ast\phi_{j}=\phi_{j}
. Therefore

\|\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\lesssim_{M}2^{k}\min(1,2^{-M(j-k)}),\hskip 10.00002ptj,k\geq 0.

Now for a harmonic function
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, consider an extension
\tilde{g}:=E_{x}g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
where
E_{x}
is in Lemma
2.2.8
. By Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
(if
0<\alpha<1
) and Definition
2.1.6
(if
\alpha\leq 0
) we have
\|\phi_{k}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\phi,E_{x}}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}2^{-k\alpha}
.
Thus, for any
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, take
k\geq 0
to be the unique integer such that
1-2^{1-k}\leq|x|<1-2^{-k}
, we get

\displaystyle|\nabla g(x)|=|\nabla\rho_{k}\ast g(x)|\leq\|\nabla\rho_{k}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\phi_{j}\ast\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\ast\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|\psi_{j}\ast\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|\phi_{j}\ast\tilde{g}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\phi}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{k}\min(1,2^{-(|\alpha|+1)(j-k)})2^{-k\alpha}\|\tilde{g}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}2^{k(1-\alpha)}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

We conclude that
|\nabla g(x)|\lesssim(1-|x|)^{\alpha-1}\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
for
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, finishing the proof of
(iv)
.
∎

We can now prove Proposition
2.4.1

(iii)
. Recall that for a real analytic function
f
, we use
\mathbf{E}f
to be the (unique) holomorphic extension to the complex domain where it is defined.

Proof of Proposition
2.4.1

(iii)
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
be such that
\Delta f=0
. By Lemma
2.4.4

(iv)

f\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, and by Poisson’s formula (See
[
Eva10
, Chapter 2.2 (45)]
for example)

f(x)=\frac{r^{2}-|x-x_{0}|^{2}}{|r\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}\int_{\partial B(x_{0},r)}\frac{f(\theta)d\sigma(\theta)}{|x-\theta|^{n}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }x_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{n},\hskip 10.00002pt0<r<1-|x_{0}|,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in B^{n}(x_{0},r).

Here
d\sigma
is the standard spherical measure.

Using (
2.4.2
) we have holomorphic extension,

\mathbf{E}f(z)=\frac{r^{2}-(z-x_{0})^{2}}{|r\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}\int_{\partial B(x_{0},r)}\frac{f(\theta)d\sigma(\theta)}{((z-\theta)^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}},\hskip 10.00002ptx_{0}\in\mathbb{B}^{n},\hskip 10.00002ptr<1-|x_{0}|,\hskip 10.00002pt|\operatorname{Im}z|<r-|\operatorname{Re}z-x_{0}|.

For
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, take
x_{0}=\operatorname{Re}z
and
r=\frac{1-|x_{0}|}{2}
, we can write

\mathbf{E}f(x+iy)=\frac{(\frac{1-|x|}{2})^{2}+y^{2}}{|\frac{1-|x|}{2}\cdot\mathbb{S}^{n-1}|}\int_{\frac{1-|x|}{2}\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{f(x+\theta)d\sigma(\theta)}{((iy-\theta)^{2})^{\frac{n}{2}}},\hskip 10.00002ptx+iy\in\mathbb{H}^{n}.

(2.4.5)

Since
|y|<\frac{1}{4}(1-|x|)=\frac{1}{2}|\theta|
for
\theta\in\frac{1-|x|}{2}\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, we have
\operatorname{Re}((iy-\theta)^{2})\approx|\theta|^{2}\approx(1-|x|)^{2}
.

Note that
\nabla f
is also harmonic, which gives
\nabla_{z}\mathbf{E}f=\mathbf{E}(\nabla_{x}f)
. Therefore by Lemma
2.4.4

(iv)
,

|\nabla\mathbf{E}f(x+iy)|\lesssim\frac{(1-|x|)^{2}}{1-|x|}\int_{\frac{1-|x|}{2}\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\frac{|\nabla f(x+\theta)|d\sigma(\theta)}{(1-|x|)^{n}}\lesssim\sup\limits_{\partial B^{n}(x,\frac{1-|x|}{2})}|\nabla f|\lesssim(1-|x|)^{\alpha-1}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

Since
\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\leq\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\approx 1-|x|
, we get
\sup_{z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{1-\alpha}|\nabla\mathbf{E}f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
.

Clearly
|\mathbf{E}f(0)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, so by definition of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}
-spaces we get
\|\mathbf{E}f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, finishing the proof.
∎

2.4.2
Construction and Hölder regularity for real inverse Laplacian
\mathbf{P}

We are about to define
\mathbf{P}f=(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
where
E
is a concrete extension operator for
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
E:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear for
-4<\alpha<1
.

Lemma 2.4.5

(
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.9.2]
)
.

Let
M\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, let
(a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{M}\subset\mathbb{R}
satisfy
b_{j}>0
,
1\leq j\leq M
and
\sum_{j=1}^{M}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=1
for
-4\leq k\leq 4
. Let
(\tilde{\chi}_{j})_{j=1}^{M}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies
\chi_{j}(t)\equiv 1
for
t
closed to 0 and each
1\leq j\leq M
. Define

E^{H}f(x^{\prime},x_{n}):=\begin{cases}f(x^{\prime},x_{n})&x_{n}>0,\\
\sum_{j=1}^{M}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(x_{n})a_{j}\cdot f(x^{\prime},-b_{j}x_{n})&x_{n}<0.\end{cases}

(2.4.6)

Then
E^{H}
defines an extension operator on the half plane
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}=\{x_{n}>0\}
such that
E^{H}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
for all
-4<\alpha<1
.

Corollary 2.4.6
.

Let
(a_{j},b_{j})_{j=1}^{9}\subset\mathbb{R}
satisfy
b_{j}>0
,
1\leq j\leq 9
and
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{-k}=1
for
-4\leq k\leq 4
. For
1\leq j\leq 9
, let
\tilde{\chi}_{j}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\tilde{\chi}_{j}\equiv 1
in an neighborhood of
0
.
Then

Ef(e^{-\rho}\theta):=\begin{cases}f(e^{-\rho}\theta)&\rho>0,\\
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(\rho)f(e^{\rho/b_{j}}\theta)&\rho<0,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\rho\in\mathbb{R},\hskip 10.00002pt\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1},

(2.4.7)

defines an extension operator on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
E:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is bounded linear for
-4<\alpha<1
.

Moreover its formal adjoint
E^{*}
is given by

E^{*}g(x)=g(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|x|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(b_{j}\log|x|)g\Big(\frac{x}{|x|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big),\hskip 10.00002ptx\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(2.4.8)

Proof.

Clearly
(\rho,\theta)\mapsto e^{-\rho}\theta
is a diffeomorphism from
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
to
\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
and maps
\{\rho>0\}
onto
\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\{0\}
. By
[
Tri10
, Theorem 2.10.2(i)]
we see that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\alpha}
is preserved by bounded diffeomorphism. Therefore, by passing to coordinate covers of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
if necessary (since we only need to worry about the place where
\rho
is closed to
0\in\mathbb{R}
), by Lemma
2.4.5
with
b_{j}
replacing by
\frac{1}{b_{j}}
, we know (
2.4.7
) is an extension operator for
\mathbb{B}^{n}
and has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-boundedness for
-4<\alpha<1
.

To get (
2.4.8
), let
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}Ef(y)g(y)dt=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}d\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}}Ef(e^{-\rho}\theta)g(e^{-\rho}\theta)e^{-n\rho}d\rho

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}f(e^{-\rho}\theta)g(e^{-\rho}\theta)e^{-n\rho}d\rho+\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(\rho)\int_{\mathbb{R}_{-}}f(e^{\rho/b_{j}}\theta)g(e^{-\rho}\theta)e^{-n\rho}d\rho\bigg)d\theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}d\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}f(e^{-r}\theta)g(e^{-r}\theta)e^{-nr}dr+\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}b_{j}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}d\theta\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\tilde{\chi}_{j}(b_{j}r)f(e^{-r}\theta)g(e^{b_{j}r}\theta)e^{nr(b_{j}+1)}e^{-nr}dr

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(x)\bigg(g(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\cdot\tilde{\chi}_{j}(b_{j}\log|x|)}{|x|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}g\Big(\frac{x}{|x|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dx.

This gives (
2.4.8
) and finishes the proof.
∎

We now give an concrete formula of
\mathbf{P}
based on (
2.4.8
),

Definition 2.4.7
.

We define
\mathbf{P}
to be

\mathbf{P}f(x):=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(t)\bigg(\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-t)+\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\Big(x-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dt.

(2.4.9)

Here
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{3},\frac{5}{3})
satisfies
\chi|_{[\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}]}\equiv 1
, and
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R},b_{j}>0
satisfy
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=1
for
-4\leq k\leq 4
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4.1

(i)
.

By (
2.4.8
) we see that
\mathbf{P}f
is of the form
\mathbf{P}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(t)E^{*}(\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-\cdot))(t)dt
for
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, where
E^{*}
is the adjoint of some extension operator
E
which is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-bounded for
-4<\alpha<1
. Therefore
\mathbf{P}f(x)=(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and we get

\mathbf{P}f=(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
. In particular
\Delta\mathbf{P}f=(\Delta\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=f
holds.

By construction, such
E
has compact support, namely there is a bounded open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
(in fact we can take
U=B^{n}\big(0,e^{\max_{j}\frac{1}{3b_{j}}}\big)
) such that
\operatorname{supp}Ef\subset U
has compact support for every
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. Therefore by Lemma
2.1.40
,
\mathbf{P}=[f\mapsto(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast Ef)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is bounded linear for all
-4<\alpha<1
, in particular for
-4<\alpha<-1
.
∎

To prove Proposition
2.4.1

(ii)
, we need to decompose the integral in (
2.4.9
) into the domains
\{t:|\operatorname{Re}z-t|<\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}\}
and
\{t:|\operatorname{Re}z-t|>\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}\}
. We need the following estimate.

Lemma 2.4.8
.

Let
a,b,\chi
be given in Definition
2.4.7
. There is a
C=C(n,a,b,\chi)>0
, such that for every
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
and
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
satisfying
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}
, and for every multi-index
\nu
such that
|\nu|\leq 1
,

\bigg|(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-t)+\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg|\leq C|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-2-|\nu|}(1-|t|)^{4}.

(2.4.10)

Proof.

That
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}
implies
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 2|\operatorname{Im}z|
, so by (
2.4.3
) the left hand side of (
2.4.10
) is finite.

When
1-|t|\geq\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
, we have
|\operatorname{Re}z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}|\geq\frac{1}{b_{j}}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
. Thus by (
2.4.3
),

\textstyle\text{LHS of }\eqref{Eqn::SecHolLap::AprioriBddforPEqn}\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{1-n}+|\operatorname{Re}z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}|^{1-n}\lesssim_{n,b}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{1-n}\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-3-n}(1-|t|)^{4}.

We then focus on the case
1-|t|<\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
.

By assumption
\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}b_{j}(-b_{j})^{k}=-1
for
0\leq k\leq 3
. Therefore, by the Taylor’s expansion of degree 3, for any
\tilde{\chi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
that equals 1 in the neighborhood of 0, there is a
C^{\prime}=C_{a,b,\tilde{\chi}}^{\prime}>0
such that

\textstyle\big|\psi(\rho)+\tilde{\chi}(\rho)\sum_{j=1}^{9}a_{j}b_{j}\psi(-b_{j}\rho)\big|\leq C^{\prime}\|\psi\|_{C^{4}}\cdot\rho^{4},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ R>0,\ \psi\in C^{4}[-R\max_{1\leq j\leq 9}b_{j},R],\ \rho\in[0,R].

(2.4.11)

For fixed
t
and
z
, we define
I_{t}:=\big[\log|t|\max\limits_{1\leq j\leq 9}b_{j},-\log|t|\big]
and
\psi_{z,t}^{\nu}(\rho):=e^{-n\rho}\cdot(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-e^{-\rho}\frac{t}{|t|})
for
\rho\in I_{t}
. Since for
\rho\in I_{t}
we have
|\operatorname{Re}z-e^{-\rho}\frac{t}{|t|}|\geq|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
and by assumption
|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 2|\operatorname{Im}z|
holds in this case, using (
2.4.3
) we have
\|\psi_{z,t}^{\nu}\|_{C^{4}(I_{t})}\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-2-|\nu|}
.

Thus taking
\tilde{\chi}(\rho):=\chi(e^{-\rho})
and
\rho=-\log|t|(=R)
in (
2.4.11
) with
\psi=\psi_{z,t}^{\nu}
we get

\bigg||t|^{n}(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})(z-t)+\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{nb_{j}}}(\partial^{\nu}\mathbf{\Gamma})\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg|\lesssim|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-2-|\nu|}(1-|t|)^{4}.

Note that
1-|t|<\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
implies
\frac{1}{3}<|t|<1
, so by multiplying
\frac{1}{|t|^{n}}
we get (
2.4.10
) for the case
1-|t|<\frac{1}{2}|\operatorname{Re}z-t|
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Corollary 2.4.9
.

If
(1-|t|)^{4}f(t)\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, then the following integral converges locally uniformly for
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
:

\int_{\{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2}\}}f(t)\Big(\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-t)-\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\big)\Big)dt.

(2.4.12)

Proof.

By Lemma
2.4.8

\left|\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-t)-\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\big)\right|\leq C|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-2}(1-|t|)^{4}
holds for
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\cap B^{n}(\operatorname{Re}z,\frac{1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}{2})
, where
C=C_{n,a,b,\chi}
. Therefore (
2.4.12
) is bounded by

\|t\mapsto(1-|t|)^{4}f(t)\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\sup_{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:2|\operatorname{Re}z-t|\geq 1-|\operatorname{Re}z|}C|\operatorname{Re}z-t|^{-n-2}\leq C(1-|\operatorname{Re}z|)^{-n-2}.

The term
(1-|\operatorname{Re}z|)^{-n-2}
is locally bounded for
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, thus we complete the proof.
∎

2.4.3
Construction of the holomorphic extension
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}

We now extend
\mathbf{P}
to the complex cone
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

In Sections
2.4.3
and
2.4.4
, we use a function
1
1

1

In
[
Mor58
]
he use
1-\lambda
of our
\lambda
.

\lambda:\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{B}^{n}\to[0,1]
as the following

\lambda(t,x):=\begin{cases}1-\frac{2|t-x|}{1-|x|},&\text{if }2|t-x|\leq 1-|x|\\
0,&\text{if }2|t-x|\geq 1-|x|\end{cases}.

(2.4.13)

Immediately we have:

(
\Lambda
.1)

\lambda
is locally Lipschitz, and the function
[(t,x+iy)\mapsto\lambda(t,x)\cdot y]:\mathbb{B}^{n}\times\mathbb{H}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is bounded Lipschitz.

(
\Lambda
.2)

For every
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
x+iy\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, we have
4|\lambda(t,x)\cdot y|<1-|x|
and
|(1-\lambda(t,x))y|\leq\frac{1}{2}|t-x|
.

For each
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, let

\textstyle S_{z}:=\{t+i\lambda(t,x)y:t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}\},\hskip 10.00002ptV(t,z):=1+iy\cdot\partial_{t}\lambda(t,x)=1+i\sum_{j=1}^{n}y^{j}(\partial_{t^{j}}\lambda)(t,x).

(2.4.14)

By
(

Λ

.2)
we see that
S_{z}\subset\mathbb{H}^{n}
, and
z-\zeta\in\{w\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:|\operatorname{Re}w|>|\operatorname{Im}w|\}
for each
\zeta\in S_{z}
, so
\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\zeta)
is defined for
\zeta\in S_{z}
.

Fix
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
, by viewing
S_{z}
as a set parameterized by
t
, we have a change of variable
\zeta=\zeta(t,z):=t+i\lambda(t,x)y
. Its Jacobian matrix is
\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial t}(t,z)=1+iy\otimes\partial_{t}\lambda(t,x)
, so
\det\frac{\partial\zeta}{\partial t}(t,z)=1+iy\cdot\partial_{t}\lambda(t,x)
. We denote the “volume form”
V(t,z)
as

V(t,x+iy):=1+iy\cdot\partial_{t}\lambda(t,x),\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{B}^{n},\hskip 10.00002ptx+iy\in\mathbb{H}^{n}.

In this way for an integrable function
g
on
S_{z}
, we can formally write

\textstyle\int_{S_{z}}g(\zeta)d\zeta=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}g(t+i\lambda(t,x)y)(1+iy\cdot\partial_{t}\lambda(t,x))dt.

Definition 2.4.10
.

Let
(a_{j})_{j=1}^{9},(b_{j})_{j=1}^{9},\chi
to be as in Definition
2.4.7
. Let
S_{z}
be as in (
2.4.14
) for
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Define

\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(z):=\int_{S_{z}}f(\zeta)\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\zeta)d\zeta+\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\chi(|t|)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}f(t)\mathbf{\Gamma}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)dt,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n}),

(2.4.15)

in the sense that,

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(x+iy)=

\displaystyle\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t+i\lambda(t,x)y)\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-t+i(1-\lambda(t,x))y)V(t,z)dt

(2.4.16)

\displaystyle+\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\chi(|t|)}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}f(t)\mathbf{\Gamma}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)dt

(2.4.17)

\displaystyle+\int_{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|t-x|>\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t)\bigg(\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-t)-\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\chi(|t|)}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dt

(2.4.18)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}f(z)+\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}f(z)+\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{3}f(z)

(2.4.19)

By
(

Λ

.2)
, we see that (
2.4.16
) is a classical Lebesgue integral pointwisely for
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
and all
f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
. The same holds for (
2.4.17
).

When
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, by Lemma
2.4.4

(ii)
we have
[t\mapsto(1-|t|)^{4}f(t)]\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{C})
. By Corollary
2.4.9
, (
2.4.18
) is integrable as well.

Note that
S_{x+i0}\equiv\mathbb{B}^{n}
. So comparing the expressions (
2.4.9
) and (
2.4.15
) we see that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(x+i0)=\mathbf{P}(f(\cdot+i0))(x)
. Therefore to prove that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
is the holomorphic extension of
\mathbf{P}
, what we need is the following:

Proposition 2.4.11
.

Let
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, then
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f
gives a holomorphic function defined on
\mathbb{H}^{n}
.

Moreover we have
(\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\mathbf{P}[f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]
and
\Delta\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f=f
, where
\Delta=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{(\partial z^{j})^{2}}
.

We postpone its proof after Proposition
2.4.12
. Essentially we can assume
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
so that the two integrals in (
2.4.15
) converge individually:

Lemma 2.4.12
.

For
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, the function
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}\mapsto\int_{S_{z}}f(\zeta)\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\zeta)d\zeta
is holomorphic. Moreover

\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\int_{S_{z}}f(\zeta)\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\zeta)d\zeta=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f(t+i\lambda(t,x)y)\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}(x-t+iy(1-\lambda(t,x)))V(t,z)dt,\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq n,\hskip 10.00002ptz\in\mathbb{H}^{n}.

(2.4.20)

Here we use the comma notation
\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}(\zeta)=(\partial_{z^{j}}\mathbf{\Gamma})(\zeta)
for
\zeta
in the domain.

Proof.

Using the comma notation we can write
(\partial_{z^{j}}f)(\zeta)=f_{,x^{j}}(\zeta)=-if_{,y^{j}}(\zeta)=f_{,j}(\zeta)
.

In the following computations, we use abbreviations
f=f(t+i\lambda(t,x)y)
,
\mathbf{\Gamma}=\mathbf{\Gamma}(x-t+i(1-\lambda(t,x))y)
and
V=V(t,z)=1+iy\cdot\lambda_{,t}(t,x)
. First we claim to have the following (cf.
[
Mor58
, (4.12)]
):

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(y^{k}(f_{,k}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k})V\nabla\lambda+y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}(\nabla\lambda)_{,t^{k}}).

(2.4.21)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}(\lambda f\mathbf{\Gamma}V)-i\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}\lambda\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma})=f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,t^{j}}+\lambda(f_{,j}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j})V\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for }j=1,\dots,n.

(2.4.22)

Here
\nabla\lambda=(\lambda_{,x^{1}},\dots,\lambda_{,x^{n}},\lambda_{,t^{1}},\dots,\lambda_{,t^{n}})
.

Indeed by the chain rule, (
2.4.21
) is done by the following,

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}f_{,k}\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda+\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}iy^{k}f_{,l}y^{l}\lambda_{,t^{k}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda-\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}\nabla\lambda+\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}iy^{k}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,l}y^{l}\lambda_{,t^{k}}\nabla\lambda+\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda_{,t^{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{k,l=1}^{n}y^{k}(1+iy^{l}\lambda_{,t^{l}})(f_{,k}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k})\nabla\lambda+\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda_{,t^{k}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}(f_{,k}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k})V\nabla\lambda+\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\nabla\lambda_{,t^{k}}.

Since
\partial_{t}\lambda
is the component of
\nabla\lambda
, using (
2.4.21
) we get (
2.4.22
) by the following

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}(\lambda f\mathbf{\Gamma}V)-i\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}\lambda\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}V+\lambda(f_{,j}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j})V+i\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}\lambda\lambda_{,t^{j}}(f_{,k}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k})V+i\lambda f\mathbf{\Gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}\lambda_{,t^{j}t^{k}}

\displaystyle\textstyle-i\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}\lambda_{,t^{k}}\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}-i\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,t^{j}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(1+i\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}\lambda_{,t^{k}}\big)+\lambda(f_{,j}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j})V-i\sum_{k=1}^{n}y^{k}\lambda_{,t^{k}}\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,t^{j}}+\lambda(f_{,j}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j})V.

Next we compute
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vdt
and
\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vdt
. Note that
\lambda=0
and
\nabla\lambda=0
near
t\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, so both (
2.4.21
) and (
2.4.22
) have integrals zero on
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vdt=

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}V+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\big(if_{,k}y^{k}\lambda_{,x^{j}}\mathbf{\Gamma}V-if\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}y^{k}\lambda_{,x^{j}}V+if\mathbf{\Gamma}y^{k}\lambda_{,t^{k}x^{k}}\big)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt+i\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\big(y^{k}(f_{,k}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k})V\lambda_{,x^{j}}+y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,x^{j}t^{k}}\big)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt+i\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,x^{j}})dt

\displaystyle(\text{by }\eqref{Eqn::SecHolLap::PfTildePHolo::Tmp1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt+i\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}y^{k}f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,x^{j}}d\sigma_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt.

\displaystyle(\nabla\lambda|_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}=0)

\displaystyle\textstyle\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vdt=

\displaystyle\textstyle i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\big(f_{,j}\lambda\mathbf{\Gamma}V+f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}(1-\lambda)V+f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,t^{j}}\big)dt

\displaystyle(f_{,j}=-if_{,y^{j}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt+i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\big(f\mathbf{\Gamma}\lambda_{,t^{j}}+\lambda(f_{,j}\mathbf{\Gamma}-f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j})V\big)dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt+i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}(\lambda f\mathbf{\Gamma}V)dt+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{k}}(y^{k}\lambda\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma})dt

\displaystyle(\text{by }\eqref{Eqn::SecHolLap::PfTildePHolo::Tmp2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt+i\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\lambda f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vd\sigma_{j}(t)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}y^{k}\lambda\lambda_{,t^{j}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}d\sigma_{k}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle i\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt.

\displaystyle(\lambda|_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}=0)

Here we use
d\sigma_{j}
as the
j
-th outer normal direction of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}=\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Therefore
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vdt=i\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}Vdt=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,j}Vdt
, finishing the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.4.11
.

When
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
, two integrals in (
2.4.15
) converge individually.

By Proposition
2.4.12
the first integral
z\mapsto\int_{S_{z}}f(\zeta)\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-\zeta)d\zeta
is holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. Clearly the second integral in (
2.4.15
) is holomorphic since
z\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-t/|t|^{b_{j}+1})
is a holomorphic in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
as well. So
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f\in\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
when
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
.

For general
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, by Lemma
2.4.4

(ii)
we have
f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n},(1-|x|)^{4}dx;\mathbb{C})
. Take
f_{\varepsilon}(z):=f((1-\varepsilon)z)
for
\varepsilon>0
, we have
f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{H}^{n};\mathbb{C})
for each
\varepsilon
, and
f_{\varepsilon}|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
converges in
L^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n},(1-|x|)^{4}dx;\mathbb{C})
. Therefore in the notation (
2.4.19
) we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{3}f_{\varepsilon}(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{3}f(z)
locally uniformly in
z
.

Since the convergence
f_{\varepsilon}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}f
holds automatically in
\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, we see that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}f_{\varepsilon}(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{1}f(z)
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}f_{\varepsilon}(z)=\tilde{\mathbf{P}}_{2}f(z)
locally uniformly in
z
as well.
Therefore
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f_{\varepsilon}(z)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(z)
locally uniformly in
z\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
. By Proposition
2.4.12

\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f_{\varepsilon}(z)
is holomorphic, we conclude that
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(z)
is also holomorphic.

For
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, by Lemma
2.4.4

(iii)

f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{C})
so
\mathbf{P}[f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]\in\mathscr{C}^{(-2)+}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{C})
is defined.

By (
2.4.14
) we have
S_{x+i0}=\mathbb{B}^{n}
for all
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. By comparing (
2.4.9
) with (
2.4.16
), (
2.4.17
) and (
2.4.18
), we get
(\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f)|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\mathbf{P}[f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]
for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
.

And since
\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
is a totally real submanifold with full dimension
n
, by uniqueness of the holomorphic extension we have
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f=\mathbf{E}\big[\mathbf{P}[f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]\big]
for all
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
. Therefore
\Delta_{z}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f=\mathbf{E}\big[\Delta_{x}\mathbf{P}[f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]\big]=\mathbf{E}[f|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}]=f
holds for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
as well.
∎

2.4.4
Hölder-Zygmund regularities for
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}

Recall
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}
in Definition
2.4.10
. In this subsection we are going to prove
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H})
for
-4<\alpha<-1
. By Proposition
2.4.11
we already know
\tilde{\mathbf{P}}:\mathscr{C}^{(-4)+}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})\to\mathcal{O}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
is the holomorphic extension of
\mathbf{P}
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4.1

(ii)
.

What we remain to do is to show that for
-4<\alpha<-1
, there is a
C=C_{n,\alpha}>0
such that

|\nabla_{z}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(z)|\leq C_{n,\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n})}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha+1},\hskip 10.00002ptf\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbb{H}^{n}).

By Proposition
2.4.12
we have for
k=1,\dots,n
and
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{H}^{n}
,

\displaystyle\partial_{z^{k}}\tilde{\mathbf{P}}f(z)=

\displaystyle\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t+i\lambda(t,x)y)\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}(x-t+i(1-\lambda(t,x))y)(1+iy\cdot\partial_{t}\lambda(t,x))dt

(2.4.23)

\displaystyle+\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\chi(|t|)}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)dt

(2.4.24)

\displaystyle+\int_{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|t-x|>\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t)\bigg(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}(z-t)-\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\chi(|t|)}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dt.

(2.4.25)

We need to show the absolute value of (
2.4.23
), (
2.4.24
) and (
2.4.25
) are all bounded by a constant times
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha+1}
.

We first estimate (
2.4.23
). By Lemma
2.4.4

(ii)
we have
|f(z)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}
; by (
2.4.3
) and (
2.4.13
) we have
|F_{,k}(z-t+i(1-\lambda)y)|\lesssim|x-t|^{1-n}
; by property
(

Λ

.1)
we have
|1+iy\cdot\partial_{t}\lambda|\lesssim 1
. Therefore

\Big|\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}Vdt\Big|\lesssim_{n,\alpha}\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}\operatorname{dist}(t+i\lambda y,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}\cdot|x-t|^{1-n}dt

(2.4.26)

Since
t+i\lambda y
lays in a line segment connecting
z
and a point in
\partial B^{n}(x,\frac{1-|x|}{2})
, by convexity of
\mathbb{H}^{n}
we get
\operatorname{dist}(t+i\lambda(t,x)y,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\geq\lambda(t,x)\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})+(1-\lambda(t,x))\operatorname{dist}(\partial B^{n}(x,\frac{1-|x|}{2}),\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})
. Since
\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\approx\frac{1-|x|}{2}-2|y|
and
\operatorname{dist}\big(\partial B^{n}(x,\frac{1-|x|}{2}),\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}\big)\approx\frac{1-|x|}{2}
, by plugging in the expression of
\lambda(t,x)
we get (recall
\alpha+1<0
)

\displaystyle\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}\operatorname{dist}(t+i\lambda y,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha}\cdot|x-t|^{1-n}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}\big(\lambda\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})+(1-\lambda)\operatorname{dist}\big(\partial B^{n}(x,\tfrac{1-|x|}{2}),\partial\mathbb{H}^{n}\big)\big)^{\alpha}\cdot|x-t|^{1-n}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{B^{n}(0,\frac{1-|x|}{2})}\Big(\big(1-\tfrac{2|s|}{1-|x|}\big)\big(\tfrac{1-|x|}{2}-2|y|\big)+\tfrac{2|s|}{1-|x|}\tfrac{1-|x|}{2}\Big)^{\alpha}|s|^{1-n}ds

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{1-|x|}{2}}\Big(\tfrac{1-|x|}{2}-2|y|+\tfrac{4|y|r}{1-|x|}\Big)^{\alpha}dr\lesssim_{\alpha}\Big(\tfrac{1-|x|}{2}-2|y|\Big)^{\alpha+1}\approx_{\alpha}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha+1}.

Putting this in (
2.4.26
) we control (
2.4.23
).

For (
2.4.24
) and (
2.4.25
), we use
\operatorname{dist}(t,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})\approx\operatorname{dist}(t,\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})=1-|t|
for
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. By Lemma
2.4.4

(ii)

|f(t)|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-|t|)^{\alpha}
.

By (
2.4.3
) we have
|\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}})|\lesssim|x-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}|^{1-n}\leq(1-|x|)^{1-n}
since
\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\notin\mathbb{B}^{n}
when
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. So we bound (
2.4.24
) by the following

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t)\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\chi(|t|)}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)dt\Big|\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{|t-x|<\frac{1-|x|}{2}}(1-|t|)^{\alpha}(1-|x|)^{1-n}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-|x|)^{\alpha+1}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha+1}.

By Lemma
2.4.8
we bound (
2.4.25
) by the following

\displaystyle\bigg|\int_{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|t-x|>\frac{1-|x|}{2}}f(t)\bigg(\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}(z-t)-\sum_{j=1}^{9}\frac{a_{j}b_{j}\chi(|t|)}{|t|^{n(b_{j}+1)}}\mathbf{\Gamma}_{,k}\Big(z-\frac{t}{|t|^{b_{j}+1}}\Big)\bigg)dt\bigg|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}:|t-x|>\frac{1-|x|}{2}}(1-|t|)^{\alpha}|x-t|^{-n-3}(1-|t|)^{4}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\bigg(\int_{|t|<\frac{3|x|-1}{2}}+\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}\frac{3|x|-1}{2}<|t|<1\\
|t-x|>\frac{1-|x|}{2}\end{subarray}}\bigg)(1-|t|)^{4+\alpha}|x-t|^{-n-3}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{0}^{\frac{3|x|-1}{2}}(1-r)^{\alpha+4}r^{n-1}dr\int_{|x|-r}^{1}\rho^{-n-3}\rho^{n-2}d\rho+\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\int_{\frac{3|x|-1}{2}}^{1}(1-r)^{\alpha+4}dr\int_{\frac{1-|x|}{2}}^{1}\rho^{-n-3}\rho^{n-2}d\rho

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\Big(\int_{0}^{|x|}(1-r)^{\alpha}dr+(1-|x|)^{\alpha+5}(1-|x|)^{-4}\Big)\approx\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}(1-|x|)^{\alpha+1}\lesssim\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathcal{O}}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H})^{\alpha+1}.

Here we use
\rho
as the radial parameter of polar coordinates on
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
centered at
\frac{x}{|x|}
.

Thus (
2.4.23
), (
2.4.24
) and (
2.4.25
) are all bounded by
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\operatorname{dist}(z,\partial\mathbb{H}^{n})^{\alpha+1}
. The proof is now complete.
∎

Chapter 3
On Singular Subbundles, Rough Involutivity and Integrability

In many literature people use “distributions” for subbundles or generalized subbundles. In the thesis we keep the word strictly referring to
generalized functions
. We still use “subbundles” for
regular tangent subbundles
.

In Sections
3.1
and
3.2
we only discuss the characterization of the regular subbundles. In Section
3.4
we give a brief discussion on singular subbundles.

Definition 3.0.1
.

A
generalized complex tangential subbundle
on a
C^{1}
manifold
\mathfrak{M}
is a subset
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
,
\mathcal{G}_{p}:=\mathcal{G}\cap\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
is a complex linear subspace of
\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}^{+}
be a positive generalized index (recall Definition
2.1.23
) and assume that
\mathfrak{M}
is at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
. We say the generalized subbundle
\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, if for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there are a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and finitely many
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
complex vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}TU)
, such that the fiber
\mathcal{G}_{q}:=\mathcal{G}\cap\mathbb{C}T_{q}\mathfrak{M}
is a linear subspace generated by
X_{1}|_{q},\dots,X_{m}|_{q}
for every
q\in U
.

We say a generalized subbundle
\mathcal{G}
is a
regular subbundle
, or just a subbundle, if its fibres have constant rank, i.e. the map
p\in\mathfrak{M}\mapsto\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{G}\cap\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M})
is constant.

We say a generalized subbundle
\mathcal{G}
is
singular
, if it is not regular.

The definition is equivalent if we remove the finiteness assumption of the vector fields. See Proposition
3.4.4
.

To discuss the involutivity of generalized subbundles, it is more convenient to consider this property on module of sections.

Definition 3.0.2
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
be a generalized index such that
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
. Let
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\alpha\geq\beta-1
and
\alpha>-\beta
. Let
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
be a submodule over the ring
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
.

We say
\mathscr{F}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-involutive, if for every vector fields
X,Y\in\mathscr{F}
, the Lie bracket
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{F}
.

One can also consider the characterization on sheaves of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
rings. But sheaves are more useful than modules only when we discuss real-analytic structures, or other function structures that have rigidity. In our discussion everything are at most smooth, and we can use partition of unity to patch the local constructions into a global one.

When
\mathcal{S}
is a regular subbundle, in Proposition
3.2.1
we see that the involutivity of the submodule
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
does not depend on the choice of
\beta
in Definition
3.0.2
. However for a general
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
submodules
\mathscr{F}
it is possible that
\mathscr{F}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-involutive but is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-involutive for some
\beta<\alpha-1
. See Examples
3.4.9
and
3.4.11
.

3.1
Distributional sections of regular subbundles

In this section we give some equivalent characterizations of sections in the sense of distributions. We focus on complex bundles, since for real bundles all properties and characterizations below work and the proof are simpler.

Using Lemmas
2.1.34
and
2.1.36
, we can define objects on manifolds via patchings, which are analogous to
[
Hör03
, Definition 6.3.3]
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
\kappa>1
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold with maximal
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-atlas
\mathfrak{A}=\{\psi:U_{\psi}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{\psi}
. Namely, each
\psi\in\mathfrak{A}
is a homeomorphism
\psi=(\psi^{1},\dots,\psi^{n}):U_{\psi}\xrightarrow{\sim}\psi(U_{\psi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
; we have
\psi\circ\phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\phi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi});\mathbb{R}^{n})
whenever
\psi,\phi\in\mathfrak{A}
satisfy
U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing
; and
\mathfrak{A}
is maximal with these properties.

(i)

Let
\beta\in(1-\kappa,\kappa]\cup\{\mathrm{LogL}-1\}
. A (locally)
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-complex function
f
is a collection
\{f_{\psi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U_{\psi});\mathbb{C}):\psi\in\mathfrak{A}\}
such that

f_{\psi}=f_{\phi}\circ(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ whenever }U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi}\neq\varnothing.

(3.1.1)

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
the space of all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-functions on
\mathfrak{M}
.

(ii)

Let
\beta\in(1-\kappa,\kappa-1]\cup\{\mathrm{LogL}-1\}
. A (locally)
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-complex vector field
X
is a collection
\{X_{\psi}=(X_{\psi}^{1},\dots,X_{\psi}^{n})\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U_{\psi});\mathbb{C}^{n}):\psi\in\mathfrak{A}\}
such that

X_{\psi}^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\big(X_{\phi}^{j}\circ(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})\big)\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial\psi^{i}}{\partial\phi^{j}}\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\Big),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ whenever }U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi}\neq\varnothing.

(3.1.2)

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
the space of all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
complex vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
. We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
the space of all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
real vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
.

(iii)

Let
\beta\in(1-\kappa,\kappa-1]\cup\{\mathrm{LogL}-1\}
. Let
k=1,\dots,n
. A (locally)
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
complex
k
-form
\omega
is a collection
\{\omega^{\psi}=(\omega^{\psi}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}})_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}=1}^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U_{\psi});\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}}):\psi\in\mathfrak{A}\}
such that
\omega^{\psi}_{\dots i_{j}i_{j+1}\dots}=-\omega^{\psi}_{\dots i_{j+1}i_{j}\dots}
for every
1\leq j\leq k-1
,
1\leq i_{j},i_{j+1}\leq n
,
\psi\in\mathfrak{A}
. And for every
\phi,\psi\in\mathfrak{A}
such that
U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi}\neq\varnothing
,

\omega^{\psi}_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}=\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{k}=1}^{n}\big(\omega^{\psi}_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}\circ(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})\big)\cdot\Big(\frac{\partial\phi^{j_{1}}}{\partial\psi^{i_{1}}}\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\Big)\dots\Big(\frac{\partial\phi^{j_{k}}}{\partial\psi^{i_{k}}}\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\Big),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{\phi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}.

(3.1.3)

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
the space of all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
complex
k
-form on
\mathfrak{M}
. We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
the space of all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
real
k
-form on
\mathfrak{M}
.

(iv)

If
2-\kappa<\beta\leq\kappa-1
(provided
\kappa>\frac{3}{2}
), for a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}

k
-form
\omega=\{\omega_{i_{1}\dots i_{k}}^{\psi}\}
on
\mathfrak{M}
, the exterior differential
d\omega=\{(d\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}}^{\psi}\}
is defined in the way that

\sum_{1\leq i_{1}<\dots<i_{k+1}\leq n}(d\omega)_{i_{1}\dots i_{k+1}}^{\psi}dx^{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{i_{k+1}}:=\sum_{1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{k}\leq n}d(\omega_{j_{1}\dots j_{k}}^{\psi})\wedge dx^{j_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{j_{k}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\psi(U_{\psi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{x}.

Remark 3.1.2
.

(a)

Here (
3.1.1
), (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
) are given by the pullback equations
f_{\psi}=(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})^{*}f_{\phi}
,
X_{\psi}=(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})^{*}X_{\phi}
and
\omega_{\psi}=(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})^{*}\omega_{\phi}
. By Lemma
2.1.34
if
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{-}
and Lemma
2.1.36
if
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}-1
, the right hand sides of (
3.1.1
), (
3.1.2
) and (
3.1.3
) are well-defined
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
objects.

(b)

In particular, to show a function
f
(or a vector fields
X
or a form
\omega
) is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, it suffices to show
f_{\psi_{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi_{j}(U_{\psi_{j}});\mathbb{C})
(or
X_{\psi_{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi_{j}(U_{\psi_{j}});\mathbb{C}^{n})
or
\omega^{\psi_{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi_{j}(U_{\psi_{j}});\mathbb{C}^{n^{k}})
) on a coordinate cover
\{\psi_{j}\}_{j}
, namely a subcollection of atlas
\{\psi_{j}:U_{\psi_{j}}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{j}
such that
\bigcup_{j}U_{\psi_{j}}=\mathfrak{M}
. In this way to study the local problems we can use one coordinate chart near a fixed point and work on subsets in Euclidean spaces.

(c)

We do not talk about “bounded
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
functions or vector fields” on a general manifold, since a manifold may not have structures like metric to control the size of functions or vector fields.

Remark 3.1.3
.

The notations
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
are different from taking
\mathfrak{N}=T\mathfrak{M}
or
\mathfrak{N}=\wedge^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
in Notation
2.3.1
. By
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
we mean the space of vector fields, i.e. all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
maps
X:\mathfrak{M}\to T\mathfrak{M}
that satisfies
X(p)\in T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
.

In practice there are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
maps
F:\mathfrak{M}\to T\mathfrak{M}
we use in the proof such that
F(p)\notin T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. For example, the map
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\exp_{X}(t,\cdot)=X\circ\exp_{X}(t,\cdot):\mathfrak{M}\to T\mathfrak{M}
sends every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
to a tangent vector in
T_{\exp(t,p)}\mathfrak{M}
which in general is not
T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. We do not use the word “
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
” for such map
F
in the thesis.

Remark 3.1.4
.

A
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-function on manifold can be identified as a linear functional by the way of
[
GKOS01
, Chapter 3.1]
. We sketch the construction as the following:

For a (smooth or non-smooth)
n
-dimensional manifold
\mathfrak{M}
with atlas
\mathfrak{A}=\{\psi:U_{\psi}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}
, we define the
volume bundle

\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}}
to be a real rank 1 vector bundle on
\mathfrak{M}
as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}}:=\Big(\coprod_{\psi\in\mathfrak{A}}\psi(U_{\psi})\times\mathbb{R}\Big)\Big/\left\{(x,t)\sim(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}(x),g_{\phi\psi}(x)t):x\in\psi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}),t\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

(3.1.4)

\displaystyle\text{where }g_{\phi\psi}(x):=|\det\nabla(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})(x)|,\hskip 10.00002ptx\in\psi(U_{\phi}\cap U_{\psi}).

When
(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{A})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold, then
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
vector bundle. Similar to Definition
3.1.1
a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}
-section of
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}}
can be defined by a collection
s=\{s_{\psi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U_{\psi})):\psi\in\mathfrak{A}\}
such that
s_{\psi}=(s_{\phi}\circ(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}))\cdot|\det\nabla(\phi\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}})|
.

In this case take a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
coordinate cover
\{\psi_{i}:U_{\psi_{i}}\subset\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}\}_{i\in I}
of
\mathfrak{M}
and take a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\kappa}(U_{\psi_{i}})\}_{i\in I}
, we have a pairing: for
f=(f_{\psi})_{\psi\in\mathfrak{A}}\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
and
s=(s_{\psi})_{\psi\in\mathfrak{A}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa-1}_{c}(\mathfrak{M})
,

\langle f,s\rangle:=\sum_{i\in I}\int_{\psi_{i}(U_{\psi_{i}})}\chi_{i}(\psi_{i}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(x))f_{\psi_{i}}(x)s_{\psi_{i}}(x)dx.

The pairing is still defined for
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
, thus we have a map
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\kappa}(\mathfrak{M};\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{\prime}
for
\beta\in(1-\kappa,\kappa]
. The pairing is canonical in the sense that it does not depend on the choice of coordinates and partition of unity. Therefore we can say
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\kappa}(\mathfrak{M};\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{\prime}
for all
0<\beta<1
.

Similarly we have the embedding of the space of vector fields
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\kappa}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{\prime}
and the space of differential forms
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})\subset\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\kappa}(\mathfrak{M};(\wedge^{k}T\mathfrak{M})\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}})^{\prime}
for
1\leq k\leq n
. For complex vector fields and complex forms the almost same embedding hold.

If
\mathfrak{M}
is a oriented manifold, then
\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}}
is canonically isomorphic to
\wedge^{n}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}
, the top degree alternating tensor of cotangent bundle. In this case a distribution is indeed an
n
-current, see
[
GKOS01
, Example 3.1.17]
. We leave the details to the reader.

To make the result more general we can consider general vector bundles and their subbundles.

Definition 3.1.5
.

Let
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
\kappa>1
,
\alpha\in(0,\kappa]
and let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
manifold with maximal
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-atlas
\mathfrak{A}
. A rank
m

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
complex vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
over
\mathfrak{M}
is a
(n+m)
-dimensional topological manifold with a surjective topological submersion
\pi:\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
and an atlas
\mathfrak{B}
such that

•

Elements in
\mathfrak{B}
are homeomorphisms
\Phi=(\psi,\phi):\pi^{-1}(U_{\psi})\xrightarrow{\sim}\psi(U_{\psi})\times\mathbb{C}^{m}(\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{C}^{m})
where
\psi\in\mathfrak{A}
.

•

For every
\Phi_{1}=(\psi_{1},\phi_{1}),\Phi_{2}=(\psi_{2},\phi_{2})\in\mathfrak{B}
such that
U_{\psi_{1}}\cap U_{\psi_{2}}\neq\varnothing
, the map
\phi_{1}\circ\Phi_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\psi_{2}(U_{\psi_{1}}\cap U_{\psi_{2}})\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-map such that
\phi_{1}\circ\Phi_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\mathbb{C}^{m}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
is linear for each
x\in\psi_{2}(U_{\psi_{2}})
. In other words we can write
g_{\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\psi_{2}(U_{\psi_{1}}\cap U_{\psi_{2}});\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
such that

\Phi_{1}\circ\Phi_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(x,v)=(\psi_{1}\circ\psi_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(x),g_{\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}}(x)\cdot v),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall x\in\psi_{2}(U_{\psi_{1}}\cap U_{\psi_{2}}),\hskip 10.00002ptv\in\mathbb{C}^{m}.

•

\mathfrak{B}
is maximal among the above two conditions.

Let
\beta\in(\max(1-\kappa,-\alpha),\alpha]\cup\{\mathrm{LogL}-1\}
. A
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-section of
\mathcal{E}
is a collection
\xi=\{\xi_{(\psi,\phi)}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U_{\psi});\mathbb{C}^{m}):(\psi,\phi)\in\mathfrak{B}\}
such that

\xi_{(\psi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}=g_{(\psi,\phi)(\psi^{\prime},\phi^{\prime})}\cdot(\xi_{(\psi,\phi)}\circ\psi\circ\psi^{\prime\mathrm{Inv}}),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\psi^{\prime}(U_{\psi^{\prime}}\cap U_{\psi})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ whenever }U_{\psi^{\prime}}\cap U_{\psi}\neq\varnothing.

We denote by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
the space of all (complex)
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-sections of
\mathcal{E}
.

For real vector bundles the definition is the same except we replace
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}
by
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
.

Remark 3.1.6
.

(a)

Just like manifolds, a coordinate cover
\{\Phi_{j}\}\subset\mathfrak{B}
of
\mathcal{E}
uniquely determines its bundle atlas.

(b)

As the direct correspondences to Remark
3.1.2
, we see that for each
\Phi,\Phi^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}
we can say
\xi_{\Phi}=(\Phi^{\prime}\circ\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}})^{*}\xi_{\Phi^{\prime}}
. The well-definedness of the pullback is guaranteed by Lemmas
2.1.34
and
2.1.36
. And to check a
\xi
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
it suffices to check
\xi_{(\psi_{j},\phi_{j})}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi_{j}(U_{\psi_{j}});\mathbb{C}^{m})
where
\{(\psi_{j},\phi_{j})\}\subset\mathfrak{B}
such that
\{\psi_{j}\}
is a coordinate cover of
\mathfrak{M}
. We leave the proof to readers.

(c)

Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a complex tangent subbundle and let
\beta\leq 0
. By Define
3.1.5
we define the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-sections of
\mathcal{S}
by considering
\mathcal{S}
as an abstract vector bundle. The definition is different from
[
Yao20
, Definition 1.12]
, where we define
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
to be the subset of space of vector fields
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
. Nevertheless Definition
3.1.5
for
\mathcal{S}
and
[
Yao20
, Definition 1.12]
coincide by Proposition
3.1.10
.

Lemma 3.1.7
.

Let
\kappa\in(1,\infty]
,
\alpha\in(0,\kappa]
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold, and let
\mathcal{E}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
complex vector bundle over
\mathfrak{M}
. Let
\gamma\in(0,\alpha]
and
\beta\in(\max(1-\kappa,\gamma),\gamma]\cup\{\mathrm{LogL}-1\}
.

(i)

The space of sections
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
is a finitely generated module over the ring of function
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
. In particular if
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
generate the module, then
\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{N}(p)
span the fibre
\mathcal{E}_{p}
for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(ii)

Sections has local property. More precisely, let
\{U_{j}\}_{j\in I}
be an open cover of
\mathfrak{M}
, then
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
if and only if
\xi|_{U_{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{j};\mathcal{E}|_{U_{j}})
for all
j
.

(iii)

We have equality of tensor products, or the so-called based change formula,

\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E}).

(3.1.5)

Remark 3.1.8
.

(a)

Algebraically tensor products can only be equal in the sense of a unique isomorphism. Here we can make them equal set theoretically by embedding both sides of (
3.1.5
) in a larger ambient spaces. For example, we can view them as linear functionals on
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{c}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E}^{*}\otimes\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathfrak{M}})
(see Remark
3.1.4
).

(b)

We can use either side of (
3.1.5
) to define the space of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-sections of
\mathcal{E}
. We see that the definition does not depend on the regularity assumption
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
: the space is the same if we view
\mathcal{E}
as a
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-vector bundle. In fact the space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
is defined as long as
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{(-\beta)+}
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1.7
.

We assume that
\dim\mathfrak{M}=n
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{E}=m
.

(i)
: This is the non-smooth version of the Swan’s Theorem. One can find the proof in
[
Lew14
, Theorem 5.3]
. In fact one can take
N=(\dim\mathfrak{M}+1)\cdot\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{E}
many generators.

(ii)
: Recall that
\xi
is defined to be a collection
\{\xi_{\Phi}:\Phi\in\mathfrak{B}\}
of distributions.

Let
(\psi,\phi)\in\mathfrak{B}=\mathfrak{B}^{\mathfrak{M}}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
bundle chart of
\mathfrak{M}
. By assumption
\{U_{j}\cap U_{\psi}\}
covers
U_{\psi}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, the domain of
(\psi,\phi)
. Therefore
\xi_{\psi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\psi(U_{\psi});\mathbb{C}^{m})
if and only if
\xi_{\psi}|_{\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{j})}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\psi(U_{\psi}\cap U_{j});\mathbb{C}^{m})
for all
j\in I
, whenever
U_{\psi}\cap U_{j}\neq\varnothing
. Since
(\psi,\phi)|_{U_{j}\cap U_{\psi}}\in\mathfrak{B}^{U_{j}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
bundle chart of
U_{j}
for each
j
, we finish the proof.

(iii)
: Take finitely many
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-sections
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N}
that generates
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
. Clearly a
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-section of
\mathcal{E}
can be written as some
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-linear combinations of
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{N}
, which gives a based change formula

\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})=\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})=\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E}).

(3.1.6)

On the other hand, if
R
is a commutative ring,
S
is an
R
-commutative algebra,
P
is a
S
-module and
Q
is a
R
-module, we have

P\otimes_{R}Q=P\otimes_{S}(S\otimes_{R}Q).

Taking
R=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
,
S=\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
,
P=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
and
Q=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
; or
R=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
S=\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
P=\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
and
Q=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
, we get the right equality of (
3.1.5
).

To prove the left equality of (
3.1.5
), using
(ii)
it suffices to show that for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
such that
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})
.

Indeed, by re-ordering the indices we can assume that
\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{m}(p)
form a basis of
\mathcal{E}_{p}
. So
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{m}
for a local basis of some neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and for each
m+1\leq j\leq N
we can write
\xi_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}f_{j}^{k}\xi_{k}
for some
f_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
.

By shrinking
U
we can find a coordinate chart
\psi\in\mathfrak{A}
of
\mathfrak{M}
whose domain is
U
. Let
\phi:\mathcal{E}|_{U}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
be given by
\phi(q,\sum_{j=1}^{,}v^{j}\xi_{j}):=(\psi(q),(v^{1},\dots,v^{m}))
. We see that
(\psi,\phi)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
bundle chart of
\mathcal{E}
.

Clearly for every
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})
we have
\eta|_{U}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}(\eta_{(\psi,\phi)}^{j}\circ\psi)\cdot\xi_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})
. Conversely for
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})
, we can write
\eta=\sum_{j=1}^{N}g^{j}\xi_{j}
where
g^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})
. By linear dependence of
\xi_{m+1},\dots,\xi_{N}
we can write
\eta=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{m}g^{j}f_{j}^{k}\xi_{k}
. Therefore by definition of
(\psi,\phi)
we have
\eta_{(\psi,\phi)}=\big(\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{m}(g^{j}f_{j}^{k})\circ\psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\big)_{k=1}^{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\psi(U);\mathbb{C}^{m})
i.e.
\eta\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})
. This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.1.9
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
vector bundle and let
\mathcal{V}\leq\mathcal{E}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
subbundle. By viewing
\mathcal{V}
itself as an abstract vector bundle we can define
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
for
\beta\in(\max(1-\kappa,-\alpha),\alpha]
as well.

For pointwise defined section we can identify the collection
\{\xi_{\Phi}:\Phi\in\mathfrak{B}^{\mathcal{E}}\}
with a map
\xi:\mathfrak{M}\to\mathcal{E}
. In this way we get the classical inclusion
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
. Therefore by Lemma
3.1.7

(iii)
, we obtain
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
by the following:

\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E}).

(3.1.7)

Now we can give some properties of distributional sections of a vector subbundle. In the following if we take
\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
then we get the characterizations of distributional sections of a tangent subbundle.

Proposition 3.1.10

(Characterization of distributional sections)
.

Let
\kappa>1
,
0\leq\alpha\in(0,\kappa]
and
\beta\in(\max(1-\kappa,-\alpha),\alpha]
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold, let
\mathcal{E}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
vector bundle over
\mathfrak{M}
and let
\mathcal{V}\leq\mathcal{E}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
vector bundle.
Let
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
. Let
\max(-\beta,0)<\gamma\leq\alpha
and
\max(1-\kappa,-\alpha)<\delta\leq\beta
. The following are equivalent:

(S.1)

X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
in the sense of (
3.1.7
). That is
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
.

(S.2)

X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
.

(S.3)

X\in\mathscr{C}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
, or equivalently
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
, in the sense of (
3.1.7
).

(S.4)

\langle\theta,X\rangle=0\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
for every dual section
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V}^{\bot}_{\mathcal{E}})
. Here
\mathcal{V}^{\bot}_{\mathcal{E}}\leq\mathcal{E}^{*}
is the dual bundle of
\mathcal{V}
in
\mathcal{E}
, given by

\mathcal{V}^{\bot}_{\mathcal{E}}:=\{(p,l)\in\mathcal{E}^{*}:\langle l,v\rangle=0\in\mathbb{C},\ \forall v\in\mathcal{V}_{p}\}.

Remark 3.1.11
.

By the equivalence of
(S.1)
and
(S.2)
, we see that definition of
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-sections does not depend on the regularity assumption that
\mathcal{V}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. Also see Remark
3.1.8

(b)
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1.10
.

The equivalence of
(S.1)
and
(S.2)
follows from Lemma
3.1.7

(iii)
.
The equivalence of
(S.1)
and
(S.3)
comes from the standard computation:

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})=

\displaystyle(\mathscr{C}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V}))\cap(\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V}).

To see
(S.3)

\Rightarrow

(S.4)
, we write
X=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f^{i}Y_{i}
where
f^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
and
Y_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
, then

\langle\theta,X\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\langle\theta,f^{i}Y_{i}\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f^{i}\langle\theta,Y_{i}\rangle=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for every }\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\text{ 1-form }\theta\text{ on }\mathcal{V}^{\bot}.

It remains to show
(S.4)

\Rightarrow

(S.1)
.

By Lemma
3.1.7

(i)
we can find finitely many
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
sections
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}
of
\mathcal{E}
that generates the module
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
over the ring
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
. In particular
Y_{1}(p),\dots,Y_{N}(p)
spans
\mathcal{V}_{p}\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}
for all
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

We can find an open cover
\{U_{\zeta}\}_{\zeta}
, associated with
r
-element subsets
I_{\zeta}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
and vector fields
Z^{\zeta}_{1},\dots,Z^{\zeta}_{n-r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{\zeta})
for each
\zeta
, such that
\{Y_{i}\}_{i\in I}\cup\{Z^{\zeta}_{1},\dots,Z^{\zeta}_{n-r}\}
form a local basis for
\mathcal{E}|_{U_{\zeta}}
. So for each
\zeta
,
(Y_{i})_{i\in I_{\zeta}}
are linearly independent and span
\mathcal{V}|_{U_{\zeta}}
.

Let
(\mu_{\zeta}^{1},\dots,\mu_{\zeta}^{r},\theta_{\zeta}^{1},\dots,\theta_{\zeta}^{n-r})
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
1-forms defined on
U_{\zeta}
that form the dual basis to vector fields
(Y_{\sigma_{\zeta}(1)},\dots,Y_{\sigma_{\zeta}(r)},Z^{\zeta}_{1},\dots,Z^{\zeta}_{n-r})
where we take
\sigma_{\zeta}:\{1,\dots,r\}\to I_{\zeta}
such that
\sigma_{\zeta}(1)<\dots<\sigma_{\zeta}(r)
. Therefore every
\tilde{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{\zeta};\mathcal{E}|_{U_{\zeta}})
can be written as

\tilde{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\mu_{\zeta}^{j},\tilde{X}\rangle Y_{\sigma_{\zeta}(j)}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}\langle\theta_{\zeta}^{k},\tilde{X}\rangle Z^{\zeta}_{k},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }U_{\zeta}\text{ for each }\zeta.

Let
\{\chi_{\zeta}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{c}(U_{\zeta})\}_{\zeta}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
partition of unity to the open cover
\{U_{\zeta}\}_{\zeta}
, thus we have

X=\sum_{\zeta}\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\chi_{\zeta}\mu_{\zeta}^{j},X\rangle\cdot Y_{\sigma_{\zeta}(j)}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}\langle\chi_{\zeta}\theta_{\zeta}^{k},X\rangle\cdot Z^{\zeta}_{k}\Big).

Clearly
\chi_{\zeta}\theta_{\zeta}^{1},\dots,\chi_{\zeta}\theta_{\zeta}^{n-r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V}^{\bot})
are globally defined, since
\theta_{\zeta}^{1},\dots,\theta_{\zeta}^{n-r}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-sections of
V^{\bot}|_{U_{\zeta}}
.

By assumption
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathfrak{X})\cap C^{-1,\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
, we have
\langle\chi_{\zeta}\theta_{\zeta}^{k},X\rangle=0\in C^{-1,\delta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
, so

X=\sum_{\zeta}\sum_{j=1}^{r}\langle\chi_{\zeta}\mu_{\zeta}^{j},X\rangle\cdot Y_{\sigma_{\zeta}(j)})=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(\sum_{\zeta}\langle\chi_{\zeta}\mu_{\zeta}^{\sigma_{\zeta}^{-1}(j)},X\rangle\Big)\cdot Y_{j}.

(3.1.8)

Here we let
\mu^{\sigma_{\zeta}^{-1}(j)}_{\zeta}=0
if
j\notin I_{\zeta}(=\sigma_{\zeta}(\{1,\dots,r\}))
.

Since
\langle\chi_{\zeta}\mu_{\zeta}^{j},X\rangle\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
and the sums in (
3.1.8
) are locally finite, we know
X
is the linear combinations of
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
with coefficients in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
. Therefore
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{V})
, which is the condition
(S.1)
.
∎

3.2
Distributional involutivity for regular subbundles

In this part we provide some equivalent characterizations of distributional involutivity for a tangent subbundle. Recall the definition of distributional involutivity in Definition
0
. See also in Section
5.1.1
for some historical remark.

Recall that in the smooth setting, when
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is a smooth complex tangent subbundle over a smooth manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, we have the following equivalent characterizations of involutivity:

(
3
.A)

C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
is a Lie subalgebra of
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
, i.e. if
X
and
Y
are smooth sections of
\mathcal{S}
then
[X,Y]
is also a smooth section of
\mathcal{S}
.

(
3
.B)

If
X\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, then the Lie derivative
\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
.

(
3
.C)

On the exterior algebra
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{\bullet})=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\dim\mathfrak{M}}C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
, the (two-sided) ideal
C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})\wedge C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{\bullet}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
is closed under differential.

(
3
.D)

On space of 2-forms we have
d(C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot}))\subseteq C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})\wedge C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
.

Here we recall that
\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta,Y\rangle=X\langle\theta,Y\rangle-\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle
for all smooth vector fields
X,Y
and 1-form
\theta
. The equality is still true for rough vector fields and 1-forms as long as the products are defined.

These characterizations can all be generalized to non-smooth subbundles.

We first describe some of the characterizations involving Lie brackets. Recall the involutivity definition of modules in Definition
3.0.2
.

Proposition 3.2.1

(Characterizations of distributional involutivity I)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold and let
\mathcal{S}
be a rank
r

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle of
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
.

Let
\beta\in(\frac{1}{2},\alpha]
and
\gamma\in(-\beta,\beta-1]
, the following conditions for
\mathcal{S}
are equivalent:

(I.1)

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-involutive. That is, for every
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
we have
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
.

(I.2)

\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
is
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-involutive.

(I.3)

\mathcal{S}
is distributional involutive (by Definition
0
). That is, for every
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, we have
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=0\in\mathscr{C}^{(-\frac{1}{2})+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
.

(I.4)

For every
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, we have
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=0\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
.

(I.5)

For every
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, we have
d\theta(X,Y)=0\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
.

(I.6)

For every
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, we have
\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
.

(I.7)

For every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-local basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
for
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are pairwise commutative in the sense of distributions.

(I.8)

If
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
are global generators for
\mathcal{S}
(that is
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
span
\mathcal{S}
at every point in
\mathfrak{M}
), then there are
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
for
1\leq i,j,k\leq N
such that

[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{N}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }\mathfrak{M},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\ 1\leq i,j\leq N.

(3.2.1)

Remark 3.2.2
.

(a)

In the statements,
(I.1)
and
(I.6)
are the direct generalizations to
(.A)
and
(.B)
respectively.

(b)

By the equivalence of
(I.1)
and
(I.2)
or
(I.3)
and
(I.4)
, we see that the definition of distributional involutivity does not depend on the regularity assumption
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

In this way the characterizations are still equivalent if we replace every
\alpha
by
\beta
in
(I.4)
-
(I.8)
.

(c)

The above characterizations do not use the information of
\dim\mathfrak{M}
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}
. That is why we can extend Definition
0
, the involutivity of a subset (subbundle) of
\mathcal{S}
, to Definition
3.0.2
, the involutivity of a submodule
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
.

(d)

In particular from
(I.8)
we can focus on the specific choice of
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
rather than on the ambient space
\mathcal{S}
. In the setting of regular subbundles, we only consider the module spanned by
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
, where
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(p),\dots,X_{N}(p))\leq\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
have constant rank among all
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

Proof of Proposition
3.2.1
.

We consider the directions
(I.2)

\Rightarrow

(I.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.4)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.5)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.6)
;
(I.4)

\Rightarrow

(I.7)

\Rightarrow

(I.2)
,
(I.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.8)
, and
(I.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.3)
.

When
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
, by Lemma
2.1.18

(ii)
we have
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
,
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle,d\theta(X,Y)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
.

(I.2)

\Rightarrow

(I.1)
: Let
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
. By condition
(I.2)

[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
. Since we have
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
, by Proposition
3.1.10

(S.3)

\Rightarrow

(S.1)
,
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
.

The direction
(I.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.4)
follows from Proposition
3.1.10

(S.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(S.4)
, where
[X,Y]
is a section of
\mathcal{S}
if and only if
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=0
for all
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
.

(I.4)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.5)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.6)
: For
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, in the sense of distributions we have
d\theta(X,Y)=X\langle\theta,Y\rangle-Y\langle\theta,X\rangle-\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=-\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle
and
\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta,Y\rangle=X\langle\theta,Y\rangle-\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle
. Thus
