\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=-d\theta(X,Y)=\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta,Y\rangle.

(3.2.2)

If one term in (
3.2.2
) is zero then the other two vanish as well. Thus we get
(I.4)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.5)
.
Note that
(I.6)
is equivalent as saying
\langle\mathrm{Lie}_{X}\theta,Y\rangle=0
for all
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
and
\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, thus
(I.6)
is equivalent to
(I.4)
and
(I.5)
.

(I.4)

\Rightarrow

(I.7)
: This is done by Lemma
2.3.9

(iii)
.

(I.7)

\Rightarrow

(I.2)
: Let
Y_{1},Y_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
, by locality of distributions (see Lemma
3.1.7

(ii)
) it suffices to show that for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
such that
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{S}|_{U})
. Fix
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, let
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
and
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{S}|_{U})
be as in
(I.7)
. By Proposition
3.1.10

(S.4)

\Rightarrow

(S.1)
we can write
Y_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}f_{i}^{j}X_{j}
for some
f_{i}^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
,
i=1,2
and
1\leq j\leq r
. Therefore

[Y_{1},Y_{2}]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}[f_{1}^{i}X_{i},f_{2}^{j}X_{j}]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}f_{1}^{i}(X_{i}f_{2}^{j})X_{j}-f_{2}^{j}(X_{j}f_{1}^{i})X_{i}+f_{1}^{i}f_{2}^{j}[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\big(f_{1}^{j}(X_{j}f_{2}^{i})-f_{2}^{j}(X_{j}f_{1}^{i})\big)X_{i}.

We see that
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]
is the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
linear combinations of
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
, thus
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathcal{S}|_{U})
. By Proposition
3.1.10

(S.1)
\Rightarrow
(S.4)

[Y_{1},Y_{2}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{S}|_{U})
. Since
p\in\mathfrak{M}
is arbitrary, we get
[Y_{1},Y_{2}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})(\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}))
, proving
(I.2)
.

The proof of
(I.8)

\Rightarrow

(I.1)
is identical to the proof of
(I.7)

\Rightarrow

(I.2)
except we do not need to localize the objects on an open subset. We omit the details to the readers.

(I.1)

\Rightarrow

(I.8)
: Note that by Lemma
3.1.7

(i)
we can find finitely many
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
that spans
\mathcal{S}
at every point.

By assumption
(I.1)
,
[X_{i},X_{j}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
for all
1\leq i,j\leq N
.
So by Proposition
3.1.10

(S.4)
\Rightarrow
(S.1)
,
[X_{i},X_{j}]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
.
Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
generates
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
as a module over
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
, we can write
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{N}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
for some
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. This gives the condition
(I.8)
.

Now we have all the done all the equivalence of each conditions except
(I.3)
.

(I.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.3)
: Clearly
(I.3)
implies
(I.4)
. We proved
(I.4)

\Rightarrow

(I.1)
so we have
(I.3)

\Rightarrow

(I.1)
.

Conversely, for
X,Y,\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
in the assumption of
(I.3)
, we can assume that
X,Y,\theta\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon_{0}}
for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. Using
(I.1)

\Leftrightarrow

(I.2)
for arbitrary
\frac{1}{2}<\beta\leq\alpha
and

, we see that
(I.1)
implies that
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
is

-involutive, for every

. Therefore
[X,Y]\in\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon_{0}-\frac{1}{2}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
. Thus by Proposition

(S.1)

(S.4)
with

, we see that
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=0\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon_{0}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
. This gives the condition
(I.3)
and completes the proof.
∎

In Proposition
3.2.1
we mostly focus on the side of vector fields. We can consider the characterizations using differential forms which are related to the sections of
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
.

Proposition 3.2.3

(Characterizations of distributional involutivity II)
.

Let
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},\infty)
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold and let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a rank
r

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle.

Then
\mathcal{S}
is distributional involutive if and only if either of the following condition holds:

(I.9)

Let
1\leq k\leq n
and
\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
satisfy
\omega(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})=0
for every
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
. Then we have
d\omega(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k+1})=0
as distributions for every
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k+1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})
.

(I.10)

d(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot}))\subseteq(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})\wedge\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}))\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C})
. That is, for every
\lambda\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
,
d\lambda\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{2}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
can be written as a finite sum

d\lambda=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}\cdot\theta^{i}\wedge\mu^{i},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for some }N\geq 1,\hskip 10.00002ptf_{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}),\hskip 10.00002pt\theta^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mu^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}).

(I.11)

For every
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n-r+1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
we have

\lambda^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda^{n-r}\wedge d\lambda^{n-r+1}=0\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{n-r+2}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M}).

(3.2.3)

Here
(I.10)
is the direct generalization to
(.D)
.

We shall see that
(I.9)
generalizes
(.C)
, because the space of all differential forms that annihilate sections of
\mathcal{S}
is indeed an ideal of the exterior algebra
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{\bullet})
.

To prove the equivalence of
(I.10)
and
(I.9)
we need the following:

Lemma 3.2.4
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
complex subbundle. Then for
1\leq k\leq n
,

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})\wedge\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{k-1}\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})=\{\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\Lambda^{k}):\omega(X_{1},\dots,X_{k})=0\ \forall X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S})\}.

(3.2.4)

Proof.

Let
W
be a
n
-dimensional (complex) vector space and
V\leq W
is a rank
r
(complex) subspace. By results in standard linear algebra, as the subspaces of alternating tensors we have

V_{W}^{\bot}\wedge\textstyle\bigwedge^{k-1}W^{*}=\big\{\phi\in\textstyle\bigwedge^{k}W^{*}:\phi(v_{1},\dots,v_{k})=0\text{ for all }v_{1},\dots,v_{k}\in V\big\}.

(3.2.5)

Here
V_{W}^{\bot}=\{l\in W^{*}:\langle l,v\rangle=0,\ \forall v\in V\}
.

For each
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, by taking
W=\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
and
V=\mathcal{S}_{p}
in (
3.2.5
), we get (
3.2.4
) and complete the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.2.3
.

By Lemma
3.2.4
we see that

(I.10)
is the special case to
(I.9)
when
k=1
. So
(I.9)

\Rightarrow

(I.10)
.

We are going to prove
(I.8)

\Rightarrow

(I.9)
and
(I.10)

\Rightarrow

(I.11)

\Rightarrow

(I.7)
. This would complete the proof since by Proposition
3.2.1
,
(I.8)
and
(I.7)
are both equivalent characterizations of involutivity.

(I.8)

\Rightarrow

(I.9)
: Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}
satisfy the condition
(I.8)
, and let
\omega
be a
k
-form annihilating all sections of
\mathcal{S}
. In particular
\omega(X_{i_{1}},\dots,X_{i_{k}})=0
for all
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\}\subseteq\{1,\dots,N\}
. By passing to
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-linear combinations it suffices to show that
\omega(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k+1})=0
for
\{Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k+1}\}\subseteq\{X_{1},\dots,X_{N}\}
.

Indeed by direct computation along with (
3.2.1
) we have

\displaystyle d\omega(X_{i_{0}},\dots,X_{i_{k}})=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq u<v\leq k}(-1)^{u+v}\omega([X_{i_{u}},X_{i_{v}}],X_{i_{0}},\dots,X_{i_{u-1}},X_{i_{u+1}}\dots,X_{i_{v-1}},X_{i_{v+1}},\dots,X_{i_{k}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq u<v\leq k}(-1)^{u+v}\sum_{l=1}^{N}c_{i_{u}i_{v}}^{l}\cdot\omega(X_{l},X_{i_{0}},\dots,X_{i_{u-1}},X_{i_{u+1}}\dots,X_{i_{v-1}},X_{i_{v+1}},\dots,X_{i_{k}})=0.

This gives
(I.9)
.

(I.10)

\Rightarrow

(I.11)
:
Let
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n-r+1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
, by assumption
(I.10)
we can write
d\lambda^{n-r+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}f_{i}\theta^{i}\wedge\mu^{i}
for some
\theta^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
. So

\lambda^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda^{n-r}\wedge d\lambda^{n-r+1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda^{n-r}\wedge\theta^{i}\wedge(f_{i}\mu^{i})

On the other hand
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
is a cotangent subbundle with rank
(n-r)
, we see that
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n-r},\theta^{i}
are linear dependent at every point in
\mathfrak{M}
, thus
\lambda^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda^{n-r}\wedge\theta^{i}\equiv 0
. We conclude that
\lambda^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda^{n-r}\wedge d\lambda^{n-r+1}=0
as a distributional differential form.

(I.11)

\Rightarrow

(I.7)
: Fixed a point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, by Lemma
2.3.9
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-chart
(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
near
p
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
on
U
that span
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
and have the form
X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}b_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}
. What we need is to show that
[X_{j},X_{k}]=0
for all
1\leq j<k\leq r
.

Clearly
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r},\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{n-r}})
span the tangent space at every point in
U
. The dual basis has the form
(dx^{1},\dots,dx^{r},\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{n-r})
where
\theta^{l}=dy^{l}-\sum_{j=1}^{r}b_{j}^{l}dx^{j}
,
l=1,\dots,n-r
. Thus for
1\leq l\leq n-r

\displaystyle d\theta^{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\frac{\partial b_{i}^{l}}{\partial x^{j}}dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}\frac{\partial b_{i}^{l}}{\partial y^{k}}dx^{i}\wedge dy^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\Big(\frac{\partial b_{i}^{l}}{\partial x^{j}}-\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial x^{i}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}\big(b_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial b_{i}^{l}}{\partial y^{k}}-b_{i}^{k}\frac{\partial b_{i}^{k}}{\partial y^{l}}\big)\Big)dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial y^{k}}dx^{i}\wedge\theta^{k}

\displaystyle\equiv\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\langle\theta^{l},[X_{i},X_{j}]\rangle dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j}\pmod{\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{r}}.

Thus

\theta^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\theta^{n-r}\wedge d\theta^{l}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq r}\langle\theta^{l},[X_{i},X_{j}]\rangle\theta^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\theta^{n-r}\wedge dx^{i}\wedge dx^{j},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq l\leq n-r.

By shrinking
U
if necessary we can find global sections
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{S}^{\bot})
such that
\lambda^{l}|_{U}=\theta^{l}
. By assumption
(I.11)
we see that
\langle\theta^{l},[X_{i},X_{j}]\rangle=0\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. Since
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}\langle\theta^{l},[X_{i},X_{j}]\rangle\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{l}}
we know
[X_{i},X_{j}]=0
are commutative, proving the condition
(I.7)
.
∎

3.3
Remarks on Integrability and Problems in Regular Subbundles

Unlike involutivity, it is more difficult to discuss integrability in the low regularity setting.

On real tangent subbundles, from discussion of the real Frobenius theorem we get the following definition:

Definition 3.3.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a continuous rank
r
real tangent subbundle over a
n
-dim
C^{1}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
.

We say
\mathcal{V}
is
locally integrable via parameterizations
if for every point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there exists a neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}_{v}
of
(0,0)
and a map
\Phi(u,v)
that is homeomorphism onto its image such that

•

\Phi(0,0)=p
.

•

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u^{r}}:U\to T\mathfrak{M}
are continuous maps.

•

For every
(u,v)\in U
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u^{1}}(u,v),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial u^{r}}(u,v)
form a basis for
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(u,v)}
.

In this setting, Theorem
1
implies the following:

Proposition 3.3.2
.

An involutive log-Lipschitz real tangent subbundle must be locally integrable via parameterizations.

It is not difficult to see that a Hölder involutive real subbundles may not be locally integrable via parameterizations since it may not admit a foliation. See Example
3.4.11
.

We can talk about integrability pointwisely. For convenience we define them on generalized subbundles (recall Definition
3.0.1
).

Definition 3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a continuous generalized subbundle over a
n
-dim
C^{1}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
.

•

We say
\mathcal{G}
is
pointwisely integrable
, if for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a
C^{1}
-submanifold
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathfrak{M}
containing
p
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=T\mathfrak{S}
, i.e.
\mathcal{G}\cap T_{q}\mathfrak{M}=T_{q}\mathfrak{S}
for all
q\in\mathfrak{S}
. We call
\mathfrak{S}
an
integral submanifold
for
\mathcal{G}
near
p
.

•

We say
\mathcal{G}
is
uniquely integrable
, if in addition to pointwisely integrability the integral submanifold is unique: If both
\mathfrak{S}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{S}_{2}
are integral submanifolds for
\mathcal{S}
near
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, then there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
such that
\mathfrak{S}_{1}\cap U=\mathfrak{S}_{2}\cap U
.

On the Hölder regular subbundles, it is easy to see that pointwise integrability does not implies unique integrability. For example, for
0<\alpha<1
, the subbundle
\mathcal{V}:=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+|y|^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial y})
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,y}
is pointwise integrable because it is of rank 1 and every continuous vector field has ODE existence.
\mathcal{V}
is not uniquely integrable because on
(0,0)
,

\gamma_{0}(t):=(t,0),\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma_{1}(t):=(t,((1-\alpha)\max(0,t))^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}}),\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{R},

give two integral curves for
\mathcal{V}
that has different images.

For regular subbundles that have Osgood regularity, the integrability conditions in Definitions
3.3.1
and
3.3.3
are all coincide.

Definition 3.3.4
.

Let
\mu
be a modulus of continuity, that is,
\mu:[0,1]\to[0,1]
is a strictly increasing map such that
\mu(0)=0
. We say
\mu
is a Osgood modulus, if
\int_{0}^{1}dr/\mu(r)=\infty
.

For an open subset
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we define
\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega)
be the same as in (
2.1.25
).

We say a function
f:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is locally Osgood continuous, if for every precompact open
\Omega^{\prime}\Subset\Omega
, there is a Osgood modulus
\mu
such that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\mu}(\Omega^{\prime})
. We say a subbundle is Osgood continuous, if it is spanned by vector fields whose coefficients are (locally) Osgood continuous.

Lemma 3.3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be a regular subbundle which is Osgood continuous. Then the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathcal{V}
is locally integrable via parameterizations.

(ii)

\mathcal{V}
is pointwise integrable.

(iii)

\mathcal{V}
is uniquely integrable.

Proof.

Clearly

(i)
implies
(ii)
, and
(iii)
implies
(ii)
.

We assume that
\dim\mathfrak{M}=n
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{V}=r
.

By passing to a local coordinate chart we can assume that
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open subset. Applying Lemma
2.3.9
we can find a linear coordinates
(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r})
and Osgood vector fields
X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}a_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}
,
1\leq j\leq r
that span
\mathcal{V}
. By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 3.2]
the multi-flow
\exp_{X}
is locally defined near
(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By
[
BCD11
, Corollary 3.6]
(where we can consider a single vector field
\tilde{X}(t,x):=(t\cdot X(x),0)
in
\mathbb{R}^{n+r}
) we see that
\exp_{X}
is a continuous map.

(ii)

\Rightarrow

(iii)
and
(i)
: It suffices to consider the case
p=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By taking scaling we can assume that
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are defined on
3\mathbb{B}^{n}
, and
\|(a_{j}^{k})\|_{C^{0}(3\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{(n-r)\times n}}\leq 1
. Thus
\exp_{X}:\mathbb{B}^{r}\times\mathbb{B}^{n}\to 3\mathbb{B}^{n}
is defined.

Now for every
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n-r}
there is a
r
-dimensional integral submanifold containing
(0,y)
. Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
spans
\mathcal{V}
and their
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
-components do not vanish, we can find a regular
C^{1}
-parameterization
\phi^{y}:\mathbb{B}^{r}\to 3\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
\phi^{y}(0)=(0,y)
and
\phi^{y}(t)=(t,\ast)
for every
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

By assumption
\frac{\partial\phi^{y}}{\partial t^{1}}(t),\dots,\frac{\partial\phi^{y}}{\partial t^{r}}(t)\in\mathcal{V}_{\phi^{y}(t)}
, thus
(t\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t})\phi^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{V}_{\phi^{y}(t)}
. However
(t\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t})\phi^{y}(t)=(t,\ast)
, we must have
(t\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial t})\phi^{y}(t)=(t\cdot X)(\phi^{y}(t))
, for every
t\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
. We conclude that
\phi^{y}(t)=\exp_{X}(t,(0,y))
.

Therefore
\phi^{y}
is uniquely determined by
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
and
y\in\mathbb{B}^{n-r}
, thus
\phi^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the unique integral manifold containing
0
, giving the condition
(iii)
.

Let
\Phi(t,s):=\exp_{X}(t,(0,s))
, we see that
\Phi
is continuous and
\Phi(t,s)=\phi^{s}(t)
. Since
\phi^{s}
is
C^{1}
whose derivatives span
\mathcal{V}
, we conclude that
\Phi
is
C^{1}
in
t
and
\nabla_{t}\Phi(t,s)
span
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,s)}
.

Notice that
F(x,y)=(x,e^{-x\cdot X}y)
is a continuous map which is the inverse map of
\Phi
, we conclude that
\Phi
is homeomorphism onto its image. This finishes the proof of
(i)
.
∎

Remark 3.3.6
.

It is not known to the author whether
(ii)

\Rightarrow

(iii)
in Lemma
3.3.5
remained true for general continuous real subbundles, or even for subbundles with Hölder continuity. It is also not known to the author whether local integrability (via parameterizations) always implies involutivity when the subbundles are
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
.

On complex subbundles there is a different type of integrability. The following definition can be founded in
[
Trè92
]
and
[
BCH08
]
.

Definition 3.3.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
C^{2}
manifold and let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1}
complex tangent subbundle of rank
r
.

We say
\mathcal{S}
is
locally integrable via differentials
, if for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there are neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and
C^{1}
functions
f^{1},\dots,f^{n-r}:U\to\mathbb{C}
, such that
df^{1}(q),\dots,df^{n-r}(q)\in\mathbb{C}T_{q}^{*}\mathfrak{M}
are linearly independent for all
q\in U
, and
\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}(df^{1},\dots,df^{n-r})^{\bot}
.

We cannot use parameterizations since an involutive subbundle may not necessarily be spanned by coordinate vector fields unless it is a complex Frobenius structure.

Example 3.3.8
.

In Remark
2.3.13

(ii)
, on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,t}
we see that the subbundle generated by Mizohata operator
X=\partial_{t}+it\partial_{x}
cannot be the span of coordinate vector fields. On the other hand
(\operatorname{Span}X)^{\bot}=\operatorname{Span}(dx-itdt)=\operatorname{Span}(d(x-it^{2}/2))
, so
\operatorname{Span}X
is locally integrable via differentials.

It is known that real-analytic involutive structures are always locally integrable, while there exists a smooth involutive structure which is not locally integrable, see
[
BCH08
, Section I.16]
.

It is difficult to generalize Definition
3.3.7
for non
C^{1}
complex subbundles since it does not make sense to talk about linearly independence of differentials pointwisely. On one hand, without linearly independence, it is possible that the set
\operatorname{Span}(df^{1},\dots,df^{n-r})^{\bot}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
has fibre whose rank is larger than
r
. On the other hand, in the non
C^{1}
-setting, it is also possible to construct a span of differentials whose dual bundle has rank less than
r
:

Lemma 3.3.9
.

For a
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
function
f:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
, we associate a set
\mathcal{V}^{f}\subseteq T\mathfrak{M}
given by the following:

\mathcal{V}^{f}:=\{(p,X(p))\in T\mathfrak{M}:X\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})\text{ for some open }U\ni p\text{ such that }\langle df,X\rangle=Xf=0\text{ as distributions}\}.

Then there is a Lipschitz function
f
such that
\mathcal{V}^{f}=\{0\}
.

We see that if
f
is
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{3}{2}+}
, then
\mathcal{V}^{f}=(\operatorname{Span}df)^{\bot}
is the classical pointwise dual set.

Proof.

Take
f_{0}(x):=|x|
, we claim that

\mathcal{V}^{f_{0}}=\{(p,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}:p\neq 0,\ p\cdot v=0\}\cup\{(0,0)\}

(3.3.1)

Indeed
f_{0}
is regular for
x\neq 0
, so
\mathcal{V}^{f_{0}}_{p}=(\operatorname{Span}df_{0}(p))^{\bot}
for
p\neq 0
. At
p=0
, if
X
is a continuous vector fields near
0
such that
Xf=0
, then
\frac{x}{|x|}\cdot X(x)=0
. Let
x\to 0
along each ray, we see that
\theta\cdot X(0)=0
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
, i.e.
X(0)=0
. Therefore
\mathcal{V}^{f_{0}}_{0}=\{0\}
.

Now let
f=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}3^{-j}|x-q_{j}|
where
\{q_{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}=\mathbb{Q}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an enumeration of rational point. We claim that
\mathcal{V}^{f}=\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\{0\}
is the zero subbundle as desired.

Indeed for each
j=1,2,3,\dots
, let
f_{j}(x):=\sum_{k\neq j}^{\infty}3^{-k}|x-q_{k}|
. We see that
\nabla f_{j}(x)=\sum_{k\neq j}3^{-k}(x-q_{k})/|x-q_{k}|
for all
x\notin\{q_{k}:k\neq j\}
, and therefore the following limit exists (in the calculus setting):

\lim_{x\to q_{j};x\notin\{q_{k}:k\neq j\}}\nabla f_{j}(x)=\nabla f_{j}(q_{j})=\sum_{k\neq j}3^{-k}\frac{q_{j}-q_{k}}{|q_{j}-q_{k}|}.

Let
U_{j}\ni q_{j}
be an open set and let
X\in C^{0}(U_{j};\mathbb{R}^{n})
be a continuous vector field such that
Xf_{j}=\nabla f_{j}\cdot X=0
almost everywhere. Since
X
is continuous, we see that
\lim_{r\to 0}\|\nabla f(x)\cdot X(q_{j})\|_{L^{\infty}(B(q_{j},r))}=0
. On the other hand, for each irrational direction
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
(i.e.
(\theta\cdot\mathbb{R})\cap\mathbb{Q}^{n}=\{0\}
), we see that in the calculus setting

\lim_{r\to 0}\nabla f(q_{j}+r\theta)\cdot X(q_{j})=\theta\cdot X(q_{j})+\lim_{r\to 0}\nabla f_{j}(q_{j}+r\theta)\cdot X(q_{j})=(\theta+\nabla f_{j}(q_{j}))\cdot X(q_{j}).

Therefore
(\theta+\nabla f_{j}(q_{j}))\cdot X(q_{j})=0
for almost every
\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Since any positive (surface) measurable subset of
\nabla f_{j}(q_{j})+\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
contains
n
-linearly independent vectors, we conclude that
X(q_{j})=0
.

Since
\{q_{j}\}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, if
X:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathcal{V}^{f}
is any continuous vector field, then we must have
X(q_{j})=0
for all
q_{j}\in U
. By continuity of
X
and density of
\{q_{j}\}
, we see that
X\equiv 0
. This is also true for
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
-vector fields as well since
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}\subsetneq C^{0}
.
Therefore
\mathcal{V}^{f}
is a zero subbundle.
∎

Remark 3.3.10
.

If we consider
\operatorname{Span}(df)
as a “
L^{\infty}
-cotangent subbundle”, which is an almost everywhere defined section to the Grassmannian spaces, then
\operatorname{Span}(df)^{\bot}
is still a rank
(n-1)
tangent subbundle though only almost everywhere defined. The above example shows that there is no nonzero continuous vector field which is almost everywhere contained in
\operatorname{Span}(df)^{\bot}
.

Remark 3.3.11
.

Example
3.3.9
does not have issue with Convention
4.1.22
. In Convention
4.1.22
we have a product manifold
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
endowed with a product coordinate chart
(\mu,\nu)=(\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{n},\nu^{1},\dots,\nu^{q})
. We consider a subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
and functions
f^{1},\dots,f^{n-r}
which are
C^{1}
in
\mu
, such that
\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mu}d\mu
is a collection of
(n-r)
linearly independent differentials and
\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial f}{\partial\mu}d\mu,d\nu)^{\bot}
.

Even though
f^{1},\dots,f^{n-r}
are not
C^{1}
, on each leaf
\mathfrak{M}\times\{q_{0}\}
, the restriction
\mathcal{S}|_{\mathfrak{M}\times\{q_{0}\}}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is locally integrable via differential. Since we have
(\mathcal{S}|_{\mathfrak{M}\times\{q_{0}\}})^{\bot}=\operatorname{Span}d(f(\cdot,q_{0}))
, where
d(f^{1}(\cdot,q_{0})),\dots,d(f^{n-r}(\cdot,q_{0}))
are continuous linearly independent differentials on an open subset of
\mathfrak{M}
.

We can naturally to ask whether the Frobenius theorem holds in the general Osgood setting:

Problem 3.3.12
.

Let
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be an Osgood subbundle which is involutive in the sense of distributions. Does
\mathcal{V}
always pointwise integrable? If so, does
\mathcal{V}
always locally integrable via parameterizations.

We know that if such parameterization
\Phi(t,s):\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\to\mathfrak{M}
exists, then applying
[
BCD11
, Lemma 3.4]
, the regularity estimate
\Phi\in C^{1}_{t}(\Omega^{\prime};C^{0}_{s}(\Omega^{\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M}))
is the best possible we can get.

Problem
3.3.12
is reduced to the flow commuting problem, when vector fields are merely Osgood:

Problem 3.3.13
.

Let
X,Y
be two Osgood vector fields in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
satisfying
[X,Y]=0
in the sense of distributions. Do we must have
e^{tX}\circ e^{sY}=e^{sY}\circ e^{tX}
locally for small
t
and
s
?

Unfortunately we do not know how to prove the Problem
3.3.13
for non log-Lipschitz vector fields in general. One attempt to deal with the Problem
3.3.13
is to show whether an Osgood subbundle is asymptotically involutive (see
[
LTW19
, Definition 1.17]
). Based on this, we can ask an even weaker question:

Problem 3.3.14
.

Is there a modulus of continuity
\mu
such that the following hold?

•

\lim\limits_{r\to 0}\mu(r)^{-1}\cdot r\log\frac{1}{r}=0
.

•

If
\mathcal{V}
is a tangent subbundle with modulus of continuity
\mu
, and if
\mathcal{V}
is involutive in the sense of distributions, then
\mathcal{V}
is asymptotically involutive.

A major difficulty in understanding Problem
3.3.13
is to verify (
2.1.72
). But the exponential term in (
2.1.73
) is somehow subtle.

3.4
Remarks and Examples on Nonsmooth Singular Subbundles

We recall the Definition
3.0.1
for generalized tangent subbundles and Definition
3.0.2
for involutivity for modules of sections. In this section we focus only on real singular subbundles since we do not have a good analogy for the singular version of complex Frobenius theorem.

For completeness we define integrability on submodules:

Definition 3.4.1
.

Let
\alpha>0
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold, and let
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
be a submodule over the ring
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. We say
\mathscr{F}
is pointwise integrable, if for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and a
C^{1}
-submanifold
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq U
containing
p
, such that
T_{q}\mathfrak{S}=\operatorname{Span}\{X(q):X\in\mathscr{F}\}
for every
q\in\mathfrak{S}
.

Similar to Proposition
3.3.2
, Theorem
2
can be restated as the following:

Proposition 3.4.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold and let
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
be a finitely generated module over the ring
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. If
\mathscr{F}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
-involutive, then
\mathscr{F}
is pointwise integrable.

Given a generalized subbundle, we can still talk about
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-sections for
\beta>0
. To make it more general, we define sections for generalized subbundle over general vector bundles.

Definition 3.4.3
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1}
manifold which is at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. Let
\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
real vector bundle. A
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
generalized subbundle of
\mathcal{E}
is a subset
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{E}
, such that
\mathcal{G}_{p}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{p}
is a (real) vector subspace for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, and for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and finite sections
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})
such that
\mathcal{G}_{q}=\operatorname{Span}(\xi_{1}(q),\dots,\xi_{m}(q))(\leq\mathcal{E}_{q})
for every
q\in U
.

We define
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}:=\max\{\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{p}:p\in\mathfrak{M}\}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{G}):=\{\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E}):\xi(p)\in\mathcal{E}_{p},\ \forall p\in\mathcal{E}\}
.

The definition for complex vector bundles and complex generalized subbundles are the same and we omit the details.

We can remove the finiteness assumption of local generators for
\mathcal{G}
in Definitions
3.0.1
and
3.4.3
due to the following:

Proposition 3.4.4

(Serre-Swan for generalized subbundles)
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,\infty)\cup\{\infty\}
and
n\geq 1
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional manifold (which is at least
C^{1}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
) and let
\mathcal{E}\twoheadrightarrow\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
real vector bundle.

Let
\mathcal{G}\subset\mathcal{E}
be a generalized subbundle and has rank
r
. Then the following are equivalent:

(i)

\mathcal{G}_{p}=\operatorname{Span}\{X(p):X\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})\}
for every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(ii)

There are at most
r(n+1)
many (global) sections
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{r(n+1)}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
such that
\mathcal{G}_{p}
is spanned by
\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{r(n+1)}(p))
for all
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

(iii)

For every
p\in\mathcal{G}
there is a
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
such that
\mathcal{G}_{q}=\operatorname{Span}\{X(q):X\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathcal{E}|_{U})\}
for every
q\in U
.

(iv)

\mathcal{G}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
generalized subbundle, from Definition
3.4.3
.

Here we need Ostrand’s theorem on colored dimension:

Lemma 3.4.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional topological manifold and let
\mathfrak{P}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
be a closed subset. Let
\mathscr{U}=\{U_{i}:i\in I\}
be an open cover of
\mathfrak{P}
. Then there are
(n+1)
-collections
\mathscr{V}_{1},\dots,\mathscr{V}_{n+1}
of distinct open sets on
\mathfrak{M}
such that:

(a)

For each
1\leq j\leq n+1
,
\mathscr{V}_{j}
is a collection of disjoint open sets of .

(b)

For each
1\leq j\leq n+1
and each
V\in\mathscr{V}_{j}
, there exists an
U\in\mathscr{U}
such that
V\subseteq U
.

(c)

\bigcup_{j=1}^{n+1}\bigcup_{V\in\mathscr{V}_{j}}V\supseteq\mathfrak{P}
is an open cover.

Proof.

A closed subset of
n
-dimensional topological manifold has covering dimension at most
n
, see
[
Mun00
, Exercise 50.9 and Theorem 50.1]
. The result then follows from
[
Ost65
, Theorem 1]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.4.4
.

Clearly
(ii)

\Rightarrow

(iv)

\Rightarrow

(iii)
. By the standard partition of unity argument we have
(iii)

\Rightarrow

(i)
.

Our goal is to prove
(i)

\Rightarrow

(ii)
. We proceed by induction on
k=0,1,\dots,r
of the following

\exists\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k(n+1)}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{G}),\text{ such that }\operatorname{rank}\big(\operatorname{Span}\{\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{k(n+1)}(p)\}\big)\geq\min(\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{p},k),\ \forall p\in\mathfrak{M}.

(3.4.1)

The base case
k=0
is trivial as there is no section to take.

Let
k\geq 1
and suppose we have the case
k-1
. We define a set
\mathfrak{N}_{k}:=\{p\in\mathfrak{M}:\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{p}\geq k\}
. For
p\in\mathfrak{N}_{k}
by assumption
(i)
we can find sections
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{G})
such that
\eta_{1}(p),\dots,\eta_{k}(p)\in\mathcal{E}_{p}
are linearly independent. Thus
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}
are linearly independent in a neighborhood of
p
, which means
p
is an interior point of
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
. We conclude that
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
is an open set.

By induction hypothesis there are global
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
sections
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{(k-1)(n+1)}
of
\mathcal{G}
such that their span at each
p\in\mathfrak{M}
has rank
\geq\min(\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{p},k-1)
. Let
p\in\mathfrak{N}_{k}
, since
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
is open, we can find a precompact neighborhood
U^{p}\Subset\mathfrak{N}_{k}
of
p
and a section
\eta^{p}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U^{p};\mathcal{G}|_{U^{p}})
such that

\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Span}\{\xi_{1}(q),\dots,\xi_{(k-1)(n+1)}(q),\eta^{p}(q)\}\geq k,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall q\in U^{p}.

(3.4.2)

Applying Lemma
3.4.5
to the open cover
\{U^{p}:p\in\mathfrak{N}_{k}\}
of
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
, there are collections
\mathscr{V}_{1}=\mathscr{V}_{1}^{k},\dots,\mathscr{V}_{n+1}=\mathscr{V}_{n+1}^{k}
of open sets, associated with an index function
\tau:\mathscr{V}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathscr{V}_{n+1}\to\mathfrak{M}
, such that

(a)

\mathscr{V}_{j}
are disjoint collections, for each
j=1,\dots,n+1
.

(b)

V\subseteq U^{\tau(V)}
holds for every
V\in\mathscr{V}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathscr{V}_{n+1}
, where
U^{\tau(V)}\Subset\mathfrak{N}_{k}
is the neighborhood of
\tau(V)\in\mathfrak{N}_{k}
.

(c)

\mathscr{V}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathscr{V}_{n+1}
is an open cover of
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
.

We can assume that
\mathfrak{M}
is connected, hence
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
is second counterable, which means that
\mathscr{V}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathscr{V}_{n+1}
is a collection of at most counterable elements.
By reordering the index we can write
\mathscr{V}_{j}=\{V_{j,1},V_{j,2},V_{j,3},\dots\}
. For each
j
, if
\mathscr{V}_{j}
is a finite set, then we define
V_{j,l}:=\varnothing
for
l\geq\#\mathscr{V}_{j}+1
.

We endow
\mathcal{E}
with a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
bundle metric so that the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-norms are defined on the space of sections of
\mathcal{E}
, for every
0<\beta\leq\alpha
such that
\beta<\infty
.

Since each open subset in
\mathscr{V}_{1}\cup\dots\cup\mathscr{V}_{n+1}=\{V_{j,l}:1\leq j\leq n+1,\ l\geq 0\}
is precompact in
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
, we can take a partition of unity
\{\chi_{j,l}:1\leq j\leq n+1,\ l\geq 0\}\subset\mathscr{C}_{c}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{N}_{k})
associated with
\{V_{j,l}\}_{j,l}
, where we emphasize that each
\chi_{j,l}
has compact support in
\mathfrak{N}_{k}
.

We then define for
j=1,\dots,n+1
,

\xi_{(k-1)(n-1)+j}:=\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}2^{-l}\frac{\chi_{j,l}\cdot\eta^{\tau(V_{j,l})}}{\|\chi_{j,l}\cdot\eta^{\tau(V_{j,l})}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,l)}}}.

(3.4.3)

Here the
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,l)}
norm is only technical. One can use
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-norm if
\alpha<\infty
, and
\mathscr{C}^{l}
-norm if
\alpha=\infty
.

Clearly the sum in (
3.4.3
) converges in the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{E})
with respect to the fixed bundle metric. In particular we know
\xi_{(k-1)(n-1)+j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{G})
.

For each
p\in\mathfrak{N}_{k}
, there is a
1\leq j_{0}\leq n+1
and
V_{j_{0},l}\in\mathscr{V}_{j_{0}}
such that
\chi_{j_{0},l}(p)\neq 0
.
By property
(a)
, the sum in (
3.4.3
) has at most 1 nonzero at each point. Thus for such
j
,
\xi_{(k-1)(n+1)+j_{0}}(p)=c_{j_{0}}(p)\eta^{\tau(V_{j_{0},l})}(p)
for some constant
c_{j_{0}}(p)>0
that comes from (
3.4.3
).

Thus by (
3.4.2
), for such
j_{0}=j_{0}(p)
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Span}(\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{k(n+1)}(p))\geq

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Span}(\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{(k-1)(n+1)}(p),\xi_{(k-1)(n+1)+j_{0}}(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Span}(\xi_{1}(p),\dots,\xi_{(k-1)(n+1)}(p),\eta^{\tau(V_{j_{0},l})}(p))\geq k.

Therefore (
3.4.1
) is satisfied for all
p\in\mathfrak{N}_{k}
.

On the other hand, if
p\notin\mathfrak{N}_{k}
, then we have
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{p}\leq k-1
. By induction hypothesis (
3.4.1
) is already satisfied. Therefore
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k(n+1)}
are the desired sections for the case
k
in (
2.2.24
).

By reaching the case
k=r
, we get
r(n+1)
many sections and complete the proof.
∎

Unlike Definition
2.3.5
, on a singular subbundle
\mathcal{G}
of rank
r
, locally we may not be able to find
r
-many sections that span
\mathcal{G}
in a neighborhood. See the following example.

Example 3.4.6
.

On
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we let
\mathcal{G}_{1}:=\{(0,0)\}\cup\{(x,v):x\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\backslash\{0\},\ x\cdot v=0\}\subset T\mathbb{R}^{3}
. So
\mathcal{G}_{1}=\{(0,0)\}\cup\coprod_{x\neq 0}T_{\frac{x}{|x|}}\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Clearly
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{1}=2
.

Take finitely many vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
(in fact we can take
m=3
) that span
T\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we see that
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is spanned by smooth vector fields
\big[x\mapsto e^{-1/|x|}X_{j}(\frac{x}{|x|})\big]
,
1\leq j\leq m
. Therefore
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is a smooth singular subbundle.

Suppose we can find continuous vector fields
Y_{1},Y_{2}:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that
\mathcal{G}_{1}|_{U}
is spanned by
Y_{1}
and
Y_{2}
. Since
U\ni 0
, we have
B^{3}(0,\delta)\Subset U
for some
\delta>0
. Thus on the boundary
\partial B^{3}(0,\delta)=\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
Y_{1}|_{\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}}
and
Y_{2}|_{\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}}
span
\mathcal{G}_{1}|_{\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}}\simeq T\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Since
T\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a regular vector bundle over
\mathbb{S}^{2}
with rank
2
, we see that
Y_{1}|_{\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}}
and
Y_{2}|_{\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}}
are linearly independent at every point in
\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}
. However by the Hairy Ball Theorem
Y_{1}|_{\delta\mathbb{S}^{2}}
must have zero points, contradiction.

Therefore we need at least 3 vector fields that span
\mathcal{G}_{1}
in a neighborhood of
0
.

Unlike the regular case it does not make sense to talk about sections of singular subbundle which are not pointwisely defined.

Example 3.4.7
.

Consider
\mathcal{G}_{2}:=((\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})\times\mathbb{R}^{1})\cup\{(0,0)\}\subset T\mathbb{R}
. It is a smooth singular subbundle since it is generated by
x\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
.

Note that
\mathcal{G}_{2}(x)=\mathbb{R}^{1}
for almost every
x\in\mathbb{R}
since
\{0\}\subset\mathbb{R}
is a zero measured set. If we define a “
L^{\infty}
-section” of
\mathcal{G}_{2}
to be a
L^{\infty}
-vector field that lays in
\mathcal{G}_{2}
for almost every point, then
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{2})=L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};T\mathbb{R})
. There is no distinguishment between
\mathcal{G}_{2}
and
T\mathbb{R}
in this setting.

Another reason why we cannot define distributional sections of
\mathcal{G}
is that we do not have (
3.1.6
) for
\alpha,\gamma>0
.

Example 3.4.8
.

Consider
\mathcal{G}_{3}:=((0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{1})\cup((-\infty,0]\times\{0\})\subset T\mathbb{R}
, this is a smooth singular subbundle generated by the smooth vector field
e^{-\frac{1}{x}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
.

For every non-integer
\gamma>0
, we see that
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{3})
implies
|X(x)|\leq C|x|^{\gamma}
for
x>0
closed to 0. (In fact
|X(x)|\leq C|x|^{\gamma}\log\frac{1}{|x|}
for
x>0
closed to
0
, if
\gamma=1,2,3,\dots
.)

On the other hand, suppose
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{3})
for some non-integer
\alpha>\gamma
, we can write
X=\sum_{j=1}^{N}f^{j}Y_{j}
where
Y_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{3})
and
f^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
. Clearly
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{3})
,
f^{j}
are bounded near
0
and
|Y_{j}(x)|\leq C_{j}|x|^{\alpha}
for
x>0
near
0
. Therefore
|X(x)|\leq C\max(0,x)^{\alpha}\ll\max(0,x)^{\gamma}
for
x
near
0
.

To summarize, for non-integers
0<\gamma<\alpha
, we have
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})\otimes_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{3})\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R};\mathcal{G}_{3})
and the inclusion is strict. (This is still true if
\alpha
or
\gamma
is a positive integer.)

The following example shows that the
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-involutivity of modules are not equivalent if we change the index
\beta
.

Example 3.4.9

(
[
Lew14
, Example 8.3]
)
.

Let
\alpha>0
. Let
\mathscr{F}_{3}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};T\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-submodule generated by
X(x,y)=(x^{2}+y^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
and
Y(x,y)=(x^{4}+y^{4})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
.

By direct computation

\textstyle[X,Y](x,y)=-\frac{2y(x^{4}+y^{4})}{x^{2}+y^{2}}X+\frac{4x^{3}(x^{2}+y^{2})}{x^{4}+y^{4}}Y.

Note that the coefficients
\frac{2y(x^{4}+y^{4})}{x^{2}+y^{2}}
and
\frac{4x^{3}(x^{2}+y^{2})}{x^{4}+y^{4}}
are uniquely determined, because
X
and
Y
are linearly independent almost everywhere (they are linearly independent on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
).

We see that
\mathscr{F}_{3}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{Lip}}
-involutive but not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-involutive for any
\beta>1
. In particular if
\alpha>2
, then
\mathscr{F}_{3}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-involutive.

Nevertheless,
\mathcal{G}_{4}:=\operatorname{Span}\mathscr{F}_{3}
is still pointwise integrable. If we choose the submodule
\mathscr{F}_{3}^{\alpha}
to be the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
span of
X
and
Y^{\prime}:=(x^{2}+y^{2})\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, then
[X,Y^{\prime}]=-2yY^{\prime}+2xX
, which means
\mathscr{F}_{3}^{\alpha}
is always
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-involutive.

In the classical setting, we know if a smooth singular subbundle
\mathcal{G}\subseteq T\mathfrak{M}
is (pointwise) integrable, then
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{G})
is (smoothly) involutive, and the converse direction is false, see Example
3.4.11
below.

In the smooth setting, the Hermann’s theorem
[
Her60
]
(see also
[
Lew14
, Section 8.3]
and
[
Lav18
, Theorem 3]
) says the following, which is the smooth version to Proposition
3.3.2
:

Proposition 3.4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-generalized subbundle of a smooth manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. Suppose there exists a finitely generated
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-module
\mathscr{F}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\mathcal{G})
that spans
\mathcal{G}
(i.e.
\mathcal{G}_{p}=\operatorname{Span}\{X(p):X\in\mathscr{F}\}
for all
p\in\mathfrak{M}
) such that
\mathscr{F}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-involutive. Then
\mathcal{G}
is pointwise integrable.

In other words the
\mathcal{G}_{5}
in Example
3.4.11
below cannot be spanned by a finitely generated
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}
-module.

However, if we allow to use certain distributional involutivity on singular subbundles, then the finitely generated involutivity may not imply integrability.

Example 3.4.11

(
[
Lew14
, Example 8.2]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{5}:=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x},e^{-\frac{1}{x}}\mathbf{1}_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial y})\subset T\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We see that
\mathcal{G}_{5}
is a smooth singular subbundle,
\mathcal{G}_{5}(x,y)=\mathbb{R}_{x}\times\{0\}
if
x\leq 0
and
\mathcal{G}_{5}(x,y)=\mathbb{R}^{2}
if
x>0
.

For every
y\in\mathbb{R}
,
\mathcal{G}_{5}(0,y)=\mathbb{R}\times\{0\}
has rank 1. If
\gamma(t)=(x(t),y(t))
is a
C^{1}
integral curve passing
(0,y)
, say
x(t)>0
when
t>0
small, then we must have
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{G}_{5}(\gamma(t))=2>\operatorname{rank}\operatorname{Span}(\dot{\gamma}(t))=1
when
t>0
small. Thus
\mathcal{G}_{5}
is not pointwise integrable.

On the other hand, for every
\alpha>1
, let
X:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
and
Y^{\alpha}:=\max(0,x)^{\alpha}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
, we see that
\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}_{5}:=\{X,Y^{\alpha}\}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};T\mathbb{R}^{2})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-submodule of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields, and

\textstyle[X,Y^{\alpha}]=\alpha\max(0,x)^{\alpha-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}=(\operatorname{p.\!v.}\frac{\alpha}{x})\cdot Y^{\alpha}.

Here
\operatorname{p.\!v.}\frac{1}{x}=\frac{d}{dx}\log|x|
, which is a well-defined
\mathscr{C}^{-1}
-function on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,y}
by Lemma
2.1.8
.

Therefore
\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}_{5}
is finitely generated and
\mathscr{C}^{-1}
-involutive, but for the ground space of
\mathscr{F}^{\alpha}
, the singular subbundle
\mathcal{G}_{5}
is not (pointwise) integrable.

For more examples and discussions of the singular Frobenius theorem, we refer
[
Lew14
]
and
[
Lav18
]
to readers.

Remark 3.4.12
.

Based on Theorem
2
or Proposition
3.4.2
, we know
\mathcal{G}_{5}
cannot support a
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
-module which is
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
-involutive.

However, we do not know whether Proposition
3.4.2
is still true if we replace the
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}
-involutivity by
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-involutivity for
\beta<0
, even when the module is smooth. More precisely we can ask the following problem.

Problem 3.4.13
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a smooth manifold and let
\mathscr{F}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
be a submodule over
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. Let
-1<\beta<0
.

Suppose
\mathscr{F}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-involutive. Does
\mathscr{F}
always pointwise integrable?

The major difficulty of Problem
3.4.13
is somehow similar to that for Problem
3.3.13
. If we consider
A_{1},\dots,A_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in the proof of Lemma
2.3.26
, then for the right hand side of (
2.3.19
) we only have
|\frac{d}{dt}F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(t)|\leq C_{2}^{\prime}(2^{|\beta|\sigma}|F^{\sigma}\circ\gamma^{\sigma}(t)|+2^{-(1-\delta/2)\sigma})
, which blows up too fast and cannot tends to 0 if we try to use Gronwall’s inequality.

Chapter 4
The Variants of Malgrange’s Method

In this chapter we give two key estimates, one for proving the complex Frobebius theorem, Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
, one for proving the quantitative Frobenius theorem, Theorem
8
. These two estimates are inspired by Malgrange’s proof
of the Newlander-Nirenberg Theorem
[
Mal69
]
, see Sections
4.1.1
and
4.2.1
for their overviews.

4.1
Estimates of Elliptic Structures with Parameters

Let
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
be two smooth manifolds. In this section we consider the product manifold
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
as the ambient space, so
(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}=\coprod_{q\in\mathfrak{N}}\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is a subbundle of
\mathbb{C}T(\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})
whose (complex) rank equals to the (real) dimension of
\mathfrak{M}
.

We consider a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
involutive complex tangential subbundle
\mathcal{S}
of
\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
such that
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
. Note that
(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
is automatically involutive, so
\mathcal{S}
is a complex Frobenius structure.

We recall the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N})
(
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
,
\beta>0
) in Definition
2.3.2
, the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-subbundle
\mathcal{S}
in Definition
2.3.6
, and the involutivity of
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
in Definition
2.3.7
(also see Remark
2.3.8
). We endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard (real and complex) coordinate system
t=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})
,
z=(z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively.

To shorten the indices, we set for
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\gamma^{\sim\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma,\alpha+1\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\gamma^{\wedge\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma-,\alpha+1\right),

\displaystyle\text{for }\alpha\in(1,\infty);

(4.1.1)

\displaystyle\gamma^{\sim\alpha}=\gamma^{\wedge\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma-,(2-\tfrac{1}{\alpha})\gamma,\alpha+1\right),

\displaystyle\text{for }\alpha\in(\tfrac{1}{2},1];

\displaystyle(\gamma-)^{\sim\alpha}=(\gamma-)^{\wedge\alpha}:=\min\left(\gamma-,(2-\tfrac{1}{\alpha})\gamma-,\alpha+1\right),

\displaystyle\text{for }\alpha\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\infty).

Here for
\beta\in\{\gamma,\gamma-:\gamma\in\mathbb{R}_{+}\}
,
\beta^{\sim\alpha}
stands for the parameter regularity of the coordinate charts, and
\beta^{\wedge\alpha}
stands for the parameter regularity of the coordinate vector fields.

Theorem 4.1

(Elliptic structures with parameter)
.

Let
r,m,q\geq 0
, let
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathfrak{N}
be two smooth manifolds with dimensions
(r+2m)
and
q
respectively. Let
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
be two indices such that either

•

\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1)\cup(1,\infty)
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
; or

•

\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},1]
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{-}(=\{\gamma-:\gamma\in(0,\infty)\})
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-involutive subbundle of rank
r+m
such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
.

Then for any
u_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
and
v_{0}\in\mathfrak{N}
, there are neighborhoods
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
u_{0}
,
V\subseteq\mathfrak{N}
of
v_{0}
and a map
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\times V\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
such that

(1)

F^{\prime\prime\prime}:V\to\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
is a smooth coordinate chart of
\mathfrak{N}
that is independent of
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
, such that
F^{\prime\prime\prime}(v_{0})=0
;
F^{\prime}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}
is a smooth map that is independent of
V\subseteq\mathfrak{N}
, such that
F^{\prime}(u_{0})=0
.

(2)

F:U\times V\to F(U\times V)
is homeomorphism. And for each
v\in V
,
[u\mapsto(F^{\prime}(u),F^{\prime\prime}(u,v))]:U\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate chart.

(3)

F^{\prime\prime}(u_{0},v_{0})=0
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
XF^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
for every
C^{\infty}
vector field
X
on
\mathfrak{M}
.

(4)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are well-defined
\mathscr{C}^{\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}
-vector fields and span
\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}
.

For the parameterization side, let
\Phi=(\Phi^{\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime}):F(U\times V)\to\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
be the inverse map of
F
, and let
\Omega:=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
be any neighborhood of
(0,0,0)
which is contained in
F(U\times V)
. Then

(5)

\Phi^{\prime\prime}:V\to\mathfrak{N}
is a smooth regular parameterization. Moreover
\Phi^{\prime\prime}=(F^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\mathrm{Inv}}
.

(6)

\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
is homeomorphic onto its image, and
\Phi(0,0,0)=(u_{0},v_{0})
. For each
s\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}
,
\Phi^{\prime}(\cdot,s):\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\to\mathfrak{M}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-regular parameterization.

(7)

\Phi^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi^{\prime}}{\partial t^{j}},\frac{\partial\Phi^{\prime}}{\partial z^{k}},\frac{\partial\Phi^{\prime}}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(8)

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi(t,z,s)}\leq\mathbb{C}T_{\Phi^{\prime}(t,z,s)}\mathfrak{M}
is spanned by
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,z,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,z,s),\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}}(t,z,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}}(t,z,s)
, for every
(t,z,s)\in\Omega
.

In particular,

(9)

When
\beta^{\sim\alpha}>1
, i.e.
\min(\beta,(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta,\alpha+1)>1
,
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta,\alpha+1)}
-coordinate chart and
\Phi
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta,\alpha+1)}
-regular parameterization.

Remark 4.1.1
.

(i)

When
\alpha>1
we shall see in Section
5.3.3
that the index “
\beta-
” for
XF^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta-}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta-)}
are both sharp.

(ii)

Theorem
4.1
implies Street’s result
[
Str20b
, Theorem 1.1]
for the sharp estimate on elliptic structure by considering
\alpha=\beta>1
and forgetting the
s
-parameter. (Recall
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}(U,V)=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U\times V)
from Lemma
2.2.15
.)

Remark 4.1.2
.

For
\alpha>1
, our results allow the range
\beta\in(0,1]
and
\beta\in(\alpha,\alpha+1]
.

For the case
\beta\leq 1
, it does not make sense to talk about
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-coordinate charts nor
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-parameterizations. Nevertheless by
(2)
we have

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|_{(u,v)}=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,v))^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}|_{u}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|_{(u,v)}=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,v))^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|_{u}
, since
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,v))
is a coordinate chart for a fix
v\in V
. Alternatively by
(5)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}\circ F
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}\circ F
are both continuous. Either way
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
are collections of pointwise defined vector fields.

For the case
\beta>\alpha
, the parameter part is actually more regular than the part of spatial variable. In this case
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields on
U\times V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}
.

Remark 4.1.3
.

If we only consider a family of elliptic structures rather than a family of complex Frobenius structures, and assume
\alpha,\beta
are non-integers, then Theorem
4.1
is much stronger than
[
Gon20
, Theorem 1.3]
:

•

We consider all
(\alpha,\beta)
such that
\alpha>1
and
0<\beta\leq\alpha+1
. In
[
Gon20
, Theorem 1.3]
only the case
\alpha>\beta+3\geq 4
and the case
\alpha=\beta
is considered.

•

For the regularity of the coordinate charts we have the sharp estimate
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(U,V)
.
[
Gon20
, Theorem 1.3]
only gives
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-,\beta-}(U,V)
.

We give a detailed overview to the idea of its proof in Section
4.1.1
using Malgrange’s factorization.

4.1.1
Overview of the estimate

For vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
, we write
X=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
following from the convention in Section
1.2
.

The most important part is to find the map
F^{\prime\prime}:U\times V\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
that has the desired regularity property. We factorize
F^{\prime\prime}
to the following composition of maps:

F^{\prime\prime}=\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}\circ H\circ L.

(4.1.2)

Endow
\mathbb{R}^{r},\mathbb{C}^{m},\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinates
\tau=(\tau^{1},\dots,\tau^{r})
,
w=(w^{1},\dots,w^{m})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively.
There are smooth coordinate charts
(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime}):U_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}
and
L^{\prime\prime\prime}:V_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{N}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
, such that by taking
L=(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime},L^{\prime\prime\prime}):U_{0}\times V_{0}\to\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
,
L_{*}\mathcal{S}
has a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{(\tau,w),s}
-local basis on
\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
with the form

X=\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
X^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&0&A^{\prime}\\
0&I_{m}&A^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\tau}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002ptA:=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}\\
A^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix},

(4.1.3)

where
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times m})}
is small enough. This can be done by some scaling argument, see Lemma
4.1.18
.

For simplicity we only illustrate the case
\alpha>1
, where we can assume
0<\beta\leq\alpha+1
. When
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
the argument is the same except we replace the indices
\beta
and
\beta-
by
(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta
and the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
by
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}+\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}\mathscr{C}^{(2-\frac{1}{\alpha})\beta}
.

There is a “
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{(\tau,w),s}
-coordinate change”
(\sigma,\zeta,s)=H(\tau,w,s)
where
H:\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}_{\tau,w}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s}\to\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}_{\sigma,\zeta}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s}
is of the form
H(\tau,w,s)=(\tau,H^{\prime\prime}(\tau,w,s),s)
, such that the local generators for
(H\circ L)_{*}\mathcal{S}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and real-analytic in
(\sigma,\zeta)
-variable. More precisely, the canonical local basis of
(H\circ L)_{*}\mathcal{S}
has the the form

\begin{pmatrix}T\\
Z\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&0&B^{\prime}\\
0&I_{m}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\sigma}\\
\partial_{\zeta}\\
\partial_{\bar{\zeta}}\end{pmatrix},\ B=\left.\begin{pmatrix}B^{\prime}\\
B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\right|_{(\sigma,\zeta,s)}:=\left.\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right|_{H^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\sigma,\zeta,s)}.

(4.1.4)

Here
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
are defined on
\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}_{\sigma,\zeta}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s}\subset(\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m})\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
and admit
(\sigma,\zeta)
-variables holomorphic extensions to the domain
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{H}^{r+2m}\times\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{q}\subset\mathbb{C}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
.

In this case
H\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\tau,w}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\tau,w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
, note that
H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau},H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\tau,w}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}_{\tau,w}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta-}_{(\tau,w),s}
has a regularity loss on the parameter, while
T,Z\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\sigma,\zeta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta}_{(\sigma,\zeta),s}
retrieve the full
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
regularity on the parameter.

We denote by
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{Z}
the collections of holomorphic vector fields corresponding to
T
and
Z
. Finally, applying Proposition
2.3.45
, the holomorphic Frobenius theorem with parameter, on
\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{Z}
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\sigma,\zeta}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
-map
G^{\prime\prime}(\sigma,\zeta,s)
such that
(H\circ L)_{*}\mathcal{S}^{\bot}
is spanned by
dG^{\prime\prime},d\bar{\zeta},ds
. See Lemma
4.1.21
.

In this way, the composition map
\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}\circ H\circ L
is as desired.

To make
B(\sigma,\zeta,s)
real-analytic, we impose extra conditions on
H
: we require
H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\tau,w}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\tau,w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}
, and
H
satisfies the following condition:

\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\partial T_{j}}{\partial\sigma^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\partial Z_{k}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{k}}=0.

(4.1.5)

Rewriting (
4.1.5
) in the
(\tau,w,s)
-space using the coordinate change
H
, we get: for
l=1,\dots,m
,
(\tau,w)\in\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}
and
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left(\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\tau}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right)_{j}\left(\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right)_{j}^{l}\Bigg|_{(\tau,w;s)}

(4.1.6)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left(\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\tau}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right)_{k+r+m}\!\!\left(\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\!\!\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right)_{k+r}^{l}\Bigg|_{(\tau,w;s)}=0.

See Lemma
4.1.19
for this deduction and Proposition
4.1.6
for the existence of such
H
.

We write
B^{\prime}=(b_{j}^{l})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq j\leq r\\
1\leq l\leq m\end{subarray}}
and
B^{\prime\prime}=(b_{r+k}^{l})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq m\\
1\leq l\leq m\end{subarray}}
.
By Lemma
2.3.9

(iii)
the involutivity of
\mathcal{S}
implies that
T_{1},\dots,T_{r},Z_{1},\dots,Z_{m}
are commutative. Writing out the conditions
[T_{j},T_{j^{\prime}}]=0
,
[T_{j},Z_{k}]=0
and
[Z_{k},Z_{k^{\prime}}]=0
in terms of
B^{\prime}
and
B^{\prime\prime}
and combining (
4.1.5
), we have the following:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial b_{j^{\prime}}^{l}}{\partial\sigma^{j}}-\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial\sigma^{j^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Big(b_{j^{\prime}}^{q}\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{q}}-b_{j}^{q}\frac{\partial b_{j^{\prime}}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{q}}\Big),

\displaystyle 1\leq j<j^{\prime}\leq r,1\leq l\leq m;

(4.1.7)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial b_{r+k}^{l}}{\partial\sigma^{j}}-\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial\zeta^{k}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Big(b_{r+k}^{q}\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{q}}-b_{j}^{q}\frac{\partial b_{r+k}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{q}}\Big),

\displaystyle 1\leq j\leq r,1\leq k,l\leq m;

\displaystyle\frac{\partial b_{r+k^{\prime}}^{l}}{\partial\zeta^{k}}-\frac{\partial b_{r+k}^{l}}{\partial\zeta^{k^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{q=1}^{m}\Big(b_{r+k^{\prime}}^{q}\frac{\partial b_{r+k}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{q}}-b_{r+k}^{q}\frac{\partial b_{r+k^{\prime}}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{q}}\Big),

\displaystyle 1\leq k<{k^{\prime}}\leq m,1\leq l\leq m;

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\partial b_{j}^{l}}{\partial\sigma^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\partial b_{r+k}^{l}}{\partial\bar{\zeta}^{k}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle 1\leq l\leq m.

(
4.1.7
) is a quasilinear PDE system of the form
DB=\Theta(B,\nabla_{\sigma,\zeta}B)
, where
D
is a first order differential operator with constant (vector-valued) coefficients and
\Theta
is a product map with constant (vector-valued) coefficients. We can check that
D^{*}D=\Delta_{\sigma}+\Box_{\zeta}
is the real and complex Laplacian acting on the components. So (
4.1.7
) is an elliptic PDE system and in Proposition
4.1.12
we will see that
B
admits a
(\sigma,\operatorname{Re}\zeta,\operatorname{Im}\zeta)
-variable holomorphic extension. See Lemma
4.1.20
.

4.1.2
The existence proposition

In this subsection we use
\tau=(\tau^{1},\dots,\tau^{r})
,
w=(w^{1},\dots,w^{m})
,
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
as the standard (real or complex) coordinate system for
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
respectively. We consider the unit balls
\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{r+2m}
and
\mathbb{B}^{q}\subset\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
.

Convention 4.1.4
.

We denote
\mathcal{I}:\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}\to\mathbb{B}^{2m}
,
\mathcal{I}(\tau,w,s):=w
as the projection map.

For a matrix
B=(b_{j}^{k})
, we use
b_{j}^{k}
to denote the
j
-th row
k
-th column entry of
B
.

We use
A=\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}\\
A^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}:\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}_{\tau,w}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times m}
as a matrix map,
H^{\prime\prime}=(H^{\prime\prime 1},\dots,H^{\prime\prime m}):\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}_{\tau,w}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}
as a vector-valued map whose image is set to be a
m
-dimensional row vector. We use

H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime 1}}{\partial\tau^{1}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime m}}{\partial\tau^{1}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime 1}}{\partial\tau^{r}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime m}}{\partial\tau^{r}}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002ptH^{\prime\prime}_{w}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime 1}}{\partial w^{1}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime m}}{\partial w^{1}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime 1}}{\partial w^{m}}&\cdots&\frac{\partial H^{\prime\prime m}}{\partial w^{m}}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}=\begin{pmatrix}H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
H^{\prime\prime}_{w}\\
H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}

(4.1.8)

Let
\Psi,\Theta,\Lambda
be matrix-valued functions of
H^{\prime\prime}
and
A
as follow:

\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]:=\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix},\ \Theta[A;H^{\prime\prime}]:=\begin{pmatrix}I&H^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
&H^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}H^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix},\ \Lambda[A;H^{\prime\prime}]:=\Theta[A;H^{\prime\prime}]^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}+A^{\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}+A^{\prime\prime}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.1.9)

Remark 4.1.5
.

One can see that
\Psi,\Theta,\Lambda
are all rational functions in the components of
A
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}
.

We are going to consider the following PDE system, whose deduction is in Lemma
4.1.19
.

Recall
\partial_{\tau}=[\partial_{\tau^{1}},\dots,\partial_{\tau^{r}}]^{\top}
in Section
1.2
. In this subsection we prove the following:

Proposition 4.1.6
.

Let
\mathscr{X}^{1},\mathscr{X}^{0},\mathscr{X}^{-1}
be bi-parameter Hölder-Zygmund structures such that:

(X.1)

\mathscr{X}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\subseteq\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\subseteq C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\cap\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})
.

(X.2)

\nabla_{\tau,w}:\mathscr{X}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}:\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
are bounded.

(X.3)

\Delta_{\tau}+\Box_{w}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\partial_{\tau^{j}}^{2}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\partial_{w^{k}\bar{w}^{k}}^{2}:\{f\in\mathscr{X}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q}):f|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}=0\}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})
is invertible.

(X.4)

The product maps
\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\times\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})
and
\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\times\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q})
are bilinearly bounded.

Using Convention
4.1.4
, there exists a
\delta_{0}=\delta_{0}(\mathscr{X},r,m,q)>0
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(\mathscr{X},r,m,q)>1
, such that if
A\in\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times m})
satisfies
\|A\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}<\delta_{0}
, then there is a unique
H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{X}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{m})
that solves the following PDE system (
4.1.6
) and satisfies the boundary condition
H^{\prime\prime}|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{r+2m})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}=\mathcal{I}
, and satisfies
\|H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{1}}<C_{0}\|A\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}
.

Before we prove Proposition
4.1.6
, we need to show the PDE (
4.1.6
) is well-defined, in the sense that
\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}](\tau,w,s)
and
\Theta[A;H^{\prime\prime}](\tau,w,s)
are invertible matrices for all
(\tau,w,s)\in\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
.

For
\mathscr{X}^{1},\mathscr{X}^{0},\mathscr{X}^{-1}
that satisfies the assumptions of Proposition
4.1.6
, and for
\varepsilon>0
, we define spaces

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon}:=\{A\in\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times m}):\|A\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}<\varepsilon\},

(4.1.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon}:=\{H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{X}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{m}):H^{\prime\prime}|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{r+2m})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}\equiv\mathcal{I},\ \|H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{1}}<\varepsilon\},

with metrics induced by the norm structures of their respective ambient Banach spaces.

Lemma 4.1.7
.

There is a
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(r,m,q,\mathscr{X})>0
such that

(i)

For every
A\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}
,
H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}
,
(\tau,w)\in\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}
and
s\in\mathbb{B}^{q}
, the matrices
\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}](\tau,w,s)
and
\Theta[A;H^{\prime\prime}](\tau,w,s)
given in (
4.1.9
) are both invertible.

(ii)

For every
A\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}
and
H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}
,

\|\Lambda[A;H^{\prime\prime}]\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}\leq\varepsilon_{1}^{-1}(\|H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{1}}+\|A\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}).

(4.1.11)

(iii)

Denote by
T[A;H^{\prime\prime}]^{l}
the left hand side of (
4.1.6
) for
l=1,\dots,m
, and
T[A;H^{\prime\prime}]:=(T[A;H^{\prime\prime}]^{l})_{l=1}^{m}
as the vector-valued function. Then
T:\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{m})
is a real-analytic map, in particular second order Fréchet differentiable. Moreover

\frac{\partial T}{\partial H^{\prime\prime}}[A;H^{\prime\prime}]\Big|_{A=0;H^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{I}}=\Delta_{\tau}+\Box_{w}:\{F\in\mathscr{X}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{m}):F|_{(\partial\mathbb{B}^{r+2m})\times\mathbb{B}^{q}}=0\}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{m}).

(4.1.12)

Here
\Delta_{\tau}+\Box_{w}=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\tau^{j}\partial\tau^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial w^{k}\partial\bar{w}^{k}}
is the mixed real and complex Laplacian.

Proof.

We let
\varepsilon_{1}
be a small constant which may change from line to line.

Note that when
H^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{I}
and
A=0
, we have

\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}=\begin{pmatrix}0_{r\times m}\\
I_{m}\\
0_{m\times m}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002pt\Psi[\mathcal{I}]=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}\\
&I_{m}\\
&&I_{m}\end{pmatrix}=I_{r+2m},\hskip 10.00002pt\Theta[0;\mathcal{I}]=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}+0_{r\times r}&0_{r\times m}\\
0_{m\times r}&I_{m}+0_{m\times m}\end{pmatrix}=I_{r+m}.

By cofactor representations of the matrices,
\Psi^{-1}
,
\Theta^{-1}
and
\Lambda
are rational functions in the components of
A
,
\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}
, which can be expressed as power series expansions in the components of
A
,
\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}
at the point
A=0
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}=\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}=\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}
.

By Lemma
2.2.30
, inverting a
\mathscr{X}^{0}
-matrix is a real-analytic map near the identity matrix. Therefore there is a
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}>0
such that if
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}<\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\|\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}<\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}
, then
\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]
and
\Theta[A;H^{\prime\prime}]
are invertible matrices at every point in
\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
, and moreover as maps between
\mathscr{X}^{0}
-spaces,

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}:\{\|\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I})\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}<\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\}\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+2m)\times(r+2m)}),

\displaystyle\Lambda:\{\|A\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}<\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\}\times\{\|\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I})\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}<\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}\}\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times m}),

are both real-analytic.

By the assumption
(X.2)
there is a
\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}<C^{-1}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}
where
C=C(\mathscr{X},r,m,q)>0
is a large constant, such that
H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}}
implies
\|\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I})\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}<\tilde{\varepsilon}_{1}
.

Thus by
(X.1)
, when
\varepsilon\leq\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}
,
\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]
and
\Theta[A;H^{\prime\prime}]
are pointwise invertible in
\mathbb{B}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{B}^{q}
. This finishes the proof of
(i)
. Moreover we have the following real-analytic maps:

\displaystyle\Psi^{-1}:\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}}\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+2m)\times(r+2m)}),\hskip 10.00002pt\Lambda:\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}}\to\mathscr{X}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+2m)\times m}).

(4.1.13)

Since
\Psi[\mathcal{I}]=I_{r+2m}
and
\Lambda[0;\mathcal{I}]=0
, taking the first order terms of the power expansion we have

\displaystyle\|\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]^{-1}-I_{r+2m}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}-\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\Lambda[A;H^{\prime\prime}]\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}+\|\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}-\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}},

(4.1.14)

whenever
A\in\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}}
and
H^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{2}}
. Therefore by (
4.1.14
) with possibly shrinking
\varepsilon_{1}
, we get (
4.1.11
) and prove
(ii)
.

We now prove
(iii)
. From the expression (
4.1.6
),
T[A;H^{\prime\prime}]
is the linear combinations to the components of
\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]^{-1}\otimes\nabla_{\tau,w}\Lambda[A;H^{\prime\prime}]
. As the maps in (
4.1.13
),
\Psi^{-1}
and
\Lambda
are both real-analytic and hence Fréchet differentiable. Therefore

\big[(A,H^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\nabla_{\tau,w}\Lambda[A;H^{\prime\prime}]\big]:\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+m)\times m\times(r+2m)}),

is also real-analytic and differentiable.

By
(X.4)
that the product map
\mathscr{X}^{0}\times\mathscr{X}^{-1}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}
is bounded hence is differentiable, we see that

\big[(A,H^{\prime\prime})\mapsto\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]^{-1}\otimes\nabla_{\tau,w}\Lambda[A;H^{\prime\prime}]\big]:\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{r+2m},\mathbb{B}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{(r+2m)^{2}\times m(r+m)(r+2m)}),

is also differentiable. Taking linear combinations we get that
T:\mathcal{U}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\times\mathcal{V}_{\mathscr{X},\varepsilon_{1}}\to\mathscr{X}^{-1}
is Fréchet differentiable.

Since
H^{\prime\prime}\mapsto\Lambda[0;H^{\prime\prime}]
is a real-analytic function to the components of
\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}
, taking second order power expansion at
\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}=\nabla_{\tau,w}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}=\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}
we have

\Lambda[0;H^{\prime\prime}]=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&\\
&I_{m}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\end{pmatrix}+O(|\nabla_{\tau,w}H^{\prime\prime}-\nabla_{\tau,w}\mathcal{I}|^{2}).

By Corollary
2.2.26

(i)
again, since
\varepsilon_{1}
is small, we have

\bigg\|\Lambda[0;H^{\prime\prime}]-\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\end{pmatrix}\bigg\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}\lesssim\|\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I})\|_{\mathscr{X}^{0}}^{2}\lesssim\|H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\mathscr{X}^{1}}^{2}.

(4.1.15)

Taking
\nabla_{\tau,w}
on
\Lambda[0;H^{\prime\prime}]
, since by (
4.1.14
) that
\Psi[H^{\prime\prime}]^{-1}=I_{r+2m}+O(|\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I})|)
near
H^{\prime\prime}=\mathcal{I}
, we have

\displaystyle T[0;H^{\prime\prime}]^{l}=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{r}\left((I_{r+2m}+O(\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}))\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\tau}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right)_{j}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\end{pmatrix}+O(\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}))^{2}\right)_{j}^{l}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left((I_{r+2m}+O(\nabla_{\tau,w}(H^{\prime\prime}-\mathcal{I}))\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\tau}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\right)_{k+r+m}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\bar{H}^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}\\
