,
\operatorname{Span}d(G^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,s))=\operatorname{Span}(T(\cdot,s),Z(\cdot,s))|_{U_{1}}^{\bot}=(H\circ L)_{*}\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times\{s\}}^{\bot}
. Equivalently we have
\operatorname{Span}(dG^{\prime\prime},ds)=(H\circ L)_{*}\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times V_{1}}^{\bot}
in the sense of Convention
4.1.22
.

Therefore,
(\operatorname{Span}d(G^{\prime\prime}\circ H),ds)|_{H^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})}=L_{*}\mathcal{S}|_{H^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})}
in the sense of Convention
4.1.22
, which means

\operatorname{Span}(d(G^{\prime\prime}\circ H\circ L),dL^{\prime\prime\prime})|_{(H\circ L)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})}=\mathcal{S}|_{(H\circ L)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})}^{\bot}\text{ in the sense of Convention \ref {Conv::EllipticPara::CoSpan}}.

(4.1.42)

Next, we claim that

(L.3)

\big(dL^{\prime},d(\operatorname{Re}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H\circ L(\cdot,v_{0})),d(\operatorname{Im}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H\circ L(\cdot,v_{0}))\big)
is a collection of
(r+2m)
-real differentials that are linearly independent at
u_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
.

Indeed, we have
\operatorname{Span}(d(\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}\circ H),ds)=L_{*}\bar{\mathcal{S}}^{\bot}
in the sense of Convention
4.1.22
, which means for each
s\in V_{1}
,

\operatorname{Span}(d(\operatorname{Re}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s)),d(\operatorname{Im}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s))^{\bot}=\operatorname{Span}(d(G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s)),d(\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}\circ H(\cdot,s)))^{\bot}=L_{*}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{H^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times\{s\})},

has rank
r=\dim\mathfrak{M}-(r+2m)
. Therefore,
(d(\operatorname{Re}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s),d(\operatorname{Im}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s))
is a linearly independent collection in the domain for all
s\in V_{1}
.

On the other hand, by (
4.1.3
) we have

L_{*}\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}+A^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\frac{\partial}{\partial w}+A^{\prime\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}})
, so
L_{*}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}+\frac{1}{2}A^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial w}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{A}^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}}
, which means
L_{*}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})^{\bot}\cap\operatorname{Span}d\tau=\{0\}
. We conclude that
(d\tau,d(\operatorname{Re}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s)),d(\operatorname{Im}G^{\prime\prime}\circ H(\cdot,s))
is a linearly independent collection in the domain for all
s\in V_{1}
. Since
dL^{\prime}=L^{*}d\tau
and
dL^{\prime\prime\prime}=L^{*}ds
, taking compositions with
L
we obtain
(L.3)
.

Now endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinates
(t,z,s)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m},s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
. We take

F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):=(L^{\prime},\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}\circ H\circ L,L^{\prime\prime\prime}):(H\circ L)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}.

(4.1.43)

By
(L.1)
,
(H.3)
,
(G.1)
and Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
we have

(F.1)

F^{\prime}(u_{0})=0
,
F^{\prime\prime}(u_{0},v_{0})=0
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}(v_{0})=0
. And for every product neighborhood
U\times V\subseteq(H\circ L)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})
of
(u_{0},v_{0})
, we have
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

In particular
(1)
is satisfied, since
L^{\prime\prime\prime}
is already a smooth coordinate chart.

By
(F.1)

(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}(U,V;\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m})
for every
U\times V\subseteq(H\circ L)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})
. By
(L.3)
,
(dF^{\prime},dF^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,v_{0}))
has full rank
r+2m
in the domain.
Therefore, by Lemma
2.2.38

(iii)
, we can find a small enough neighborhood
U\times V\subseteq(H\circ L)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U_{1}\times V_{1})
of
(u_{0},v_{0})
, such that
F:U\times V\to F(U\times V)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
is homeomorphism, and

(F.2)

F^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{(t,z),s}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N})
whenever
\Omega^{\prime}_{t}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{z}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{s}\subseteq F(U\times V)
.

Define
\Phi:=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\Omega\to U\times V
where
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}_{s}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{z}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{s}
is an arbitrary product neighborhood of
(0,0,0)
in the assumption of Theorem
4.1
. We are going to show that
F
and
\Phi
are as desired.

Firstly, by
(F.1)
, we get
\Phi^{\prime\prime}=(L^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\mathrm{Inv}}=(F^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\mathrm{Inv}}
is a smooth parameterization, which is the result
(5)
.

By
(F.1)
and
(F.2)
we know
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. Thus
(9)
holds and
F
,
\Phi
are both homeomorphic to their images respectively. Since
F(\cdot,v)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
,
\Phi(\cdot,s)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
for each
v
and
s
, we know
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,v))
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-chart and
\Phi^{\prime}(\cdot,s)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-parameterization, which gives
(2)
and
(6)
.

To prove
(3)
and
(7)
, we use
\widehat{U}:=(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime})(U)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}
,
\widehat{V}:=L^{\prime\prime\prime}(V)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
,
\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}:=F^{\prime\prime}\circ L^{\mathrm{Inv}}
and
\widehat{\Phi}:=L\circ\Phi
.
Since
(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime})
and
L^{\prime\prime\prime}
are smooth charts, it is equivalent but more convenient to work on the chart
\widehat{F}:\widehat{U}\times\widehat{V}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
and the parameterization
\widehat{\Phi}:\Omega\to\widehat{U}\times\widehat{V}
:
it suffices to show the following

\begin{gathered}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}(\widehat{U},\widehat{V};\mathbb{C}^{m}),\quad\nabla_{\tau,w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}(\widehat{U},\widehat{V};\mathbb{C}^{(r+2m)\times m}),\\
\widehat{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(t,z),s}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\widehat{U}\times\widehat{V}),\quad\nabla_{t,z}\widehat{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{(r+2m)\times(r+2m)}).\end{gathered}

(4.1.44)

Here for every vector field
X\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};T\mathfrak{M})
,
(L^{\prime},L^{\prime\prime})_{*}X
must be the smooth linear combinations of
\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau},\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}}
on
\widehat{U}
. Thus
\nabla_{\tau,w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
implies
XF^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Proof of (
4.1.44
)
: By (
4.1.43
) we have
\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}(\tau,w,s)=\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}(H(\tau,w,s))=\overline{G^{\prime\prime}}(\tau,H^{\prime\prime}(\tau,w,s),s)
.
By
(H.3)
and
(G.1)
we have
G^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\sigma,\zeta),s}
,
H\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
,
\nabla_{\sigma,\zeta}G^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\sigma,\zeta),s}
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}H\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
. Therefore, by Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
we get
\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
and
\nabla_{\tau,w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}=((\nabla_{\sigma,\zeta}\overline{G^{\prime\prime}})\circ H)\cdot\nabla_{\tau,w}H\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
.

Since
F\circ(L^{\mathrm{Inv}})(\tau,w,s)=(\tau,\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}(\tau,w,s),s)
, we see that
\widehat{\Phi}
has the form

\widehat{\Phi}(t,z,s)=(t,\widehat{\Psi}(t,z,s),s),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }\widehat{\Psi}:\Omega^{\prime}_{t}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{z}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w},\hskip 10.00002pt\widehat{\Psi}(t,z,s):=\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}(t,\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(z).

Since
\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
, applying Lemma
2.2.38

(iii)
to the map
[(\tau,w),s)\mapsto(\tau,\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}(\tau,w,s))]
we get
\widehat{\Psi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(t,z),s}
.

Using the chain rules on
z=\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{\Phi}(t,z,s))=\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}(t,\widehat{\Psi}(t,z,s),s)
we have (recall
\nabla_{w}=[\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}}]^{\top}
),

\displaystyle I_{2m}

\displaystyle=((\nabla_{w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})\circ\widehat{\Phi})\cdot\nabla_{z}\widehat{\Psi},

\displaystyle 0_{r\times m}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\tau}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\circ\widehat{\Phi}+((\nabla_{w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})\circ\widehat{\Phi})\cdot\nabla_{t}\widehat{\Psi};

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{z}\widehat{\Psi}

\displaystyle=(\nabla_{w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\circ\widehat{\Phi},

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}\widehat{\Psi}

\displaystyle=((\nabla_{w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{\tau}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})\circ\widehat{\Phi}.

Since
\nabla_{\tau,w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
, by Lemma
2.2.38

(ii)
we have
(\nabla_{w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
thus
(\nabla_{w}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime})^{-1}\cdot\nabla_{\tau}\widehat{F}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(\tau,w),s}
. Applying Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
with
\widehat{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{(t,z),s}
, we get
\nabla_{t,z}\widehat{\Psi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(t,z),s}
. Therefore
\nabla_{t,z}\widehat{\Phi}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(t,z),s}
finishing the proof of (
4.1.44
) and hence the proof of
(3)
and
(7)
.

Now by
(F.1)
and
(7)
we see that
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta^{\sim\alpha}}_{u,v}
and
\nabla_{t,z}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{(t,z),s}
. By Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
we see that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\circ F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}}\circ F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U,V;\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
. Thus
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\circ F
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}}\circ F
are both well-defined vector fields and have regularity
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta^{\wedge\alpha}}=\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta^{\wedge\alpha})}
.

By definition,
dF^{\prime}=F^{*}dt
,
dF^{\prime\prime\prime}=F^{*}ds
,
dF^{\prime\prime}=F^{*}dz
and
d\overline{F^{\prime\prime}}=F^{*}d\bar{z}
. So by (
4.1.42
) and (
4.1.43
), in the sense of Convention
4.1.22
,

\textstyle\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z})=\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}d\bar{z},F^{*}ds)^{\bot}=\operatorname{Span}(d\overline{F^{\prime\prime}},dF^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\bot}=\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}.

This proves
(4)
. Immediately
(8)
follows.
Now the whole proof is complete.∎

4.2
Improving the Regularity of Rough 1-forms

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
. Suppose
\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{n}

are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}

1
-forms on an open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
which span
the cotangent space at every point in
U
. If we know that
d\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for each
j
, it is not necessarily true that
\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. However, one can always
change coordinates so that the forms are in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}

(see also Corollary
4.2.13
).

The next result is a special case of this idea, where we present an intital setting where we may find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffemorphism such that
F_{*}\lambda^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Theorem 4.2

(Improving regularity of rough 1-forms)
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
. Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
and
y=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n})
be two coordinate systems for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
There exists
c=c(n,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that the following holds.

Suppose
\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
,
i=1,\dots,n
are 1-forms on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
\operatorname{supp}(\lambda^{i}-dx^{i})\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
for each
i
, and

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\|\lambda^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})})\leq c.

(4.2.1)

Then, there exists a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism
F:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}
, such that
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
F_{*}\lambda^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
for
i=1,\dots,n
, and moreover

\displaystyle\|F-\operatorname{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|F_{*}\lambda^{i}-dy^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}\leq c^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\|\lambda^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}\right).

(4.2.2)

Remark 4.2.1
.

Notice that if
\tilde{c}(n,\alpha,\beta)>0
is a small constant that satisfies the results in Theorem
4.2
with
c=\tilde{c}
, then any constant
c<\tilde{c}
would satisfies the results as well.
Therefore by shrinking
c
if possible, we can make the left hand side of (
4.2.1
) bounded by, say
\frac{1}{12}
.

4.2.1
Outline of the proof: the dual Malgrange method

The proof of Theorem
4.2
is inspired by Malgrange’s proof
of the Newlander-Nirenberg Theorem
[
Mal69
]
.

Let
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
and
y=(y^{1},\dots,y^{n})
be two coordinate systems on the unit ball
\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
. In this section, we write 1-forms
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
,
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
as

\lambda^{i}=dx^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}^{i}dx^{j},\hskip 10.00002pt\eta^{i}=dy^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}^{i}dy^{j},\hskip 10.00002pti=1,\dots,n,

(4.2.3)

and define coefficient matrices

A:=(a_{j}^{i})_{n\times n}=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}^{1}&\cdots&a_{n}^{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
a_{1}^{n}&\cdots&a_{n}^{n}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002ptB:=(b_{j}^{i})_{n\times n}=\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}^{1}&\cdots&b_{n}^{1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
b_{1}^{n}&\cdots&b_{n}^{n}\end{pmatrix}.

(4.2.4)

In this section,
\lambda^{1},\ldots,\lambda^{n}
are given
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}

1
-forms on
\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
which span the cotangent space at every point.
And
\eta^{i}:=F_{*}\lambda^{i}
are the push-forward
1
-forms by the unknown

\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism
F:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
which we are solving for. Thus,
\eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{n}
are also
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}

1
-forms defined on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
which span the cotangent
space at every point.

As in Malgrange’s work
[
Mal69
]
,
the main idea is to choose
F
so that the matrix
B
satisfies a nonlinear
elliptic PDE. That
\eta=F_{*}\lambda\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
will follow
from the classical interior regularity of elliptic PDEs. We will show
such an
F
exists by showing that it suffices for
F
to satisfy
a different nonlinear elliptic PDE, whose solution is guaranteed by
classical elliptic theory.

Given collections
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
and
(\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n})
of 1-forms on
\mathbb{B}^{1}
, as above, that both span their co-tangent spaces at every point, we define Riemannian metrics
g
and
h
by

\begin{split}&g=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}:=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}^{k}+a_{i}^{k})(\delta_{j}^{k}+a_{j}^{k})dx^{i}dx^{j},\\
&h=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}h_{ij}dy^{i}dy^{j}:=\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}(\delta_{i}^{k}+b_{i}^{k})(\delta_{j}^{k}+b_{j}^{k})dy^{i}dy^{j}.\end{split}

(4.2.5)

Here,
\delta_{i}^{j}
,
\delta_{ij}
,
\delta^{ij}
are the Kronecker delta functions:

\delta_{i}^{j}=\delta_{ij}=\delta^{ij}=\begin{cases}1,&i=j,\\
0,&i\neq j.\end{cases}

(4.2.6)

We use the following notations from classical Riemannian differential geometry:

\begin{split}&g^{ij}:=g(dx^{i},dx^{j}),\quad\sqrt{\det g}:=\left|\frac{\lambda^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\lambda^{n}}{dx^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dx^{n}}\right|,\\
&h^{ij}:=h(dy^{i},dy^{j}),\quad\sqrt{\det h}:=\left|\frac{\eta^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\eta^{n}}{dy^{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dy^{n}}\right|.\end{split}

(4.2.7)

Remark 4.2.2
.

(a)

We can write
h=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\eta^{i}\cdot\eta^{i}
. It is non-degenerate since
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
span the cotangent space at every point. Moreover
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
form an orthogonal basis with respect to this metric
h
. Similar remarks hold for
g=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda^{i}\cdot\lambda^{i}
.

(b)

Using matrix notations in (
4.2.3
) we have
(h_{ij})_{n\times n}=(I+B)^{\top}(I+B)
and so we know
(h^{ij})_{n\times n}=(h_{ij})^{-1}=(I+B)^{-1}\left((I+B)^{-1}\right)^{\top}
and
\sqrt{\det h}=\det(I+B)
. Similarly, we have
(g_{ij})=(I+A)^{\top}(I+A)=(g^{ij})^{-1}
and
\sqrt{\det g}=\det(I+A)
. More importantly, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 4.2.3
.

Let
B
,
h^{ij}
and
\sqrt{\det h}
be as above. Then
h^{ij}
and
\sqrt{\det h}
are rational functions of the components of
B
. Moreover for every
\gamma>0
, there is a
c_{n,\gamma}>0
such that

h^{ij},\sqrt{\det h}:\left\{B\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}):\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}<c_{n,\gamma}\right\}\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n}),\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,j\leq n,

are norm continuous maps, with

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|h^{ij}-\delta^{ij}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq c_{n,\gamma}^{-1}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\sqrt{\det h}-1\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq c_{n,\gamma}^{-1}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}.

Remark 4.2.4
.

The same results hold for
g^{ij}
and
\sqrt{\det g}
. Namely,
\|g^{ij}-\delta^{ij}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|\sqrt{\det g}-1\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq c_{n,\alpha}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
holds with the same constant
c_{n,\alpha}>0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1.17
, the space
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
is closed under matrix multiplication. By Remark
4.2.2

(b)

\sqrt{\det h}=\det(I+B)
is a polynomial in the components of
B
, so in particular is a norm continuous function on
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Note that
\det(I+0)=1
so we have
\|\sqrt{\det h}-1\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\gamma}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}
when the right hand side is small.

By Lemma
2.2.30

(v)
, choosing
c_{n,\gamma}<\tilde{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\alpha,n)
where
\tilde{c}
is the constant in Lemma
2.2.30

(v)
, we see that the map
B\mapsto(I+B)^{-1}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-norm continuous with
\|(I+B)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq 2\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}
.

Thus, in the domain
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}<c_{n,\gamma}
, the map
B\mapsto(I+B)^{-1}\left((I+B)^{-1}\right)^{\top}
is also continuous and satisfies
\|(I+B)^{-1}\left((I+B)^{-1}\right)^{\top}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\lesssim\|(I+B)^{\top}(I+B)-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\lesssim\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}
. It follows with
(h^{ij})_{n\times n}=(h_{ij})^{-1}_{n\times n}=(I+B)^{-1}\left((I+B)^{-1}\right)^{\top}
, we have that
h^{ij}
are norm continuous and
\|h^{ij}-\delta^{ij}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\lesssim\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}
.

By possibly shrinking
c_{n,\gamma}
we get
\|\sqrt{\det h}-1\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq c_{n,\gamma}^{-1}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}
and
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|h^{ij}-\delta^{ij}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq c_{n,\gamma}^{-1}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}
.
∎

Convention 4.2.5
.

Given a Riemannian metric
g
, we use the co-differential
\vartheta_{g}
as the adjoint of differential with respect to
h
. That is, for any
k
-form
\phi
and any compactly supported
(k-1)
-form
\psi
,

(\vartheta_{g}\phi,\psi)_{g}:=(\phi,d\psi)_{g}=\int g(\phi,d\psi)\ d\operatorname{Vol}_{g},

where
d\operatorname{Vol}_{g}
is the Riemannian volume density induced by
g
. In local coordinates,
d\operatorname{Vol}_{g}=\sqrt{\det g}\ d\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
, where
d\operatorname{Vol}_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}
is the usual Lebesgue density on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We write
\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}=\vartheta
for the usual co-differential with respect to the flat metric
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\delta_{ij}dy^{i}dy^{j}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Lemma 4.2.6
.

Let
\alpha>0
, and let
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
1-forms defined on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
that span the cotangent space at every point, with
(a_{i}^{j})
,
g
,
(g^{ij})
, and
\sqrt{\det g}
given in (
4.2.4
), (
4.2.5
), and (
4.2.7
).

Suppose
F=\operatorname{id}+R:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism that satisfies

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\Big(\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}\frac{\partial R^{k}}{\partial x^{i}}\Big)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\big(\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}a_{i}^{k}\big),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.8)

Then for the pushforward 1-forms
\eta^{k}=F_{*}\lambda^{k}
,
k=1,\dots,n
, the coefficients
(b_{i}^{j})
defined in (
4.2.4
) satisfy

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\left(\sqrt{\det h}h^{ij}b_{j}^{k}\right)=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.9)

Proof.

Note that the composition of a
C^{1}
-function and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism is still
C^{1}
, and being compactly supported is preserved under homeomorphism, so we have

C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}_{x}^{n})=\{v\circ F:v\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}_{y}^{n})\}.

(4.2.10)

By assumption
R\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
, and
a_{i}^{k},g^{ij},\sqrt{\det g}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, so (
4.2.8
) makes sense in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x})\subsetneq C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x})^{\prime}
and the equality
can be viewed as elements of the dual of
C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

For any
u\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, integrating by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\bigg\langle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\bigg(\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}\Big(\frac{\partial R^{k}}{\partial x^{i}}-a_{i}^{k}\Big)\bigg),u\bigg\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}}=-\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}g^{ij}\Big(\frac{\partial R^{k}}{\partial x^{i}}-a_{i}^{k}\Big)\frac{\partial u}{\partial x^{j}}\sqrt{\det g}dx

\displaystyle=-\langle dR^{k}-(\lambda^{k}-dx^{k}),du\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x};g}=-\langle dF^{k}-\lambda^{k},du\rangle_{\mathbb{B}_{x}^{n};g}

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mathbb{B}_{x}^{n};g}
are the dual pairs for linear functionals and test functions induced by
g
. Namely, for
u,v\in C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\phi,\psi\in C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
,
\langle u,v\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n};g}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}uv\sqrt{\det g}dx
and
\langle\phi,\psi\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n};g}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}g(\phi,\psi)\sqrt{\det g}dx
.

Using (
4.2.8
), we get
\langle dF^{k}-\lambda^{k},du\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x};g}=0
for all
u\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
. By (
4.2.10
) we have
\langle dF^{k}-\lambda^{k},d(v\circ F)\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x};g}=0
for all
v\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y})
.

Note that
F_{*}(g(\phi,\psi))=(F_{*}g)(F_{*}\phi,F_{*}\psi)=h(F_{*}\phi,F_{*}\psi)
for all
\phi,\psi\in C^{0}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
, and
F_{*}\sqrt{\det g}=\sqrt{\det F_{*}g}=\sqrt{\det h}
, so
we have, for every
v\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y})
,

0=\langle dF^{k}-\lambda^{k},d(v\circ F)\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x};g}=\langle F_{*}(dF^{k}-\lambda^{k}),F_{*}F^{*}dv\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y};h}=\langle dy^{k}-\eta^{k},dv\rangle_{\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y};h}=\int_{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}h^{ij}b_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial v}{\partial y^{i}}\sqrt{\det h}dy.

Integrating by parts, we obtain (
4.2.9
).
∎

We will choose a coordinate chart
F
so that (
4.2.8
)
is satisfied, and therefore (
4.2.9
)
will be satisfied as well.

To prove Theorem
4.2
we will
prove the following:

•

There exists a
R\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
satisfying (
4.2.8
) with boundary condition
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
. Moreover,
we can choose
R
with
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
.
Thus, by taking
c>0
small, we may take
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}
small.

•

When
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}
is small,
F=\operatorname{id}+R
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism of
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
And under the assumption
\operatorname{supp}A\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we have
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
and
\|d\eta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|d\lambda\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
.
In particular, by taking
c>0
small, we may take

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|d\eta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
small.

•

Using that
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
satisfies (
4.2.9
), if
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|d\eta\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
is small, we will show
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
.

4.2.2
The existence proposition

In this section, we show that there exists a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism
F=\operatorname{id}+R
solving (
4.2.8
) and which satisfies
good estimates.

Proposition 4.2.7
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
. There is a
c_{1}=c_{1}(n,\alpha,\beta)\in(0,1)
such that, if
A=(a_{j}^{k})_{n\times n}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
satisfies

-

A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<c_{1}
,

then the matrix
(I+A)(x)
is invertible for every
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
, and there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-map
F=\operatorname{id}+R
on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that

(i)

R
solves the equation

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\Big(\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}\frac{\partial R^{k}}{\partial x^{i}}\Big)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\big(\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}a_{i}^{k}\big),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(
4.2.8
)

with boundary condition
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
, and we have

\textstyle\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(4.2.11)

(ii)

F:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism such that
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

(iii)

Let
\Phi=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}
be its
inverse map, then

\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(4.2.12)

In particular
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq c_{1}^{-1}
.

Remark 4.2.8
.

The map
F
in Proposition
4.2.7
is uniquely determined by
A
. This is due to the well-posedness of the Dirichlet problem for the second order elliptic equations, since
R
satisfies (
4.2.8
) with
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
.

Remark 4.2.9
.

As we will see in the proof of Theorem
4.2
, the map
F

from Proposition
4.2.7

is the map of the same name in Theorem
4.2
.

Proof.

We let
c_{1}
be a small constant which may change from line to line.
Note that if (
4.2.11
) and (
4.2.12
) are valid for some
\tilde{c}_{1}
, then they are also valid for any
0<c_{1}\leq\tilde{c}_{1}
.

First pick
c_{1}<\tilde{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n},\alpha,n)
where
\tilde{c}
is the constant in Lemma
2.2.30

(v)
. By Lemma
2.2.30

(v)
, the assumption
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<c_{1}(<\tilde{c}_{\mathbb{B}^{n},\alpha,n})
implies that
I+A
is invertible at every point and

(I+A)^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Therefore,
g
given in (
4.2.5
) is indeed a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-Riemannian metric.

By the assumption
\operatorname{supp}A\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we have
\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\delta^{ij}
. Since
(\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}(x))_{n\times n}
is an invertible matrix for
x\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, the second order operator
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\partial_{x^{j}}(\sqrt{\det g}\cdot g^{ij}\partial_{x^{i}})
is uniformly elliptic on
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
Classical existence theorems (for example,
[
GT01
, Theorem 8.3]
) show that for each
k=1,\dots,n
there exists
2
2

2

Here
H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
stands for the classical
L^{2}
-Sobolev space of order
1
, and
H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})=H_{0}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})^{*}
is the
L^{2}
-Sobolev space of order
-1
.
a
R^{k}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
that satisfies (
4.2.8
) with Dirichlet boundary condition
R^{k}\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
, since
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\left(\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}a_{i}^{k}\right)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subset H^{-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
.
By a classical regularity estimate (see
[
GT01
, Theorem 8.34]
or
[
FR95
, Theorem 15]
), we know
R^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

To show
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
, we write (
4.2.8
) as

\Delta R^{k}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\left(\left(\delta^{ij}-\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\right)\frac{\partial R^{k}}{\partial x^{i}}\right)+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\left(\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}a_{i}^{k}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.13)

By Remark
4.2.4
(see also Lemma
4.2.3
), we see that
\|\delta^{ij}-\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
.
Therefore

\|\Delta R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\sum_{i,j,k=1}^{n}\left(\|\delta^{ij}-\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|\partial_{x^{i}}R^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}a_{i}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\right)\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}+\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}},

(4.2.14)

where the implicit constants depend only on
n
and
\alpha
but not
A
or
R
.

The assumption
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
implies that
R=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\mathbb{B}^{n}}\Delta R
, where
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
is the zero Dirichlet boundary solution operator given in Notation
2.1.43
. Since, by Lemma
2.1.41
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\mathbb{B}^{n}}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
is bounded, (
4.2.14
) implies

\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\tilde{C}_{1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\tilde{C}_{1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})},

(4.2.15)

where
\tilde{C}_{1}=\tilde{C}_{1}(n,\alpha)>1
is a constant depending only on
n
and
\alpha
but not
A
or
R
.

Choosing
c_{1}
small enough so that
c_{1}\tilde{C}_{1}\leq\frac{1}{3}
, then we get
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq\frac{1}{3}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}+\tilde{C}_{1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
when
A
satisfies the assumption
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<c_{1}
. Therefore

\textstyle\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\frac{3}{2}\tilde{C}_{1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{1}{2}c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{when }\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<c_{1}\leq(3\tilde{C}_{1})^{-1}.

(4.2.16)

This is part of the estimate in (
4.2.11
).

Next we show
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. Note that by the support assumption
\operatorname{supp}A\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
we have
\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\delta^{ij}
and
a_{i}^{k}\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
, so the right hand side of (
4.2.13
) is zero in
\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Therefore each
R^{k}
is a harmonic functions in the domain
\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

The estimate
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
implies
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\partial(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}))}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
since the trace map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\partial(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}))
is bounded (see Remark
2.1.42
). By classical interior estimates of harmonic functions (for example,
[
GT01
, Theorem 2.10]
) since
\Delta R\big|_{\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
, we have

\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(\partial\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|R\|_{C^{\lceil\beta\rceil+1}(\partial\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|R\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{2}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(4.2.17)

Therefore, along with the fact that
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
,
R\big|_{\partial(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}=R\big|_{\partial\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\cup R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}
has
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
norm bounded by a constant times
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. By classical regularity estimates of harmonic functions (also see Lemma
2.1.41
) on
\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
we know

\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(\partial(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}))}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

In particular, there is a
\tilde{C}_{2}=\tilde{C}_{2}(n,\alpha,\beta)>0
that depends on neither
A
nor
R
such that

\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\tilde{C}_{2}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(4.2.18)

Taking
c_{1}<\frac{1}{2}\tilde{C}_{2}^{-1}
we have
\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\frac{1}{2}c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. Combining this with (
4.2.16
), completes the proof of (
4.2.11
).

We can take
c_{1}>0
possibly smaller so that
c_{1}<\frac{1}{3}(K_{2}\tilde{C}_{1})^{-1}
, where
K_{2}=K_{2}(n,\alpha,\beta)
is the constant in Proposition
2.1.44
and
\tilde{C}_{1}
is the constant in (
4.2.15
). By (
4.2.16
) we know
\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq K_{2}^{-1}
. So by Proposition
2.1.44
, the map
F=\operatorname{id}+R
has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-inverse. We conclude
\Phi=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Since
\|R\|_{C^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}
, by possibly shrinking
c_{1}
we can ensure
\|R\|_{C^{0}}+\|\nabla R\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. So
F(0)=R(0)\in B^{n}(0,\frac{1}{2})
, which implies
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq B^{n}(0,\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{6})\subset\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, and
|F(x_{1})-F(x_{2})|\geq|x_{1}-x_{2}|-|R(x_{1})-R(x_{2})|\geq\frac{1}{2}|x_{1}-x_{2}|
for
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
Thus, if
|F(x)-F(0)|<\frac{1}{6}
then
|x-0|<\frac{1}{3}
; i.e.,
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})
. So
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
finishing the proof of
(ii)
.

Finally we prove
(iii)
. Note that by Proposition
2.1.44

(i)
, (
4.2.12
) gives
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim 1
and , which is
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq c_{1}^{-1}
by choosing
c_{1}
small.

By Proposition
2.1.44

(i)
and using that
c_{1}<\frac{1}{3}(C_{0}\tilde{C}_{1})^{-1}
, we get
\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq K_{2}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq K_{2}\tilde{C}_{1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{1}{2}c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
, which proves half of (
4.2.12
).

To show the second half of (
4.2.12
), we need to show
\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
.

The assumption
R\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
implies
F\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\operatorname{id}\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\Phi\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}
and therefore

(\nabla\Phi-I)\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=((\nabla\Phi)\circ\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}})\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}-I=(\nabla F)^{-1}\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}-I.

Fix
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(2\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that
\chi\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of
\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
, so
\nabla R(x)=\chi(x)\nabla R(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{B}^{n}
near
\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

We shrink
c_{1}>0
so that
c_{1}<\tilde{c}_{\mathbb{B}^{n},\beta,n}\cdot(2\tilde{C}_{2}\|\chi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}})^{-1}
, where
\tilde{C}_{2}
is in (
4.2.18
), and
\tilde{c}_{\mathbb{B}^{n},\beta,n}
is in Lemma
2.2.30

(v)
. Then by Lemma
2.1.17

(ii)
the assumption
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}<c_{1}
implies

\|\chi\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq 2\|\chi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq 2\tilde{C}_{2}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n})}<\tilde{c}_{\mathbb{B}^{n},\beta}.

Therefore we can apply Lemma
2.2.30

(v)
to
\chi\nabla R\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
to obtain
\|(I+\chi\nabla R)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq 2\|\chi\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
. Hence, along with (
2.1.27
),

\|(I+\nabla R)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|(I+\chi\nabla R)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|\chi\nabla R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\chi}\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n}\backslash\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

So by possibly shrinking
c_{1}>0
, we get
\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\frac{1}{2}c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
, which completes the second half of (
4.2.12
).
∎

We now have pushforward 1-forms
\eta^{1}=F_{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}=F_{*}\lambda^{n}
. Their norms admit some control, as the next lemma shows.

Lemma 4.2.10
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
. There is a
c_{2}=c_{2}(n,\alpha,\beta)>0
such that the following holds.
Let
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{c}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
be the coefficient matrix for
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
(see (
4.2.3
)) satisfying the assumptions of Proposition
4.2.7
and also satisfying

(a)

\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<c_{2}
.

(b)

For
k=1,\dots,n
,
d\lambda^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
with
\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}<c_{2}
.

Suppose
\Phi=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}
satisfies the conclusions of Proposition
4.2.7
. Then, for the 1-forms
\eta^{k}=\Phi^{*}\lambda^{k}
(
k=1,\dots,n
) with coefficient matrix
B=(b^{i}_{j})_{n\times n}
(see (
4.2.3
)), we have:

(i)

B
satisfies the PDE system (
4.2.9
).

(ii)

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<c_{2}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
.

(iii)

d\eta^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)
for
k=1,\dots,n
, with
\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}<c_{2}^{-1}\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
.

Proof.

Part
(i)
is obtained in Lemma
4.2.6
.

For part
(ii)
, write
\Phi=(\phi^{1},\dots,\phi^{n})
, where
\phi^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}(\mathbb{B}^{n})
,
k=1,\dots,n
. Therefore

\eta^{k}=\Phi^{*}\Big(dx^{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}^{k}dx^{i}\Big)=d\phi^{k}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}^{k}\circ\Phi)d\phi^{i},\hskip 10.00002ptb_{j}^{k}=\frac{\partial(\phi^{k}-y^{k})}{\partial y^{j}}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}(a_{i}^{k}\circ\Phi)\frac{\partial\phi^{i}}{\partial y^{j}},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j,k\leq n.

(4.2.19)

From (
4.2.19
) we know that
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|A\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|\nabla\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. By Proposition
4.2.7

(iii)
we know
\|\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim 1
and
\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. By
Proposition
2.1.30

(i)
we get
\|A\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. Combining these we get

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|A\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|\nabla\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}.

(4.2.20)

Since we have
A\equiv 0
outside
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
in particular
A\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}=0
, it follows that
\eta^{k}=d\phi^{k}
on
\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}
.
Therefore
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}
.
So by Proposition
4.2.7

(iii)

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\|\nabla\Phi-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}

(4.2.21)

By choosing
c_{2}>0
small, (
4.2.20
) and (
4.2.21
) complete the proof of
(ii)
.

Finally, for
(iii)
, we apply
Proposition
2.1.44

(ii)

with
\lambda=\lambda^{k}
,
for each
k=1,\dots,n
. Since
d(F_{*}\lambda^{k})=d\eta^{k}
, by (
2.1.28
) we get
\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
. Taking
c_{2}
smaller, we complete the proof.
∎

4.2.3
The regularity proposition

In this part, we show that the 1-forms
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
are indeed
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, by using the interior regularity theory for elliptic PDEs.

Proposition 4.2.11
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
.
There is a
c_{3}=c_{3}(n,\alpha,\beta)>0
, such that if
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
with coefficient matrix
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
(see (
4.2.3
)) such that
B
solves the PDE (
4.2.9
),
B\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
with

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n}\right)}<c_{3},

(4.2.22)

then
B\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
. Moreover

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq c_{3}^{-1}\Big(\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\Big).

(4.2.23)

Proof.

We can write
B=\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta B+(B-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta B)
, where
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\mathcal{P}_{0}^{\mathbb{B}^{n}}
is given in Lemma
2.2.35
, which is the zero Dirichlet boundary solution operator on the unit ball.

Note that
B-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta B
is the harmonic function whose boundary value equals to
B\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}
(which might not be zero). By Lemma
2.1.41
using the assumption
B\big|_{\partial\mathbb{B}^{n}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, we get
B-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta B\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
and

\|B-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

(4.2.24)

We can rewrite (
4.2.9
) as

-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}b_{i}^{k}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\left(\big(\sqrt{\det h}h^{ij}-\delta^{ij}\big)b_{j}^{k}\right),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.25)

The left hand side of (
4.2.25
) is
\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{k}
. By Lemma
4.2.3
, the right hand side of (
4.2.25
) is the derivatives of rational functions of the components of
B
, which vanish to second order at
B=0
. More precisely, using Lemma
4.2.3
, we can rewrite (
4.2.25
) as

\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{k}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}b_{i}^{k}=-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.26)

Here
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}
are rational functions (see Lemma
4.2.3
) defined in a neighborhood of origin in
\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
with
|\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(u)|\lesssim|u|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}^{2}
for suitably small matrices
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and we have

|\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(u_{1})-\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(u_{2})|\lesssim(|u_{1}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}+|u_{2}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}})|u_{1}-u_{2}|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{when }u_{1},u_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\text{ small}.

(4.2.27)

We can pass this fact from matrices to matrix-valued functions. Indeed,
\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}
has convergent power expansion in a neighborhood of
0
as

\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(u)=\sum_{r=2}^{\infty}\sum_{j_{1},\dots,j_{r},l_{1},\dots,l_{r}=1}^{n}a_{i,r;l_{1}\dots l_{r}}^{k;j_{1}\dots j_{r}}u_{j_{1}}^{l_{1}}\dots u_{j_{r}}^{l_{r}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{converging when }|u|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}\text{ is small.}

(4.2.28)

Here
a_{i,r;l_{1}\dots l_{r}}^{k;j_{1}\dots j_{r}}\in\mathbb{R}
. The power expansion starts at
r=2
since the zero and the first order terms all vanish.

By Lemma
2.1.17
, we can replace
u\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
in (
4.2.28
) by
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
, and as in (
4.2.27
), for
\gamma>0
there is a
\tilde{C}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}>0
, such that when
\|f_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|f_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\tilde{C}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}^{-1}
,

\|\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(f_{1})-\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(f_{2})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\tilde{C}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}(\|f_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|f_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})})\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq i,k\leq n.

(4.2.29)

Using the fact that
-\Delta\eta^{k}=d\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}\eta^{k}+\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k}
, we further have

\Delta\eta^{k}=d\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B)-\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{i}}\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B)dy^{j}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left\langle\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k},\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\right\rangle dy^{j}.

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the pairing between

1
forms and vector fields.

On the other hand,
\Delta\eta^{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Delta(\delta_{j}^{k}+b_{j}^{k})dy^{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Delta b_{j}^{k}dy^{j}
, therefore

\Delta b_{j}^{k}=\left\langle\Delta\eta^{k},\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\right\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{i}}\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(B)-\left\langle\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k},\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\right\rangle,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{n}_{y},\hskip 10.00002ptk=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.30)

Let
\tilde{\xi}_{0}:=\min(\tilde{C}_{\mathcal{R},\alpha}^{-1},\tilde{C}_{\mathcal{R},\beta}^{-1})
where
\tilde{C}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}
is the constant in (
4.2.29
). Let
\xi=\xi_{B}\in(0,\tilde{\xi}_{0}]
to be determined. We define metric spaces
\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi}
and an operator
\mathcal{T}_{B}:\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\tilde{\xi}_{0}}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
by

\begin{gathered}\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi}:=\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}):\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\xi\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}),\quad\text{for }\gamma\in\{\alpha,\beta\}\text{ and }\xi\in(0,\tilde{\xi}_{0}].\\
\mathcal{T}_{B}[f]^{k}_{j}:=b_{j}^{k}-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta b_{j}^{k}-\Big\langle\mathcal{P}_{0}(\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k}),\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\Big\rangle+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{P}_{0}\Big(\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{R}_{i}^{k}(f)}{\partial y^{j}\partial y^{i}}\Big),\quad 1\leq j,k\leq n.\end{gathered}

(4.2.31)

We endow
\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi}
with the metric induced by the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}
, which makes
\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi}
a complete metric space.

Note that from (
4.2.31
) and (
4.2.30
) we have
B=\mathcal{T}_{B}[B]
. Our goal is to show that when
c_{3}
and
\xi
are both suitably small, we have
B\in\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\xi}
and that
\mathcal{T}_{B}
is a contraction mapping on both
\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\xi}
and
\mathscr{X}_{\beta,\xi}
, thus by uniqueness of the fixed point we conclude that
B
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-matrix and
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}<\xi
.

By Lemma
2.1.41
,
\mathcal{P}_{0}:\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-2}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})
is bounded for
\gamma\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
. By (
4.2.29
), we know for every
f_{1},f_{2}\in\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi}
,

\|\mathcal{T}_{B}[f_{1}]-\mathcal{T}_{B}[f_{2}]\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|\mathcal{P}_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-2}\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\|\nabla^{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\to\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-2}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\mathcal{R}_{k}^{l}(f_{1})-\mathcal{R}_{k}^{l}(f_{2})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\xi C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}\|f_{1}-f_{2}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}.

(4.2.32)

where
C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}>1
is a constant that only depends on
n,(\mathcal{R}_{j}^{k}),\gamma
but not on
B,\xi,f_{1},f_{2}
.

On the other hand
\mathcal{T}_{B}[0]_{j}^{k}=b_{j}^{k}-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta b_{j}^{k}-\big\langle\mathcal{P}_{0}(\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k}),\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{j}}\big\rangle
. By (
4.2.24
), for
\gamma\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
,

\|\mathcal{T}_{B}[0]\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|\mathcal{T}_{B}[0]\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\|B-\mathcal{P}_{0}\Delta B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\mathcal{P}_{0}(\vartheta_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}d\eta^{k})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}.

(4.2.33)

So by possibly increasing
C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}
, we have, for
f_{1}\in\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi}
,
using (
4.2.32
) and (
4.2.33
),

\|\mathcal{T}_{B}[f_{1}]\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq\|\mathcal{T}_{B}[f_{1}]-\mathcal{T}_{B}[0]\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}+\|\mathcal{T}_{B}[0]\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\leq C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}\Big(\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}+\xi\|f_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}}\Big).

(4.2.34)

Take
c_{3}>0
satisfying
c_{3}<\frac{1}{4}\max(1,C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\alpha},C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\beta})^{-2}
, and take

\xi=\xi_{B}:=2\max(C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\alpha},C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\beta})\left(\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\right).

(4.2.35)

By the assumption (
4.2.22
),
\xi_{B}\leq\frac{1}{2}\max(C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\alpha},C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\beta})^{-1}<\tilde{\xi}_{0}
, so
\mathcal{T}_{B}
is defined on
\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\tilde{\xi}_{0}}
and by (
4.2.34
)
\mathcal{T}_{B}
maps
\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi_{B}}
into
\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi_{B}}
for
\gamma\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
.

Since
\xi_{B}C^{\prime}_{\mathcal{R},\gamma}<\frac{1}{2}
for
\gamma\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
, using (
4.2.32
),

\mathcal{T}_{B}
is a contraction mapping on the domain
\mathscr{X}_{\gamma,\xi_{B}}
, for
\gamma\in\{\alpha,\beta\}
.

Note that
\xi_{B}\geq\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
, and so
B\in\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}):\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\xi_{B}\}=\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\xi_{B}}
. Therefore,
B
is a fixed point for
\mathcal{T}_{B}
in
\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\xi_{B}}
, which is unique since
\mathcal{T}_{B}
is a contraction mapping on
\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\xi_{B}}
.

On the other hand
\mathcal{T}_{B}
also has a unique fixed point in
\mathscr{X}_{\beta,\xi_{B}}\subsetneq\mathscr{X}_{\alpha,\xi_{B}}
. Therefore, by uniqueness,
B\in\mathscr{X}_{\beta,\xi_{B}}=\{f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}):\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\xi_{B}\}
.
In particular,
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\xi_{B}
. Thus by (
4.2.35
),

\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq\xi_{B}\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{l}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}.

Thus, we have established (
4.2.23
)
which completes the proof.
∎

4.2.4
The proof of Theorem
4.2
and a qualitative regularity improving

Using Propositions
4.2.7
and
4.2.11
we can prove Theorem
4.2
.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

Let
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>0
be the small constants in Proposition
4.2.7
, Lemma
4.2.10
, and Proposition
4.2.11
. We take
c=\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\min(c_{1},c_{2}c_{3})
in the assumption of Theorem
4.2
.

Let
F
,
R=F-\operatorname{id}
,
\Phi=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}
,
A
,
B
and
\eta^{i}=F_{*}\lambda^{i}
be as in Proposition
4.2.7
. Recall
\eta^{i}
and
B=(b_{i}^{j})
are given in (
4.2.3
) and (
4.2.4
).

When the assumption (
4.2.1
) is satisfied, by Proposition
4.2.7

(ii)
we have that
F
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism and satisfies
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\cap\frac{3}{4}\mathbb{B}^{n}
. And by (
4.2.11
), we have
\|F-\operatorname{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}=\|R\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\leq c_{1}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{1}{2n}c^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. This implies half of the estimate (
4.2.2
).

By Lemma
4.2.10

(ii)
and
(iii)
, we have
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}<c_{2}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
and
\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}<c_{2}^{-1}\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}
,
k=1,\dots,n
.

Thus,
\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}<c_{2}^{-1}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+c_{2}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}<2c_{2}^{-1}c<c_{3}
.
By (
4.2.23
) in Proposition
4.2.11
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|\eta^{k}-dy^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq n\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq nc_{3}^{-1}\left(\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|B\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\partial\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\eta^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq nc_{3}^{-1}\cdot c_{2}^{-1}\left(\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\right)\leq n^{2}(c_{2}c_{3})^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left(\|\lambda^{k}-dx^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\lambda^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\right).

This gives the second half of the estimate (
4.2.2
) since
n^{2}(c_{2}c_{3})^{-1}\leq\frac{1}{2}c^{-1}
.
∎

In Theorem
4.2
, we assumed (
4.2.1
) which is a smallness assumption. When (
4.2.1
)
is not satisfied, we may use a scaling argument to transfer to a setting where it is satisfied, as the next result shows.

Proposition 4.2.12

(The scaling argument)
.

Let
\alpha>0
,
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]

and let
\mu_{0},\tilde{c},M>0
. There exists a
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(n,\alpha,\beta,\mu_{0},\tilde{c},M)\in(0,\mu_{0}]
that satisfies the following:

Suppose
\xi^{1},\dots,\xi^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that
\xi^{i}\big|_{0}=dx^{i}\big|_{0}
for
i=1,\dots,n
and
d\xi^{1},\dots,d\xi^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}\left(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}\right)
with estimate

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|d\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}<M.

(4.2.36)

Then there are 1-forms
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that

(i)

\lambda^{i}\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\cdot(\phi_{\kappa_{0}}^{*}\xi^{i})\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}
,
i=1,\dots,n
, where
\phi_{\kappa_{0}}(x):=\kappa_{0}\cdot x
is the scaling map.

(ii)

\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
with the constant
c=\tilde{c}
. That is,

•

\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
span the cotangent space at every point in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

•

\operatorname{supp}(\lambda^{i}-dx^{i})\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

•

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\|\lambda^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}})\leq\tilde{c}
.

Proof.

First we construct 1-forms
\rho^{1},\dots,\rho^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
such that for
i=1,\dots,n
,

(a)

\rho^{i}\big|_{0}=0
and
d\rho^{i}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=d\xi^{i}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}
.

(b)

There is a
C_{0}=C_{0}(n,\alpha,\beta,\mu_{0})>0
that does not depend on
\xi^{i}
, such that

\|\rho^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq C_{0}(\|\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|d\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}).

(4.2.37)

Take a
\chi_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that
\chi_{0}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 1
. Define

\tilde{\rho}^{i}:=-\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast\vartheta d(\chi_{0}\xi^{i})=-\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast\vartheta(\chi_{0}\cdot d\xi^{i}+d\chi_{0}\wedge\xi^{i}),\hskip 10.00002pt\rho^{i}:=\tilde{\rho}^{i}-(\tilde{\rho}^{i}\big|_{0}),\hskip 10.00002pti=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.38)

Recall
\vartheta
is the codifferential from Notation
2.1.38
, and
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is the fundamental solution of Laplacian as in (
2.1.26
).
The convolution is defined in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
using Lemma
2.1.40
, since the support
\operatorname{supp}\vartheta d(\chi_{0}\xi^{i})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\chi_{0}\Subset\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n}
is compact.

Clearly
\rho^{i}\big|_{0}=0
. Similar to the proof of Proposition
2.1.44
, since
\chi_{0}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 1
, we have

d\xi^{i}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=d(\chi_{0}\xi^{i})\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=-(\vartheta d+d\vartheta)(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast d(\chi_{0}\xi^{i}))\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=-(\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast d\vartheta d(\chi_{0}\xi^{i}))\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=d\tilde{\rho}^{i}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=d\rho^{i}\big|_{\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

So condition
(a)
is satisfied.

By Lemma
2.1.40
we have, for every
\mu>0
,

\|\mathcal{G}\ast\vartheta\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta,\mu}\|\omega\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mu\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\omega\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{c}(\mu\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(4.2.39)

Take
\omega=\vartheta(\chi_{0}\cdot d\xi^{i}+d\chi_{0}\wedge\xi^{i})
,
\mu=\mu_{0}
in (
4.2.39
) and by Lemma
2.1.17
, we have

\displaystyle\|\rho^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\leq\|\tilde{\rho}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}+|\tilde{\rho}^{i}(0)|\leq 2\|\tilde{\rho}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}

(4.2.40)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta,\mu_{0}}\|\vartheta(\chi_{0}\cdot d\xi^{i}+d\chi_{0}\wedge\xi^{i})\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-2}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta,\mu_{0}}\|\chi_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\|d\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}+\|d\chi_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta,\mu_{0},\chi_{0}}\|d\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}.

(
4.2.40
) gives us the
C_{0}
for condition
(b)
. This complete the proof of
(a)
and
(b)
and we get
\rho^{1},\dots,\rho^{n}
as desired.

Fix
\chi_{1}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that
\chi_{1}\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 1
. For
\kappa>0
, let
\phi_{\kappa}(x):=\kappa\cdot x
, so
\phi_{\kappa}
maps
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
into
\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
when
\kappa\in(0,\mu_{0}]
. For
\kappa\in(0,\mu_{0}]
, we define 1-forms
\lambda_{\kappa}^{1},\dots,\lambda_{\kappa}^{n}
and
\tau_{\kappa}^{1},\dots,\tau_{\kappa}^{n}
by

\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}:=dx^{i}+\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\chi_{1}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}(\xi^{i}-dx^{i}),\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{\kappa}^{i}:=\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\chi_{1}\cdot(\phi_{\kappa}^{*}\rho^{i})+\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\mathcal{G}\ast\vartheta\left(d\chi_{1}\wedge\phi_{\kappa}^{*}(\xi^{i}-dx^{i})\right),\hskip 10.00002pti=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.41)

Since
\xi^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
\rho^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, we have
\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
,
\tau_{\kappa}^{i}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})
(by (
4.2.39
)) and
\operatorname{supp}(\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\chi_{1}\Subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
. And since
\chi_{1}\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\equiv 1
and
\phi_{\kappa}^{*}dx=\kappa dx
,

\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=dx^{i}+\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}(\xi^{i}-dx^{i})\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\tfrac{1}{\kappa}(\phi_{\kappa}^{*}\xi^{i})\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}.

(4.2.42)

We write
\xi^{i}
and
\rho^{i}
,
i=1,\dots,n
as

\xi^{i}=dx^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}a^{i}_{j}(x)dx^{j},\hskip 10.00002pt\rho^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}b^{i}_{j}(x)dx^{j},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }a^{i}_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n}),\ b^{i}_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n}).

(4.2.43)

By assumption
\xi^{i}\big|_{0}=dx^{i}\big|_{0}
and
\rho^{i}\big|_{0}=0
for
i=1,\dots,n
, so
a^{i}_{j}(0)=b^{i}_{j}(0)=0
for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
. And we have

\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}=dx^{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\chi_{1}(x)a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)dx^{j},\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{\kappa}^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\Big(\chi_{1}(x)b^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)dx^{j}+\mathcal{G}\ast\vartheta\big(a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)d\chi_{1}\wedge dx^{j}\big)\Big),\hskip 10.00002pti=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.44)

Since
\phi_{\kappa}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\frac{\mu_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
and
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{1}\Subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, by condition
(a)
we have
\chi_{1}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}d\rho^{i}=\chi_{1}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}d\xi^{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. It follows that
d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}=d\tau_{\kappa}^{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
; indeed,

d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-d\tau_{\kappa}^{i}=\tfrac{1}{\kappa}d\chi_{1}\wedge\phi_{\kappa}^{*}(\xi^{i}-dx^{i})+\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\chi_{1}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}d\xi^{i}-\tfrac{1}{\kappa}d\chi_{1}\wedge\phi_{\kappa}^{*}(\xi^{i}-dx^{i})-\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\chi_{1}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}d\rho^{i}=\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\chi_{1}\cdot\phi_{\kappa}^{*}(d\xi^{i}-d\rho^{i})=0.

(4.2.45)

Applying Lemmas
2.1.17
and
2.1.12
to
a^{i}_{j}
we have

\|\chi_{1}\cdot a^{i}_{j}(\kappa\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\leq 2\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|a^{i}_{j}(\kappa\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha,\mu_{0}}\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\|a^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\kappa\in(0,\mu_{0}].

(4.2.46)

Using (
4.2.44
) and (
4.2.46
), we deduce that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|\chi_{1}(x)a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim_{\alpha,\mu_{0}}\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|a^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

(4.2.47)

By (
4.2.45
) we have
d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}=d\tau_{\kappa}^{i}
, and therefore

\|d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\|d\tau_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\|\tau_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})},\hskip 10.00002pti=1,\dots,n.

(4.2.48)

Applying Lemma
2.1.12
to
a^{i}_{j}
and
b^{i}_{j}
we get that for
0<\kappa<\mu_{0}
,

\displaystyle\|\chi_{1}\cdot b^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|b^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\kappa^{\min(\beta,\frac{1}{2})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|b^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\leq\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|b^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

(4.2.49)

\displaystyle\|a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)d\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\|a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\|a^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})}.

(4.2.50)

Letting
\omega=a_{j}^{i}(\kappa x)d\chi_{1}\wedge dx^{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\mu=1
in (
4.2.39
), we see that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|d\tau_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\lesssim_{\beta}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\tau_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{Eqn::Rough1Form::ScalingTmpDlambdaDkappa}}

(4.2.51)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\big(\|\chi_{1}(x)b_{j}^{i}(\kappa x)dx^{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}+\big\|\mathcal{G}\ast\vartheta\big(a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)d\chi_{1}\wedge dx^{j}\big)\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\big)

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{Eqn::Rough1Form::ScalingProofLambdaCoordinate}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\big(\|\chi_{1}(x)b_{j}^{i}(\kappa x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)d\chi_{1}\wedge dx^{j}\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\big)

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{Eqn::Rough1Form::ScalingProof3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\big(\|b_{j}^{i}(\kappa x)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|a^{i}_{j}(\kappa x)d\chi_{1}\big\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\big)

\displaystyle\text{since }\alpha\geq\beta-1

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta}\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\|\chi_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\Big(\|a^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})}+\|b^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\Big)

\displaystyle\text{by \eqref{Eqn::Rough1Form::ScalingTmp2.1} and \eqref{Eqn::Rough1Form::ScalingTmp2.2}}.

Note that
\chi_{1}
is a fixed cut-off function whose
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-norms depend only on
n,\alpha
. So combining (
4.2.47
) and (
4.2.51
) we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\|\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}})\lesssim_{\alpha,\beta,\mu_{0}}\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\big(\|a^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|b^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\big).

(4.2.52)

By (
4.2.43
) we have
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|a^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim 1+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
and
\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|b^{i}_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\rho^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}
. And combining (
4.2.52
) with (
4.2.37
), we can find a
C_{1}=C_{1}(n,\alpha,\beta,\mu_{0})>0
that does not depend on the other quantities, such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\leq C_{1}\cdot\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(1+\|\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}+\|d\xi^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n})}\Big),\hskip 10.00002pt\forall\kappa\in(0,\mu_{0}].

(4.2.53)

Now applying assumption (
4.2.36
) to (
4.2.53
) we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\leq\kappa^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}C_{1}\cdot(M+n).

Since
\|(\langle\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\rangle)_{n\times n}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\lesssim_{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
, we can find a
\tilde{c}^{\prime}=\tilde{c}^{\prime}(n,\alpha)>0
such that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\tilde{c}^{\prime}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{implies}\hskip 10.00002pt\textstyle\|(\langle\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\rangle)_{n\times n}\|_{C^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

In particular
\left(\langle\lambda_{\kappa}^{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\rangle\right)_{n\times n}=I+\left(\langle\lambda_{\kappa}^{i}-dx^{i},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\rangle\right)_{n\times n}
is invertible at every point in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
, which means
(\lambda_{\kappa}^{1},\dots,\lambda_{\kappa}^{n})
span the tangent space at every point in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

We take
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(n,\alpha,\beta,\mu_{0},M,\tilde{c})>0
such that

0<\kappa_{0}<\mu_{0}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt\kappa_{0}^{\min(\alpha,\frac{1}{2})}C_{1}\cdot(M+n)\leq\min(\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime}).

Take
\lambda^{i}=\lambda_{\kappa_{0}}^{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. We have
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\lambda^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}\leq\min(\tilde{c},\tilde{c}^{\prime})
. By our assumption on
\tilde{c}^{\prime}
,
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
span the tangent space at every point in
\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Note that by (
4.2.44
)
\operatorname{supp}(\lambda_{\kappa_{0}}^{i}-dx^{i})\subseteq\operatorname{supp}\chi_{1}\Subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
. This shows conclusion
(ii)
is satisfied.

By (
4.2.42
) we get the conclusion
(i)
, finishing the proof.
∎

We can give a sufficient and necessary condition for some 1-forms to have better regularity in some special coordinate system:

Corollary 4.2.13
.

Let
\alpha>0
and let
\beta\in[\alpha,\alpha+1]
be such that
\alpha+\beta>1
. Let
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
1-forms on a smooth manifold
\mathfrak{M}
of dimension
n
, which span the cotangent space at every point. Then following are equivalent:

(1)

For every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there exist a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}

such that
\Phi(0)=p
and
\Phi^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

(2)

d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
. (see Definition
3.1.1

(iii)
).

Remark 4.2.14
.

(i)

When
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
vector fields that span the cotangent space at every point, we can apply Corollary
4.2.13
by taking
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
to be the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, and we see that
\Phi^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}\lambda^{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
if and only if
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.

(ii)

When
\beta>\alpha+1
, the result is still true if we replace
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
by
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}
in
(2)
. See
[
SY22
, Definition 2.15]
.

(iii)

The assumption
\alpha+\beta>1
can be removed. When
\alpha+\beta\leq 1
, in particular
0<\alpha\leq\beta<1
, we cannot pullback a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}
-form under a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-map. However
the statement
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
still make sense because for a 1-form
\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{0+}
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism
\phi
, we have
d\xi\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
if and only if
d(\phi^{*}\xi)\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, see
[
SY22
, Proposition 2.8]
.

Proof.

By passing to a local coordinate system, we can assume
\mathfrak{M}
to be an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2)
: For such
\Phi
, we have
d(\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i})\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
for
i=1,\dots,n
. By Lemma
2.1.17

(i)
along with direct computation (see
[
SY22
, Lemma 4.4]
) we see that
\Phi^{*}d\lambda^{i}=d(\Phi^{*}\lambda^{i})
.
So
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the desired coordinate chart that shows
d\lambda^{1},\dots,d\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1)
: Let
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

By passing to local coordinate system and applying an invertible linear transformation we can find a
\mu_{0}>0
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate chart
F_{0}:U_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n}_{x}
such that

•

F_{0}(p)=0
.

•

((F_{0})_{*}\lambda^{i})\big|_{0}=dx^{i}\big|_{0}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

Take
c>0
be the small constant in Theorem
4.2
. By Proposition
4.2.12
with
\tilde{c}=c
, we can find a
\kappa_{0}\in(0,\mu_{0}]
and 1-forms
\tilde{\lambda}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
such that for scaling map
\phi_{\kappa_{0}}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
\phi_{\kappa_{0}}(x)=\kappa_{0}x
, we have

(a)

\tilde{\lambda}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}^{n}
span the cotangent space at every point.

(b)

\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{\lambda}^{i}-dx^{i})\subsetneq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{n}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

(c)

(\tilde{\lambda}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}^{n})\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\cdot((F_{0}^{-1}\circ\phi_{\kappa_{0}})^{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,(F_{0}^{-1}\circ\phi_{\kappa_{0}})^{*}\lambda^{n})\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}
.

(d)

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|\tilde{\lambda}^{i}-dx^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|d\tilde{\lambda}^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}}<c
.

We set
F_{1}:=\phi_{\kappa_{0}}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
.

Applying Theorem
4.2
to
\tilde{\lambda}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\lambda}^{n}
we obtain a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-chart
F_{2}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{B}^{n}
such that
F_{2}(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\supseteq B^{n}(F_{2}(0),\frac{1}{6})
,
\|F_{2}-\operatorname{id}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}}<c
and
(F_{2})_{*}\tilde{\lambda}^{1},\dots,(F_{2})_{*}\tilde{\lambda}^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
.

By
(c)
,
\left((F_{2}\circ F_{1}\circ F_{0})_{*}\lambda^{i}\right)\big|_{F_{2}(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})}={\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{0}}}\cdot(F_{2})_{*}\tilde{\lambda}^{i}\big|_{F_{2}(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
for
i=1,\dots,n
. We can take an
affine linear transformation

F_{3}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that
F_{3}(F_{2}(0))=0
and
F_{3}(B^{n}(F_{2}(0),\frac{1}{6}))\supseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
.

Now we have
F_{0}(U_{0})\supseteq\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
F_{1}(\mu_{0}\mathbb{B}^{n})\supseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
,
F_{2}(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\supseteq B^{n}(F_{2}(0),\frac{1}{6})
and
F_{3}(B^{n}(F_{2}(0),\frac{1}{6})\supseteq\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Take
\Phi:=(F_{3}\circ F_{2}\circ F_{1}\circ F_{0})^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathfrak{M}
. Since
F_{0},F_{1},F_{2},F_{3}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-diffeomorphism onto their images, we know know
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
diffeomorphism. Moreover, we have
\Phi(0)=p
because
F_{0}(p)=0
,
F_{1}(0)=0
,
F_{3}(F_{2}(0))=0
. Thus,
\Phi
is the diffeomorphism we desire with
U:=\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n})
, completing the proof.
∎

4.2.5
Remarks in harmonic coordinates

Given a non-smooth Riemannian metric
g
on a manifold,
\mathfrak{M}
, DeTurck and Kazdan showed that
g
has optimal regularity
in harmonic coordinates
[
DK81
, Lemma 1.2]
(in the Zygmund-Hölder sense),
but may not have optimal regularity in geodesic normal coordinates
[
DK81
, Example 2.3]
(in fact, the regularity
of
g
in geodesic normal coordinates may be two derivatives worse than the regularity in harmonic coordinates).

We present analogous results for vector fields.
Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be
C^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-vector fields on a
C^{2}
-manifold
\mathfrak{M}
that form a local basis for the tangent space at every point. In Section
5.3.4
we give an example showing that the canonical coordinate system with respect to
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
may not have optimal regularity

In Section
4.2.1
we defined a Riemannian metric
g=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda^{i}\cdot\lambda^{i}
where
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
is the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
(see Remark
4.2.2
).
With respect to this metric,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
form an orthonormal basis at every point. Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in C^{1}
,
