we can talk about the metric Laplacian
\Delta_{g}
with respect to
g
.

Proposition 4.2.15
.

In harmonic coordinates with respect to
g
,
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
have optimal regularity.

More precisely, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
and
g
be as above, and let
\beta>1
. Suppose there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-atlas
\mathfrak{A}
which is compatible with the
C^{2}
-atlas of
\mathfrak{M}
, such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
on
\mathfrak{A}
, let
\psi:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a harmonic coordinate chart
3
3

3

Such a harmonic coordinate chart
\psi
always exists locally when
\beta>1
, see also
[
DK81
, Lemma 1.2]
.
, then
\psi_{*}X_{1},\dots,\psi_{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(V;\mathbb{R}^{n})
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\psi
is a
\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta+1}
-map with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{A})
. Once this is done, using that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to
(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{A})
, we get that
\psi_{*}X_{1},\dots,\psi_{*}X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Since the statement
\psi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta+1}(U;V)
is local,
we may without loss of generality, shrink
U
.
By doing so, the hypotheses of the proposition
imply that there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-coordinate chart
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{n}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
on
U
(respect to
\mathfrak{A}
). In this coordinate chart we can write
\Delta_{g}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\det g}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}(\sqrt{\det g}g^{ij}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})
where
g^{ij}
and
\sqrt{\det g}
are as in (
4.2.7
).

By assumption
\Delta_{g}\psi^{k}=0
for
k=1,\dots,n
. Note that on
(x^{1},\dots,x^{n})
,
\Delta_{g}
is a second order divergent form elliptic operator with
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-coefficients. By a classical elliptic estimates (for example,
[
Tay11
, Proposition 4.1]
) we have that
\psi^{k}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to
\mathfrak{A}
, completing the proof.
∎

Remark 4.2.16
.

In fact the coordinate chart we construct in Proposition
4.2.7
(also see (
4.2.8
)) is closely related to harmonic coordinates.

Remark 4.2.17
.

While Proposition
4.2.15
shows that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
have optimal regularity with respect to harmonic coordinates, this fact along does not give a practical test for what the optimal regularity is. Corollary
4.2.13
, on the other hand, provides such a test.

Remark 4.2.18
.

Proposition
4.2.15
shows that harmonic coordinates induces a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
-atlas with respect to which
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
. It is possible that the harmonic coordinates induces some
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}
-atlas for some
\gamma>\beta
while
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are only
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
with respect to this atlas; see Example
4.2.19
below.

Example 4.2.19
.

Endow
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with standard coordinates
(x,y)
, and let
\theta\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be a function which is not smooth. Set
X:=\cos(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\sin(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
Y:=-\sin(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+\cos(\theta(x,y))\frac{\partial}{\partial x}
. The corresponding metric is
g=dx^{2}+dy^{2}
since
X,Y
form an orthonormal basis with respect to the standard Euclidean metric, thus
\Delta_{g}=\Delta=\partial_{x}^{2}+\partial_{y}^{2}
.

Therefore the singleton
\{(x,y):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}\}
is an atlas of harmonic coordinates for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and since harmonic functions are real-analytic, we know the collection of harmonic coordinates with respect to
\Delta_{g}
defines an real-analytic structure for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(which coincides with the standard real-analytic structure). Even though the differential structure induced by the harmonic coordinates is real analytic,
X
and
Y
cannot be smooth under any coordinate system (since they are not smooth with respect to these
harmonic coordinates).

Chapter 5
The Theorems and The Proofs

5.1
The Real Frobenius Theorems

5.1.1
History remark for rough involutivity

For vector fields
X
and
Y
which are not
C^{1}
, the commutator
[X,Y]
may not be defined pointwise. Because of this, any Frobenius type theorem for subbundles of less than
C^{1}
-regularity requires a suitable notion of involutivity. This leads to the following:

Question 5.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a tangent subbundle which is not
C^{1}
, and let vector fields
X,Y
be sections of
\mathcal{V}
.

•

What is the good definition of the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
?

•

With this definition how can we say whether
[X,Y]
is a section of
\mathcal{V}
?

When
X
and
Y
are Lipschitz vector fields, the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
makes sense as a vector field with
L^{\infty}
-coefficients. And we can talk about sections and involutivity almost everywhere. In
[
Sim96
]
, Simić gives the following definition:

Definition 5.1.2

(Almost everywhere characterization)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold, let
\mathcal{V}
be a Lipschitz subbundle of
T\mathfrak{M}
, and let
X,Y
be two Lipschitz sections of
\mathcal{V}
.
We say
[X,Y]
is an
almost everywhere section
of
\mathcal{V}
, if as a vector field with
L^{\infty}
-coefficients,
[X,Y](p)\in\mathcal{V}_{p}
holds for almost every
p\in\mathfrak{M}
.

We say
\mathcal{V}
is
almost everywhere involutive
, if for any Lipschitz sections
X,Y
of
\mathcal{V}
, the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
is an almost everywhere section of
\mathcal{V}
.

Later Rampazzo
[
Ram07
]
gave another characterizations using set-valued Lie brackets:

Definition 5.1.3

(Set valued characterization)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold, let
\mathcal{V}
be a Lipschitz subbundle of
T\mathfrak{M}
, and let
X,Y
be two Lipschitz sections of
\mathcal{V}
. The
set-valued Lie bracket

[X,Y]_{set}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
is defined as the convex hall
[X,Y]_{set}(p):=\operatorname{conv}([X,Y]_{set}^{+}(p))\subseteq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
,
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, where

[X,Y]_{set}^{+}:=\overline{\{(q,[X,Y](q))\in T\mathfrak{M}:X,Y\text{ are differentiable at }q\}}\subseteq T\mathfrak{M}.

And we say
\mathcal{V}
is
set-valuedly involutive
, if
[X,Y]_{set}\subseteq\mathcal{V}
for every Lipschitz sections
X,Y
of
\mathcal{V}
.

Definitions
5.1.2
and
5.1.3
are equivalent, see
[
Ram07
, Section 2]
and
[
Ram07
, Theorem 4.11 (I) and (III)]
.

An advantage of set valued bracket is that it is defined pointwise everywhere.
Note that a Lipschitz function is differentiable almost everywhere, so
[X,Y]_{set}(p)\neq\varnothing
for all
p\in M
.

Proposition 5.1.4

(Lipschitz Frobenius theorem,
[
Sim96
,
Ram07
]
)
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
C^{1,1}
manifold, and let
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be a Lipschitz tangent subbundle of rank
r
. If
\mathcal{V}
is either almost everywhere involutive or set-valuedly involutive, then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there is a neighborhood
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}_{s}
of
0
and a bi-Lipschitz parameterization
\Phi(t,s):\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
satisfying the following:

(i)

\Phi(0)=p
.

(ii)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}:\Omega\to T\mathfrak{M}
are Lipschitz continuous maps.

(iii)

For any
(t,s)\in\Omega
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,s)\in T_{\Phi(t,s)}\mathfrak{M}
form a basis of the linear subspace
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,s)}
.

Both
[
Sim96
]
and
[
Ram07
]
only deal with Lipschitz vector fields and Lipschitz subbundles. What happen if they are non-Lipschitz? Indeed,
[X,Y]
is not necessarily
L^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
so we cannot use an almost everywhere characterization. A non-Lipschitz section can be nowhere differentiable so we cannot use set-valued bracket either.

To resolve this problem, we use generalized functions. When the vector fields
X,Y
are log-Lipschitz, or more generally when
X,Y
are Hölder
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, by Corollary
2.1.19
,
[X,Y]
is a vector field with
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-coefficients, see Lemma
2.1.34
and Definition
3.1.1
.

By imitating Definition
0
we can give a definition for the distributional sections of
\mathcal{V}
as follows:

Definition

(Distributional sections of subbundles)
.

Let
\alpha>0
and
\beta>-\alpha
. Let
\mathcal{V}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
tangent subbundle of a manifold
\mathfrak{M}
. We say a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-vector field
Z=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z^{i}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
is a distributional section of
\mathcal{V}
, if for any log-Lipschitz section
\theta
of
\mathcal{V}^{\bot}
, written as
\theta=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta_{j}dx^{j}
, the generalized function
1
1

1

Using Lemma
2.1.18

(ii)
, the right hand side of (
5.1.1
) makes sense as a distribution in
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
. Also see Definition
3.1.1
.

\langle\theta,Z\rangle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}Z^{j}\theta_{j}\in\mathscr{D}^{\prime}(\mathfrak{M}),

(5.1.1)

is identically zero in the sense of distributions.

One can see that this is the characterization in Proposition
3.1.10

(S.4)
. See also Remark
3.1.6

(c)
.

The characterization of distributional sections and distributional involutivity recover the above characterizations when
\mathcal{V}
is Lipschitz. Indeed, for Lipschitz vector fields
X,Y
that are sections of
\mathcal{V}
,
[X,Y]=0
lays in
\mathcal{V}
almost everywhere if and only if
\langle\theta,[X,Y]\rangle=0
, so Definitions
0
and
5.1.2
are equivalent.

Recently in
[
LTW19
]
, a version of involutivity was introduced using approximations by smooth functions.
[
LTW19
]
works on differential forms (see
[
LTW19
, Definition 1.12]
). An equivalent characterization using vector fields is as follows:

Definition 5.1.5
.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be a continuous rank-
r
tangent subbundle over
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
We say
\mathcal{V}
is
strongly asymptotically involutive
, if for any
p\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
p
, and sequence of
C^{1}
-vector fields
\{X_{1}^{\nu},\dots,X^{\nu}_{r}\}_{\nu=1}^{\infty}
on
U
, such that

(i)

For each
j=1,\dots,r
,
\{X_{j}^{\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{\infty}
uniformly converges to a continuous vector field
X_{j}
, such that for each
q\in U
,
X_{1}(q),\dots,X_{r}(q)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
form a linear basis of
\mathcal{V}_{q}
.

(ii)

There exists
\{c_{jk}^{l\nu}\}_{\nu=1}^{\infty}\subset C^{0}(\bar{U})
for
1\leq j,k,l\leq r
, such that

\exists t_{0}>0,\ \lim\limits_{\nu\to\infty}\bigg(\max\limits_{1\leq j,k\leq r}\Big\|[X_{j}^{\nu},X_{k}^{\nu}]-\sum_{l=1}^{r}c_{jk}^{l\nu}X_{l}^{\nu}\Big\|_{C^{0}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})}\bigg)\cdot\exp\big(t_{0}\cdot\max\limits_{1\leq l\leq r}\|\nabla X_{l}^{\nu}\|_{C^{0}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\big)=0.

(5.1.2)

In
[
LTW19
]
it is shown that if
\mathcal{V}
is strongly asymptotically involutive, then locally there exists a topological parameterization
\Phi(t,s)\in C^{1}_{t}(C^{0}_{s})
such that
\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
span
\mathcal{V}
. However, given a continuous subbundle, we do not know an algorithm to check whether such an approximation exists. In contrast, Definition
0
can be directly checked using coordinate systems.

In the log-Lipschitz setting we prove that distributional involutivity implies strongly asymptotically involutivity. See Corollary
2.1.50
and Remark
2.1.51
.

5.1.2
The Frobenius theorem for log-Lipschitz subbundles: proof of Theorem
1

For application to the proof of Theorems
4
and
5
, we give a refinement of estimates in the results of Theorem
1
.

Theorem 5.1
.

Keep the assumptions of Theorem
1
, by shrinking the neighborhood
\Omega
that has the form
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}_{s}
and write the inverse map
F:=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
as
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
where
F^{\prime}:\Phi(\Omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
and
F^{\prime\prime}:\Phi(\Omega)\to\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
, then
F^{\prime}\in C^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega);\mathbb{R}^{r})
. Moreover

(5)

For any
\varepsilon>0
there is an open subset
\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
of
0
, such that
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Omega^{\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\mathrm{LogL},1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Omega^{\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
. Moreover by taking
\Omega_{\varepsilon}=\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega_{\varepsilon});\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
.

(5’)

Suppose
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}
. Then for the same
\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime}
in
(5)
, automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{2,1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Omega^{\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1,1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon},\Omega^{\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
.

(5”)

Suppose
\mathcal{V}
is little log-Lipschitz. Then automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL},1-}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
. and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega);\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
.

(5’​’​’)

Let
\alpha\in\{\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup(1,\infty]\cup\{k+\mathrm{Lip},k+\mathrm{LogL}:k=1,2,3,\dots\}
. Suppose
\mathfrak{M}
is smooth and
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
, then automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha}(\Omega;T\mathfrak{M})
,
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega);\mathbb{R}^{r})
and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Phi(\Omega);\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
.

Note that
(5)
,
(5”)
and
(5’​’​’)
in Theorem
5.1
are stronger statement than
(4)
,
(4’)
and
(4”)
in Theorem
1
, respectively.

Proof of Theorems
1
and
5.1
.

By Remark
3.1.2
and by passing to a local coordinate chart of the manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, we can assume that
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open subset with the base point
p=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

By Lemma
2.3.9
we can find a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p=0
, a linear coordinate system
(x,y)=(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r})
for
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and commutative log-Lipschitz vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
defined on
U
that span
\mathcal{V}
at every point in
U
and has the form (
2.3.4
), i.e. for some
a_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U)
,

X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}a_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq r.

(5.1.3)

Let
\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
be an open neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
such that
\{(0,s)\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}:v\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}\}\Subset U
. We can take a small enough number
\tau_{0}>0
such that the following map
\Phi(t,s):B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}\to U
is defined:

\Phi(t,s):=\Phi(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},s):=e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(0,s),\hskip 10.00002pt|t|<\tau_{0},\hskip 10.00002pts\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}.

(5.1.4)

Clearly
\Phi(0,0)=0
which gives
(1)
.

Applying Lemma
2.3.31
with
S=\{0\}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, by choosing
\tau_{0}>0
small enough,
\Phi
is well-defined, differentiable in
t
, and satisfies

\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}\Phi(t,s)=X_{j}(\Phi(t,s)),\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq r,\hskip 10.00002pt|t|<\tau_{0},\hskip 10.00002pts\in\Omega^{\prime\prime}.

Since
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are linearly independent and span
\mathcal{V}
in the domain, we see that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t,s),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t,s)
spans
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,s)}
for
(t,s)
in the domain
B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
. This gives
(3)
.

Next we prove that
\Phi
is homeomorphism onto its image by constructing the inverse map. We still use
(x,y)
as the standard coordinate system for
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Thus we have the natural coordinate maps
x^{j}(q^{1},\dots,q^{n})=q^{j}
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
y^{k}(q^{1},\dots,q^{n})=q^{r+k}
for
1\leq k\leq n-r
and
q\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Define
F^{\prime}(q):=x(q)
and
F^{\prime\prime}=(\lambda^{1},\dots\lambda^{n-r}):\Phi(\Omega)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}_{q}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
as

\lambda^{j}(q):=y^{j}(e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q)),\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq n-r.

(5.1.5)

We are going to show
\Phi\circ(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})\circ\Phi=\operatorname{id}
. This implies
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
and thus
\Phi
is homeomorphic onto its image.

Indeed, since
X_{j}
are of the form (
5.1.3
), we see that
\Phi
can be written as

\Phi(t,s)=(t,\phi(t,s)),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }\phi:B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-r}.

Also we have

e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q)=(0,y(e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q))),\hskip 10.00002ptx(e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q))=0,\hskip 10.00002ptq\in\Phi(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}).

(5.1.6)

Therefore for
(t,s)\in B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
, using
\Phi(t,s)=(t,\phi(t,s))
we have

\displaystyle(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})(\Phi(t,s))=

\displaystyle(x(t,\phi(t,s)),y(e^{-t^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-t^{1}X_{1}}(\Phi(t,s))))

(5.1.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t,y(e^{-t^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-t^{1}X_{1}}e^{t^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}X_{r}}(0,s)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t,y(0,s))=(t,s).

For
q\in\Phi(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime})
, by (
5.1.6
) we have

\displaystyle\Phi((F^{\prime}(q),F^{\prime\prime}(q))=

\displaystyle\Phi(x(q),y(e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q)))

(5.1.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{q^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{q^{r}X_{r}}(0,y(e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{q^{1}X_{1}}\dots e^{q^{r}X_{r}}(e^{-q^{r}X_{r}}\dots e^{-q^{1}X_{1}}(q))=q.

We get
\Phi\circ(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})=\operatorname{id}
and
(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})\circ\Phi=\operatorname{id}
. Since
B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
is open, we know
\Phi(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime})=(x,\lambda)^{-1}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime})
is also open.
This completes the proof of
(2)
whenever
\Omega
is an open neighborhood of
0
satisfying
\Omega\subseteq B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
.

Now
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime})
and it remains to prove the additional results in Theorem
5.1
, which implies
(4)
and
(4’)
and
(4”)
.

Since
F^{\prime}(q)=x(q)
arise from the coordinate chart, the regularity of
F^{\prime}
is the same as the regularity assumption for
\mathfrak{M}
.

By Lemma
2.3.9

(ii)
, since the log-Lipschitz vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
in (
5.1.3
) are uniquely determined by
\mathcal{V}
and the coordinate system
(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r})
, we see that

•

If
\mathcal{V}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
where
\alpha\in\{\mathrm{logl},\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup[1,\infty]\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=1,2,3,\dots\}
, then
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are automatically
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
as well.

By Corollary
2.3.42
we see that

•

For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\tau_{\varepsilon}\in(0,\tau_{0})
such that
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(\Phi(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime});\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
. In particular by Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}=X_{j}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL},1-\varepsilon}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

•

If
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}
, then automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{2}L^{\infty}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}L^{\infty}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
for
1\leq j\leq r
. In particular
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{2,1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,1-\varepsilon}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
.

•

If
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{logl}}
, then automatically
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1+\mathrm{LogL},1-}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}(\Phi(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime});\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
. In particular
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}=X_{j}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL},1-}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

By Lemmas
2.3.41
and
2.2.38

(i)
and Corollary
2.1.31

(i)
we see that

•

If
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
where
\alpha\in\{\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup(1,\infty]\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=1,2,3,\dots\}
, then
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0}),\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Phi(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime});\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
. In particular
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}=X_{j}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime};U)
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

Take
\Omega^{\prime}:=B^{r}(0,\tau_{0})
and
\Omega^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}:=B^{r}(0,\tau_{\varepsilon})
. The above results give
(5)
,
(5’)
,
(5”)
and
(5’​’​’)
respectively, finishing the whole proof.
∎

5.1.3
A PDE counterpart of Theorem
1

We can interpret Theorem
1
in terms of the first-order PDE system following from
[
Ram07
, Section 5]
:

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
n
-dimensional
C^{1,1}
-manifold. Let
L_{1},\dots,L_{r}
be some first order differential operators on
\mathfrak{M}
with log-Lipschitz coefficients, such that

L_{j}L_{k}-L_{k}L_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{r}c_{jk}^{l}\cdot L_{l},

(5.1.9)

for some
c_{jk}^{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
,
1\leq j,k,l\leq r
, in the sense of distributions.

Suppose there is a
(n-r)
-dimensional
C^{1}
-submanifold
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathfrak{M}
such that

\operatorname{Span}(L_{1}|_{q}\dots,L_{r}|_{q})\oplus T_{q}\mathfrak{S}=T_{q}\mathfrak{M},\hskip 20.00003pt\forall q\in\mathfrak{S}.

(5.1.10)

Let
0<\beta\leq 1
and
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{S})
. Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{S}
and any
0<\varepsilon<\beta
, there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
, such that there exists a unique solution
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
to the Cauchy problem

\begin{cases}L_{j}f=0,&1\leq j\leq r,\\
f|_{\mathfrak{S}\cap U}=h.\end{cases}

(5.1.11)

Here
L_{j}f=0
holds in the sense of distributions.

Applying Lemma
2.1.18

(ii)
with
L_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T\mathfrak{M})
and
df\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
, we know that
L_{j}f\in C^{\beta-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
is defined as a distribution.

If in addition
L_{1},\dots,L_{r}
have little log-Lipschitz coefficients, we can show that
f
given (
5.1.11
) is indeed
\mathscr{C}^{\beta-}
. We omit the proof to reader.

The proof is based on the construction of
\Phi
in (
5.1.4
), along with the following proposition on inverse functions.

Lemma 5.1.6
.

Let
(x,y)=(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r})
be the standard coordinate system of
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
be an open neighborhood of
0
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
be commutative log-Lipschitz vector fields on
U
that have the form
X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}a_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

Let
\Gamma=(\Gamma^{1},\dots,\Gamma^{n-r}):\mathbb{R}^{n-r}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
be a
C^{1}
-map such that
\Gamma(0)=0
and
\nabla\Gamma(0)=0
. There is a neighborhood
U_{0}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
such that

e^{\Gamma(s)\cdot X}(0,\mu(s))=(\Gamma(s),s),

defines a unique map
\mu:U_{0}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
. Moreover,

•

\mu
is hemeomorphism onto its image. In particular
\mu(U_{0}^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
is open.

•

For any
\varepsilon>0
there is a smaller neighborhood
U_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq U_{0}^{\prime\prime}
such that
\mu\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
and
\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\mu(U_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime});\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
.

Proof.

By assumption
X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}a_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}
, we get
\mu(s)=y(e^{-\Gamma(s)\cdot X}(\Gamma(s),s))
. Thus
\mu
is uniquely defined and is a continuous map in the domain and satisfies
\mu(0)=0
.
By Lemma
2.3.31
(since
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are commutative) we see that for every
0<\varepsilon<1
there is a neighborhood
\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
of
0
such that
\mu\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
.

It remains to show that for every
0<\varepsilon<1
there are a
c_{\varepsilon}>0
and a neighborhood
U^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\tilde{U}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
of
0
, such that

|e^{-\Gamma(s_{1})\cdot X}(\Gamma(s_{1}),s_{1})-e^{-\Gamma(s_{2})\cdot X}(\Gamma(s_{2}),s_{2})|\geq c|s_{1}-s_{2}|^{\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s_{1},s_{2}\in U_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}.

(5.1.12)

Once (
5.1.12
) is done, using
e^{-\Gamma(s)\cdot X}(\Gamma(s),s)=(0,\mu(s))
we know
\mu
is injective in
U_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\mu(U_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime})\to\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
is
\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}
. In particular this implies
\mu
and
\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are both continuous. Taking
U_{0}^{\prime\prime}:=U_{1/2}^{\prime\prime}
then we finish the proof.

To prove (
5.1.12
), for any open neighborhood
V_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
of
0
such that
\{e^{u\cdot X}(\Gamma(s),s):s\in V_{1},|u|\leq|\Gamma(s)|\}\Subset U
, we have

|e^{(\Gamma(s_{2})-\Gamma(s_{1}))\cdot X}(\Gamma(s_{1}),s_{1})-(\Gamma(s_{2}),s_{2})|\geq|s_{1}-s_{2}|-\|\nabla\Gamma\|_{C^{0}(V_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}\|X\|_{C^{0}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n})}|s_{2}-s_{1}|,\hskip 10.00002pts_{1},s_{2}\in V_{1}.

Since
\nabla\Gamma(0)=0
, by continuity we can choose
V_{1}
small enough so that
\|\nabla\Gamma\|_{C^{0}(V_{1})}\|X\|_{C^{0}(U)}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Thus

|e^{(\Gamma(s_{2})-\Gamma(s_{1}))\cdot X}(\Gamma(s_{1}),s_{1})-(\Gamma(s_{2}),s_{2})|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|s_{1}-s_{2}|,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s_{1},s_{2}\in V_{1}.

(5.1.13)

Let
W_{1}\Subset U
be an open set containing
\{(\Gamma(s),s):s\in V_{1}\}
. By Lemma
2.3.31
, for each
\varepsilon
we can find
\delta>0
such that
e^{t\cdot X}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(W_{1};U)
uniformly for
t\in B^{r}(0,\delta)
. Take
U^{\prime\prime}:=\{s\in V_{1}:|\Gamma(s)|<\frac{1}{2}\delta\}
, we have for every
s_{1},s_{2}\in U^{\prime\prime}
,

|e^{(\Gamma(s_{2})-\Gamma(s_{1}))\cdot X}(\Gamma(s_{1}),s_{1})-(\Gamma(s_{2}),s_{2})|\leq\sup_{|u|<\delta}\|e^{u\cdot X}\|_{C^{0,1-\varepsilon}(W_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n})}|e^{-\Gamma(s_{1})\cdot X}(\Gamma(s_{1}),s_{1})-e^{-\Gamma(s_{2})}(\Gamma(s_{2}),s_{2})|^{1-\varepsilon}.

(5.1.14)

Combining (
5.1.13
) and (
5.1.14
) we get (
5.1.12
) with
c:=(2\sup_{|u|<\delta}\|e^{u\cdot X}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}(W_{1})})^{-\frac{1}{1-\varepsilon}}>0
, finishing the proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

By (
5.1.10
) and that
\dim\mathfrak{S}=n-r
, we know that
L_{1},\dots,L_{r}
are linearly independent at every point in
\mathfrak{S}
. By continuity there is a neighborhood
\tilde{U}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
\mathfrak{S}
such that they are still linearly independent.

We can define a tangent subbundle
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}|_{\tilde{U}}
which is spanned by
L_{1},\dots,L_{r}
. By construction
\mathcal{V}
is a rank
r
log-Lipschitz subbundle. By Proposition
3.2.1

(I.8)
\Rightarrow
(I.4)
,
\mathcal{V}
is involutive in the sense of distributions.

By passing to an invertible linear transform we can assume that
\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{n-r}}|_{p}
span
T_{p}\mathfrak{S}\leq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. Applying Lemma
2.3.9
and shrinking
\tilde{U}
if necessary, we can find a coordinate system
(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r})
and a log-Lipschitz local basis
(X_{1},\dots,X_{r})
for
\mathcal{V}
on
\tilde{U}
such that
[X_{j},X_{k}]=0
for
1\leq j,k\leq r
and have expression
X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{k=1}^{n-r}a_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{k}}
. Thus by shrinking
\tilde{U}
, in the coordinate
(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r})
we can write

\mathfrak{S}\cap\tilde{U}=\{(\Gamma(s),s)\},\hskip 10.00002pts\in U_{0}^{\prime\prime},

(5.1.15)

where
U_{0}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
is a small neighborhood of
0
and
\Gamma:U_{0}^{\prime\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
is a
C^{1}
-map.

By taking a translation we can assume
p=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, thus
\Gamma
satisfies
\Gamma(0)=0
and
\nabla\Gamma(0)=0
.

Take
\tilde{\varepsilon}>0
be such that
\beta(1-\tilde{\varepsilon})^{2}\geq\beta-\varepsilon
. Recall in the proof of Theorem
1
in Section
5.1.2
, the map
\Phi(t,s)=e^{t\cdot X}(0,s)
is defined in a neighborhood of
(t,s)=(0,0)
, such that

•

\Phi
is a locally homeomorphism and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
is of the form
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}=(x,\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is given in (
5.1.5
).

•

There is a neighborhood
U_{1}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
0
, such that
\lambda:U_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
is
\mathscr{C}^{1-\tilde{\varepsilon}}
.

•

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}=X_{j}\circ\Phi
hold for
j=1,\dots,r
.

If
f
is a Hölder function satisfying
X_{j}f=0
for
j=1,\dots,r
, then
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}(f\circ\Phi)(t,s)=(X_{j}f)\circ\Phi(t,s)=0
. Therefore for
(t,s)
in a small enough neighborhood of
0
,

f(\Phi(t,s))=f(\Phi(\Gamma(\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s)),s))=f(\Gamma(\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s)),\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(s)).

(5.1.16)

Here
\mu
is a map given in Lemma
5.1.6
which is homeomorphism onto its image, and there is a neighborhood
U_{2}^{\prime\prime}
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
such that
\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\tilde{\varepsilon}}(U_{2}^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
.

Thus if
f|_{U\cap\mathfrak{S}}=h
, then in a neighborhood of
0
we must have

\displaystyle f(x,y)

\displaystyle=f(\Phi(x,\lambda(x,y)))=f(\Gamma(\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\lambda(x,y))),\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\lambda(x,y)))=h(\Gamma(\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\lambda(x,y))),\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(\lambda(x,y))).

Thus
f
is completely determined by
h
. This completes the proof of the uniqueness.

Let
U:=\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}(U^{\prime\prime}_{2})\cap U_{1}
. By composition we have
\mu^{\mathrm{Inv}}\circ\lambda\in\mathscr{C}^{(1-\tilde{\varepsilon})^{2}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{n-r})
. Since
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathfrak{S})
, taking compositions we get
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta(1-\tilde{\varepsilon})^{2}}(U)\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\beta-\varepsilon}(U)
, finishing the proof of the existence and hence the whole proof.
∎

5.1.4
The bi-layer Frobenius theorem

In this subsection we apply Theorem
1
to give a 2-step Frobenius theorem which is used in the proof of Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
.

In the proof of Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
, we need to apply the real Frobenius theorem on
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and on
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
simultaneously. In order to achieve the best possible regularity, we need a slightly different proof.

In the following, we endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinate system
t=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r})
,
x=(x^{1},\dots,x^{m})
and
s=(s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
respectively. For the case
\kappa=\infty
, we use the convention
\kappa-1=\infty
.

Theorem 5.3

(Bi-layer Frobenius theorem)
.

Let
\kappa\in(2,\infty]
, Let
r,m,q
be non-negative integers. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
(r+m+q)
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold. Let
\mathcal{V}\leq\mathcal{E}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
be two log-Lipschitz involutive real tangent subbundles such that
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+}
has rank
r
, and
\mathcal{E}
has rank
r+m
.

Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, there is a neighborhood
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
of
(0,0,0)
and a topological parameterization
\Phi(t,x,s):\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
, such that by denoting
U:=\Phi(\Omega)
and
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
:

(1)

\Phi(0)=p
and
F(p)=(0,0,0)
.

(2)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}:\Omega\to T\mathfrak{M}
are all continuous maps for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
. In other words
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
are all defined continuous vector fields on
U
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(3)

For each
\omega\in\Omega
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(\omega)\in T_{\Phi(\omega)}\mathfrak{M}
span
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(\omega)}
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(\omega)
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{m}}(\omega)\in T_{\Phi(\omega)}\mathfrak{M}
span
\mathcal{E}_{\Phi(\omega)}
.
In other words
\mathcal{V}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
, and
\mathcal{E}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}
.

Let
\gamma\in(1,\infty]\cup\{k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=1,2,\dots\}
such that
\gamma\leq\kappa-1
. Suppose
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
, then automatically

(4)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\kappa}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma}(U;\mathbb{R}^{m})
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma}(U;T\mathfrak{M}|_{U})
.

(5)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\min(\gamma-1,\mathrm{LogL})}(U;T\mathfrak{M}|_{U})
.

Moreover for every
0<\varepsilon<1
there is a neighborhood
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime\prime}(\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m})
, such that by writing
\Omega_{\varepsilon}:=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}
and
U_{\varepsilon}:=\Phi(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
,

(6)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma+1,\min(\gamma,1+\mathrm{LogL}),1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1-\varepsilon}(U_{\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(7)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,1+\mathrm{LogL}),1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma-1,\mathrm{LogL}),(\gamma-1)\circ(1-\varepsilon)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

Let
\beta\in[1,\infty]\cup\{\mathrm{logl},k+\mathrm{LogL},k+\mathrm{Lip}:k=0,1,2,\dots\}
and we assume in addition that
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. Then we have the following regularity estimates:

(5’)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\min(\gamma-1,\beta)}(U;T\mathfrak{M}|_{U})
.

Moreover, when
\beta=1
, automatically

(6’)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\min(\gamma,2)}L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1}L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\min(\gamma-1,1)}L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(7’)

In particular let
\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}
and
U_{\varepsilon}=\Phi(\Omega_{\varepsilon})
be as above, then
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma+1,\min(\gamma,2),1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,2),1-\varepsilon}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma-1,1),(\gamma-1)\circ(1-\varepsilon)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
, automatically

(6”)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma+1,\min(\gamma,1+\mathrm{LogL}),1-}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{1-}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(7”)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,1+\mathrm{LogL}),1-}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma-1,\mathrm{LogL}),(\gamma-1)\circ(1-)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
, automatically

(6’​’​’)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma+1,\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\min(\gamma,\beta)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\beta}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(7’​’​’)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\min(\gamma,\beta)}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma-1,\beta),(\gamma-1)\circ\beta}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

For definition of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}
-type maps, see Definitions
2.2.18

(ii)
and
2.3.2
. Recall in Definition
2.1.23
that we have orders
1-\varepsilon<1-<\mathrm{LogL}<1<\mathrm{Lip}<1+\varepsilon
for all
\varepsilon>0
.

Here the regularity results for
F
and those for
\Phi
do not directly imply each other.

Remark 5.1.7
.

For the regularity estimates of
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}
we use the notation of index composition in Definition
2.1.29
: by Corollary
2.1.31
we have, for every
0<\varepsilon<1
and
\beta>\mathrm{Lip}
,

•

\alpha\in(0,1)
:
\alpha\circ(1-\varepsilon)=\alpha(1-\varepsilon)
,
\alpha\circ(1-)=\alpha\circ\mathrm{LogL}=\alpha\circ 1=\alpha-
and
\alpha\circ\mathrm{Lip}=\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha
.

•

\alpha\in\{\mathrm{LogL},1\}
:
\alpha\circ(1-\varepsilon)=(1-\varepsilon)-
,
\alpha\circ(1-)=\alpha\circ\mathrm{LogL}=\alpha\circ 1=1-
,
\alpha\circ\mathrm{Lip}=\mathrm{LogL}
and
\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha
.

•

\alpha=\mathrm{Lip}
:
\alpha\circ\delta=\delta
for
\delta\in\{1-\varepsilon,1-,\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{Lip}\}
,
\alpha\circ 1=\mathrm{LogL}
and
\alpha\circ\beta=\alpha
.

•

\alpha>\mathrm{Lip}
:
\alpha\circ\delta=\min(\delta,\alpha)
for all
\delta\in\{1-\varepsilon,1-,\mathrm{LogL},1,\mathrm{Lip},\beta\}
.

Remark 5.1.8
.

Theorem
5.3
is necessary in the proof of Theorems
4

(5)
and
5

(5)
, where we need a double foliations for
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. Say
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
for some
\mathrm{Lip}\leq\beta<\gamma<2
, only using “single” Frobenius theorem then we will not achieve the optimal regularity estimate: if we foliate
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
first, then the pullback of
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is only
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
which is not
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
; if we foliate
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
first, then the pullback of
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-1}
, which is worse than log-Lipschitz, where real Frobenius theorem cannot apply.

In Theorem
5.3
, if we take either
m=0
i.e.
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{E}
or
r=0
,
\gamma=\kappa-1
, i.e.
\mathcal{V}=\{0\}
, we get the Hölder estimate immediately for the classical real Frobenius theorem (see also Theorem
1

(4”)
and Theorem
5.1

(5’​’​’)
).

Proof of Theorem
5.3
.

We can pick an initial
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-coordinate system near
p

(u,v,w)=(u^{1},\dots,u^{r},v^{1},\dots,v^{m},w^{1},\dots,w^{q}):U_{0}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q},

(5.1.17)

such that

•

u(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
v(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,
w(p)=0\in\mathbb{R}^{q}
;

•

\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{r}}|_{p}
form a real basis for
\mathcal{V}_{p}\leq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
;

•

\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{r}}|_{p},\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{m}}|_{p}
form a real basis for
\mathcal{E}_{p}\leq T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
.

Thus, we can identify
\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}
with their pushforwards on the open neighborhood of
(0,0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
. In other words, we can assume
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}
and
p=(0,0,0)
.

Applying Lemma
2.3.9
, on
\mathcal{V}
and on
\mathcal{E}
respectively, we can find a smaller neighborhood
U_{1}\subseteq U_{0}
of
p=0
, a
C^{1}
-local basis
T=[T_{1},\dots,T_{r}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{V}
on
U_{1}
, and a
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
-local basis
\tilde{X}=[X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}]^{\top}=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}]^{\top}
for
\mathcal{E}
on
U_{1}
, that have the following form:

T=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+A^{\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial v}+A^{\prime\prime}\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{X}=\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
X^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I_{r}&&B^{\prime}\\
&I_{m}&B^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial w}\end{pmatrix},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{on }U_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}.

(5.1.18)

Here
A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r\times m})
,
A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r\times n})
,
B^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r\times n})
and
B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{m\times n})
.

For
\beta,\gamma
in the assumptions, by Lemma
2.3.9

(ii)
, if
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
and
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, then automatically
A^{\prime},A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
and
B^{\prime},B^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
.

By (
5.1.18
) we see that
T_{1},\dots,T_{r},X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are linearly independent, so they form a
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
-local basis for
\mathcal{E}
on
U_{1}
.

We can now define our map
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}\to\mathfrak{M}
for a small enough
\Omega
, as

\Phi(t,x,s):=\exp_{T}(t,\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}(x,(0,0,s)))=e^{tT}e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}((0,0,s)).

(5.1.19)

The notation
\exp_{T},\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}
are given in Definition
2.3.30
. By taking
\tau_{0}
in Lemma
2.3.41
small enough we see that
\Phi
is defined is when
\Omega
is small enough.

(1)
is immediately since
\Phi(0)=(0,0,0)=p
.

We first show that
\Phi
is homeomorphisms onto its image by constructing the inverse map.

Denote
\tilde{u}:U_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}
,
\tilde{v}:U_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}
and
\tilde{w}:U_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}
by
\tilde{u}(u,v,w)=u
,
\tilde{v}(u,v,w):=v
and
\tilde{w}(u,v,w):=w
as the natural projections
2
2

2

We abuse of notation here. It is more convenient to use
u,v,w
as variables in the proof. Thus we use
\tilde{u},\tilde{v},\tilde{w}
for
u,v,w
in (
5.1.17
).
. We define maps
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime})
in a neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{r}_{u}\times\mathbb{R}^{m}_{v}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}
as

\begin{gathered}F^{\prime}:=\tilde{u},\quad F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,w):=\tilde{v}(e^{-u\cdot T}(u,v,w)),\quad F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v,w):=\tilde{w}(e^{-F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,w)\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}e^{-u\cdot T}(u,v,w)).\end{gathered}

(5.1.20)

Similar to the arguments in (
5.1.7
) and (
5.1.8
), using (
5.1.18
) we have

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(\Phi(t,x,s))

\displaystyle=\tilde{u}(e^{tT}e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,s))=\tilde{u}(t,\ast,\ast)=t;

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(t,x,s))

\displaystyle=\tilde{v}(e^{-tT}(e^{tT}e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,s)))=\tilde{v}(e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,s))=\tilde{v}(0,x,\ast)=x;

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\Phi(t,x,s))

\displaystyle=\tilde{w}(e^{-F^{\prime\prime}(\Phi(t,x,s))\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,s)))=\tilde{w}(e^{-xX^{\prime\prime}}e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,s))=\tilde{w}(0,0,s)=s;

\displaystyle\Phi(F(u,v,w))

\displaystyle=e^{uT}e^{F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,w)X^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v,w))=e^{uT}e^{F^{\prime\prime}\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}(e^{-F^{\prime\prime}\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}e^{-uT}(u,v,w))=(u,v,w).

Since
F
and
\Phi
are both continuous in their domains, we conclude that by choosing
\Omega
small,
\Phi
is homeomorphism onto its image, hence a topological parameterization. In particular, in what follows we assume that the domain
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}
of
\Phi
, the domain
U:=\Phi(\Omega)
of
F
, and the domain
U_{1}
of
(T,X)
satisfy the following:

\exp_{T}(\Omega^{\prime},U)\subseteq U_{1},\hskip 10.00002pt\exp_{X}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},U)\subseteq U_{1}.

(5.1.21)

Note that
F^{\prime}=\tilde{u}
implies
F^{\prime}(U)=F^{\prime}(\Phi(\Omega))=\Omega^{\prime}
.

Now by construction (
5.1.19
) we have for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
, in
\Omega
,

\textstyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}(t,x,s)=T_{j}\circ\Phi(t,x,s),\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}(t,x,s)=((e^{tT})_{*}X_{k})\circ\Phi(t,x,s)=(X_{k}\cdot\nabla_{u,v,w}(e^{tT}))\circ(e^{xX^{\prime\prime}}(0,0,s)).

(5.1.22)

In other words

\textstyle F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}=T_{j},\hskip 10.00002ptF^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}(u,v,w)=((e^{uT})_{*}X_{k})(u,v,w)=(X_{k}\cdot\nabla_{u,v,w}(e^{uT}))\circ(e^{-uT}(u,v,w)).

(5.1.23)

Since
T_{j},X_{k}
are continuous, and
\exp_{T}
is
C^{1}
, we see that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}
are all continuous, finishing the proof of
(2)
.

By construction (
5.1.17
) and (
5.1.19
), we know

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}(0)=T_{j}(0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial u^{j}}\big|_{p}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}(0)=X_{r+k}(0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial v^{k}}\big|_{p}
,
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
, are all linearly independent. By continuity we see that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(\omega)
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{m}}(\omega)
are still linearly independent for
\omega\in\mathbb{R}^{r+m+q}
closed 0. Since
T_{1}=\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,T_{r}=\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
are sections of
\mathcal{V}
, we see that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(\omega)
spans
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(\omega)}
when
\omega
small. Thus to prove
(3)
it suffices to prove that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{j}}(\omega)\in\mathcal{E}_{\Phi(\omega)}
for each
\omega
in the domain.

Applying Theorem
1
(or Theorem
2
) to the subbundle
\mathcal{E}
at the point
\Phi(\omega)\in\mathfrak{M}
, we see that there is a
C^{1}
(in fact
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}
) submanifold
\mathfrak{S}\subset\mathfrak{M}
containing
p
such that
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=T\mathfrak{S}
. Since
\mathcal{V}\leq\mathcal{E}
, we know
T_{1}|_{\mathfrak{S}},\dots,T_{r}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
are all (
C^{1}
) sections of
T\mathfrak{S}
. Therefore by shrinking
\Omega
if necessary, we have
e^{tT}|_{\Omega\cap\mathfrak{S}}:\Omega\cap\mathfrak{S}\to\mathfrak{S}
. Since
e^{tT}
is
C^{1}
, by restricted on
\mathfrak{S}
,
(e^{tT})_{*}X_{j}
are also sections of
T\mathfrak{S}=\mathcal{E}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
. In particular
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{j}}(\omega)=((e^{tT})_{*}X_{j})\circ\Phi(\omega)\in\mathcal{E}_{\Phi(\omega)}
, finishing the proof of
(3)
.

We now begin the regularity estimate and we assume
\mathcal{V}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
,
\mathcal{E}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. Thus
T\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
and
X^{\prime},X^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
in their domains.

Clearly
F^{\prime}=\tilde{u}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
. By Corollary
2.3.42
we have
\exp_{T}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
, thus by (
5.1.20
) we get
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
. By (
5.1.23
) and (
5.1.18
) we have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}=T_{j}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
, finishing the proof of
(4)
.

For the rest of the regularity estimates, by Corollary
2.3.42
we have

•

\exp_{T}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\gamma}(\Omega^{\prime},U;U_{1})
and
\nabla_{u,v,w}\exp_{T}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\gamma-1}(\Omega^{\prime},U;\mathbb{R}^{r+m+q})
.

•

\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1}L^{\infty}(\Omega^{\prime\prime},U;U_{1})
. Here we use
1+\beta=1+\mathrm{LogL}
when
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
.

•

When
\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},1\}
, for every
0<\varepsilon<1
there is
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subseteq\Omega^{\prime\prime}
such that
\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,1-\varepsilon/2}(\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon},U;U_{1})
.

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
,
\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,1-}(\Omega^{\prime\prime},U;U_{1})
.

•

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
,
\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}(\Omega^{\prime\prime},U;U_{1})
.

For convenience and unification of the discussion, we set for
0<\varepsilon<1
,

\textstyle\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}:=1-\varepsilon/2,\text{ if }\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},1\};\hskip 10.00002pt\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}:=1-,\text{ if }\beta=\mathrm{logl};\hskip 10.00002pt\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}:=\beta,\text{ if }\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}.

(5.1.24)

Here
\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}
stands for the regularity index of
\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}
, on
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}
if
\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},1\}
, and on
\Omega^{\prime\prime}
if
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
.

Also we use the convention
\mathrm{logl}+1:=1+\mathrm{LogL}
. Therefore
\exp_{X^{\prime\prime}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}}
on
\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\times U
if
\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},1\}
, and on
\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times U
if
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
.

Therefore applying Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
to
\Phi
in (
5.1.19
),
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
in (
5.1.20
),
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}
in (
5.1.19
) and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}
in (
5.1.23
) we get

•

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\gamma\circ(\beta+1),\gamma\circ\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}}_{t,x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\min(\gamma,\beta^{\vee\varepsilon})}_{t,x,s}
. This gives the estimate of
\Phi
in
(6)
,
(7’)
,
(6”)
and
(6’​’​’)
.

•

F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min((\beta+1)\circ\gamma,\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}\circ\gamma)}_{u,v,w}=\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma,\beta^{\vee\varepsilon})}
(since
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
). This gives the estimate of
F
in
(6)
and
(6”)
, and
(6’​’​’)
if
\beta\leq\gamma
.

•

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}=T_{j}\circ\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma\circ(\gamma+1),\gamma\circ\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\gamma\circ\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}}_{t,x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,\beta+1),\min(\gamma,\beta^{\vee\varepsilon})}_{t,x,s}
. This gives the estimate of
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}
in
(7)
,
(7’)
,
(7”)
and
(7’​’​’)
.

•

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta\circ(\beta+1),\beta\circ\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}}_{x,s}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\gamma-1)\circ(\beta+1),(\gamma-1)\circ\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}}_{t,x,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\min(\beta,\gamma-1)\circ(\beta+1),\min(\beta,\gamma-1)\circ\beta^{\vee\varepsilon}}_{t,x,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\min(\beta,\gamma-1),(\gamma-1)\circ\beta^{\vee 2\varepsilon}}_{t,x,s}
. This gives the estimate of
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{k}}
in
(7)
,
(7’)
,
(7”)
and
(7’​’​’)
.

•

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta\circ\gamma}_{u,v,w}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma-1)\circ\gamma}_{u,v,w}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma-1)}
. This gives
(5)
and
(5’)
.

Also when
\beta=1
, by Corollary
2.1.31
we see that
\{\Phi(t,\cdot,s):t\in\Omega^{\prime},s\in\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma\circ 2}_{x}=\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma,2)}_{x}
,
\{\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}(t,\cdot,s):t\in\Omega^{\prime},s\in\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\gamma\circ 1}_{x}=\mathscr{C}^{1}_{x}
and
\{\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x}(t,\cdot,s):t\in\Omega^{\prime},s\in\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\}\subset\mathscr{C}^{1\circ 2}_{x}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma-1)\circ 2}_{x}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma-1,1)}_{x}
are all bounded sets. Hence
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma,2)}_{x}L^{\infty}_{t,s}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1}_{x}L^{\infty}_{t,s}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial x^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\gamma-1,1)}_{x}L^{\infty}_{t,s}
, finishing the proof of
(6’)
.

The only thing remains to prove is that
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
when
\beta>\gamma(>1)
, since the above composition argument only shows
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\beta,\gamma)}
when
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
.

To get
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
, we need to show the following

F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v,w)=\tilde{w}(e^{-u\cdot X^{\prime}-v\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}(u,v,w)),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ in a neighborhood of }p=(0,0,0)\in\mathfrak{M}.

(5.1.25)

Once (
5.1.25
) is known, by the property
\exp_{X}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}
(since
\beta>1
) from Lemma
2.3.41
, we get
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
and complete the proof of
(6’​’​’)
.

To prove (
5.1.25
), we fix a point
(u,v,w)\in U
in the domain of
F
and let
\mathfrak{S}:=\{(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})\in U:F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u^{\prime},v^{\prime},w^{\prime})=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v,w)\}
. By shrinking
U
if necessary, since we only care about a neighborhood of
p
, we can assume that
\mathfrak{S}
is a connected set.

Since
\min(\gamma,\beta)>1
, by (
5.1.20
) we know
F
is a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart, thus
dF^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a collection of
q
differentials which are linearly independent at every point, therefore the level sets of
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
are all
q
-dimensional
C^{1}
-submanifolds, in particular
\mathfrak{S}
is
C^{1}
. By the result
(3)
we know that
\mathcal{E}=\operatorname{Span}(dF^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\bot}
, therefore
\mathcal{E}|_{\mathfrak{S}}=T\mathfrak{S}
.

On the other hand,
T_{1},\dots,T_{r},X_{1},\dots,X_{r+m}
are all sections of
\mathcal{E}
, thus by restricting to
\mathfrak{S}
they are all tangent vector fields on
\mathfrak{S}
. By uniqueness of
C^{1}
ODE flows we know both
e^{-F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,w)\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}e^{-u\cdot T}(u,v,w)
and
e^{-u\cdot X^{\prime}-v\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}(u,v,w)
are in
\mathfrak{S}
.

On the other hand by (
5.1.18
) we see that
e^{-F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,w)\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}e^{-u\cdot T}(u,v,w)
and
e^{-u\cdot X^{\prime}-v\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}(u,v,w)
lay in
\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{w}
. Thus to prove (
5.1.25
) it remains to show
\mathfrak{S}\cap(\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
is a singleton.

Suppose otherwise there are two distinct points
q_{1},q_{2}\in\mathfrak{S}\cap(\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
, by connectedness of
\mathfrak{S}
the distance
\operatorname{dist}_{\mathfrak{S}}(q_{1},q_{2})
is finite. Therefore the set
S:=\{q\in\mathfrak{S}:\operatorname{dist}(q,q_{1})^{2}+\operatorname{dist}(q,q_{2})^{2}<\operatorname{dist}(q_{1},q_{2})^{2}\}
is an open subset of
\mathfrak{S}
whose boundary contains
q_{1},q_{2}
. By mean value theorem, since
\dim S=r+m
, there is a point
q_{3}\in S
such that the displacement
q_{1}-q_{2}\in T_{q_{3}}S
. But
\mathfrak{S}
is spanned by
X_{1}|_{\mathfrak{S}},\dots,X_{r+m}|_{\mathfrak{S}}
whose
(u,v)
-coordinate components are all non-vanishing, contradiction.

Now
\mathfrak{S}\cap(\{(0,0)\}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
is singleton and we conclude that
e^{-F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,w)\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}e^{-u\cdot T}(u,v,w)=e^{-u\cdot X^{\prime}-v\cdot X^{\prime\prime}}(u,v,w)
, which gives (
5.1.25
). By composition we get
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, finishing the proof of
(6’​’​’)
and hence the whole proof.
∎

Remark 5.1.9
.

It would be natural to generalize the result to the multi-layers case, where we have a flag of involutive tangent subbundles
0\leq\mathcal{V}_{1}\leq\dots\leq\mathcal{V}_{k}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
. And the parameterization
\Phi(t^{(1)},\dots,t^{(k)},s)=e^{t^{(1)}T_{(1)}}\dots e^{t^{(k)}T_{(k)}}(\Gamma(s))
gives the parameterization with desired regularities, where
T_{(j)}
are collections of vector fields such that
T_{(1)},\dots,T_{(k)}
are linearly independent and
\mathcal{V}_{j}=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}(T_{(1)},\dots,T_{(j)})
for each
j
. We will not use this generalization in the paper.

5.1.5
A singular Frobenius theorem on log-Lipschitz vector fields: proof of Theorem
2

First we give an analog of Lemma
2.3.9
in the linear dependent setting.

Lemma 5.1.10
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
C^{1,1}
-manifold with a fixed point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
. Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
be log-Lipschitz (real) vector fields on
\mathfrak{M}
. Let
r
be the rank of
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(p),\dots,X_{m}(p))\in T_{p}\mathfrak{M}
. Then there is a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
, a coordinate system
(x,y)=(x^{1},\dots,x^{r},y^{1},\dots,y^{n-r}):U\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-m}
, and vector fields
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
such that

(i)

(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
and
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})
are
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
linear combinations each other. In particular for every
q\in U
,
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(q),\dots,X_{m}(q))=\operatorname{Span}(Y_{1}(q),\dots,Y_{m}(q))
.

(ii)

Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
have the form

Y_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}+\sum_{l=1}^{n-r}b_{j}^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{l}},\hskip 10.00002ptY_{k}=\sum_{l=1}^{n-r}b_{k}^{l}\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{l}},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq j\leq r<k\leq m,

(5.1.26)

where
b_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
satisfy
b_{j}^{k}(0)=0
for all
1\leq j\leq m
,
1\leq k\leq n-r
.

Proof.

The argument is almost the same to the proof of Lemma
2.3.9
. We choose a coordinate system
(x,y)
near
p
such that
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(p),\dots,X_{m}(p))=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}|_{p},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{r}}|_{p})
. By reordering the index if necessary we can assume that
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are linearly independent at
p
. By continuity
X_{1},\dots,X_{r}
are linearly independent in a neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
of
p
. By shrinking
U
if necessary we can assume that
U
is contained in the domain of
(x,y)
.

Write
X^{\prime}=[X_{1},\dots,X_{r}]^{\top}
and
X^{\prime\prime}=[X_{r+1},\dots,X_{m}]^{\top}
as two row collections of vector fields. By assumption of
(x,y)
we can write
X^{\prime}=A_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+A_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
X^{\prime\prime}=A_{3}\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+A_{4}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
where
A_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r\times r})
is invertible at every point in the domain,
A_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r\times(n-r)})
,
A_{3}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{(m-r)\times r})
and
A_{4}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{(m-r)\times(n-r)})
satisfy
A_{2}(p)=0
and
A_{4}(p)=0
.

By cofactor representation of inverse matrices or Lemma
2.1.28
, we see that
A_{1}^{-1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r\times r})
. We define
Y^{\prime}=[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{r}]^{\top}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}=[Y_{r+1},\dots,Y_{m}]^{\top}
as

Y^{\prime}:=A_{1}^{-1}X^{\prime},\hskip 10.00002ptY^{\prime\prime}:=X^{\prime\prime}-A_{3}Y^{\prime}=X^{\prime\prime}-A_{3}A_{1}^{-1}X^{\prime}.

Clearly
X^{\prime\prime}=A_{1}Y^{\prime}
and
X^{\prime\prime}=Y^{\prime\prime}+A_{3}Y^{\prime}
, we see that
(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})
and
(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})
are
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}
linear combinations each other.

By construction we have
Y^{\prime}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}+B_{1}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
and
Y^{\prime\prime}=B_{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial y}
where
B_{1}:=A_{1}^{-1}A_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r\times(n-r)})
and
B_{2}:=A_{4}-A_{3}A_{1}^{-1}A_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{(m-r)\times(n-r)})
. Since
A_{2}(p)=0
and
A_{4}(p)=0
, we get
B_{1}(p)=0
and
B_{2}(p)=0
as well, finishing the proof.
∎

We can now start the proof by using new generators
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
from Lemma
5.1.10
.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

By Remark
3.1.2
and by passing to a local coordinate chart of the manifold
\mathfrak{M}
, we can assume that
\mathfrak{M}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}
is an open subset with the base point
p=0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

By Lemma
5.1.10
we can find a coordinate system
(x,y):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{n-r}
of
p
and log-Lipschitz vector fields
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
on
U
which are
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
invertible linearly combinations of
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
and have the form (
5.1.26
).

We can write
Y_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}f_{j}^{k}X_{k}
and
X_{j}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}g_{j}^{k}Y_{k}
for
1\leq j\leq m
where
f_{j}^{k},g_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}(U)
. Thus by (
1.1.2
), for
1\leq j,k\leq m
,

\displaystyle[Y_{j},Y_{k}]=

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m}[f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}X_{j^{\prime}},f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}Y_{k^{\prime}}]=\sum_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m}f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}(X_{j^{\prime}}f_{k}^{k^{\prime}})X_{k^{\prime}}-f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}(X_{k^{\prime}}f_{j}^{l})X_{j^{\prime}}+f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}[X_{j^{\prime}},Y_{k^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime},l=1}^{m}\bigg(f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}(X_{j^{\prime}}f_{k}^{k^{\prime}})g_{k^{\prime}}^{l}-f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}(X_{k^{\prime}}f_{j}^{l})g_{j^{\prime}}^{l}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m}f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}c_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}^{l^{\prime}}g_{l^{\prime}}^{l}\bigg)Y_{l}=:\sum_{l=1}\tilde{c}_{jk}^{l}Y_{l}.

Here
\tilde{c}_{jk}^{l}:=\sum_{j^{\prime},k^{\prime}=1}^{m}\big(f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}(X_{j^{\prime}}f_{k}^{k^{\prime}})g_{k^{\prime}}^{l}-f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}(X_{k^{\prime}}f_{j}^{l})g_{j^{\prime}}^{l}+\sum_{l^{\prime}=1}^{m}f_{j}^{j^{\prime}}f_{k}^{k^{\prime}}c_{j^{\prime}k^{\prime}}^{l^{\prime}}g_{l^{\prime}}^{l}\big)
for
1\leq j,k,l\leq m
. By Lemma
2.1.22

(ii)
we see that
\tilde{c}_{jk}^{l}\in\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}-1}(U)
.

On the other hand by direct computation (cf. (
2.3.5
)) we see that
[Y_{j},Y_{k}]
lays in the span of
\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{n-r}}
, thus
\tilde{c}_{jk}^{l}=0
for all
1\leq l\leq r
.

By (
5.1.26
) we see that
Y_{1}(0),\dots,Y_{r}(0)
are linearly independent and
Y_{r+1}(0)=\dots=Y_{m}(0)=0
. Therefore
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m}
satisfy the assumptions of Proposition
2.3.27
, and by (
2.3.22
) we see that the map
\Phi(t):=e^{t^{1}Y_{1}}\dots e^{t^{r}Y_{r}}(p)
is
C^{1}
near
t=0
and satisfies
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}=Y_{j}\circ\Phi(t)
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

Since
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{r}
are linearly independent, we know
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(t),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(t)
are linearly independent for every
t
in the domain. Therefore by shrinking the domain if necessary,
\Phi
gives a
C^{1}
-parameterization of a
r
-dimensional submanifold
\mathfrak{S}
, such that
\mathfrak{S}\ni p
and

\textstyle T\mathfrak{S}=\operatorname{Span}(\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\Phi_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}})=\operatorname{Span}(Y_{1}|_{\mathfrak{S}},\dots,Y_{r}|_{\mathfrak{S}}).

On the other hand,
Y_{j}(\Phi(0))=Y_{j}(0)=0
for all
r+1\leq j\leq m
, so by Proposition
2.3.27

(i)
we see that
Y_{j}(\Phi(t))\equiv 0
for all
r+1\leq j\leq m
as well. We conclude that
T\mathfrak{S}=\operatorname{Span}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{m})|_{\mathfrak{S}}=\operatorname{Span}(X_{1},\dots,X_{m})|_{\mathfrak{S}}
is the desired submanifold.

Now for every
q\in B_{X}(p,\infty)
, let
r^{\prime}=r^{\prime}(q)
be the rank of
\operatorname{Span}(X_{1}(q),\dots,X_{m}(q))
. Repeating the argument on the point
q
, we can locally find a
r^{\prime}
-dimensional
C^{1}
submanifold containing
q
, whose tangent bundle is spanned by the restrictions of
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
. Now the set
B_{X}(p,\infty)
is path connected, so
q\mapsto r^{\prime}(q)
must be a constant function. Therefore
r^{\prime}(q)\equiv r
and we conclude that
B_{X}(p,\infty)
is a
r
-dimensional immersed submanifold.
∎

5.2
Hölder Estimates for Nirenberg’s Complex Frobenius Theorem

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a (smooth) manifold. We recall the following terminologies:

We say a
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
complex tangent subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is
involutive
, if for every
\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
-vector fields
X,Y
that are sections of
\mathcal{S}
, the Lie bracket
[X,Y]
is a (distributional) section of
\mathcal{S}
. (See Definition
0
.)

•

An
elliptic structure
on
\mathfrak{M}
is an involutive complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}=\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

A
complex structure
3
3

3

In this paper we use “complex structure” to refer the almost complex structure satisfying involutivity condition. See also
[
BCH08
, Section I.8]
.
on
\mathfrak{M}
is an involutive complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}=\mathcal{S}\oplus\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

•

An
essentially real structure
is a complex subbundle
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathcal{S}=\bar{\mathcal{S}}
.

Here
\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\{(p,u-iv):p\in\mathfrak{M},\ u,v\in T_{p}\mathfrak{M},\ u+iv\in\mathcal{S}_{p}\}
is the complex conjugate subbundle of
\mathcal{S}
.

The real involutive subbundles are one-one correspondent to essentially real structures in the way that, given an involutive
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathfrak{M}
,
\mathcal{S}:=\mathcal{V}\otimes\mathbb{C}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is essentially real, and converse given an essential real
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
,
\mathcal{V}:=\mathcal{S}\cap T\mathfrak{M}
is an involutive real subbundle.

5.2.1
History remarks and ideas of the proof

The complex Frobenius theorem was first introduced by Louis Nirenberg. It is a generalization of the real Frobenius theorem and the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem.

Proposition 5.2.1

(L. Nirenberg
[
Nir57
]
)
.

Let
r,m,N
be nonnegative integers satisfying
r+2m\leq N
. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be an
N
-dimensional smooth manifold and let
\mathcal{S}
be a smooth complex Frobenius structure on
\mathfrak{M}
, such that

\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r
and
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
.
Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a smooth coordinate system
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}_{s}
near
p
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
is spanned by the smooth vector fields
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

There are many previous discussions of complex Frobenius theorem in the nonsmooth setting.
Nijenhuis & Woolf
[
NW63
]
studied Newlander-Nirenberg theorem with nonsmooth parameters, which is a special case of nonsmooth complex Frobenius theorem. In their assumptions, all structures are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
.

Later Hill & Taylor
[
HT07
]
studied nonsmooth complex Frobenius structures. They assumed the complex Frobenius structure to be at least
C^{1,1}
in order to achieve the condition called “Hypothesis V” in their paper.

Although they did not say explicitly, a corollary they proved is the following:

Proposition 5.2.2

(
[
HT07
]
)
.

Let
\gamma>\frac{1}{2}
,
\beta>0
and let
\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N}
be two manifolds. Let
\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})\times\mathfrak{N}
be a complex subbundle that admits generators with coefficients in
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N})
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{\mathfrak{M}\times\{s\}}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
is a complex structure for all
s\in\mathfrak{N}
. Then locally there is a map
F:U\times V\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\times\mathfrak{N}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}
, such that
F(\cdot,s)
is a
C^{1}
coordinate chart and
F(\cdot,s)^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F(\cdot,s)^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{\mathfrak{M}\times\{s\}}
. Moreover
F
has regularity
4
4

4

For the mixed regularity notation
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, see Definition
2.2.1
.

F\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,V;\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

Remark 5.2.3
.

If we are begin with a such
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, our regularity result in Theorem
4.1
(where we consider
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}
) is stronger than Proposition
5.2.2
. Indeed, by interpolation the generators of
\mathcal{S}
belong to
\mathscr{C}^{\theta\alpha}\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha}(\mathfrak{M},\mathfrak{N};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for every
0<\theta<1
. By Proposition
5.2.2
for every
\theta\in(0,1)
such that
\theta\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
, we can find a map
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\theta\alpha+1}\mathscr{C}^{(1-\theta)\alpha}
representing
\mathcal{S}
. For overall regularity of
F
, by taking
\theta
such that
\theta\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\min(\theta\alpha+1,(1-\theta)\alpha)
maximize:

•

When
\alpha>2
, by Theorem
4.1
we can find a
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, while for Proposition
5.2.2
, by taking
\theta=\frac{\alpha-1}{2\alpha}
,
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha+1}{2}}
is the best possible.

•

When
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq 2
and
\alpha\neq 1
, by Theorem
4.1
we can find a
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}
, while for Proposition
5.2.2
, for every
0<\varepsilon<1-\frac{1}{2\alpha}
we can find a
F\in\mathscr{C}^{(1-\varepsilon)\alpha-\frac{1}{2}}
by taking
\theta=\frac{1}{2\alpha}+\varepsilon
.

Notice that for both cases Theorem
4.1
gives a better estimate.

Recently, Gong
[
Gon20
]
proved a better estimate for complex Frobenius structure. He showed that for a non-integer
\alpha>1
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
, locally one can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
-coordinate chart
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{N-r-2m}
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
span
\mathcal{S}
. Though he did not have the sharp result, the statement
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is endpoint sharp (up to a loss of
\varepsilon>0
derivatives), see Remark
4.1.3
.
The proof technique in his paper is quite different from ours. He used homotopy formulae with Nash-Moser iterations.

Our result is stronger because we have not only the optimal regularity of the coordinate chart that
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, but also the optimal regularity for other ingredients like the components of the coordinates and the coordinate vector fields. See Section
5.3.0
.

As the origin of the complex Frobenius theorem, the sharp estimate to Newlander-Nirenberg is due to Malgrange
[
Mal69
]
. He proved that for
\alpha>1
one can always obtain a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
local complex coordinate system for a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
integrable almost complex structure.
[
HT03
]
showed that his argument also works for
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq 1
. We remark that we cannot replace
(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha},\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1})
by
(C^{k},C^{k+1})
, see Theorem
7
and Section
5.3.2
.

Later Street used Malgrange’s method and obtained a sharp estimate for elliptic structure.

Proposition 5.2.4

(
[
Str20b
]
, Sharp estimate for elliptic structures)
.

Let
\alpha>1
, let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold, and let
r,m
be integers satisfying
\dim\mathfrak{M}=r+2m
. Endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
with standard coordinate system
(t,z)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m})
.

Let
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-elliptic structure of rank
r+m
. Then for any
p\in\mathfrak{M}
there exists a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate system
F:U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}
near
p
, such that
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

Note that in Proposition
5.2.4
we have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
because
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
. However in a more general setting (Theorem
3
), due to the influence of the parameters, we only have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
. Both results for
F
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are sharp.

To prove Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
, we separate the discussion from the case
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma>\alpha
. Notice that both
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
are an essentially real structure.

For the case
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, we only foliate
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. By applying the real Frobenius theorem, we get a foliation of
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifolds, such that by restricted to each leaf
\mathcal{S}
becomes an elliptic structure. The theorems are then reduced to the estimate of a family of elliptic structures, which is done in Theorem
4.1
. Theorem
4.1
implies Proposition
5.2.4
which can be viewed as the “parameter free” case. See also Remarks
4.1.1
,
4.1.2
and
4.1.3
.

The idea is based on Malgrange’s factorization method for the sharp Newlander-Nirenberg theorem, whose sketch can be found in, for example
[
BCH08
, Page 47]
and
[
HT03
, Section 3]
. Malgrange’s idea is to find an intermediate coordinate change
H
such that the generators for
H_{*}\mathcal{S}
satisfy an elliptic PDE system. Using the PDE we can show that
H_{*}\mathcal{S}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-family of real-analytic elliptic structures. Finally we construct another coordinate change
G
using holomorphic Frobenius theorem, now with parameter, so that
G_{*}H_{*}\mathcal{S}
is the span of
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
. Thus
F=G\circ H
is the desired coordinate chart. Also see Section
4.1.1
for a detailed sketch to the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

Note that in Theorem
4.1
we work on the elliptic structures rather than the complex structures. In this way we do not have to pick generators for
\mathcal{S}
that satisfy the “Hypothesis V” in
[
HT07
]
.

For the case
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
where
\gamma\geq\alpha+1
, we follow Nirenberg’s original proof by applying real Frobenius theorem twice on the essentially real structures
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
, see
[
BCH08
, the proof of Theorem I.12.1]
. This ends up with a family of complex structures. We complete the proof by applying Theorem
4.1
with the special case
r=0
.

To get the optimal Hölder-Zygmund regularity during this reduction, we combine the two real Frobenius theorems into one step, which is done in Theorem
5.3
.

When
\alpha>1
, unlike the sharp Newlander-Nirenberg theorem or Street’s result where
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields, in our result we show that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is optimal.

One reason for the regularity loss comes from solving elliptic pdes. Say we have a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle for some
\alpha>0
. In the first step of Malgrange’s proof of the sharp Newlander-Nirenberg theorem we need to solve an elliptic pde system for
f=f(x)
that has the following form:

\Delta f=\phi(f,\nabla f,\nabla^{2}f,a,\nabla a).

(5.2.1)

Here
a=a(x)\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
is the vector-valued coefficient that comes from the subbundle, and
\phi
is a rational function which is defined and small when
f
and
a
are both small.

Since Laplace’s equation gains two derivatives in Hölder-Zygmund spaces, we can find a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
solving (
5.2.1
). This is why we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate system for a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-integrable almost complex structure. In particular the coordinate vector fields are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
because
\nabla f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

When we do estimate on the complex Frobenius structure, we need to solve a pde system that has similar expression to (
5.2.1
) but with a parameter: we need
f=f(x,s)
for the system
\Delta_{x}f=\phi(f,\nabla_{x}f,a,\nabla_{x}a)
with
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coefficient
a=a(x,s)
. See (
4.1.6
) for the actual formula.

We need to introduce some suitable function spaces that capture the property that the pde system “gains 2 derivatives on
x
-variable”. Note that by comparing to
a(x,s)
, the function
\phi(f,\nabla_{x}f,a,\nabla_{x}a)
lose one derivative in
x
, and by taking inverse Laplacian,
f
should gain 1 derivative in
x
. Thus we can ask what is the good function space
\mathscr{X}=\mathscr{X}_{x,s}
to work on, such that if
a\in\mathscr{X}_{x,s}
then
\nabla_{x}f\in\mathscr{X}_{x,s}
.

Unfortunately the space
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
does not satisfy our need. When
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
we may not find a solution
f
