such that
\nabla f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
. This is roughly because the
x
-variable Riesz transform (say
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
s\in V\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{m}
)

R_{j}f(x,s):=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{n+1}{2})}{\pi^{\frac{n+1}{2}}}\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}\backslash B^{n}(0,\varepsilon)}\frac{y_{j}-x_{j}}{|y-x|^{n+1}}f(y,s)dy,\hskip 10.00002ptj=1,\dots,n,

does not map
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{n}\times V)
into itself. That is why
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
is the best one we can get.

To see that why
R_{j}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}\not\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
holds, we consider the bi-parameter decomposition
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\cap L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}
(see Lemma
2.2.15
): we have
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}}\approx\sup_{s}\|f(\cdot,s)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}}+\sup_{x}\|f(x,\cdot)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}}
. We see that
R_{j}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}_{s}
is still bounded because Riesz transforms are bounded in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-spaces. However
R_{j}:L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}\not\to L^{\infty}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}
is not bounded because Riesz transforms are unbounded on
L^{\infty}
-spaces.

In application we use the Hölder-Zygmund space
\mathscr{X}_{x,s}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}
, thus
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{x}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x,s}
and
\nabla f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}_{x,s}
, both of which are optimal.

To construct the example of complex Frobenius structure such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, we follow a similar but more sophisticated construction to
[
Yao21
]
. See Propositions
5.3.11
and
5.3.16
in Section
5.3.3
.

When
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
, in Theorem
4.1
we are only able to get
F\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}
instead of
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. Because in solving the existence of (
5.2.1
), the image of the product map
(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{x}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{0}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s})\times(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{x}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s})
is not contained in
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{x}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{s}
: there is an extra gain in
\mathscr{C}^{0}_{x}\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}_{s}
, which contributes to the additional regularity loss in parameters. We do not know whether the
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}
-smoothness is sharp or not.

5.2.2
The general versions of Theorems
3
,
4
,
5
, and their proofs

Now we give more general statements for Theorems
3
,
4
and
5
with the estimate of mixed regularity for the parameterizations. In the proof we combine the cases
\alpha<1
and
\alpha>1
together, but separate the discussion between
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\geq\alpha+1
.

Recall Definitions
2.2.12
,
2.2.18
and
2.3.2
for the
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-spaces, Definition
2.3.5
for a local basis, and (
1.2.2
) for the column and matrix convention of Jacobian matrices. Recall in Definition
2.1.23
we use order
1-<\mathrm{LogL}<1<\mathrm{Lip}<1+\varepsilon
for generalized indices, and we do not include
\mathrm{logl}
in the index set
\mathbb{R}_{\mathbb{E}}
.

We endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinate system
(t,z,s)=(t^{1},\dots,t^{r},z^{1},\dots,z^{m},s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
.

When
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is as rough as
\mathcal{S}
, we have the following:

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
\kappa\in(2,\infty]
,
\alpha\in(\frac{1}{2},\kappa-1]
and
\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{logl},\mathrm{Lip}\}\cup(1,\kappa-1]
satisfy
\alpha\leq\beta
if
\beta\neq\mathrm{logl}
(see Definition
2.1.23
) and
\alpha\leq 1
if
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
. Let
r,m,q
be nonnegative integers. Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
(r+2m+q)
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifold.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure over
\mathfrak{M}
that has complex rank
r+m
, such that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-subbundle and has complex rank
r+2m
.
Then for any
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
and
0<\varepsilon<2\alpha-1

(
\varepsilon
is only required for the case
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
)
there is an open neighborhood
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
of
(0,0,0)
and a topological parameterization
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
, such that by denoting
U:=\Phi(\Omega)
and
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:U\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
:

(1)

\Phi(0)=p_{0}
and
F(p_{0})=(0,0,0)
.

(2)

\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
are all continuous maps for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
. In other words
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}
are all defined continuous vector fields on
U
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(3)

For any
\omega\in\Omega
, the complex subspace
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi(\omega)}
is spanned by
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}}(\omega),\cdots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}(\omega),\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}}(\omega),\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}}(\omega)\in\mathbb{C}T_{\Phi(u)}\mathfrak{M}
. In other words
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
.

In particular,

(4)

(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U}
has a local basis
(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}})
.

(5)

(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U}
has a local basis
(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}})
.

Moreover for the regularity estimates, when
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
(in particular
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq 1
):

(6)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq k\leq m
.

(7)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(8)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}
vector fields on
U
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
(in particular
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq 1
), or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:

(6’)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq k\leq m
.

(7’)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(8’)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}
vector fields on
U
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha<1
:

(6”)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,2\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,2\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,2\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(7”)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(8”)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1}
vector fields on
U
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

When
\beta>1
and
1<\alpha\leq\beta

(
\alpha
is a real number)
:

(6’​’​’)

\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
,
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

(7’​’​’)

F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m})
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

(8’​’​’)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
vector fields on
U
for
1\leq j\leq r
and
1\leq k\leq m
.

As a corollary, when
\alpha>1
,

(9)

\Phi
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
regular parameterization, and
F
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
coordinate chart.

(10)

\mathcal{S}^{\bot}|_{U}
has a local basis
(F^{*}d\bar{z}^{1},\dots,F^{*}d\bar{z}^{m},F^{*}ds^{1},\dots,F^{*}ds^{q})
.

When
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is at least one order more regular than
\mathcal{S}
, we have the following:

Theorem 5.5
.

Let
\alpha,\beta,\kappa
,
r,m,q\geq 0
,
\mathfrak{M}
and
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
be the same as in Theorem
5.4
.

Suppose in addition to the assumptions of Theorem
5.4
, there is a
\gamma\in(1,\kappa-1]
such that
\gamma\geq\alpha
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
-subbundle.

Then for any
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
and
0<\varepsilon<2\alpha-1

(
\varepsilon
is only required for the case
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
)
there are an open neighborhood
\Omega=\Omega^{\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
of
(0,0,0)
, and a map
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
, such that
\Phi
satisfies all the consequences in Theorem
5.4
except
(6)
,
(6’)
,
(6”)
,
(6’​’​’)
. And we have the following:

(11)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
, or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}_{t,z,s,\mathrm{loc}}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}_{t,z,s,\mathrm{loc}}
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha\neq 1
:
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}_{t,z,s,\mathrm{loc}}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{r}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}_{t,z,s,\mathrm{loc}}
.

(12)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha,(2\alpha-1-\varepsilon)\cdot\min(\gamma-1,1)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
:
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha,(2\alpha-1)\cdot\min(\gamma-1,1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
:
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{1}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{m}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha,(\gamma-1)\circ\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega^{\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime},\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M})
.

(13)

For
F=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
, the vector fields
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}}
defined on
U=\Phi(\Omega)
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
.

Remark 5.2.5
.

For
(\gamma-1)\circ\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)
in
(12)
we recall the composition notation in Definition
2.1.29
and Remark
5.1.7
.

Thus
(11)
is stronger than the estimates of
\Phi
and
\nabla_{t}\Phi
in
(6)
,
(6’)
,
(6”)
and
(6’​’​’)
, and
(13)
is stronger than the estimates of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
in
(8)
,
(8’)
,
(8”)
and
(8’​’​’)
.

When
\gamma>2
or
(\beta,\gamma)\in\{(\mathrm{LogL},2),(\mathrm{logl},2)\}
, the results in
(12)
are stronger than the estimates of
\nabla_{z}\Phi
in
(6)
,
(6’)
,
(6”)
and
(6’​’​’)
. But when
\frac{3}{2}<\gamma\leq 2
and
(\beta,\gamma)\notin\{(\mathrm{LogL},2),(\mathrm{logl},2)\}
, in particular
\frac{1}{2}<\alpha\leq\gamma-1(\leq 1)
, the
s
-variable regularity in
(12)
is weaker than those of
\nabla_{z}\Phi
in
(6)
,
(6’)
and
(6”)
.

Some of the results are directly implied by others, we mark them as below.

Remark 5.2.6
.

(i)

The case
\alpha=\infty
(which implies
\beta=\gamma=\kappa=\infty
) is the classical result
[
Nir57
]
(see Proposition
5.2.1
).

(ii)

(8’​’​’)
is implied by
(6’​’​’)
and
(7’​’​’)
.

We have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\circ F
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}=\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}}\circ F
for
1\leq j\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq m
.
(6’​’​’)
shows that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}},\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
and
(7’​’​’)
shows that
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\kappa,\alpha,\beta)}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. Taking compositions we get
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t^{j}}\circ F,\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial z^{k}}\circ F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
. ∎

(iv)

(10)
,
(4)
and
(5)
are all implied by
(3)
.

For the complex conjugate of
(3)
,
\bar{\mathcal{S}}|_{U}
has local basis
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}
. Taking intersection we get that
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
. Taking union we get that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}|_{U}
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}
.

When
\alpha>1
, since
(F^{*}dt,F^{*}dz,F^{*}d\bar{z},F^{*}ds)
is the dual basis of
(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s})
, and by
(3)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
span
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
. Taking the dual bundle, we get that
F^{*}d\bar{z},F^{*}ds
span
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}|_{U}
. ∎

The proofs of Theorems
5.4
and
5.5
are combinations of the real Frobenius theorem, the Theorems
5.3
or
5.1
, and the estimate of the parameterized elliptic structures, the Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
5.4
.

By Remark
5.2.6

(i)
only the case
\alpha<\infty
needs a proof.

Throughout the proof we choose
\varepsilon>0
such that
0<\varepsilon<\alpha-\frac{1}{2}
.

By Lemma
2.3.14
since
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, we know
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
as well. This is useful when
\alpha=1
and
\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{logl}\}
.

We fix the point
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
.
First we apply real Frobenius theorem, the Theorem
5.1
to
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
. We endow
\mathbb{R}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinate system
(\nu,\lambda)=(\nu^{1},\dots,\nu^{r+2m},\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{q})
.

Since
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is at least log-Lipschitz and has rank
r+2m
, by Theorem
5.1
, we can find a neighborhood
\Omega_{1}=\Omega^{\prime}_{1}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r+2m}_{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{\lambda}
of
(0,0)
and a topological parameterization
\Phi_{1}:\Omega_{1}\to\mathfrak{M}
, which does not depend on
\beta
, such that by taking
U_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\Omega_{1})
and
F_{1}=(F_{1}^{\prime},F_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}):U_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{r+2m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
F_{1}:=\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
, we have

(S1.1)

\Phi_{1}(0,0)=p_{0}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\nu^{r+2m}}:\Omega\to T\mathfrak{M}
are continuous.

(S1.2)

(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U_{1}}
has a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
local basis
(F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{r+2m}})
.

(S1.3)

When
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, automatically
F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{r+2m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U_{1};T\mathfrak{M}|_{U_{1}})
.

(S1.4)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
: for every
0<\varepsilon<1
there is a neighborhood
\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega_{1}^{\prime}
of
0
such that

\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{2-,1-\varepsilon/2}_{\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\nabla_{\nu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-,1-\varepsilon/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
.

When
\alpha=1
: automatically
\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{2,1-\varepsilon/2}_{\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\nabla_{\nu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{1,1-\varepsilon/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
: automatically
\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{2-,1-}_{\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\nabla_{\nu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1};T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
: automatically

\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+1,\beta}_{\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\nabla_{\nu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1};T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
.

(S1.5)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
: for
U_{1,\varepsilon}:=\Phi(\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}^{\prime}\times\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime})
,
F_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1};\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
and
F_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1,\varepsilon};\mathbb{R}^{q})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
: automatically

and

.

When

: automatically

and

.

Now
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}=(F_{1})_{*}\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\Omega_{1}
. By
(S1.2)
,
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}\leq\Phi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{r+2m}})|_{\Omega_{1}}=(\mathbb{C}T\Omega_{1}^{\prime})\times\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

By Lemma
2.3.14
since
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, we know
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
as well.
Thus by

and
(S1.4)
,

(S1.3’)

.

(S1.4’)

\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{\nu,\mathrm{loc}}L^{\infty}_{\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathfrak{M})
and
\nabla_{\nu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\nu,\mathrm{loc}}L^{\infty}_{\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega_{1}^{\prime},\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime};T\mathfrak{M}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{r+2m})
.

Applying Lemma
2.3.17
on

:

(S1.6)

When

:
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}\leq(\mathbb{C}T\Omega^{\prime}_{1})\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}

4.1
applies to

Clearly

Proof of Theorem
5.5
.

We use a construction that is slightly different from the proof of Theorem
5.4
. Instead of applying Theorem
5.1
, we use Theorem
5.3
in the first step.

Still we assume
0<\varepsilon<\alpha-\frac{1}{2}
in the case
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
. Also by Lemma
2.3.14
(since
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
) we know
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
as well, which is useful when
\alpha=1
and
\beta\in\{\mathrm{LogL},\mathrm{logl}\}
.

We endow
\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{2m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinate system
(\mu,\nu,\lambda)=(\mu^{1},\dots,\mu^{r},\nu^{1},\dots,\nu^{2m},\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{q})
.

Since
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
has rank
r+2m
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1+}
has rank
r
, by Theorem
5.3
we can find a neighborhood
\Omega_{1}=\Omega^{\prime}_{1}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime}_{1}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{R}^{2m}_{\nu}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{\lambda}
of
(0,0,0)
and a topological parameterization
\Phi_{1}:\Omega_{1}\to\mathfrak{M}
, which does not depend on
\beta
and
\gamma
, such that by taking
U_{1}:=\Phi_{1}(\Omega_{1})
and
F_{1}=(F_{1}^{\prime},F_{1}^{\prime\prime},F_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}):U_{1}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{R}^{2m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
as
F_{1}:=\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
, we have

(S2.1)

\Phi_{1}(0,0,0)=p_{0}
and
\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\mu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\mu^{r}},\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\nu^{2m}}:\Omega_{1}\to T\mathfrak{M}
are continuous.

(S2.2)

(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U_{1}}=\operatorname{Span}(F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{r}})
, and
(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U_{1}}=\operatorname{Span}(F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{r}},F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}})
.

(S2.3)

When

and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, automatically
F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
and

on

for

and

.

(S2.4)

When

:
\forall\varepsilon>0

\exists
a neighborhood

of
0
such that on

When

,

and

on

.

When

: automatically we have

,
\nabla_{\mu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\min(\gamma,2-),1-}_{\mu,\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}
,

and

on
\Omega_{1}
.

When

When

Here

holds because

and

(S2.4’)

When

\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
:
\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,\beta}_{\mu,\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}
and
\nabla_{\mu}\Phi_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,\beta}_{\mu,\nu,\lambda,\mathrm{loc}}
on
\Omega_{1}
.

By
(S2.2)
,
\mathcal{S}
is contained in the span of
F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{r}},F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
. By
(S2.3)
and Lemma
2.3.17
,
(S1.6)
still holds.

By the standard linear algebra argument we can find a linear complex coordinate system
w=(w^{1},\dots,w^{m})
on
\mathbb{R}^{2m}_{\nu}
such that

\textstyle(\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S})_{(0,0)}=\operatorname{Span}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{1}}|_{(0,0)},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{r}}|_{(0,0)},\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{1}}|_{(0,0)},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{m}}|_{(0,0)}\big)\leq\mathbb{C}T_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}).

In particular
(\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S})_{(0,0)}\oplus\operatorname{Span}\big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}^{1}}|_{(0,0)},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}^{m}}|_{(0,0)})=\mathbb{C}T_{(0,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\mu}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w})
. Here we have identified
\mathbb{R}^{2m}_{\nu}
with
\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}
.

Applying Lemma
2.3.9
(by taking
\mathfrak{M}=\Omega_{1}^{\prime}\times\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathfrak{N}=\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
in the lemma), we can find a neighborhood
W=W^{\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\Omega_{1}^{\prime}\times\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}
of
(0,0,0)\in\Omega
(
W\subset\Omega_{1,\varepsilon}
if
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
) such that
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}
has the following local basis on
W
,

X=\begin{pmatrix}X^{\prime}\\
X^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I&&A^{\prime}\\
&I&A^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\mu}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}.

where by
(S1.6)
,
A^{\prime}
and
A^{\prime\prime}
are matrix functions defined in
W
such that:

\displaystyle\text{When }\beta=\mathrm{LogL}:

\displaystyle A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})}(W^{\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{r\times m}),

\displaystyle A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})}(W^{\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}).

(5.2.2)

\displaystyle\text{When }\beta=\mathrm{logl}:

\displaystyle A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-}(W^{\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{r\times m}),

\displaystyle A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-}(W^{\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime},W^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}).

\displaystyle\text{When }\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}:

\displaystyle A^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(W;\mathbb{C}^{r\times m}),

\displaystyle A^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(W;\mathbb{C}^{m\times m}).

Since
\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{r}}
are sections of
\Phi_{1}^{*}(\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}})\subset\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}
, by Lemma
2.3.9

(ii)
we have
A^{\prime}=0
, i.e.
X_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}
for
1\leq j\leq r
.

By Lemma
2.3.9

(iii)
, since
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}
is involutive, we see that
[X_{j},X_{k}]=0
for
1\leq j\leq r<k\leq r+m
. So
\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}A^{\prime\prime}(\mu,w,s)=0
for
1\leq j\leq r
in the domain, which means
A^{\prime\prime}(\mu,w,s)=A^{\prime\prime}(w,s)
.

Define a complex tangent subbundle
\mathcal{T}\leq(\mathbb{C}TW^{\prime\prime})\times W^{\prime\prime\prime}
as
\mathcal{T}:=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial w}+A^{\prime\prime}(w,s)\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}})
. We see that
\mathcal{T}
is an involutive subbundle of rank
m
,

\mathcal{T}\cap\bar{\mathcal{T}}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{T}+\bar{\mathcal{T}}=(\mathbb{C}TW^{\prime\prime})\times W^{\prime\prime\prime},\hskip 10.00002pt(\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S})|_{W}=(\mathbb{C}TW^{\prime})\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\mathcal{T}.

(5.2.3)

Moreover by (
5.2.2
), we have

(S2.6)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
\mathcal{T}\leq(\mathbb{C}TW^{\prime\prime})\times W^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha(1-\varepsilon/2)}
-subbundle.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
:
\mathcal{T}\leq(\mathbb{C}TW^{\prime\prime})\times W^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha-}
-subbundle.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
:
\mathcal{T}\leq(\mathbb{C}TW^{\prime\prime})\times W^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}
-subbundle, i.e.
\mathcal{T}\leq\mathbb{C}(TW^{\prime\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime\prime})
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-subbundle.

Endow
\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
with standard coordinate system
(z,s)=(z^{1},\dots,z^{m},s^{1},\dots,s^{q})
. By Theorem
4.1
(by taking
r=0
in this theorem), we can find a neighborhood
\Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
of
(0,0)
and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-parameterization
\Phi_{2}=(\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime},\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}):\Omega_{2}\to W^{\prime\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime\prime}
, such that by taking
U_{2}:=\Phi_{2}(\Omega_{2})
and
F_{2}=(F_{2}^{\prime\prime},F_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}):U_{2}\to\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
,
F_{2}:=\Phi_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
, we have

(S2.7)

\Phi_{2}(0,0,0)=(0,0)
and
\nabla_{z}\Phi_{2}
is continuous..

(S2.8)

\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}=\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(s):\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}\to W^{\prime\prime\prime}
is a smooth parameterization that does not depend on
z
.

(S2.9)

F_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}=F_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)\in C^{\infty}
does not depend on
(\mu,\nu)
.

(S2.10)

\mathcal{T}|_{U_{2}}
has a local basis
(F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}})
.

(S2.11)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
\Phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)}(\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};W^{\prime\prime})
and
\nabla_{z}\Phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)}(\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{2m\times 2m})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
, or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:
\Phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}(\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};W^{\prime\prime})
and
\nabla_{z}\Phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)-}_{z,s}
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha\neq 1
:
\Phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}(\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime},\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};W^{\prime\prime})
and
\nabla_{z}\Phi_{2}^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}_{z,s}
.

Moreover for any
U_{2}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq W^{\prime\prime}
and
U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq W^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that
U_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq U_{2}
, we have regularity estimates:

(S2.12)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
F_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)}(U_{2}^{\prime\prime},U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
, or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:
F_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}(U_{2}^{\prime\prime},U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{m})

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha\neq 1
:
F_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}(U_{2}^{\prime\prime},U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{m})
.

(S2.13)

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)}(U_{2}^{\prime\prime},U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}TU_{2}^{\prime\prime})
for
1\leq k\leq m
.

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
:
F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)-}(U_{2}^{\prime\prime},U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}TU_{2}^{\prime\prime})
for
1\leq k\leq m
.

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
:
F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}},F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}(U_{2}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}TU_{2}^{\prime\prime})
for
1\leq k\leq m
.

Now we take
\Omega^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, and
\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\Omega_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that
\Phi_{2}(\Omega^{\prime\prime}\times\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime})\subseteq U_{2}^{\prime\prime}\times U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq\Phi_{2}(\Omega_{2})
holds for some
U_{2}^{\prime}\times U_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\subseteq U_{2}\cap(W^{\prime\prime}\times W^{\prime\prime\prime})
.
We define
\Omega^{\prime}:=W^{\prime}
and
\Phi:\Omega\to\mathfrak{M}
as

\Phi(t,z,s):=\Phi_{1}(t,\Phi_{2}(z,s))=\Phi_{1}(t,\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{2}(z,s),\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(s)),\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\Omega^{\prime},z\in\Omega^{\prime\prime},s\in\Omega^{\prime\prime\prime}.

(5.2.4)

We define
U:=\Phi(\Omega)
and
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
. Immediately by
(S2.1)
and
(S2.7)
we have
(1)
.

Taking the chain rules on (
5.2.4
) we have

\nabla_{t}\Phi(t,z,s)=\nabla_{\mu}\Phi_{1}(t,\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,s),\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(s)),\hskip 10.00002pt\nabla_{z}\Phi(t,z,s)=(\nabla_{\nu}\Phi_{1})(t,\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,s),\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(s))\cdot\nabla_{z}\Phi_{2}(z,s).

(5.2.5)

Based on
(S2.4)
,
(S2.4’)
and
(S2.11)
, applying Lemma
2.2.38

(i)
to (
5.2.4
) and (
5.2.5
), we get

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,(\alpha+1)\circ(\alpha+1),\min((\alpha+1)\circ((2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)),1-\varepsilon/2)}_{t,z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{t,z,s}
,

\nabla_{t}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\alpha+1)\circ(\alpha+1),\min((\alpha+1)\circ((2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)),1-\varepsilon/2)}_{t,z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{t,z,s}
,

\nabla_{z}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\alpha+1)\circ\alpha,\min(\gamma-1,1-)\circ((2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2))}_{(t,z,s)}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon)}_{z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha,(2\alpha-1-\varepsilon)\cdot\min(\gamma-1,1)}_{t,z,s}
.

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
, or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,(\alpha+1)\circ(\alpha+1),\min((\alpha+1)\circ((2\alpha-1)-),1-)}_{t,z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}_{t,z,s}
,

\nabla_{t}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\alpha+1)\circ(\alpha+1),\min((\alpha+1)\circ((2\alpha-1)(1-)),1-)}_{t,z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,(2\alpha-1)-}_{t,z,s}
,

\nabla_{z}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\alpha+1)\circ\alpha,\min(\gamma-1,1-)\circ((2\alpha-1)(1-))}_{t,z,s}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,(2\alpha-1)-}_{z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha,(2\alpha-1)\cdot\min(\gamma-1,1)-}_{t,z,s}
;

•

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha\neq 1
:
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,(\alpha+1)\circ(\alpha+1),\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1,\beta)}_{t,z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+1,\alpha+1,\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}_{t,z,s}
,

\nabla_{t}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\alpha+1)\circ(\alpha+1),\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1,\beta)}_{t,z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha+1,\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}_{t,z,s}
,

\nabla_{z}\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,(\alpha+1)\circ\alpha,\min(\gamma-1,\mathrm{Lip})\circ\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1))}_{t,z,s}\cdot\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}_{z,s}\subseteq\mathscr{C}^{\gamma,\alpha,(\gamma-1)\circ\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}_{t,z,s}

Thus we get
(11)
,
(12)
and in particular
(2)
.

By (
5.2.4
), we see that
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):\to\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
satisfies

F^{\prime}=F_{1}^{\prime}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}\circ F_{1}^{\prime}\hskip 10.00002ptF^{\prime\prime}=F_{2}^{\prime\prime}\circ F_{1}^{\prime\prime}\hskip 10.00002ptF^{\prime\prime\prime}=F_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}\circ F_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}.

Therefore by
(S2.5)
,
(S2.9)
and
(S2.12)
,

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r})
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{((2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2))\circ\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
, or
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{((2\alpha-1)-)\circ\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

•

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
and
\alpha=1
:
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)\circ\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,2\alpha-1)}_{\mathrm{loc}}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

This gives
(7)
,
(7’)
,
(7”)
and
(7’​’​’)
.

By (
5.2.5
), we get
\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}\Phi(t,z,s)=\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial\mu^{j}}(t,\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime}(z,s),\Phi_{2}^{\prime\prime\prime}(s))=(\Phi_{1})_{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}|_{\Phi(t,z,s)}
for
1\leq j\leq r
. So
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{j}}=F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu^{j}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
for
1\leq j\leq r
, finishing the proof of
(13)
.

By (
5.2.3
) and
(S2.10)
we see that
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\mu},F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z})
, so by writing
\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}:\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\mu}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}
as the identity we have
\Phi_{1}^{*}\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z})
. Taking pushforward of
\Phi_{1}
we get
\mathcal{S}|_{U}=F_{1}^{*}\operatorname{Span}(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{r}}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z})|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z})
, finishing the proof of
(3)
.

Using (
5.2.2
) we have
F=\widehat{F}_{2}\circ F_{1}
where
\widehat{F}_{2}:=(\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{R}^{r}},F_{2})
. By
(S2.13)
and
(S2.3)
we see that:

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}},\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2})}
-linear combinations of
\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
.

While
F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon/2}
-vector fields.

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
:
\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}},\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}
-linear combinations of
\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
.

While
F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{1-}
-vector fields.

•

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
:
\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{1}},\dots,\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}},\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}
-linear combinations of
\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
.

While
F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{1}},\dots,F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{2m}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-vector fields.

Say
\widehat{F}_{2}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}=:\sum_{j=1}^{2m}g_{j}^{k}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{j}}
. Then
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}=\sum_{j=1}^{2m}(g_{j}^{k}\circ F_{1})\cdot(F_{1}^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\nu^{j}})
. By
(S2.5)
, we see that:

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{LogL}
:
g_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)(1-\varepsilon/2)\circ(1-\varepsilon/2)}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, thus
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha-1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

•

When
\beta=\mathrm{logl}
:
g_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{((2\alpha-1)-)\circ(1-)}_{\mathrm{loc}}=\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, thus
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{(2\alpha-1)-}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

•

When
\beta\geq\mathrm{Lip}
:
g_{j}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)\circ\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}=\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, thus
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{k}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha-,2\alpha-1)}_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

The argument for
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}^{k}}
are the same. Therefore we get
(8)
,
(8’)
,
(8”)
and
(8’​’​’)
.

The rest of the results follow from Remark
5.2.6
as well.

5.3
Examples for Some Sharpness

In this chapter we discuss certain sharpness of the coordinate charts and coordinate vector fields for the Frobenius-type structures.

5.3.0
An overview to estimates of complex Frobenius structures

We only consider the case
\alpha>1
here.

Fixed four numbers
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\kappa>1
such that
\alpha\leq\min(\beta,\gamma)
and
\kappa\geq\max(\beta,\gamma)+1
. Consider all possible complex Frobenius structures
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathfrak{M}
among all
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
-manifolds
\mathfrak{M}
such that
\mathcal{S}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
and
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
.

Fix a point
p\in\mathfrak{M}
, let
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathfrak{M}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{s}
be any desired
C^{1}
-coordinate chart near a fixed point
p
that represents
\mathcal{S}
, that is,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
span
\mathcal{S}
in
U
.

Recall the notation
\mathscr{C}^{\mu+}=\bigcup_{\nu>\mu}\mathscr{C}^{\nu}
for
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.

We list the best Hölder-Zygmund regularities the six objects:
F^{\prime}
,
F^{\prime\prime}
,
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
, as follows.

•

F^{\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\kappa+}
:
\mathfrak{M}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
, its coordinate functions are at most
\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
, so
F^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\kappa}
is optimal.

•

F^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
: This is due to the sharpness of real Frobenius theorem that
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
only guarantees
F^{\prime\prime\prime}
to be
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
. See Proposition
5.3.3
below.

•

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+}
:
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
spans
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
which is
\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
. If
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+}
then
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+}
as well.

•

F^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
: The argument is similar to the sharpness of real Frobenius theorem. See Proposition
5.3.4
below.

•

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
: This is the counter intuitive part in Theorem
3
. See Section
5.3.3
for the complete deduction and Proposition
5.3.11
, Theorem
5.3.18
for the proof. The idea comes from the proof of Theorem
7
.

When
\gamma\geq\alpha+1
(that
\mathcal{S}\cap\bar{\mathcal{S}}
is at least 1 order more regular than
\mathcal{S}
), by Theorem
5.4
, all six regularity estimates above are sharp.

When
\alpha\leq\gamma<\alpha+1
, from Theorem
5.4
we only have
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
, the rest of five estimates are still sharp.

When
\alpha\leq\gamma<\alpha+1
and
\gamma>2
, using Theorem
5.4
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-coordinate chart
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime})
to
\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}_{s}
such that
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
but
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}
. If we use Theorem
5.5
, then we can get
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
which is sharp, but neither
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-1}
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma-1)-}
nor
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-2}
is sharp.

We do not know whether we can choose suitable coordinate chart
F
so that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}
and
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
holds in the same time.

5.3.1
Some elementary examples

In this subsection we use the conventions
f_{u}=\partial_{u}f
,
f_{v}=\partial_{v}f
, etc.

For parameterizations of real tangent subbundles, we can reduce the discussion to the regularity of ODE flows, because of the following:

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let
X(x,y)=(1,a(x,y))
be a continuous real vector field in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,y}
. Let
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the rank
1
real tangent subbundle spanned by
X
.

Suppose
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{t,s}
is a neighborhood of
(0,0)
and
\Phi=(\phi,\psi):\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{t,s}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,y}
is a continuous map which is homeomorphism onto its image, such that
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

(T\mathbb{R}^{2})
is continuous, and
\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}(t,s)
spans
\mathcal{V}_{\Phi(t,s)}
for each
(t,s)\in\Omega
.
Then

(i)

the map
G(t,s):=(\phi(t,s),\psi(0,s))
is locally homeomorphism near
(0,0)
. And the map
\Psi(u,v):=(u,\psi\circ G^{\mathrm{Inv}}(u,v))
satisfies

•

\Psi
is homeomorphism onto its image.

•

\Psi(0,v)=(0,v)
and
\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial u}(u,v)=X(\Psi(u,v))
.

(ii)

Suppose in addition there is a
\alpha\in\{1-\}\cup(1,\infty)
such that
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
near
(0,0)
. Then
\Psi,\Psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
near
(0,0)
as well.

The case
\alpha=1
is true as well but we do not use that in applications.

When
X
has ODE uniqueness, we see that
\Psi(t,s)=\exp_{X}(t,s)
. In general, for example when
X
is Hölder,
\Psi
is just an integral flow for
X
.

Proof.

By assumption
\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}\in C^{0}(\Omega)
is non-vanishing, so for each
v
, the map
\phi(\cdot,v)
has a
C^{1}
-inverse.

Define
G_{1}(t,s):=(\phi(t,s),s)
and
h(u,v):=\phi(\cdot,u)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(v)
. Clearly
h
is continuous and is
C^{1}
in
u
. Therefore
G_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(u,v)=(h(u,v),v)
is continuous on
G(\Omega)
and is
C^{1}
in
u
. We see that
G_{1}
is homeomorphism onto its image.

Let
\Psi_{1}(u,v):=(u,\psi\circ G_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(u,v))=\Phi\circ G_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(u,v)
. By computation, for
(u,v)
in the domain

\textstyle\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial u}(u,v)=(1,\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}(h(u,v),v)\cdot\frac{\partial h}{\partial u}(u,v))=\frac{\partial h}{\partial u}(u,v)\cdot\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial t}(G_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(u,v)).

Since
\frac{\partial h}{\partial u}
is non-vanishing, we see that
\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial u}(u,v)
spans
\mathcal{V}_{\Psi_{1}(u,v)}
.
On the other hand
\Psi_{1}(u,v)=(u,\ast)
, we must have
\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial u}(u,v)=(1,\ast)
. Therefore
\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial u}(u,v)=X(\Psi_{1}(u,v))
.

Since
\Phi
and
G
are both homeomorphism, we see that
\Psi_{1}
is also homeomorphism, thus
v\mapsto\Psi_{1}(0,v)
is a topological embedded curve. On the other hand
\Psi_{1}(0,v)=(0,\psi(0,v))
, we see that
v\mapsto\psi(0,v)
is injective continuous, which is homeomorphism between neighborhoods of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
and
\psi(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
.

Let
G_{2}(u,v):=(u,\psi(0,v))
, we see that
G_{2}
is homeomorphism onto its image,
C^{1}
in
u
, and
G=G_{2}\circ G_{1}
. Therefore
\Psi(u,v)=\Psi_{1}(u,\psi(0,\cdot)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(v))=\Phi\circ G^{\mathrm{Inv}}(u,v)
satisfies

•

\Psi
is homeomorphism onto its image, and
C^{1}
in
u
.

•

\Psi(0,v)=\Psi_{1}(0,\psi(0,\cdot)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(v))=(0,v)
.

•

\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial u}(u,v)=\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial u}(u,\psi(0,\cdot)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(v))=X\circ\Psi_{1}(u,\psi(0,\cdot)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(v))=X(\Psi(u,v))
.

Therefore
\Psi
is as desired, finishing the proof of
(i)
.

Proof of
(ii)
: By the assumption
G_{1},G_{2}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

When
\alpha>1
, we see that
\nabla\Phi(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
has full rank 2, thus
\nabla G_{1}(0,0),\nabla G_{2}(0,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
are both of full rank as well. By the Inverse Function Theorem, we see that
G_{1},G_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-inverses near
(0,0)
, thus
\Psi
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
and has
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-inverse near
(0,0)
.

When
\alpha=1-
, the proof of
G_{1}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
is similar to the proof of Lemma
2.2.33
.

By shrinking
\Omega
if necessary we can assume
C_{0}:=(\inf_{\Omega}|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}|)^{-1}<\infty
. Therefore for
t_{1},t_{2},s_{1},s_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
that satisfy
(t_{1},s_{1}),(t_{2},s_{1}),(t_{2},s_{2})\in\Phi(\Omega)
,

\displaystyle|h(t_{1},s_{1})-h(t_{2},s_{2})|\leq

\displaystyle|h(t_{1},s_{1})-h(t_{2},s_{1})|+|h(t_{2},s_{1})-h(t_{2},s_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}|t_{1}-t_{2}|+C_{0}|\phi(h(t_{2},s_{1}),s_{1})-\phi(h(t_{2},s_{2}),s_{1})|

\displaystyle\textstyle(\sup|\frac{\partial h}{\partial u}|=(\inf|\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial t}|)^{-1}=C_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{0}|t_{1}-t_{2}|+C_{0}|t_{2}-\phi(h(t_{2},s_{2}),s_{1})|

\displaystyle(\phi(h(\cdot,s_{1}),s_{1})=\operatorname{id})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{0}|t_{1}-t_{2}|+C_{0}|\phi(h(t_{2},s_{2}),s_{2})-\phi(h(t_{2},s_{2}),s_{1})|

\displaystyle(\phi(h(\cdot,s_{2}),s_{2})=\operatorname{id})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{0}|t_{1}-t_{2}|+C_{0}\|\phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{1-\varepsilon}}|s_{1}-s_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon},

\displaystyle\forall\varepsilon>0.\hskip 10.00002pt(\text{since }\phi\in\mathscr{C}^{1-})

We conclude that
G_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
in the domain. Therefore by Corollary
2.1.31

(iii)
,
\Psi_{1}=\Phi\circ G_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
and
\Psi_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}=G_{1}\circ\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{1-}
.

Now
\Psi_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}(0,v)=(0,\psi(0,\cdot)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(v))
is a
\mathscr{C}^{1-}
-map, thus
\psi(0,\cdot)^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
, which means
G_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
. Clearly
G_{2}\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
(since
\psi\in\mathscr{C}^{1-}
). By Corollary
2.1.31

(iii)
,
\Psi=\Psi_{1}\circ G_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
and
\Psi^{\mathrm{Inv}}=G_{2}\circ\Psi_{1}^{\mathrm{Inv}}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{1-}
as well, finishing the proof.
∎

For the non-
C^{1}
case we have the following examples for the real Frobenius theorems.

Corollary 5.3.2
.

For a continuous function
f_{j}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, we set a vector field
X_{j}(x,y)=(1,f_{j}(y))
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{x,t}
and define
\mathcal{V}_{j}\leq T\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a tangent subbundle spanned by
X_{j}
.

(i)

Let
0<\alpha<1
and let
f_{1}(y):=-|y|^{\alpha}
. Then
\mathcal{V}_{1}
is not locally integrable via parameterizations (recall Definition
3.3.1
).

(ii)

Let
f_{2}(y):=y\log|y|
. Then
\mathcal{V}_{2}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{1}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{LogL}}
subbundle. And if
\Phi
is a parameterization for
\mathcal{V}_{2}
in Definition
3.3.1
near
(0,0)
, then either
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{1-}
or
\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}\notin\mathscr{C}^{1-}
.

Proof.

By direct computation, the equation
\Psi_{1}(0,s)=s
,
\frac{\partial\Psi_{1}}{\partial t}(t,s)=X_{1}(\Psi_{1}(t,s))
has forward uniqueness solution

\Psi_{1}(t,s)=\begin{cases}(t,(|s|^{1-\alpha}-(1-\alpha)t)^{\frac{1}{1-\alpha}})&0\leq t\leq\tfrac{|s|^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha},\\
0&t\geq\tfrac{|s|^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha}.\end{cases}

Clearly
\Psi_{1}
is not injective in a neighborhood of
(0,0)
, which cannot be locally homeomorphism near
(0,0)
. By Lemma
5.3.1
, there is no topological parameterization
\Phi_{1}(t,s)
near
(0,0)
such that
\frac{\partial\Phi_{1}}{\partial t}
span
\mathcal{V}_{1}
. Thus
\mathcal{V}_{1}
is not locally integrable via parameterizations, giving
(i)
.

For
X_{2}
, the ODE flow gives

e^{tX_{2}}(0,s)=(t,|s|^{e^{u}}\operatorname{sgn}s).

This map is not
\mathscr{C}^{1-}
is any neighborhood of
(0,0)
. Therefore by Lemma
5.3.1

(ii)
, if
\Phi_{2}
is a topological parameterization for
\mathcal{V}_{2}
near
(0,0)
, then either
\Phi_{2}\notin\mathscr{C}^{1-}
or
\Phi_{2}^{\mathrm{Inv}}\notin\mathscr{C}^{1-}
.
∎

For the
C^{1}
case, we have the following result for the real Frobenius theorem.

Proposition 5.3.3
.

Let
\beta>1
, and let
(u,v)
be the standard coordinate system for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We define
\mathcal{V}\leq T\mathbb{R}^{2}
as

\textstyle\mathcal{V}:=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{R}}T,\hskip 10.00002ptT:=\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+f(v)\frac{\partial}{\partial v},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }f(v):=\max(0,v)^{\beta}.

Suppose
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}_{u,v}\to\mathbb{R}^{1}_{t}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
is a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart near
(0,0)
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
spans
\mathcal{V}|_{U}
, then
\partial_{v}F^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\beta-1)+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U)
. In particular
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{R}^{2})
.

Note that
\mathcal{V}
has rank 1 so is automatically involutive.

Proof.

By direct computation

e^{tT}(u,v)=\begin{cases}\big(u+t,\frac{v}{\left(1-(\beta-1)v^{\beta-1}t\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}}\big)&\text{for }v>0\text{ and }-\infty<t<(\beta-1)v^{1-\beta},\\
(u+t,v)&\text{for }v\leq 0\text{ and }t\in\mathbb{R}.\end{cases}

First we show that for every
\delta\neq 0
, the function

g^{\delta}:I_{\delta}\to\mathbb{R},\hskip 10.00002ptg^{\delta}(v):=\begin{cases}\frac{v}{\left(1-(\beta-1)\delta v^{\beta-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}},&v>0,\\
v,&v\leq 0,\end{cases}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }I_{\delta}:=\begin{cases}\left(-\infty,(\frac{\delta}{\beta-1})^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}\right),&\delta>0,\\
\mathbb{R},&\delta<0,\end{cases}

(5.3.1)

is not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
near
v=0
. Note that
e^{uT}(0,v)=(u,g^{u}(v))
for every
u
.

Indeed by Taylor’s expansion,

\frac{v}{\left(1-(\beta-1)\delta v^{\beta-1}\right)^{\frac{1}{\beta-1}}}=v+\delta v^{\beta}+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}{-\frac{1}{\beta-1}\choose k}\left(-(\beta-1)\delta\right)^{k}\cdot v^{k\beta-k+1},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{converging for }0\leq v<(\tfrac{\delta}{\beta-1})^{\frac{1}{1-\beta}}.

Hence
g^{\delta}(v)=v+\max(0,v)^{\beta}+\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}{-\frac{1}{\beta-1}\choose k}\left(-(\beta-1)\delta\right)^{k}\cdot\max(0,v)^{k\beta-k+1}
holds near
v=0
.

Now near
v=0
,
\sum_{k=2}^{\infty}{-\frac{1}{\beta-1}\choose k}\left(-(\beta-1)\delta\right)^{k}\cdot\max(0,v)^{k\beta-k+1}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{2\beta-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-function, but
\max(0,v)^{\beta}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
. So
g^{\delta}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
near
v=0
.
Therefore the function
(u,v)\mapsto g^{u}(v)
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
near
(u,v)=(0,0)
.

By assumption
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
spans
\mathcal{V}|_{U}
. So
\mathcal{V}^{\bot}|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}dF^{\prime\prime}
, which means that we have a transport equation

\textstyle TF^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v)=0\hskip 10.00002pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 10.00002pt\partial_{u}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v)=-f(v)\cdot\partial_{v}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all }(u,v)\in U.

(5.3.2)

Since
F
is a
C^{1}
-chart, we have
(F_{u}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,0),F_{v}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,0))\neq(0,0)
. By (
5.3.2
) and
f(0)=0
, we have
F_{u}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,0)=0
. Thus
F_{v}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,0)\neq 0
.

Therefore
h(v):=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,v)
is a
C^{1}
-function that has non-vanishing derivatives in a neighborhood of
v=0
. By the Inverse Function Theorem, on a smaller neighborhood of
v=0
,
h
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism onto its image.

Suppose by contrast
F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{(\beta-1)+}
in a neighborhood of
(u,v)=(0,0)
. By (
5.3.2
) since
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{(\beta-1)+}
, we have
F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{u}\in\mathscr{C}^{(\beta-1)+}
as well, which means
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
near
(u,v)=(0,0)
. Since
h(v)=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,v)
, we have
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
in the domain and by the Inverse Function Theorem
h^{\mathrm{Inv}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
.

So
h^{\mathrm{Inv}}\circ F^{\prime\prime}(u,v)
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
-function defined in a neighborhood of
(u,v)=(0,0)
. By (
5.3.2
) and (
5.3.1
),
F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v)=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(e^{-uT}(u,v))=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(0,g^{-u}(v))
holds a neighborhood of
0
.
So
h^{\mathrm{Inv}}\circ F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v)=g^{-u}(v)
, contradicting to the fact that
(u,v)\mapsto g^{u}(v)
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
near
(0,0)
. This concludes that
F_{v}^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\beta-1)+}
near
(u,v)=(0,0)
.
Therefore
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta+}
and we finish the proof.
∎

For the complex Frobenius theorem the following example give the sharpness of
F^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
.

Proposition 5.3.4
.

Endow
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with standard coordinate system
(u,v,\theta)
. Let
\alpha>0
. We define
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathbb{R}^{3}
as

\mathcal{S}:=\operatorname{Span}_{\mathbb{C}}Z,\hskip 10.00002ptZ:=\partial_{u}+ie^{f(\theta)}\partial_{v}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }f(\theta):=\max(0,\theta)^{\alpha}.

Suppose
F=(F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{3}_{u,v,\theta}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
is a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that that
F(0)=(0,0)
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
. Then both
F^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
near
(0,0,0)
.

By assumption
Z\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{S}
is involutive since it has rank 1, and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial u},\frac{\partial}{\partial v})
is involutive as well. So
\mathcal{S}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure.

Proof.

By assumption
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{u},\partial_{v})|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}})=(\operatorname{Span}F^{*}ds)^{\bot}=(\operatorname{Span}dF^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\bot}
, so
\partial_{u}F^{\prime\prime\prime}=\partial_{v}F^{\prime\prime\prime}=0
which means
F^{\prime\prime\prime}(u,v,\theta)=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\theta)
. So
dF^{\prime\prime\prime}=F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}d\theta
and
F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}(\theta)
is non-vanishing in a neighborhood of
\theta=0
.

Now
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}|_{U}=(\operatorname{Span}Z)^{\bot}|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}d\bar{z},F^{*}ds)=\operatorname{Span}(d\bar{F}^{\prime\prime},dF^{\prime\prime\prime})
, so

Z\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}=\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{u}+ie^{f(\theta)}\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{v}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002ptF^{\prime\prime}_{v}=-ie^{-f(\theta)}F^{\prime\prime}_{u},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }U.

Here we use
F^{\prime\prime}_{u}=\partial_{u}F^{\prime\prime}
,
F^{\prime\prime}_{v}=\partial_{v}F^{\prime\prime}
and
F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}=\partial_{\theta}F^{\prime\prime}
. So

\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}dz\\
F^{*}d\bar{z}\\
F^{*}ds\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime\prime}_{u}&-F^{\prime\prime}_{u}&F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\\
\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{u}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{u}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\\
&&F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}du\\
-ie^{-f(\theta)}dv\\
d\theta\end{pmatrix}\ \Rightarrow\ \begin{pmatrix}\partial_{u}\\
ie^{f(\theta)}\partial_{v}\\
\partial_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime\prime}_{u}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{u}&\\
-F^{\prime\prime}_{u}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{u}\\
F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}&F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}\partial_{z}\\
F^{*}\partial_{\bar{z}}\\
F^{*}\partial_{s}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.3.3)

Applying Cramer’s rule for inverse matrices to the right hand equation of (
5.3.3
), we have

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}=\frac{1}{F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}(\theta)}\bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}-\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{u}}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}+ie^{f(\theta)}\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\Big)-\frac{F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}}{F^{\prime\prime}_{u}}\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial u}-ie^{f(\theta)}\frac{\partial}{\partial v}\Big)\bigg),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }U.

(5.3.4)

In particular we see that
F^{\prime\prime}_{u}
and
F^{\prime\prime}_{v}=-ie^{-f}F^{\prime\prime}_{u}
are both non-vanishing in the domain
U
.

For fixed
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,
\partial_{u}+ie^{f(\theta)}\partial_{v}
is scaled
\overline{\partial}
-operator on
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{u,v}
that annihilates
F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta_{0})
. Therefore by a scaling of the Cauchy Integral Formula, for every
\tilde{V}^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}\Subset U
,
(u,v)\in\tilde{V}^{\prime}
,
\theta\in V^{\prime\prime}
and
k\geq 0
,

F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,\theta)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial V^{\prime}}\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(\xi,\eta,\theta)(d\xi+ie^{-f(\theta)}d\eta)}{(\xi-u)+ie^{-f(\theta)}(\eta-v)},\hskip 10.00002pt\frac{\partial^{k}F^{\prime\prime}(u,v,\theta)}{\partial u^{k}}=\frac{k!}{2\pi i}\int_{\partial V^{\prime}}\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(\xi,\eta,\theta)(d\xi+ie^{-f(\theta)}d\eta)}{((\xi-u)+ie^{-f(\theta)}(\eta-v))^{k+1}}.

Since
F^{\prime\prime}\in C^{0}(\tilde{V}^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C})
, for every precompact subset
V^{\prime}\Subset\tilde{V}^{\prime}
we have

\|\partial_{u}^{k}F^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{2^{k}})}\leq k!\operatorname{dist}(V^{\prime},\partial\tilde{V}^{\prime})^{-k-1}\|F^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(\tilde{V}^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C})}<\infty,\hskip 10.00002pt\forall k\geq 0.

Using
F_{v}^{\prime\prime}=-ie^{-f(\theta)}F_{u}^{\prime\prime}
, we see that
\|\nabla_{u,v}^{k}F^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{2^{k}})}<\infty
for every
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}\Subset U
, which means
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{u,v}L^{\infty}_{\theta}(V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C})
.

Let
\gamma>0
be such that
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})
. Since
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}_{u,v}L^{\infty}_{\theta}(V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C})
for every
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}\Subset U
, by Lemma
2.2.21
we have the following:

\text{For every }\gamma>0,\text{ if }F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C}),\text{ then }\nabla_{u,v}F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty,\gamma-}_{(u,v),s}(V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C}^{2})\text{ holds for all }V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}\Subset U.

(5.3.5)

Now suppose by contrast that
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})
. Say
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+2\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U;\mathbb{C})
for some
\varepsilon>0
. Take
\gamma=\alpha+\varepsilon
in (
5.3.5
), we have
F^{\prime\prime}_{u},F^{\prime\prime}_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}(V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime};\mathbb{C})
for every precompact neighborhood
V^{\prime}\times V^{\prime\prime}\Subset U
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2}_{u,v}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
.

Since
F^{\prime\prime}_{u},F^{\prime\prime}_{v}
are both non-vanishing, we have
-ie^{f(\theta)}=F^{\prime\prime}_{v}/F_{u}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0,0)
. However
-ie^{f}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
near
\theta=0
since
f\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
near
\theta=0
. Contradiction!

This concludes the proof of
F^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
near
(0,0,0)
.

To show
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
near
(0,0,0)
, we can assume
F^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
, since otherwise
F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
in a neighborhood of
\theta=0
and by (
5.3.4
) the
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}
-component of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
already.

Let
\gamma>0
be the largest number such that
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-}
near
(0,0,0)
. By assumption
F^{\prime\prime}\in C^{1}
so
\gamma\geq 1
. Since
F^{\prime\prime}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}
near
(0,0,0)
, we see that
\gamma\leq\alpha
.

By (
5.3.5
), we know
F^{\prime\prime}_{u},F^{\prime\prime}_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-\varepsilon}
near
(0,0,0)
for every
\varepsilon>0
, in particular
F^{\prime\prime}_{u},F^{\prime\prime}_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}
near
(0,0,0)
.

We see that
F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma-1)+}
near
(0,0,0)
. Suppose otherwise, i.e.
F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-1+\varepsilon}
for some
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
. Since
F^{\prime\prime}_{u},F^{\prime\prime}_{v}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma-\frac{1}{2}}
, we have
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\gamma+\varepsilon}
. This contradicts to the maximality of
\gamma
.

Now we have
F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}/F^{\prime\prime}_{u}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\gamma-1)+}
for such
\gamma\geq 1
, in particular
F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}/F^{\prime\prime}_{u}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
. Since
Z=\partial_{u}+ie^{f}\partial_{v}
and
\bar{Z}=\partial_{u}-ie^{f}\partial_{v}
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and we already assumed
F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}\in\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
, using (
5.3.4
) again we conclude that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\notin\mathscr{C}^{(\alpha-1)+}
near
(0,0,0)
. This completes the whole proof.
∎

5.3.2
A counterexample to
C^{k}
-regularity for the Newlander-Nirenberg theorem

In this subsection we prove Theorem
7
.

For convenience we use the viewpoint of eigenbundle of
J
: Set
\mathcal{S}_{n}:=\coprod_{p}\{v\in\mathbb{C}T_{p}\mathbb{R}^{2n}:J_{p}v=iv\}
. So
J\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{j}}=i\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{j}}
for all
j
iff
d\bar{w}^{1},\dots,d\bar{w}^{n}
spans
\mathcal{V}_{n}^{\bot}|_{U}\leq\mathbb{C}T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{2n}|_{U}
. And
J
is integrable if and only if
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is involutive. See
[
BCH08
, Chapter 1]
for details.

First we can restrict our focus to the 1-dimensional case:

Proof of Theorem
7
, 1-dimension
\Rightarrow
n-dimension.

Suppose
J_{1}
is a
C^{k}
-almost complex structure on
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z^{1}}
(not compatible with the standard complex structure), such that near
0
, there is no
C^{k,1}
-complex coordinate
\varphi
satisfying
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\bot}=\operatorname{Span}d\bar{\varphi}
in the domain. Here
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\bot}
is the dual eigenbundle of
J_{1}
.

Denote by
\theta=\theta(z^{1})
a
C^{k}
1-form on
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z^{1}}
that spans
\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\bot}
.

Consider
\mathbb{R}^{2n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}_{(z^{1},\dots,z^{n})}
. We identify
\theta
as the
C^{k}
1-form on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
Take an
n
-dim almost complex structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that the dual of eigenbundle
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\bot}
is spanned by
\theta,d\bar{z}^{2},\dots,d\bar{z}^{n}
.

In other words,
\mathcal{S}_{n}
is the “tensor” of
\mathcal{S}_{1}
with the standard complex structure of
\mathbb{C}^{n-1}_{(z^{2},\dots,z^{n})}
.

If
w=(w^{1},\dots,w^{n})
is a corresponding
C^{k,1}
-complex chart for
\mathcal{S}_{n}
near
0
, then there is a
1\leq j_{0}\leq n
such that
d\bar{w}^{j_{0}}\not\equiv 0\pmod{d\bar{z}^{2},\dots,d\bar{z}^{n}}
near
0
. In other words, we have linear combinations
d\bar{w}^{j_{0}}=\lambda_{1}\theta+\lambda_{2}d\bar{z}^{2}+\dots+\lambda_{n}d\bar{z}^{n}
for some non-vanishing function
\lambda_{1}(z^{1},\dots,z^{n})
near
z=0
.

Therefore
w^{j_{0}}(\cdot,0^{n-1})
is a complex coordinate chart defined near
z^{1}=0\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose differential spans
\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\bot}|_{\mathbb{R}^{2}_{z^{1}}}\cong\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\bot}
near
0
. By our assumption on
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, we have
w^{j_{0}}(\cdot,0)\notin C^{k,1}
. So
w^{j_{0}}\notin C^{k,1}
, which means
w\notin C^{k,1}
.
∎

Now we focus on the one-dimensional case. Note that a 1-dim structure is automatically integrable.

Fix
k\geq 1
. Define an almost complex structure by setting its eigenbundle
\mathcal{S}_{1}\leq\mathbb{C}T\mathbb{R}^{2}
equals to the span of
\frac{\partial}{\partial z}+a(z)\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}
, where
a\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
has compact support that satisfies the following:

(i)

a\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
;

(ii)

\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{z}}a\notin C^{k-1,1}
near 0;

(iii)

za\in C^{k+1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and
z^{-1}a\in C^{k-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
(which implies
a(z)=o(|z|)
as
z\to 0
);

(iv)

\operatorname{supp}a\subset\mathbb{B}^{2}
;

(v)

\|a\|_{C^{0}}<\delta_{0}
for some small enough
\delta_{0}>0
(take
\delta_{0}=10^{-1}
will be okay).

Here we take
\partial_{z}^{-1}
to be the
conjugated Cauchy-Green operator
on the unit disk
\mathbb{B}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{1}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{-1}\phi(z)=\partial_{z,\mathbb{B}^{2}}^{-1}\phi(z):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{2}}\frac{\phi(\xi+i\eta)d\xi d\eta}{\bar{z}-\xi+i\eta}.

We use notation
\partial_{z}^{-1}
because it is an right inverse of
\partial_{z}
. For
f
supported in
\mathbb{B}^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
,
\partial_{z}^{-1}f=4\partial_{\bar{z}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\ast f
, where
\mathbf{\Gamma}
is 2-dimensional Newtonian potential in (
2.1.26
). Therefore by Lemma
2.1.40

\partial_{z}^{-1}:C^{m,\beta}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to C^{m+1,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is bounded linear for all
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
0<\beta<1
.

We can take
\operatorname{supp}a\subset\mathbb{B}^{2}
such that when
|z|<\frac{1}{2}
,

\textstyle a(z):=\frac{1}{100}\bar{z}^{k+1}\partial_{z}\big((-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\frac{1}{100}\partial_{z}\big(\bar{z}^{k+1}(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(5.3.6)

Note that for this
a
we have
a(z)=O\big(|z|^{k}(-\log|z|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)=o(|z|^{k})
as
z\to 0
.

Remark 5.3.5
.

Roughly speaking, Property
(i)

\operatorname{Singsupp}a=\{0\}
says that the regularity of
a(z)
corresponds to the vanishing order of
a
at
0
. To some degree, by multiplying with
a(z)
, a function gains some regularity at the origin.

We check Property
(ii)
that
\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{z}}a\notin C^{k-1,1}
here.

Lemma 5.3.6
.

Let
a(z)
be given by (
5.3.6
), and let
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2})
satisfies
\chi\equiv 1
in a neighborhood of 0. Then
\partial_{z}^{-1}(\chi a_{\bar{z}})\notin C^{k-1,1}
near
z=0
.

Proof.

Denote
b(z):=\bar{z}^{k+1}(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, so
b\in C^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
,
\chi a=\frac{1}{100}\chi b_{z}
and
\chi a_{\bar{z}}=\frac{1}{100}\chi b_{z\bar{z}}
.

First we show that
\partial_{z}^{-1}(\chi b_{z\bar{z}})-b_{\bar{z}}\in C^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
.
We write

\partial_{z}^{-1}(\chi b_{z\bar{z}})-b_{\bar{z}}=\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{z}(\chi b_{\bar{z}})-\chi b_{\bar{z}}-(1-\chi)b_{\bar{z}}-\partial_{z}^{-1}(\chi_{z}b_{\bar{z}}).

Since
\partial_{z}\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{z}(\chi b_{\bar{z}})=\partial_{z}(\chi b_{\bar{z}})
, we know
\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{z}(\chi b_{\bar{z}})-\chi b_{\bar{z}}
is anti-holomorphic. By Cauchy integral formula we get
\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{z}(\chi b_{\bar{z}})-\chi b_{\bar{z}}\in C^{\infty}
.

By assumption
0\notin\operatorname{supp}\chi_{z}
,
0\notin\operatorname{supp}(1-\chi)
and
b\in C^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
, we know
\chi_{z}b_{\bar{z}}\in C_{c}^{\infty}
and
(1-\chi)b_{\bar{z}}\in C^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
.

Note that when acting on functions supported in the unit disk,
\partial_{z}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\pi\bar{z}}\ast(\cdot)
is a convolution operator with kernel
\frac{1}{\pi\bar{z}}\in L^{1}
, so
\partial_{z}^{-1}(\chi_{z}b_{\bar{z}})=\frac{1}{\pi\bar{z}}\ast(\chi_{z}b_{\bar{z}})\in C^{\infty}
.

It remains to show
b_{\bar{z}}\notin C^{k-1,1}
near
0
. Indeed one has

\partial_{\bar{z}}^{k+1}(\bar{z}^{k+1}(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}})=(k+1)!(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}+O(1),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{as }z\to 0,

because by Leibniz rule

\displaystyle\textstyle\partial_{\bar{z}}^{k}\partial_{\bar{z}}\big(\bar{z}^{k+1}(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)=\sum_{j=0}^{k+1}{k+1\choose j}\partial_{\bar{z}}^{k+1-j}(z^{k+1})\cdot\partial_{\bar{z}}^{j}(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textstyle(k+1)!(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sum_{j=1}^{k+1}O(z^{j})O\big(z^{-j}(-\log|z|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)=(k+1)!(-\log|z|)^{\frac{1}{2}}+O\big((-\log|z|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big).\qed

Now assume
w:\tilde{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is a 1-dim
C^{1}
-complex coordinate chart defined near
0
that represents
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, then
\operatorname{Span}d\bar{w}=\operatorname{Span}(d\bar{z}-adz)|_{\tilde{U}}=\mathcal{S}_{1}^{\bot}|_{\tilde{U}}
. So
d\bar{w}=\bar{w}_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}+\bar{w}_{z}dz=\bar{w}_{\bar{z}}(d\bar{z}-adz)
, that is,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial w}{\partial\bar{z}}(z)+\bar{a}(z)\frac{\partial w}{\partial z}(z)=0,\hskip 20.00003ptz\in{\tilde{U}}.

Remark 5.3.7
.

It is worth noticing that
\partial_{w}\neq\partial_{z}+a\partial_{\bar{z}}
. Indeed
\partial_{w}
is only a scalar multiple of
\partial_{z}+a\partial_{\bar{z}}
.

Note that
w_{z}(0)\neq 0
because
(d\bar{z}-adz)|_{0}=d\bar{z}|_{0}\in\operatorname{Span}d\bar{w}|_{0}
. So by multiplying
w_{z}(0)^{-1}
, we can assume
w_{z}(0)=1
without loss of generality.
Then
f:=\log\partial_{z}w
is a well-defined function in a smaller neighborhood
U\subset\tilde{U}
of
0
, which solves

\frac{\partial f}{\partial\bar{z}}(z)+\overline{a(z)}\frac{\partial f}{\partial z}(z)=-\frac{\partial\bar{a}}{\partial z}(z)\hskip 10.00002pt\Big(=-\overline{\frac{\partial a}{\partial\bar{z}}(z)}\Big),\hskip 20.00003ptz\in U.

(5.3.7)

Property
(v)
indicates that the operator
\partial_{\bar{z}}+\bar{a}\partial_{z}
is a first order elliptic operator. Therefore we can consider a second order divergence form elliptic operator

L:=\partial_{z}(\partial_{\bar{z}}+\bar{a}\partial_{z})

whose coefficients are
C^{k}
globally and are
C^{\infty}
outside the origin.

By the classical Schauder’s estimate (see
[
Tay11
, Theorem 4.2]
, or
[
GT01
, Chapter 6 & 8]
), we have the following:

Lemma 5.3.8

(Schauder’s interior estimate)
.

Assume
u,\psi\in C^{0,1}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
satisfy
Lu=\psi_{z}
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a neighborhood of
0
. The following hold:

(a)

If
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
, then
u\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
.

(b)

If
\psi\in C^{k-1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
, then
u\in C^{k,1-\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
for all
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Our Theorem
7
for 1-dim case is done by the following proposition:

Proposition 5.3.9
.

For any neighborhood
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}_{z}
of
0
, there is no
f\in C^{k-1,1}(U;\mathbb{C})
solving (
5.3.7
).

Proof.

Suppose there is a neighborhood
U\subset\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}
of the origin, and a solution
f\in C^{k-1,1}(U;\mathbb{C})
to (
5.3.7
).

Applying Lemma
5.3.8

(a)
on (
5.3.7
) with
u=f
and
\psi=-\bar{a}_{z}
, we know that
f\in C^{\infty}(U\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
.

Take
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U;[0,1])
such that
\chi\equiv 1
in a smaller neighborhood of
0
. Denote

g(z):=\chi(z)f(z),\hskip 20.00003pth(z):=\bar{z}g(z).

So
g,h
are
C^{k-1,1}
-functions defined in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that are also
smooth away from 0
, and satisfy the following:

\displaystyle g_{\bar{z}}+\bar{a}g_{z}

\displaystyle=\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f-\chi\bar{a}_{z},

(5.3.8)

\displaystyle h_{\bar{z}}+\bar{a}h_{z}

\displaystyle=g+\bar{z}(\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f)-\chi\bar{z}\bar{a}_{z}.

(5.3.9)

By construction
\nabla\chi\equiv 0
holds in a neighborhood of
0
, so
\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{C})
.

Under our assumption that
f\in C^{k-1,1}(U;\mathbb{C})
, then the key is to show that

(I)

h\in C^{k,1-\varepsilon}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
,
\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1)
. This implies:

(II)

\bar{a}g_{z}\in C^{k-1,1-\varepsilon}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
,
\forall\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Proof of (I): By assumption
\bar{z}(\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f)\in C^{\infty}_{c}
,
g\in C^{k-1,1}
, and by Property
(iii)
,
\chi\bar{z}\bar{a}_{z}\in C^{k}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
.

Applying Lemma
5.3.8

(b)
to (
5.3.9
), with
u=h
and
\psi=g+\bar{z}(\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f)-\chi\bar{z}\bar{a}_{z}\in C^{k}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
, we get
h\in C^{k,1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
, for all
0<\varepsilon<1
.

Proof of (II): When
k\geq 2
, we know
z^{-1}a\in C^{k-1}
for Property
(iii)
. So for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, one has
\bar{a}g_{z}\in C^{k-1,1-\varepsilon}
because

\nabla_{z,\bar{z}}(\bar{a}g_{z})=\bar{a}\cdot(\partial_{z}g_{z},\partial_{\bar{z}}g_{z})+g_{z}\nabla_{z,\bar{z}}\bar{a}=\bar{z}^{-1}\bar{a}\cdot(h_{zz},h_{z\bar{z}}-g_{z})+O(C^{k-2,1})\in C^{k-2,1-\varepsilon}.

When
k=1
, for any
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
, note that
g_{\bar{z}}
is smooth outside the origin, so
\bar{a}g_{z}\in C^{0,1-\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle|\bar{a}g_{z}(z_{1})-\bar{a}g_{z}(z_{2})|\leq|\bar{a}(z_{1})||g_{z}(z_{1})-g_{z}(z_{2})|+|\bar{a}(z_{1})-\bar{a}(z_{2})||g_{z}(z_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\bar{z}_{1}^{-1}\bar{a}(z_{1})||\bar{z}_{1}g_{z}(z_{1})-\bar{z}_{2}g_{z}(z_{2})|+|\bar{z}_{1}^{-1}\bar{a}(z_{1})||\bar{z}_{1}-\bar{z}_{2}||g_{z}(z_{2})|+|\bar{a}(z_{1})-\bar{a}(z_{2})||g_{z}(z_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|z^{-1}a\|_{C^{0}}\|\nabla h\|_{C^{1-\varepsilon}}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}+\|z^{-1}a\|_{C^{0}}\|g\|_{C^{0,1}}|z_{1}-z_{2}|+\|a\|_{C^{1}}\|g\|_{C^{0,1}}|z_{1}-z_{2}|.

So for either case of
k
, we have
\bar{a}g_{z}\in C^{k-1,1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

Here as a remark,
|\bar{a}g_{z}(z_{1})-\bar{a}g_{z}(z_{2})|\lesssim_{a,g,h}|z_{1}-z_{2}|^{1-\varepsilon}
still makes sense when
z_{1}
or
z_{2}=0
, though
\bar{g}_{z}(0)
may not be defined. Indeed
\lim\limits_{z\to 0}\bar{a}g_{z}(z)
exists because
\bar{a}g_{z}
itself has bounded
C^{0,1-\varepsilon}
-oscillation on
\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\}
, and then the limit defines the value of
\bar{a}g_{z}
at
z=0
.

Based on consequence
(II)
, for (
5.3.8
) we have
