g=(g-\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}g_{\bar{z}})+\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}(\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f)-\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}(\bar{a}g_{z})-\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}(\chi\bar{a}_{z}),\hskip 20.00003pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{2}.

(5.3.10)

The right hand side of (
5.3.10
) consists of four terms, the first to the third are all
C^{k}
, while the last one is not
C^{k-1,1}
. We explain these as follows:

•

Since
\partial_{z}(g-\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}g_{\bar{z}})=0
in
\mathbb{B}^{2}
, we know
g-\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}g_{\bar{z}}
is anti-holomorphic, which is smooth in
\mathbb{B}^{2}
.

•

By assumption
\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
, so
\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}(\chi_{\bar{z}}f+\chi_{z}\bar{a}f)\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
as well.

•

By consequence
(II)

\bar{a}g_{z}\in C^{k-1,1-\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{C})
, for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, so
\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}(\bar{a}g_{z})\in C^{k,1-\varepsilon}\subset C^{k}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
.

•

However by Lemma
5.3.6
,
\partial_{\bar{z}}^{-1}(\chi\bar{a}_{z})\notin C^{k-1,1}
near 0.

Combining each term to the right hand side of (
5.3.10
), we know
g\notin C^{k-1,1}
near 0. Contradiction!
∎

Remark 5.3.10
.

The key to the proof is the non-surjectivity of
\partial_{z}:C^{k}\to C^{k-1}
, which we use to construct a function
a(z)
such that
a(0)=0
,
\operatorname{Singsupp}a=\{0\}
, and
\partial_{z}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{z}}a\notin C^{k}
.

5.3.3
The sharpness of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
in Theorem
3
: Proof of Theorem
6

In this subsection we will construct a rank-1 complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
, such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
whenever
F
is a coordinate chart representing
\mathcal{S}
near the origin.

We sketch the idea to the proof of Theorem
6
here. It is adapted from Theorem
7
, where we prove that there is a
C^{k}
-integrable almost complex structure that does not admit a
C^{k+1}
-coordinate chart.

Endow
\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
with standard coordinate system
(w,\theta)
. We consider a subbundle
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1})
as

\mathcal{S}:=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a(w,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}}),\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\text{ such that }\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}<1.

Clearly
\mathcal{S}
is involutive and
\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w},\partial_{\bar{w}})=(\mathbb{C}T\mathbb{C}_{w}^{1})\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
since
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=1
and
\sup_{w,\theta}|a(w,\theta)|<1
. So
\mathcal{S}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure.

Step 1
(Proposition
5.3.11
): We show that, the existence of a coordinate chart
F\in C^{1}
near
(0,0)
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
spans
\mathcal{S}
, is equivalent to the existence of
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
near
(w,\theta)=(0,0)
that solves

(\partial_{w}+a(w,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}})f=-\partial_{\bar{w}}a,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{or equivalently}\hskip 10.00002ptf_{w}+(af)_{\bar{w}}-a_{\bar{w}}f=-a_{\bar{w}}.

(5.3.11)

Step 2
(Proposition
5.3.16
): We construct an
a(w,\theta)
such that
a
is smooth away from
\{w=0\}=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
, and the complex Hilbert transform (also known as the Beurling transform)
\partial_{w}^{-1}\partial_{\bar{w}}a\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
near
w=0
.

Step 3
(Theorem
5.3.18
): We show that there is no
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
defined near
(w,\theta)=(0,0)
that solves (
5.3.11
). This can be done by showing that:

•

For every solution
f
to the homogeneous equation
(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})f=0
with
f(0,\theta)=0
,
f
must be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

•

There exists a non-
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
function
f
such that
f(0,\theta)=0
and
f
solves (
5.3.11
).

Thus, by the superposition principle, every solution to (
5.3.11
) cannot be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. By Step 1 we complete the proof.

To make the result more general, we consider the case of mixed regularity, where
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{w,\theta}
for arbitrary
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta>0
(recall again from Lemma
2.2.15
that
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{(w,\theta)}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\alpha}_{w,\theta}
). See Theorem
5.3.19
.

We now start the proof. We use the notation
f_{w}=\partial_{w}f
etc.

Proposition 5.3.11
.

Let
\alpha>\frac{1}{2},\beta>0
, let
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w},\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};\mathbb{C})
be such that
\sup|a|<1
, and let
\mathcal{S}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a(w,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}})
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\beta)}
-complex Frobenius structure with rank
1
. The following
(A)
and
(B)
are equivalent:

(A)

There is a continuous map
F:U_{1}\times V_{1}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
near
(0,0)
such that

(A.1)

F
is homeomorphic to its image,
\nabla_{z}F\in C^{0}(U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
and
\nabla_{w}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})\in C^{0}(U_{1}\times V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
.

(A.2)

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{3})
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times V_{1}}
.

(B)

There is a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
defined in a neighborhood
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
of
(0,0)
that solves (
5.3.11
).

The results are also true for
\alpha
or
\beta=\infty
, without any change of the proof.

For the application to Theorem
6
, we only need the contraposition of
(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
.

Proof.

(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
: Write
F=:(F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime})
and
\Phi=(\Phi^{\prime\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime}):=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}:F(U_{1}\times V_{1})\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
. By assumption
\nabla_{z}\Phi=(\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime\prime},\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime})\in C^{0}(F(U_{1}\times V_{1});\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2})
, so
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{\partial(\operatorname{Re}\Phi^{\prime\prime},\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{\prime\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime})}{\partial z}\circ F
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}=\frac{\partial(\operatorname{Re}\Phi^{\prime\prime},\operatorname{Im}\Phi^{\prime\prime},\Phi^{\prime\prime\prime})}{\partial\bar{z}}\circ F
are well-defined continuous vector fields on
\mathbb{R}^{3}_{(\operatorname{Re}w,\operatorname{Im}w,\theta)}
.

By assumption
(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U_{1}\times V_{1}}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w},\partial_{\bar{w}})
is spanned by
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
, so
\partial_{w}F^{\prime\prime\prime}=\partial_{\bar{w}}F^{\prime\prime\prime}=0
in the sense of distributions, which means
F^{\prime\prime\prime}(w,\theta)=F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\theta)
.

By assumption
d\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}=F^{*}d\bar{z}
is continuous in
w
and is annihilated by
\operatorname{Span}F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})
. Therefore

(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}=\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{w}+a\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}=0,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 10.00002ptF^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}=-\bar{a}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}.

(5.3.12)

So for the transition matrix (cf. (
5.3.3
)) we have

\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}dz\\
F^{*}d\bar{z}\\
F^{*}ds\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&-\bar{a}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\\
-a\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}\\
&&F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}dw\\
d\bar{w}\\
d\theta\end{pmatrix}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&-a\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
-\bar{a}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}\partial_{z}\\
F^{*}\partial_{\bar{z}}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.3.13)

Applying the Cramer’s rule of inverse matrices to the right hand matrix in (
5.3.13
), we have

F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}=\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}}{(1-|a|^{2})|F^{\prime\prime}_{w}|^{2}}(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})=\frac{\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}}}{(1-|a|^{2})F^{\prime\prime}_{w}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in}\hskip 10.00002ptU_{1}\times V_{1}.

(5.3.14)

Since
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C}^{3})
and
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C})
, we see that
F^{\prime\prime}_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C})
and
F^{\prime\prime}_{w}\not\equiv 0
in
U_{1}
.

Take
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subseteq U_{1}\times V_{1}
as a neighborhood of
(0,0)
such that
\sup_{(w,\theta)\in U_{2}\times V_{2}}|\frac{F^{\prime\prime}_{w}(w,\theta)}{F^{\prime\prime}_{w}(0,\theta)}-1|<1
.
We define

f(w,\theta):=\log\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}(w,\theta)}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}(0,\theta)}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\Big(\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}(w,\theta)}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}(0,\theta)}-1\Big)^{n}.

(5.3.15)

Since
\big[(w,\theta)\mapsto\frac{F^{\prime\prime}_{w}(w,\theta)}{F^{\prime\prime}_{w}(0,\theta)}\big]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
, we see that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
as well. So (
5.3.11
) follows:

f_{w}+af_{\bar{w}}=\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{w\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}}+\frac{a\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}}=\frac{(\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{w}+a\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}})_{\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}}-\frac{a_{\bar{w}}\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}}=-a_{\bar{w}}.

(5.3.16)

(B)

\Rightarrow

(A)
: This is the reverse of the above argument. By shrinking
U_{2}\times V_{2}
we assume
U_{2}
and
V_{2}
are both convex neighborhoods.

Suppose
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
is a solution to (
5.3.11
). Take
\mu:=e^{f}
, we know
\mu\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
satisfies

(a\mu)_{\bar{w}}=a_{\bar{w}}e^{f}+af_{\bar{w}}e^{f}=-f_{w}e^{f}=-\mu_{w},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in}\hskip 10.00002ptU_{2}\times V_{2}.

(5.3.17)

Therefore
\mu(\cdot,\theta)(d\bar{w}-a(\cdot,\theta)dw)
is a closed 1-form in
U_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}
, for each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. We define
F^{\prime\prime}:U_{2}\times V_{2}\to\mathbb{C}
as

F^{\prime\prime}(w,\theta):=\int_{0}^{1}(w\cdot\bar{\mu}(tw,\theta)+\bar{w}\cdot\bar{a}(tw,\theta)\bar{\mu}(tw,\theta))dt.

(5.3.18)

We see that
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}(U_{1},V_{1};\mathbb{C})
and satisfies
d\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}=\mu d\bar{w}-a\mu dw+\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}d\theta
. In other words

\nabla_{w}(F^{\prime\prime},\bar{F}^{\prime\prime})=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{w}\\
F^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\mu}&-a\mu\\
-\bar{a}\bar{\mu}&\mu\end{pmatrix}\hskip 10.00002pt\Rightarrow\hskip 10.00002pt\det\big(\nabla_{w}(F^{\prime\prime},\bar{F}^{\prime\prime})\big)=|\mu|^{2}(1-|a|^{2})>0.

Since
a,\mu\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
, we see that
F^{\prime\prime}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}
and the matrix function
\nabla_{w}(F^{\prime\prime},\bar{F}^{\prime\prime})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
is invertible at
(0,0)
. Applying Lemma
2.2.38

(iii)
to
(\operatorname{Re}F^{\prime\prime},\operatorname{Im}F^{\prime\prime})\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
, we can find a neighborhood
U_{1}\times V_{1}\subseteq U_{2}\times V_{2}
such that
F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta):U_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}
is injective for each
\theta\in V_{1}
and its inverse map
\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z,s)=F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,s)^{\mathrm{Inv}}(z)
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}_{z,s}
near
(F^{\prime\prime}(0,0),0)=(0,0)
.

Take
F(w,\theta):=(F^{\prime\prime}(w,\theta),\theta)
and
\Phi:=F^{\mathrm{Inv}}
, we see that
\Phi(z,s)=(\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z,s),s)
. Therefore
F:U_{1}\times V_{1}\to\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
is homeomorphic to its image (since
F,\Phi
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1,\beta}\subset C^{0}
), and
\nabla_{z}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})=(\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime\prime},0)
is a continuous map on
F(U_{1}\times V_{1})
. This completes the proof of
(A)(A.1)
.

Now for each
\theta\in V_{1}
,
F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta):U_{1}\to\mathbb{C}_{z}
is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-coordinate chart satisfying
d(\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta))=\mu(\cdot,\theta)d\bar{w}-a\mu(\cdot,\theta)dw
in
U_{1}
, so
(\partial_{w}+a\partial_{\bar{w}})F^{\prime\prime}=0
, which means
F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta)^{*}d\bar{z}=d\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta)
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times\{\theta\}}=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a(\cdot,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}})
. Taking a dual we get
\mathcal{S}|_{U_{1}\times\{\theta\}}=\operatorname{Span}F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta)^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
for each
\theta\in V_{1}
.

Now
F(w,\theta)=(F^{\prime\prime}(w,\theta),\theta)
and
\Phi(z,s)=(\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z,s),s)
, so
((\nabla_{z}\Phi)\circ F)(w,\theta)=((\nabla_{z}\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta))\circ F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta))(w)
hence
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|_{(w,\theta)}=F^{\prime\prime}(\cdot,\theta)^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}|_{w}
spans
\mathcal{S}_{(w,\theta)}
for each
w\in U_{1}
and
\theta\in V_{2}
, finishing the proof of
(A)(A.2)
.
∎

Remark 5.3.12
.

We do not know how to relax the assumption
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
to
\alpha>0
. The difficulty comes from (
5.3.16
) and (
5.3.17
) in the proof, where the intermediate calculations need a product of a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
-function and a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-function. On the other hand, the PDE (
5.3.11
) can still be defined when
0<\alpha\leq\frac{1}{2}
, by requiring
a
has some extra Sobolev regularity, for example we can assume
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w},\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};\mathbb{C})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w};\mathbb{C}))
(see Proposition
5.3.16

(i)
).

For Step 2, the construction of
a(w,\theta)
requires the Sobolev-valued Hölder space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{s}(V;W^{k,p}_{x}(U))
. Recall its definition in Definition
1.2.1
.

Lemma 5.3.13
.

Let
\beta>0
and
1<p<\infty
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a bounded smooth domain.

(i)

The product map
[(f,g)\mapsto fg]:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q};L^{p}(U))\times L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(U,\mathbb{R}^{q})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q};L^{p}(U))
is bounded bilinear.

(ii)

When
\beta<1
, then the product map
[(f,g)\mapsto fg]:(L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2})(U)\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U)\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(U)
is bounded bilinear.

Proof.

(i)
can follow from the boundedness of the product map
L^{p}(U)\times L^{\infty}(U)\to L^{p}(U)
and that
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{q})
is closed under multiplications. Alternatively one can apply Lemma
2.2.11
by taking
\Pi:L^{p}(U)\times L^{\infty}(U)\to L^{p}(U)
be the natural product map. We leave the details to the readers.

The proof of
(ii)
follows from
[
BCD11
, Chapter 2.8.1]
, where in the reference we use Bony’s decomposition
uv=T(u,v)+T(v,u)+R(u,v)
for distributions
u,v
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see
[
BCD11
, (2.41)]
and Remark
2.1.15
). By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.82]
(or Lemma
2.1.16
) we have
T:L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
T:\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. By
[
BCD11
, Theorem 2.85]
we have
R:W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{B}_{22}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Here
\mathscr{B}_{22}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})=H^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is the fractional
L^{2}
-Sobolev space (see for example
[
BCD11
, Definition 2.68]
or
[
Tri10
, Definition 2.3.1/2(i)]
).
We have Sobolev embedding
\mathscr{B}_{22}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
, whose proof can be found in for example
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.7.1]
5
5

5

We recall the correspondence of Besov space
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathscr{B}_{\infty\infty}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
and Triebel-Lizorkin space
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{n})=\mathscr{F}_{p2}^{0}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
from
[
Tri10
, Theorems 2.5.7 and 2.5.12]
.
. Thus
R:W^{1,2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is bounded.

Therefore
[(u,v)\mapsto uv]:(L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2})(\mathbb{R}^{2})\times\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is bilinearly bounded. Taking restrictions to
U
we complete the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.3.14
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}
be the zero Dirichlet boundary solution operators in Lemmas
2.1.41
and
2.2.35
with
U=\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w}\subset\mathbb{C}^{1}
and
V=\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
in the lemma. Define
\mathcal{T}_{0}:=4\partial_{\bar{w}}\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{T}:=4\partial_{\bar{w}}\mathcal{P}
. Then

(i)

\partial_{w}\mathcal{T}f=f
for all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{T}
has the following boundedness: for
\alpha>0
,
1<p<\infty
and
\mathscr{X}\in\{L^{\infty},\mathscr{C}^{\gamma}:\gamma>0\}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\text{ and }\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2}))\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2})).

(5.3.19)

(ii)

Moreover, if
f\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
satisfies
f|_{\{|w|>\frac{1}{2}\}}\equiv 0
, then

f-\mathcal{T}\partial_{w}f\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}).

(5.3.20)

Proof.

That
\partial_{w}\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{id}
and
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{w}\mathscr{X}_{\theta}\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{w}\mathscr{X}_{\theta}
are the immediate consequences to Lemma
2.2.35
since
\Delta_{\operatorname{Re}w,\operatorname{Im}w}=4\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial w\partial\bar{w}}
.

We have boundedness
\mathcal{P}_{0}:L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2})\to W^{2,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2})
by
[
GT01
, Theorem 9.13]
, thus
\mathcal{T}_{0}:L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
. We get
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(L^{p})\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(W^{1,p})
by Lemma
2.1.3
. Thus we prove
(i)
.

To prove
(ii)
, we need to show that
\operatorname{id}-\mathcal{T}\partial_{w}:L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{M}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is bounded for all
M>0
.

Let
g\in L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
with zero extension outside
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}
. By formula of Green functions (see for example
[
Eva10
, Theorem 2.12 and Section 2.2.c]
) we have for
w\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
,

g(w)-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}g(w)=g(w)-\int_{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}}4g(z)\partial^{2}_{z\bar{w}}\big(\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)-\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(|z|w-\tfrac{z}{|z|}\big)\big)dxdy=\int_{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}}4g(z)\partial^{2}_{z\bar{w}}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(|z|w-\tfrac{z}{|z|}\big)dxdy.

Here
z=x+iy\in\mathbb{B}^{2}
and
\mathbf{\Gamma}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log|z|
(which is the
n=2
case in (
2.4.1
)). The first equality follows from integration by part, the second equality follows from the fact that
\partial_{z\bar{w}}\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)=\partial_{z\bar{z}}\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)=\frac{1}{4}\Delta_{x,y}\mathbf{\Gamma}(z-w)=\frac{\delta_{w}(z)}{4}
.

Clearly
(z,w)\mapsto\mathbf{\Gamma}(|z|w-z/|z|)
is a smooth function on
(z,w)\in r\mathbb{B}^{2}\times\mathbb{B}^{2}
for any
r<1
. Thus for any integer
M\geq 0

\|\nabla^{M}(g-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}g)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}^{2^{k}})}\leq\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})}\sup_{|w|<\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\mathbb{B}^{2}}\big|\nabla^{M+2}_{z,w}\mathbf{\Gamma}\big(|z|w-\tfrac{z}{|z|}\big)\big|dxdy\lesssim_{M}\|g\|_{L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})}.

We conclude that
\operatorname{id}-\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}:L^{\infty}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{M}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
is bounded for all
M>0
. By Lemma
2.1.3
, we get the boundedness
\operatorname{id}-\mathcal{T}\partial_{w}:L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{M}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 5.3.15
.

Let
\beta>0
and let
(c_{k})_{k=1}^{\infty}\subset\mathbb{C}
be a sequence.
Define
g(\theta):=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}c_{k}e^{2\pi i\cdot 2^{k}\theta}2^{-\beta\theta}
, then

(i)

\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R};\mathbb{C})}\approx\sup_{k\geq 0}|c_{k}|
with the implied constant depending only on
\beta
.

(ii)

If
\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}|c_{k}|=\infty
, then
g\notin\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(I;\mathbb{C})
for every interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

Let
\{\psi_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}
. Recall from (
2.1.2
)
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{k}\subset(-2^{k-1},-2^{k+1})\cup(2^{k-1},2^{k+1})
holds for
k\geq 1
. Since the Fourier support of
e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}
is
\{2^{k}\}
, we have
\psi_{k}\ast g(\theta)=c_{k}2^{-\beta\theta}e^{2\pi i\cdot 2^{k}\theta}
for all
k\geq 1
. Therefore by Lemma
2.1.4

(i)
, we get the result
(i)
:

\textstyle\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}\approx_{\psi,\beta}\sup_{k\geq 0}2^{k\beta}\|\psi_{k}\ast g\|_{L^{\infty}}=\sup_{k\geq 1}|c_{k}|.

Now suppose
\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}|c_{k}|=+\infty
, so
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}\approx_{\psi,\beta}=\sup|c_{k}|=\infty
.

For a given interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
,
I
contains a dyadic subinterval, say
[n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}}]
where
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Take
g^{k_{0}}(\theta):=\sum_{k=k_{0}+1}^{\infty}c_{k}2^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}2^{-k\beta}
, so

\textstyle g(\theta)-g^{k_{0}}(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}c_{k}e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}2^{-\beta\theta},

must have finite
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})
-norm (though whose bound depends on
k_{0}
).

Since
g^{k_{0}}
is
2^{-k_{0}}
-periodic,
\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}=\infty
and
\|g-g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
, we know
\|g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}})}=\infty
. Therefore, we complete the proof of
(ii)
by:

\textstyle\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(I)}\geq\|g\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}([n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}}])}\geq\|g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}([n2^{-k_{0}},(n+1)2^{-k_{0}}])}-\|g-g^{k_{0}}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R})}=\infty.\qed

We now construct our
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
-function
a(w,\theta)
and finish the Step 2:

Proposition 5.3.16
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
. There is a function
A(w,\theta)
defined in
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
, that satisfies the following properties:

(i)

A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
and
[(w,\theta)\mapsto w\partial_{\bar{w}}A(w,\theta)]\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
.

(ii)

[(w,\theta)\mapsto w^{-1}A(w,\theta)]\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
for all
1<p<\frac{4}{2-\min(\alpha,2)}
. In particular
w^{-1}A\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
.

(iii)

\mathcal{T}[\partial_{\bar{w}}A]\notin L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
near
(w,\theta)=(0,0)
, where
\mathcal{T}
be the inverse
\partial_{w}
-operator in Lemma
5.3.14
.

The construction is essentially the parameter version of Lemma
5.3.6
.

Remark 5.3.17
.

By taking some simple modifications from the construction below (see (
5.3.22
)), one can also prove Proposition
5.3.16
for the case
\alpha=\infty
,
\beta<\infty
. We leave the details to readers.

Proof of Proposition
5.3.16
.

First we find a function
b\in C_{c}^{0}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
such that,

(b.1)

b\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
,
b\in W^{1,2}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
and
wb\in C_{c}^{1}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})
.

(b.2)

\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b\notin L^{\infty}
near
w=0
. Here
\mathcal{T}_{0}
is the inverse
\partial_{w}
-operator in Lemma
5.3.14
.

Indeed, we take a function
b
such that

b(w):=\partial_{w}\big(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{3}}\big)=-\tfrac{1}{6}(-\log|w|)^{-\frac{2}{3}}\tfrac{|w|^{2}}{w^{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{when }|w|<\tfrac{1}{2}.

(5.3.21)

And
b
is smoothly cutoffed outside
|w|<\frac{1}{2}
.

Immediately we get
b\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash\{0\};\mathbb{C})
and
wb(w)\in C^{1}_{c}
. And clearly we have
|\nabla b(w)|\lesssim|w|^{-1}(-\log|w|)^{-\frac{2}{3}}
, so
b\in W^{1,2}
, which gives
(b.1)
.

To prove
(b.2)
, since
\partial_{w}\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b=\partial_{\bar{w}}b
it suffices to show that if
U
is a neighborhood of
0
and
f:U\subseteq\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\to\mathbb{C}
is a function satisfies
\partial_{w}f=\partial_{\bar{w}}b
, then
f\notin L^{\infty}
near
0
.

Indeed by (
5.3.21
) in a neighborhood of
0
we have
f_{w}=(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{2}})_{w\bar{w}}
, so
f-(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{2}})_{\bar{w}}
is annihilated by
\partial_{w}
which is an anti-holmorphic function. Therefore
f-(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{2}})_{\bar{w}}
is a bounded function near
w=0
. On the other hand

(\bar{w}(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{3}})_{\bar{w}}=(-\log|w|)^{\frac{1}{3}}+O(1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{as }w\to 0,

is not bounded near
0
. We conclude that
f\notin L^{\infty}
near
0
.

Let
\rho_{0}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w})
be a Schwartz function whose Fourier transform satisfies
\hat{\rho}_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2})
and
\hat{\rho}_{0}|_{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}}\equiv 1
. Define functions
(\rho_{k},c_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
and
A(w,\theta)
as

\rho_{k}(w):=2^{n\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}\rho_{0}(2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}w),\hskip 10.00002ptc_{k}(w):=\rho_{k}\ast b(w)-\rho_{k}\ast b(0),\hskip 10.00002ptk\geq 0;\hskip 10.00002ptA(w,\theta):=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}c_{j}(w)e^{2\pi i2^{j}\theta}2^{-j\beta}.

(5.3.22)

Thus, we have
wc_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{C}}(w-z)\rho_{k}(w-z)b(z)d\operatorname{Vol}(z)=(w\rho_{k})\ast b
for
k\geq 0
.
By scaling

\|\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=\|\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}\|\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}},\hskip 10.00002pt\|w\nabla\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}=\|w\nabla\rho_{0}\|_{L^{1}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for each }k\geq 1.

(5.3.23)

Therefore,

\begin{gathered}\|c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\leq 2\|\rho_{k}\ast b\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\lesssim\|\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|b\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\lesssim 1;\\
\|w\partial_{\bar{w}}c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}}=\|\partial_{\bar{w}}(w\rho_{k})\ast b\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\|w\partial_{\bar{w}}\rho_{k}\|_{L^{1}}\|b\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim 1.\end{gathered}

(5.3.24)

Let
(\phi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
and
(\psi_{k})_{k=0}^{\infty}
be two dyadic resolutions for
\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}
and
\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
respectively (recall Definition
2.1.2
). Recall from (
2.1.2
)
\hat{\phi}_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(2\mathbb{B}^{2})
,
\hat{\psi}_{0}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(2\mathbb{B}^{1})
,
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\phi}_{j}\subset 2^{j+1}\mathbb{B}^{2}\backslash 2^{j-1}\mathbb{B}^{2}
and
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\psi}_{j}\subset 2^{j+1}\mathbb{B}^{1}\backslash 2^{j-1}\mathbb{B}^{1}
for
j\geq 1
. Since
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\rho}_{0}\subset\mathbb{B}^{2}
, by scaling we have
\operatorname{supp}\hat{\rho}_{k}\subset 2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}\mathbb{B}^{2}
. Thus,

\phi_{j}\ast_{w}A(w,\theta)=\sum_{k\geq\max(0,\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(j-1))}(\phi_{j}\ast c_{k})(w)e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}2^{-k\beta},\hskip 10.00002pt\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}A(w,\theta)=c_{j}(w)e^{2\pi i2^{j}\theta}2^{-j\beta},\hskip 10.00002ptj\geq 0.

(5.3.25)

By (
5.3.24
) and (
5.3.25
) we get
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}\cap L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
, since for
j\geq 0
,

\begin{gathered}\|\phi_{j}\ast_{w}A\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}\leq\sum_{k\geq\max(0,\frac{\alpha}{\beta}(j-1))}\|\phi_{j}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{C}^{1})}\|c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}2^{-k\beta}\lesssim_{\phi,c,\alpha,\beta}2^{-j\alpha};\\
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}A(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}\leq\|\psi_{j}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\|c_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}2^{-j\beta}\lesssim_{\psi,c,\beta}2^{-j\beta}.\end{gathered}

Taking supremum over
j\geq 0
we get
(i)
.

Using (
5.3.23
) and (
5.3.24
) we also have
\|c_{k}\|_{C^{1}}\lesssim 2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k}
and
\|w\nabla c_{k}\|_{C^{0}}\lesssim 1
. Thus

|\nabla c_{k}(w)|\lesssim\min(|w|^{-1},2^{\frac{\beta}{\alpha}k})\leq|w|^{\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)-1}2^{k\frac{\beta}{2}\cdot\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)}\leq|w|^{\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)-1}2^{k\frac{\beta}{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for all}\hskip 10.00002ptk\geq 0,\hskip 10.00002ptw\in\mathbb{C}^{1}.

By (
5.3.22
) we have
c_{k}(0,\theta)\equiv 0
for all
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus by (
5.3.25
),

|\psi_{k}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}A)(w,\theta)|=|w^{-1}c_{k}(w)|2^{-k\beta}\leq|\nabla c_{k}(w)|2^{-k\beta}\leq|w|^{\min(\frac{\alpha}{2},1)-1}2^{-k\frac{\beta}{2}},\hskip 10.00002ptk\geq 0,\hskip 10.00002ptw\in\mathbb{C}^{2},\hskip 10.00002pt\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}.

On the other hand
|w|^{-\gamma}\in L^{\frac{2}{\gamma}-}(\mathbb{B}^{2})
holds for all
0<\gamma<2
. Thus for
1<p<\frac{2}{1-\min(\alpha/2,1)}=\frac{4}{2-\min(\alpha,2)}
we have
\sup_{k\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{k\frac{\beta}{2}}\|\psi_{k}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}A)(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}<\infty
.

Clearly
2<\frac{\alpha+4}{2}<\frac{4}{2-\min(\alpha,2)}
, we finish the proof of
(ii)
.

To prove
(iii)
, since
\mathcal{T}
only acts on
w
-variable, by (
5.3.22
) we have

\mathcal{T}A_{\bar{w}}(w,\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k\alpha}e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}c_{k}(w)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}2^{-k\alpha}e^{2\pi i2^{k}\theta}\mathcal{T}_{0}(\rho_{k}\ast\partial_{\bar{w}}b)(w),\hskip 10.00002ptw\in\mathbb{C}^{1},\ \theta\in\mathbb{R}.

Now
\lim\limits_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k}\ast(\partial_{\bar{w}}b)=\partial_{\bar{w}}b
holds (as
L^{2}
-functions), and by
(b.2)

\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b\notin L^{\infty}
near
w=0
. So for any neighborhood
U\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}
of
w=0
, we have
\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}\|\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{w}c_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{C})}=+\infty
. Therefore by Lemma
5.3.15

(ii)
, for any interval
I\subseteq\mathbb{R}
containing
\theta=0
, we have
\|\mathcal{T}\partial_{\bar{w}}A\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U,I;\mathbb{C})}\gtrsim\varlimsup_{k\to\infty}\|\mathcal{T}_{0}\partial_{\bar{w}}b\|_{L^{\infty}(U;\mathbb{C})}=+\infty
.

So
\mathcal{T}\partial_{\bar{w}}A\notin L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
near
(0,0)
, finishing the proof of
(iii)
.
∎

Now we prove Step 3, which is the non-existence of
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}
to (
5.3.11
). Cf. Proposition
5.3.9
. Here we consider all
\alpha,\beta>0
.

Theorem 5.3.18

(
\bar{\partial}
-equation for sharpness of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
)
.

Let
\alpha,\beta>0
, let
A
be as in Proposition
5.3.16
. Then there is a constant
\delta=\delta(\alpha,\beta,A)>0
, such that for every neighborhood
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
of
(0,0)
there is no
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
solving (
5.3.11
) with
a(w,\theta):=\delta\cdot A(w,\theta)
.

This result contains the largest possible range for
\alpha
and
\beta
. The proof can be simplified if
\alpha>1
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\to\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
be the inverse
\partial_{w}
-operator as in Lemma
5.3.14
. Recall that
\mathcal{T}:\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))\to\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
is also bounded for
1<p<\infty
.

We define a linear operator
\mathfrak{T}=\mathfrak{T}_{A}
on functions in
\mathbb{B}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{1}
as

\mathfrak{T}[\varphi]:=\mathcal{T}(A\cdot\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi),\hskip 10.00002pt\varphi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}).

(5.3.26)

By Lemma
2.1.17

(i)
, Lemma
5.3.13

(i)
and
(ii)
,

\displaystyle\|A\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}}\|\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})},

\displaystyle\text{provided }\alpha>\tfrac{1}{2};

\displaystyle\|A\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\alpha}\|A\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2})}\|\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})},

\displaystyle\text{provided }0<\alpha<1;

\displaystyle\|A\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta}\|A\|_{L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}}\|\partial_{\bar{w}}\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))}\lesssim_{A,p}\|\varphi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,p}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))},

\displaystyle\text{for all }1<p<\infty.

Taking
p=p_{\alpha}:=4+16/\alpha
, we conclude that
\mathfrak{T}
is a bounded endomorphism on both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
.

We now take
\delta=\delta(\alpha,\beta,A)>0
small such that

\delta\big(\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})}+\|\mathfrak{T}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+16/\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))}\big)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

(5.3.27)

Note that
\mathfrak{T}
is now contraction map in these two spaces.

We claim that
a(w,\theta)=\delta A(w,\theta)
is as desired.

Suppose by contradiction there is a
U_{2}\times V_{2}\ni(0,0)
and a
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
solving (
5.3.11
). Take
0<\varepsilon_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
such that
\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\subseteq U_{2}
and
(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})\subseteq V_{2}
. We define
\tilde{\chi}
and
\chi
such that

\tilde{\chi}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0}),\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{\chi}|_{(-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0},\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{0})}\equiv 1,\hskip 10.00002pt\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\varepsilon_{0}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{0})),\hskip 10.00002pt\chi(w,\theta):=\tilde{\chi}(|w|)\tilde{\chi}(\theta).

(5.3.28)

Therefore
\chi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(U_{2})
equals to 1 in the neighborhood
\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2})
of
(0,0)
, and
\nabla_{w}\chi
vanishes in the neighborhood
\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
of
\{w=0\}=\{0\}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
.

Define
g,h\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{c}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
as
g:=\chi f
and
h(w,\theta):=wg(w,\theta)
, so

g_{w}+a\cdot g_{\bar{w}}=(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi a_{\bar{w}},\hskip 10.00002pth_{w}+a\cdot h_{\bar{w}}=g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{in }\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}.

(5.3.29)

Claim 1:

h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
and therefore
h_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
.

Rewriting the second equation of (
5.3.29
) we have

h=-\mathcal{T}(ah_{\bar{w}})+(h-\mathcal{T}h_{w})+\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}}).

In other words
h=\varphi
is a solution to the following affine linear equation:

\varphi=-\delta\mathfrak{T}[\varphi]+(h-\mathcal{T}h_{w})+\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}}).

(5.3.30)

By assumption
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
,
f\nabla_{w}\chi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}^{2})
. By Proposition
5.3.16

(i)

wa_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. Therefore by (
5.3.19
) (using
L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}(L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}_{w})
) we get

\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}})\in\mathcal{T}(\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C}))\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})).

(5.3.31)

By assumption
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
is supported in
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
, so by (
5.3.20
)
h-\mathcal{T}h_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. In particular

h-\mathcal{T}h_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C})).

(5.3.32)

Using (
5.3.31
), (
5.3.32
) and the assumption (
5.3.27
),
\big[\varphi\mapsto-\delta\mathfrak{T}[\varphi]+(h-\mathcal{T}h_{w})+\mathcal{T}(g+w(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f-\chi wa_{\bar{w}})\big]
is a contraction map in both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
and
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\cap\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
. Since (
5.3.30
) has a unique fixed point in both spaces, and
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}L^{\infty}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
is a fixed point to (
5.3.30
), we conclude that
h\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};W^{1,4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
. Thus
h_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}(\mathbb{B}^{2};\mathbb{C}))
, obtaining the Claim 1.

Claim 2:

a\cdot g_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-1}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
for some
\varepsilon>0
, and therefore
\mathcal{T}(a\cdot g_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
.

Let
(\psi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be a dyadic resolution for
\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
. By Lemma
2.1.13
, for
l\geq 0
, on
\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\psi_{l}\ast_{\theta}(ag_{\bar{w}})

\displaystyle=\psi_{l}\ast_{\theta}\Big(\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3;|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\Big)(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a)\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}})=:P_{l}^{1}+P_{l}^{2}+P_{l}^{3}.

Our goal is to show that for
\varepsilon=\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}>0
,
\sup_{l\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{l\beta}\|P_{l}^{\nu}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\varepsilon-1}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}<\infty
for
\nu=1,2,3
.

By assumption
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{R}^{1};L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C}))
and
g_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}L^{\infty}\cap\mathscr{C}^{-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\beta}{2}}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
, so we have
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\lesssim 2^{-j\beta}
and
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\lesssim 2^{-j^{\prime}\frac{\beta}{2}}
. Thus by Lemma
5.3.13

(ii)
, we have for each
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
,

\displaystyle\|P^{1}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\lesssim_{a,g}\sum_{j=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j^{\prime}=0}^{j-3}2^{-j\beta}2^{-j^{\prime}\frac{\beta}{2}}\lesssim 2^{-l\beta};

\displaystyle\|P^{3}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\infty}\cap W^{1,2}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}\lesssim_{a,g}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j,j^{\prime}\geq l-3\\
|j-j^{\prime}|\leq 2\end{subarray}}2^{-j\beta}2^{-j^{\prime}\frac{\beta}{2}}\lesssim 2^{-l\frac{3}{2}\beta}.

Taking supremum over
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}
we get
\sup_{l\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{l\beta}(\|P^{1}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}}+\|P^{3}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}})<\infty
, bounding the first and the third term.

For the second sum we use
(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}a)\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}g_{\bar{w}})=(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a))\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}wg_{\bar{w}})=(\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a))\cdot(\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}h_{\bar{w}})
. By Proposition
5.3.16

(ii)

\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a)(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}}\lesssim 2^{-j\frac{\beta}{2}}
, by Claim 1
\sup_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}h_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}}\lesssim 2^{-j^{\prime}\beta}
. And since the product map
L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}\times L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}\to L^{\frac{4(\alpha+4)}{\alpha+8}}
is bounded, we have

\displaystyle\|P^{2}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\frac{4(\alpha+4)}{\alpha+8}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\psi_{l}\|_{L^{1}}\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}\|\psi_{j}\ast_{\theta}(w^{-1}a)(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{\frac{\alpha+4}{2}}}\|\psi_{j^{\prime}}\ast_{\theta}h_{\bar{w}}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{L^{4+\frac{16}{\alpha}}}\lesssim\sum_{j^{\prime}=l-2}^{l+2}\sum_{j=0}^{j^{\prime}-3}2^{-j\frac{\beta}{2}}2^{-j^{\prime}\beta}\lesssim 2^{-l\beta}.

By Sobolev embedding
L^{\frac{4(\alpha+4)}{\alpha+8}}(\mathbb{C}^{1})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}-1}(\mathbb{C}^{1})
(also see
[
Tri10
, Chapter 2.7.1]
), we conclude that
\sup_{l\geq 0;\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{1}}2^{l\beta}\|P^{2}_{l}(\cdot,\theta)\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}-1}(\mathbb{C}^{1};\mathbb{C})}<\infty
.

Since
\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}<\frac{\alpha}{2}
, we conclude that
ag_{\bar{w}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}-1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. By Lemma
5.3.14

(i)
we get
\mathcal{T}(a\cdot g_{\bar{w}})\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{\alpha}{8+2\alpha}}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
, finishing the proof of Claim 2.

Final Step:

We use
\mathcal{T}(a\cdot g_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
to obtain a contradiction.

Now rewriting the first equation in (
5.3.29
) we have

g=-\mathcal{T}(ag_{\bar{w}})+(g-\mathcal{T}g_{w})+\mathcal{T}((\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f)-\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})-\mathcal{T}(a_{\bar{w}}).

(5.3.33)

By Claim 2, we have
\mathcal{T}(ag_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. By assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{w,\theta}
, we have
(\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
, so
\mathcal{T}((\chi_{w}+a\chi_{\bar{w}})f)\in\mathscr{C}^{1}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})\subset L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well.

Since
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}_{w,\theta}\subset L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
is supported in
\{|w|<\frac{1}{2}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
, by (
5.3.20
)
g-\mathcal{T}g_{w}\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well.

Let
\mu(\theta):=\tilde{\chi}(2\theta)
, we have
\mu|_{(-\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{0},\frac{1}{4}\varepsilon_{0})}\equiv 1
. Thus by (
5.3.28
), for every
(w,\theta)\in\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, using
\mu(\chi-1)|_{\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}\mathbb{B}^{2}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})}=0
we have

\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})(w,\theta)=\mu(\theta)\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})(w,\theta)=\mathcal{T}(\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})(w,\theta)=\big(\mathcal{T}(\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})-\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}}\big)(w,\theta).

By assumption
\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}}
is supported in
\{\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{2}<|w|<\varepsilon_{0}\}\times(-\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4},\frac{\varepsilon_{0}}{4})
, so by Proposition
5.3.16

(i)
we have
\mu(\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}}\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
. Using Lemma
5.3.14

(ii)
we get
\mathcal{T}((\chi-1)a_{\bar{w}})\in L^{\infty}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}(\mathbb{B}^{2},\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
as well.

However, by Proposition
5.3.16

(iii)
we have
\mathcal{T}(a_{\bar{w}})\notin L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
near
(0,0)
. Therefore near
(w,\theta)=(0,0)
, the left hand side of (
5.3.33
) is in
L^{\infty}_{w}\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\theta}
while the right hand side is not, this contradicts to the assumption
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
and finishes the proof.
∎

To summarize this subsection, what we obtained is the following:

Theorem 5.3.19
.

Let
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}
and
\beta>0
, let
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w},\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta};\mathbb{C})
be as in Theorem
5.3.18
(see (
5.3.22
)). Let
\mathcal{S}:=\operatorname{Span}(\partial_{w}+a(w,\theta)\partial_{\bar{w}})
be a rank 1 complex Frobenius structure in
\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
.

Then for any neighborhood
U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta}
of
(0,0)
there is no continuous map
F:U\times V\to\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
such that
F
is homeomorphic to its image,
\nabla_{w}F
and
\nabla_{z}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})
are both continuous, and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}
.

In particular we get Theorem
6
: if
\alpha=\beta>1
, then there is no
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
F:U\times V\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{s}
near
(0,0)
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U\times V}
.

Proof.

Suppose, by contrast, such continuous map
F
exists. Then by Proposition
5.3.11

(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
there are a neighborhood
U_{2}\times V_{2}\subseteq U\times V
of
(0,0)
and a function
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha,\beta}(U_{2},V_{2};\mathbb{C})
that solves the PDE
f_{w}+af_{\bar{w}}=-a_{\bar{w}}
. However by Theorem
5.3.18
such
f
would not exist, contradiction.

When
\alpha=\beta>1
, a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
z=F(w)
implies
\nabla_{w}F\in C^{0}
and
\nabla_{z}(F^{\mathrm{Inv}})\in C^{0}
automatically, so the assumptions of Proposition
5.3.11

(A)
are satisfied. By Proposition
5.3.11

(A)

\Rightarrow

(B)
and Theorem
5.3.18
the proof follows.
∎

We can extend Theorem
6
to general
r,q\geq 0
and
m\geq 1
following from a tensor construction that is similar to the proof of Theorem
7
in Section
5.3.2
.

Corollary 5.3.20
.

Let
r\geq 0
and
m,q\geq 1
be integers. Let
\alpha>1
. Then there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-complex Frobenius structure
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathbb{R}^{r+2m+q}
, such that
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
,
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r+2m
, and there does not exist a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart
F:U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r+2m+q}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{r+2m+q}
such that
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
spans
\mathcal{S}|_{U}
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{r+2m+q})
.

Remark 5.3.21
.

The result says that if
F
is a
C^{1}
-coordinate chart representing
\mathcal{S}
, then one of the vector field
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
cannot
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. It is not known to the author whether one of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
must be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-}\backslash\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{R}^{r+2m+q}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
as real smooth manifolds. Let
\tau=(\tau^{1},\dots,\tau^{r})
,
w=(w^{1},\dots,w^{m})
and
\theta=(\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{q})
be the standard coordinate systems for
\mathbb{R}^{r}
,
\mathbb{C}^{m}
and
\mathbb{R}^{q}
, respectively.

Let
a\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1};\mathbb{C})
be the function as in Theorem
5.3.18
(see (
5.3.22
), with
\beta=\alpha
). Let
Z(\tau,w,\theta):=\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{1}}+a(w^{1},\theta^{1})\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w}^{1}}
. We define
\mathcal{S}\leq\mathbb{C}T(\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q})
by

\textstyle\mathcal{S}:=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau^{1}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau^{r}},Z,\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{2}},\dots,\frac{\partial}{\partial w^{m}}).

Clearly
\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{S}=r+m
and
\operatorname{rank}(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})=r+2m
.

Now suppose
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{\theta}
is a neighborhood of
(0,0,0)
and
F=(F^{\prime},F^{\prime\prime},F^{\prime\prime\prime}):U\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{t}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{z}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{s}
is a
C^{1}
coordinate chart such that
F(0,0,0)=(0,0,0)
,
\mathcal{S}|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z})
and
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{R}^{r\times(r+2m+q)})
,
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U;\mathbb{C}^{m\times(r+2m+q)})
.
For convenience we define
A\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R}^{r}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{q};\mathbb{C}^{m\times m})
as
A(\tau,w,\theta)=\begin{pmatrix}a(w^{1},\theta^{1})&0_{1\times(m-1)}\\
0_{(m-1)\times 1}&0_{(m-1)\times(m-1)}\end{pmatrix}
. Since
\mathcal{S}^{\bot}|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(F^{*}d\bar{z},F^{*}ds)=\operatorname{Span}(d\bar{F}^{\prime\prime},dF^{\prime\prime\prime})
and
(\mathcal{S}+\bar{\mathcal{S}})|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau},\frac{\partial}{\partial w},\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{w})}|_{U}=\operatorname{Span}(dF^{\prime\prime},d\bar{F}^{\prime\prime},dF^{\prime\prime\prime})^{\bot}
, similar to (
5.3.12
), using matrix notation in Section
1.2
(also see (
4.1.8
)) we have
F^{\prime\prime}_{\tau}=0
and

\textstyle F^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}+A\cdot F^{\prime\prime}_{w}=0\in\mathbb{C}^{m\times m},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{i.e.}\hskip 10.00002pt\tfrac{\partial F^{\prime\prime j}}{\partial\bar{w}^{k}}+\sum_{l=1}^{m}A_{k}^{l}\tfrac{\partial F^{\prime\prime j}}{\partial w^{l}}=\tfrac{\partial F^{\prime\prime j}}{\partial\bar{w}^{k}}+\delta_{k}^{1}\cdot a\cdot\tfrac{\partial F^{\prime\prime j}}{\partial w^{1}}=0\text{ for all }1\leq j,k\leq m.

Therefore similar to (
5.3.13
) we have

\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}dt&F^{*}dz&F^{*}d\bar{z}&F^{*}ds\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d\tau&dw&d\bar{w}&d\theta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{\tau}\\
F^{\prime}_{w}&F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&-A\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
F^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}&-\bar{A}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
F^{\prime}_{\theta}&F^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\theta}&F^{\prime\prime\prime}_{\theta}\end{pmatrix}.

Taking transposition we get

\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\tau}\\
\partial_{w}\\
\partial_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{\tau}\\
F^{\prime}_{w}&F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&-A\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
F^{\prime}_{\bar{w}}&-\bar{A}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}F^{*}\partial_{t}\\
F^{*}\partial_{z}\\
F^{*}\partial_{\bar{z}}\end{pmatrix}.

(5.3.34)

By assumption
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{z}}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, so all matrix blocks in (
5.3.34
) are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
as well. Since
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}=(F^{\prime}_{\tau})^{-1}\cdot\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}
, we see that

\begin{pmatrix}F^{\prime}_{w}&F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&-A\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\\
-\bar{A}F^{\prime\prime}_{w}&\bar{F}^{\prime\prime}_{\bar{w}}\end{pmatrix}
is invertible at every point in
U
. Therefore there exists a
1\leq j_{0}\leq m
such that
F^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{w^{1}}(0,0)=\frac{\partial F^{\prime\prime j_{0}}}{\partial w^{1}}\neq 0
.

Let
\Gamma:\mathbb{C}^{1}_{w^{1}}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}_{\theta^{1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{r}_{\tau}\times\mathbb{C}^{m}_{w}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}_{\theta}
,
\Gamma(w^{1},\theta^{1}):=(0^{r},(w^{1},0^{m-1}),(\theta^{1},0^{q-1}))
be the natural embedding, and let
U_{1}:=\{(w^{1},\theta^{1}):\Gamma(w^{1},\theta^{1})\in U\text{ and }|F^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{w^{1}}(\Gamma(w^{1},\theta^{1}))-F^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{w^{1}}(\Gamma(0,\theta^{1}))|<\frac{1}{2}|F^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{w^{1}}(\Gamma(0,\theta^{1}))|\}\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}^{1}
. We define a function
f:U_{1}\to\mathbb{C}
by (cf. (
5.3.15
))

f(w^{1},\theta^{1}):=\log\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{\bar{w}^{1}}(\Gamma(w^{1},\theta^{1}))}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{\bar{w}^{1}}(\Gamma(0,\theta^{1}))}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{n}\bigg(\frac{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{\bar{w}^{1}}(\Gamma(w^{1},\theta^{1}))}{\bar{F}^{\prime\prime j_{0}}_{\bar{w}^{1}}(\Gamma(0,\theta^{1}))}-1\bigg)^{n}.

Since
F^{\prime\prime}_{w}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
we see that
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(U_{1};\mathbb{C})
. Since
Z\bar{F}^{\prime\prime j_{0}}=(\partial_{w^{1}}+a(w^{1},\theta^{1})\partial_{\bar{w}^{1}})\bar{F}^{\prime\prime j_{0}}=0
, using the same computation to (
5.3.16
) we see that
f
solves (
5.3.11
). Therefore by Proposition
5.3.11
and Theorem
5.3.18
, we reach a contradiction, i.e. such
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
does not exist.

To conclude, one of
F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial t^{r}},F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{1}},\dots,F^{*}\frac{\partial}{\partial z^{m}}
cannot be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, and we finish the proof.
∎

5.3.4
Canonical coordinate system lose 1 derivative

As mentioned in Section
4.2.5
, DeTurck and Kazdan showed that a Riemannian metric may not have optimal regularity with respect to
geodesic normal coordinates
[
DK81
, Example 2.3]
. A natural analog of geodesic normal coordinates for vector fields
are canonical coordinates (of the first kind).
Next, we show that vector fields may not have optimal regularity
with respect to these canonical coordinates.

Given
C^{1}
-vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
on
\mathfrak{M}
that form a basis on the tangent space at every point, recall from Definitions
2.3.30
and
2.3.32
that the canonical coordinates at
p\in\mathfrak{M}
is the map
\Phi_{p}(t^{1},\dots,t^{n}):=e^{t^{1}X_{1}+\dots+t^{n}X_{n}}p
defined via solving the ordinary differential equation, provided that it is solvable.
When
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha>1
, classical regularity theorems for ODEs (see Lemma
2.3.41
) show that
\Phi_{p}
is at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.
Therefore,
\Phi_{p}^{*}X_{1},\ldots,\Phi_{p}^{*}X_{n}
are at least
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
; which is one derivative less than the original
regularity of
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}
. The following result
shows that this loss of one derivative is sometimes inevitable.

Lemma 5.3.22
.

Endow
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with standard coordinate system
(x,y)
. Let
\alpha>1
and let
X:=\partial_{x}
and
Y:=xf(y)\partial_{x}+\partial_{y}
where
f(y):=\alpha\max(0,y)^{\alpha-1}
.

Then we can find a new
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
atlas
\mathfrak{A}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
which is compatible with the standard
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-structure on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, such that
X,Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
with respect to this the new atlas, but for the canonical coordinates
\Phi(t,s):=e^{tX+sY}(0)
we have
\Phi^{*}Y\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0)
, in particular the collection
(\Phi^{*}X,\Phi^{*}Y)
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\varepsilon}
.

Note that
X
and
Y
form a local basis of the tangent space at every point, and
f\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
.

Proof.

First we show the existence of the new atlas
\mathfrak{A}
with respect to which
X
and
Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
. In particular
X,Y
are
C^{1}
in
\mathfrak{A}
, so
\Phi
is well-defined.

Note that
[X,Y]=f(y)\partial_{x}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
. Specifically, the dual basis of
X,Y
are 1-forms
\lambda=dx-xf(y)dy,\eta=dy
which satisfy that
d\lambda=f(y)dx\wedge dy
and
d\eta=0
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}
2-forms, so the condition
(2)
in Corollary
4.2.13
is satisfied. By Corollary
4.2.13
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-atlas
\mathfrak{A}
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
X,Y
are both
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
on
\mathfrak{A}
.

Since
X,Y

are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subsetneq C^{1}
with respect to
\mathfrak{A}
, we see that
\Phi(t,s)
is well-defined near
(t,s)=(0,0)
. We can compute
\Phi
in terms of
f
.

Clearly
\Phi(t,s)=(*,s)
since
e^{s\partial_{y}}(x,y)=(x,y+s)
. We can write
\Phi(t,s)=(\phi(t,s),s)
. Define
\Phi(t,s;r)
for
r\in\mathbb{R}
as the solution to the ODE
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\Phi(t,s;r)=tX(\Phi(t,s;r))+sY(\Phi(t,s;r))
,
\Phi(t,s;0)=0
. So
\Phi(t,s)=\Phi(t,s;1)
and we have
\Phi(t,s;r)=(\phi(t,s;r),rs)
where

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\phi(t,s;r)=t+sf(rs)\phi(t,s;r),\hskip 10.00002pt\phi(t,s;0)=0,\hskip 10.00002ptt,s,r\in\mathbb{R}.

Solving this ODE we have

\phi(t,s;r)=e^{\int_{0}^{r}sf(\rho s)d\rho}\int_{0}^{r}e^{-\int_{0}^{\rho}sf(\mu s)d\mu}td\rho,\hskip 10.00002pt\phi(t,s)=\phi(t,s;1)=t\frac{e^{-\int_{0}^{s}f(\rho)d\rho}}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\int_{0}^{\rho}f(\mu)d\mu}d\rho.

Now plug in

f(y)=\begin{cases}\alpha y^{\alpha-1},&y\geq 0,\\
0&y\leq 0.\end{cases}

We have

\phi(t,s)=t\frac{e^{-s^{\alpha}}}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\rho^{\alpha}}d\rho\hskip 10.00002pt\text{when }s>0;\hskip 10.00002pt\phi(t,s)=t\hskip 10.00002pt\text{when }s\leq 0.

Thus,
\phi(t,s)=tg(s)
where

g(s)=\begin{cases}\frac{e^{-s^{\alpha}}}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\rho^{\alpha}}d\rho&s>0\\
1&s\leq 0.\end{cases}

(5.3.35)

We are going to show
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0)
. To see this, it suffices to show
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
and
g\notin\mathscr{C}_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
0
, for every
\varepsilon>0
.

By Taylor’s expansion on the exponential function we have, when
s>0
,

\displaystyle g(s)

\displaystyle=e^{-s^{\alpha}}\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{s}e^{\rho^{\alpha}}d\rho=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}s^{j\alpha}}{j!}\frac{1}{s}\int_{0}^{s}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\rho^{k\alpha}}{k!}d\rho=\sum_{j,k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j}}{j!k!}\frac{s^{j\alpha}s^{k\alpha}}{k\alpha+1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\Big)s^{l\alpha}=1-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}\big)s^{\alpha}+O(s^{2\alpha}).

In other words

g(s)=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\Big)\max(s,0)^{l\alpha}=1-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}\big)\max(s,0)^{\alpha}+\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\Big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\Big)\max(s,0)^{l\alpha},\hskip 10.00002pt\forall s\in\mathbb{R}.

Note that for
\beta>0
the function
\max(s,0)^{\beta}=\begin{cases}s^{\beta}&s\geq 0\\
0&s\leq 0\end{cases}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
but not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}
near
0
.
Indeed when
0<\beta<1
we know that
\max(s,0)^{\beta}\in C^{0,\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not
C^{0,\beta+\varepsilon}=\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}
near
0
for any
0<\varepsilon<1-\beta
, when
\beta=1
we know
\max(s,0)\in C^{0,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subsetneq\mathscr{C}^{1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not
C^{1}
near 0 so is not
\mathscr{C}^{1+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
. For
\beta>1
by passing to its derivatives we see that
\max(s,0)^{\beta}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not
\mathscr{C}^{\beta+\varepsilon}
near
0
.

So the remainder
\sum_{l=2}^{\infty}\big(\sum_{k=0}^{l}\frac{(-1)^{l-k}}{k\alpha+1}\big)\max(s,0)^{l\alpha}
is
\mathscr{C}^{2\alpha}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for all
0<\varepsilon\leq\alpha
, while the main term
1-\big(1-\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}\big)\max(s,0)^{\alpha}
is
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
but not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near 0. Therefore we conclude that
g\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
, but
g\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
s=0
.

Now we know
\Phi\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{2};\mathbb{R}^{2})
but
\Phi\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{loc}}
near
(t,s)=(0,0)
. Consider the inverse function of
\Phi
,
and set
(u(x,y),v(x,y)):=\Phi^{-1}(x,y)
; so that
\Phi^{*}Y=(Yu,Yv)\circ\Phi
. We have
v(x,y)=y
and
u(x,y)=\frac{1}{g(y)}x
. Note that
g(s)>0
for every
s
, so
y\mapsto\frac{1}{g(y)}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
y=0
, for any
\varepsilon>0
. Therefore
Yv=x\cdot\partial_{y}\frac{1}{g(y)}
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon-1}
near
(x,y)=(0,0)
. By composing with
\Phi
which is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\subset\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}
-diffeomorphism (for
0<\varepsilon<1
), we see that
\Phi^{*}Y
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon-1}
near
(t,s)=(0,0)
, for every
\varepsilon>0
. ∎

Remark 5.3.23
.

As a differentiable map
\Phi:(\mathbb{R}^{2}_{t,s},\mathrm{std})\to(\mathbb{R}^{2},\mathfrak{A})
between two
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifolds, we see that
\Phi
is not
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+\varepsilon}
near
(0,0)
for any
\varepsilon>0
. Otherwise since
X
and
Y
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}
on
\mathfrak{A}
, we have
\Phi^{*}Y\in\mathscr{C}^{\min(\alpha,\alpha-1+\varepsilon)}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\min(\varepsilon,1)}
near
(0,0)
, contradicting to Lemma
5.3.22
.

Remark 5.3.24
.

We can generalize Lemma
5.3.22
to higher dimension: on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we can take
X_{1}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}
,
X_{2}:=x_{1}\max(0,x_{2})^{\alpha-1}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{1}}+\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{2}}
and
X_{j}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{j}}
for
3\leq j\leq n
. Thus there exists a coordinate atlas
\mathfrak{A}
with respect to which
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
, while for the canonical coordinate map
\Phi
we still have
\Phi^{*}X_{2}\notin\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1+\varepsilon}
near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, for all
\varepsilon>0
. We leave the proof to reader.

5.4
A Quantitative Frobenius Theorem

5.4.1
A few remarks on quantitative estimates

To make the Frobenius theorem quantitative, instead of discussing the (regular) tangent subbundle
\mathcal{V}
, we consider a set of real generators
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
of
\mathcal{V}
, that also label the intrinsic scale of
\mathcal{V}
.
It is more practical to focus only on a leaf, unless there are extra intrinsic conditions for
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
that allow us to estimate the parameter dependence between different leaves. We refer
[
Str11
]
to readers for more discussion between qualitative and quantitative Frobenius theorems.

The involutive condition of
\mathcal{V}
shows that there are functions
c_{ij}^{k}
such that
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
(see Proposition
3.2.1

(I.8)
). Conversely the singular Frobenius theorem (say Theorem
2
) shows that if
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k=1}^{m}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
and
X_{i},c_{ij}^{k}
have certain smoothness assumptions, then we can find an (at least
C^{1}
) submanifold
\mathfrak{S}
containing a fixed point, such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
spans
T\mathfrak{S}
. In particular for a regular parameterization
\Phi:\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{r}\to\mathfrak{S}
where
r=\dim\mathfrak{S}
, we can talk about the pullback vector fields
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
on
\Omega
.

Now we can to find a better
\Phi
such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
are “normalized”. More precisely, we want
\Phi
satisfies the following:

•

\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
are “at the unit scale”:
\sup\limits_{1\leq i\leq m}\|\Phi^{*}X_{i}\|_{C^{0}}\approx 1
and
\sup\limits_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}\inf\limits_{\Omega}|\Phi^{*}X_{i_{1}}\wedge\dots\wedge\Phi^{*}X_{i_{r}}|\gtrsim 1
.

•

For a fixed
\alpha>0
, we have
\sup_{1\leq i\leq m}\|\Phi^{*}X_{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\approx 1
, provided that there exists a
\Phi
such that
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{r})
.

Here all the implied constant depend only on the diffeomorphic invariant quantities like the norms
\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}}
(see
[
Str21
, Section 5.1]
).

It is critical that the quantities should not depend on the choice of the original coordinate system.
Otherwise, if the vector fields are given in a coordinate system where the above upper and lower bounds
are bad, the estimates may be bad as well.

The first point is addressed in
[
Str11
, Section 3]
where we require
c_{ij}^{k}\in C^{2}_{X}
. The second point is discussed in
[
SS18
,
Str21
]
where
s
satisfies
s>2
, and in
[
SY22
]
we improve the assumption to
s>1
.
We also refer
[
Str20a
]
to the case where
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
can be real-analytic.

If
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are
C^{1}
, then Theorem
8
is a corollary to
[
SY22
, Theorem 9.1]
. The only thing we need to change in the thesis is to apply the log-Lipschitz version of singular Frobenius theorem (Theorem
2
) instead of the
C^{1}
-version (say
[
SS18
, Proposition 3.1]
).

Remark 5.4.1
.

Alternatively when
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
span the tangent spaces at every point, we can relax the log-Lipschitz assumption to merely
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
, where
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
make sense as distribution. Indeed in this case we do not need the apply singular Frobenius theorem since the total space
\mathfrak{M}
itself is the leaf we work on. And by Corollary
4.2.13
there is a new atlas
\mathfrak{A}
for which
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}\subset C^{1}
. For this case we leave the detail to readers.

Remark 5.4.2
.

There are two ingredients in
[
SY22
, Theorem 9.1]
which are not included in Theorem
8
:

•

Higher order function spaces along
X
: in Theorem
8
we only discuss the case where
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}
for
0<s<1
. There is a natural way to generalize the Banach space
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})
to
s\geq 1
, see
[
SS18
, Sections 2.2 and 8]
, also see
[
Str21
, Proposition 7.1]
.

•

Interior Schauder’s estimate: if
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X}
for some
\beta>\alpha
, then
\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{m}
are automatically
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
, with their
\mathscr{C}^{\beta}
-norm bounded by a constant that depends only on the previous quantities with the upper bound of
\sup_{i,j,k}\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\beta-1}_{X}}
. See
[
SY22
, Proposition 9.4 (ii)]
.

We do not include these two in the thesis, as they do not add substantial difficulty in the proof.

Remark 5.4.3
.

In Theorem
8
, by considering the coordinate chart
F=(F^{1},\dots,F^{n}):=\Phi^{\mathrm{Inv}}:\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n})\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
in Theorem
8
, we also have
F\in C^{2,\alpha-1}_{X}(\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n});\mathbb{R}^{n})
(or equivalently
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{X}(\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n});\mathbb{R}^{n})
). Indeed Theorem
8
gives
\|\Phi^{*}X\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim_{n,m,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0}}1
, thus
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{X}(\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n}))}\approx\|f\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\Phi^{*}X}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}\approx\|f\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n})}
. Using
\Phi^{*}X=(XF)\circ\Phi
, we have
\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{X}}=\|F\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{X}}+\|XF\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{X}}\approx\|F\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\|XF\circ\Phi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}
. The result
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}_{X}(\Phi(\mathbb{B}^{n});\mathbb{R}^{n})
then follows.

Theorem
8
, as well as
[
SY22
, Theorem 9.1]
, is still open for the case
0<\alpha\leq 1
. We remark the difficulty in the formulation of the problem and the proof techniques.

To formulate the correspondent result for
0<\alpha\leq 1
, if we still use the characterization
[X_{i},X_{j}]=\sum_{k}c_{ij}^{k}X_{k}
we need to explain the meaning of
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})
. In
[
SS18
,
Str21
]
we only define
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})
for
s>0
. If we know
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\beta}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for some
\beta>0
, in
[
SY22
]
we can define
\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X,\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
for
s>-\beta
, where we have not define the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}}
for
-\beta<s\leq 0
.

One way to define
\|\cdot\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}}
for negative
s
is to modify the corresponding negative Hölder norm in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. For example we can define
\|f\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s}_{X}}:=\inf\{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|g_{j}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{s+1}_{X}}:f=\sum_{j=1}^{m}X_{j}g_{j}\text{ as distributions}\}
for
-\beta<s\leq 0
.
But in this setting
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})
make sense only when we know
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\frac{1}{2}+}
under some good coordinate chart, since otherwise
\alpha-1\leq-\alpha
.

An alternative way to state the problem is to use Corollary
4.2.13

(2)
(also see
[
SY22
, Theorem 3.1 (b)]
), where we take
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
to be the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, we can use the quantities “
\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}}
” instead of the
\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}}
. In
[
SY22
, Lemma 4.8]
we can define “
\|d\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}}
” by the infimum of all
\|\eta^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{X}}
such that
d\lambda^{i}=d\eta^{i}
, which are well-defined quantities.

In this way we can formulate the problem as the following. For simplicity we only consider the case
m=n
, i.e.
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-vector fields that span the tangent space at every point in
\mathfrak{M}
.

Problem 5.4.4
.

Let
0<\alpha\leq 1
and
\mu_{0},M_{0}>0
, can we find constants
\hat{K}(n,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0})
and
K_{0}(n,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0})
such that the following holds?

Let
\mathfrak{M}
be a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-manifold and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
1-forms on
\mathfrak{M}
that form a basis on the tangent space at every point. Let
g
be the (unique)
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
Riemannian metric of
\mathfrak{M}
such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are orthonormal at every point, and let
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
1-forms
(\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n})
be the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
.

Suppose
(\mathfrak{M},g)
is a complete manifold and satisfies the following:

•

For every
\theta=(\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{n})\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and every
\gamma:[0,\mu_{0}]\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\dot{\gamma}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\theta^{j}X_{j}(\gamma(t))
for
0\leq t\leq\mu_{0}
, we have
\gamma(\mu_{0})\neq\gamma(0)
.

•

For the Hölder norm on
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{g}=\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M},g)
, there are
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
1-forms
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
such that
d\lambda^{i}=d\eta^{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq n
and

\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|\lambda^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M},g;T^{*}\mathfrak{M})}+\|\eta^{i}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathfrak{M},g;T^{*}\mathfrak{M})}<M_{0}.

(5.4.1)

Then for any
p_{0}\in\mathfrak{M}
there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-regular parameterization
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathfrak{M}
such that
\Phi(0)=p_{0}
and
\Phi^{*}X=[\Phi^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi^{*}X_{n}]^{\top}
has the form
\Phi^{*}X=\hat{K}\cdot(I+A)\nabla
, where
A(0)=0
and
\|A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}<K_{0}
.

Note that the qualitative version has done in
[
SY22
, Theorem 3.1]
, where the existence of such
\eta^{1},\dots,\eta^{n}
is done by
[
SY22
, Lemma 4.8]
.

In the proof of Theorem
8
we use the canonical coordinates
t\mapsto e^{t\cdot X}(p_{0})
to give the initial normalization. However when
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}_{\mathrm{loc}}\backslash\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+}_{\mathrm{loc}}
, we may not be able to talk about
e^{t\cdot X}
(unless
\alpha=1
) due to the non-uniqueness of Hölder ODE flows. To prove the quantitative result we must bypass the method of canonical coordinates. Solving harmonic coordinates on a suitable Carnot-Carathéodory ball may be a way.

5.4.2
Proof of Theorem
8

First by Theorem
2
, since
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
are log-Lipschitz and (
1.1.4
) is a stronger assumption than (
1.1.2
). We can find a
n
-dimensional
\mathscr{C}^{2-}
submanifold
\mathfrak{S}\subseteq\mathfrak{M}
containing
p_{0}
such that
X_{1},\dots,X_{m}
span the tangent space of
\mathfrak{S}
at every point. Thus we can replace
\mathfrak{M}
by
\mathfrak{S}
in the following discussion.

By assumption
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are linearly independent at every point in
\mathfrak{S}
, which form a local basis. Let
(\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{n})
be the dual basis of
(X_{1},\dots,X_{n})
, which are log-Lipschitz 1-forms in
\mathfrak{S}
. The assumption (
1.1.4
) implies that

d\theta^{k}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq n}c_{ij}^{k}\theta^{i}\wedge\theta^{j},\hskip 10.00002pt1\leq k\leq n.

(5.4.2)

By Lemma
2.3.39
and Remark
2.3.40
we see that
c_{ij}^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M})
, thus
d\theta^{k}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathfrak{M};\wedge^{2}T^{*}\mathfrak{M})
. By Corollary
4.2.13
we can find a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha+1}
-atlas
\mathfrak{A}
for
\mathfrak{M}
, which is compatible with the standard
\mathscr{C}^{2-}
structure of
\mathfrak{M}
, such that
\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{n}
are all
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
.
Therefore by passing to the atlas
\mathfrak{A}
, we can assume from now on that
\theta^{1},\dots,\theta^{n},X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
are all
C^{1}
.

Let
\mu_{1}=\mu_{1}(n,\mu_{0},M_{0}):=\frac{1}{10}\min(\mu_{0},\frac{1}{n\sqrt{n}M_{0}})
.
By Definition
2.3.34
and Corollary
2.3.38
, the canonical coordinate
\Phi_{0}:B^{n}(0,\mu_{1})\to\mathfrak{M}
,
\Phi_{0}(t):=e^{t\cdot X^{\prime}}(p_{0})
is a
C^{1}
regular parameterization. By Proposition
2.3.36

(ii)
and
(v)
, we can find a
K_{1}=K_{1}(n,m,\mu_{0},\alpha,M_{0})>0
such that
\Phi_{0}^{*}X^{\prime}=(I+A_{0}^{\prime})\nabla
where
\|A_{0}\|_{C^{0}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{1});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
\|A_{0}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{1});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq K_{1}
.

Write
\hat{\theta}^{i}:=\Phi^{*}\theta^{i}
and
\hat{c}_{ij}^{k}:=\Phi^{*}c_{ij}^{k}
for
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
. By Proposition
2.3.36

(iii)
we see that for
1\leq i,j,k\leq n
,
\|\hat{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{1}))}\leq 2\|\hat{c}_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{\Phi_{0}^{*}X^{\prime}}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{1}))}\leq 2\|c_{ij}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}_{X}(\mathfrak{M})}\leq M_{0}
. By Proposition
2.3.36

(v)
we get
\|A_{0}^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(B^{n}(0,\mu_{1});\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\lesssim_{n,\mu_{1},M_{0}}1
. By (
5.4.2
) we have
d\hat{\theta}^{k}=\sum_{i<j}\hat{c}_{ij}^{k}\hat{\theta}^{i}\wedge\hat{\theta}^{j}
for
1\leq k\leq n
. Therefore there is a
M_{1}=M_{1}(n,\mu_{1},\alpha,M_{0})>1
such that
\sum_{1\leq k\leq n}\|\hat{\theta}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}+\|d\hat{\theta}^{k}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}}\leq M_{1}
.

Now let
c=c(n,\alpha-1,\alpha)>0
be the constant in Theorem
4.2
. By Proposition
4.2.12
, there are a
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(n,\alpha-1,\alpha,\mu_{1},c,M_{1})\in(0,\mu_{0})
and 1-forms
\lambda^{1},\dots,\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha-1}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
that satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
4.2
and

\hat{\theta}^{i}|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{0}}(\phi_{\kappa_{0}}^{*}\lambda^{i})|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}\text{ for }1\leq i\leq n,\hskip 10.00002pt\text{ where }\phi_{\kappa_{0}}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to B^{n}(0,\mu_{1}),\hskip 10.00002pt\phi_{\kappa_{0}}(x)=\kappa_{0}\cdot x.

(5.4.3)

By Theorem
4.2
(also by Remark
4.2.1
), there is a
\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}
-diffeomorphism
F:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathbb{B}^{n}
, such that
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})
,
F_{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,F_{*}\lambda^{n}\in\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};T^{*}\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\|F_{*}\lambda-dy\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{12}
.

Let
Y=[Y_{1},\dots,Y_{n}]^{\top}
be a collection of vector fields which is the dual basis of
(F_{*}\lambda^{1},\dots,F_{*}\lambda^{n})
. We write
Y=:(I+A_{1})\nabla
, thus as matrices
F_{*}\lambda=(I+A_{1})^{-1}
. By Lemma
2.1.17

(ii)
and
\|F_{*}\lambda-dy\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{1}{12}
, we see that

\|A_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}=\|(F^{*}\lambda)^{-1}-I\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(2\|F_{*}\lambda-dy\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}})^{j}\leq\tfrac{1}{5}.\hskip 10.00002pt\text{In particular }\|I+A_{1}(F(0))\|_{C^{0}}\leq\tfrac{6}{5}.

(5.4.4)

Define an affine linear map

\psi(t):=(I+A_{1}(F(0)))\cdot\tfrac{t}{8}+F(0),\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(5.4.5)

Note that by (
5.4.4
) we have
\psi(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\frac{6}{5}\cdot\frac{1}{8}\mathbb{B}^{n}+F(0)\subset B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})
.

We define
\Phi_{1}:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to B^{n}(0,\mu_{1})
and
\Phi:\mathbb{B}^{n}\to\mathfrak{M}
by

\Phi:=\Phi_{0}\circ\Phi_{1},\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi_{1}(t):=\phi_{\kappa_{0}}\circ F^{\mathrm{Inv}}\circ\psi(t)=\kappa_{0}\cdot F^{\mathrm{Inv}}\left((I+A_{1}(F(0)))\cdot\tfrac{t}{8}+F(0)\right),\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(5.4.6)

Here
\Phi_{1}
is well-defined because
\psi(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subset B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})
,
B^{n}(F(0),\frac{1}{6})\subseteq F(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})
and
\phi_{\kappa_{0}}(\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n})\subset\mu_{1}\mathbb{B}^{n}
. Clearly
\Phi_{1}(0)=\kappa_{0}\cdot F^{\mathrm{Inv}}(F(0))=0
and thus
\Phi(0)=\Phi_{0}(0)=p_{0}
.

By (
5.4.3
),
\lambda^{i}\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}=\frac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\cdot(\phi_{\kappa_{0}}^{*}\theta^{i})\big|_{\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}}
, and the fact that
F^{\mathrm{Inv}}\circ\psi(\mathbb{B}^{n})\subseteq\frac{1}{3}\mathbb{B}^{n}
, we have

\displaystyle(\Phi_{1}^{*}\theta)(t)=[\Phi_{1}^{*}\theta^{1}(t),\dots,\Phi_{1}^{*}\theta^{n}(t)]

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}\psi^{*}(F_{*}\lambda)=\kappa_{0}dy\cdot\psi^{*}((I+A_{1}(y))^{-1})=\kappa_{0}\cdot d\psi(t)\cdot(I+A_{1}(\psi(t)))

(5.4.7)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\kappa_{0}}{8}dt\cdot(I+A_{1}(\psi(t)))^{-1}\cdot(I+A_{1}(F(0))).

Here we use
dy=[dy^{1},\dots,dy^{n}]
and
d\psi(t)=[d\psi^{1}(t),\dots,d\psi^{n}(t)]
as row vectors in (
5.4.7
).

Since
\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{1},\dots,\Phi_{0}^{*}X_{n}
and
\Phi_{1}^{*}\theta^{1},\dots,\Phi_{1}^{*}\theta^{n}
are dual basis to each other, we can write

\Phi_{0}^{*}X=:\tfrac{8}{\kappa_{0}}\cdot(I+A^{\prime})\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }A^{\prime}(t)=(I+A_{1}(F(0)))^{-1}\cdot(I+A_{1}(\psi(t)))-I,\hskip 10.00002ptt\in\mathbb{B}^{n}.

(5.4.8)

Taking
t=0
in (
5.4.8
), since
\psi(0)=F(0)
, we get
A^{\prime}(0)=0
.

Let
\widehat{K}:=\frac{8}{\kappa_{0}}
, since
\kappa_{0}=\kappa_{0}(n,\alpha,\mu_{1},M_{1})
depends only on
n,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0}
, we get
\widehat{K}=\widehat{K}(n,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0})
as desired.

Since
\|\frac{1}{8}(I+A_{1})\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{3}{20}<1
, by direct computation using the argument in the proof of Lemma
2.1.12
, we get
\|A_{1}\circ\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\|A_{1}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\frac{1}{5}
. Using the power series
A^{\prime}(t)=A_{1}(\psi(t))+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}A_{1}(F(0))^{j}\cdot(1+A_{1}(\psi(t)))
, we have

\|A^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\|A_{1}\circ\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}|A_{1}(F(0))|_{\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}}^{j}\|I+A_{1}\circ\psi\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\leq\tfrac{1}{5}+\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(\tfrac{1}{5})^{j}\cdot\tfrac{6}{5}=\tfrac{1}{2}.

This gives the desire control for
A^{\prime}
. In particular we see that
\|\Phi^{*}X^{\prime}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}=\|I+A\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}(\mathbb{B}^{n};\mathbb{R}^{n\times n})}\leq\frac{3}{2}
.

Finally the result
\|A^{\prime\prime}\|_{\mathscr{C}^{\alpha}}\lesssim_{n,m,\alpha,\mu_{0},M_{0}}1
follow from
[
SS18
, Lemma 9.33]
. Recall that in our notation we only consider use the spaces
C^{0,\alpha-1}_{X}
and
C^{1,\alpha-1}_{X}
.
∎
